**Dedication**

**To Gautama, the Supreme Arahant,**\
**who rediscovered the ancient path to liberation from worldly bondage.**

To the Elder Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs\
whose voices have crossed the centuries---\
those who left the household life,\
entered the holy life of renunciation,\
walked the liberation path taught by Gautama,\
and realized liberation from worldly bondage for themselves.

To these ancient bhante predecessors,\
whose testimonies preserve the joy,\
the training, the insights, and the realizations\
of those who sought the Deathless;\
whose words continue to speak across the ages,\
inviting us to examine our own practice\
and learn from those who walked before us.

To those who renounced what binds the mind to the world,\
who cultivated the mind,\
who saw deeply,\
who became disenchanted with the world,\
who abandoned worldly attachment,\
and who pursued the path beyond the world\
toward the Deathless realm of Nibbāna.

And to all sincere renunciants and yogis\
who still seek that same liberation today---\
who aspire not merely to find peace within the world,\
but to transcend worldly bondage\
and realize the Deathless.

**May these ancient voices be heard again.**\
**May their testimonies become living companions on the path.**\
**May their experiences illuminate our training.**\
**May their wisdom guide us toward deeper understanding,**\
**sincere practice, and the realization of liberation from worldly bondage.**

**\**

**Acknowledgments**

This book exists because the voices of the Elder Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs have survived.

Before acknowledging anyone else, I therefore wish to acknowledge those whose words fill these pages: the ancient elders of Gautama\'s Saṅgha who entered the holy life (*brahmacariya*), undertook the training, practised Gautama\'s Dhamma, and left behind their verses as testimonies to their experiences and attainments.

The *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* are far more than ancient literary collections. They preserve the voices of practitioners who walked the path of renunciation. Their verses speak of discipline, meditation, higher knowledge, disenchantment, dispassion, relinquishment, liberation, and the Deathless. They allow us to encounter the training and experiences of those who undertook the holy life, and they invite us to examine our own practice in light of their testimonies. Their words are the foundation upon which this book has been written.

I am deeply grateful for the preservation of the *Tipiṭaka*, particularly the Pāli textual tradition through which these ancient voices have been transmitted across more than two thousand years. The transmission of ancient texts inevitably raises questions of language, interpretation, historical development, and textual preservation. Nevertheless, the survival of these verses offers an extraordinary opportunity to encounter the testimonies attributed to the early disciples of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant (*Mahā Arahant*).

I offer my gratitude to the countless generations of bhikkhus and bhikkhunīs, teachers, reciters, scribes, translators, scholars, and practitioners whose efforts helped preserve the Dhamma through changing times and cultures. Without their dedication, these verses might never have reached us. Their preservation has made it possible for contemporary renunciants to listen to the experiences of their ancient predecessors and learn from their accounts of the path.

I am particularly grateful for the Pāli language itself. Working closely with the original terminology has repeatedly reminded me that words carry histories, contexts, and meanings that can become obscured through translation. Terms such as *Dhamma, brahmacariya, brahmavihāra, samādhi, paññā, nibbidā, virāga, vimutti,* and *Nibbāna* cannot always be reduced to convenient modern equivalents without losing important dimensions of their meaning. Throughout this work, I have therefore sought to remain attentive to the language of the texts, allowing the elders\' own vocabulary to guide my translations and interpretations as far as possible.

My gratitude also extends to the teachers and spiritual traditions that have shaped my understanding of the Arahanist path. My years of bhikkhu training at Pa-Auk Forest Monastery and Meditation Center in Myanmar, together with my practice of samādhi, study of the suttas, and experience of the renunciant life within the Saṅgha, have provided the practical background from which I approach these verses. The *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* preserve the testimonies of practitioners who undertook the training themselves. Their words deserve to be approached not only through intellectual analysis, but also through the perspective of someone engaged in the practice of renunciation.

This work is ultimately indebted to Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, the Tathāgata, who rediscovered and taught the ancient path to liberation. The elders whose voices appear in these collections did not merely develop independent philosophies or offer abstract reflections on existence. They undertook the training established by their Teacher, practised his Dhamma, and recorded their experiences of the path. Their testimonies direct our attention to the teaching they followed and the liberation they sought.

From the perspective of Arahanism, my deepest gratitude is directed toward Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, whose teaching points beyond worldly bondage toward the Deathless realm of Nibbāna. I am equally grateful to the generations of bhikkhus and bhikkhunīs who preserved his Dhamma and transmitted the ancient elders\' testimonies to the present day. Their dedication has made it possible for us to encounter these voices anew and to examine the liberation path they undertook.

I am also grateful to the many people whose questions, conversations, criticisms, encouragement, and sincere engagement have contributed to my thinking over the years. Not everyone who has influenced this work will agree with its conclusions, nor is agreement a necessary condition for meaningful intellectual engagement. Serious questions can be especially valuable when they compel us to return to the textual foundations, examine the evidence, and reconsider what we have understood.

That principle has been central to this work.

The purpose of *Voices of Liberation* is not to make the ancient elders conform to a predetermined modern theory. It is to listen to their testimonies as carefully as possible. As I wrote in the Prologue, **"Interpretation must not be allowed to erase the testimony of the practitioners themselves."**

I therefore acknowledge with respect those whose interpretations differ from mine. Their scholarship has contributed to the wider conversation within which this book has been written. Differences in the interpretation of Gautama\'s Dhamma need not prevent us from returning to the texts themselves. On the contrary, they can encourage more careful reading, deeper investigation, and a renewed examination of the path described by the ancient practitioners.

I am grateful to live in a time when ancient texts can be studied across languages, traditions, and geographical boundaries. Modern technology has made these sources accessible to readers who might otherwise never encounter them. This accessibility also brings responsibility. We must not simply repeat inherited interpretations without examination, but return to the sources, study their language and context, and investigate their meaning with intellectual independence and spiritual sincerity.

For me, this investigation has also been part of understanding Arahanism: the liberation path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant. This perspective places particular emphasis on the testimonies of those who undertook the holy life and pursued liberation from worldly bondage. It asks us to examine the ancient elders\' words on their own terms and to consider carefully what their accounts reveal about the training, its purpose, and its ultimate goal.

Above all, I am grateful to the readers who are willing to undertake this examination.

This book asks the reader to do something simple but demanding: **listen**.

Listen to the bhikkhus. Listen to the bhikkhunīs. Listen to those who renounced the household life, undertook the training, cultivated the mind, and investigated the nature of worldly existence. Listen to those who became disenchanted with the world, developed dispassion, relinquished attachment, and described their progress toward liberation. Listen carefully to what they considered essential, what they experienced, and what they said about the goal of their practice.

The title *Voices of Liberation* was chosen for precisely this reason. These elders are not merely subjects of later philosophical discussion. They are practitioners whose surviving verses bear witness to their lives of renunciation and their pursuit of liberation. Their words deserve to be heard not only as historical records, but also as testimonies that can inform the training of sincere renunciants today.

My final acknowledgment, therefore, belongs to the ancient elders themselves. Across more than two thousand years, their voices have continued to speak. Through their surviving words, they offer us the opportunity to learn from their experiences, compare notes with our predecessors, and examine our own practice with greater care.

May we have the humility to hear them.

May we have the discernment to understand them.

May we have the sincerity to examine our own practice in the light of their testimonies.

And may we have the determination to pursue the liberation path they undertook, toward the Deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**May the ancient voices of these Elder Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs be heard anew---not merely as echoes of the past, but as enduring testimonies to the path of liberation.**

## Preface: A Conversation with the Ancient Elders

Translating the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* as literally as possible, while writing extensive commentaries to explore their deeper meanings and historical connections with other ancient traditions, including the Vedas and Jain literature, has been a remarkably meaningful experience for me. This work has been far more than a scholarly exercise. It has also become a means of examining my own practice along the liberation path taught by Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

As I studied the testimonies of these ancient bhantes, I increasingly felt that I was comparing notes with fellow practitioners who had walked this path long before me. I wanted to understand how they trained, what they experienced, what they considered essential, and how they described their progress toward liberation. What had they discovered through renunciation? What practices had they cultivated? What difficulties had they overcome? What insights had brought them closer to the Deathless?

Their verses have inspired me, but they have also challenged me to examine my own practice honestly. Have I overlooked something important? Is there something I have not yet understood deeply enough? Is there an aspect of the training that I still need to cultivate? These questions have accompanied me throughout the work of translation and commentary. The ancient elders have thus become not merely subjects of study, but fellow renunciants whose testimonies invite me to scrutinize my own understanding and practice.

My interest in these verses extends beyond their immediate literary and historical context. I have also sought to explore their connections with the wider world of ancient renunciation, including Vedic and Jain traditions. Such comparisons help illuminate the spiritual environment in which these elders lived and trained, the ideas they encountered, and the distinctive character of the liberation path they undertook. Yet the verses themselves remain central: they preserve the voices of practitioners describing their own struggles, discoveries, and progress.

In a sense, this work has allowed me to enter into a conversation with our ancient Bhante predecessors, transcending the barriers of time and space through their surviving words. Though separated from us by centuries, they speak directly to questions that remain essential to the renunciant life. Their testimonies encourage us to look more deeply, practise more sincerely, and distinguish between merely understanding the teaching and undertaking the training that leads toward liberation. For me, this living dialogue with the ancient elders has been one of the most remarkable aspects of writing this book.

I have come to regard the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* as indispensable companions for those who sincerely undertake the renunciant path. They preserve something that abstract explanations alone cannot provide: the voices of those who actually walked the path, expressing their experiences in their own words. Their verses allow us to look beyond general principles and encounter the practice of liberation as lived and understood by individual renunciants.

It is my hope that *Voices of Liberation* will serve a similar purpose for renunciants today. Through literal translation, extensive commentary, and careful historical comparison, I seek to make these ancient testimonies accessible not merely as literature to be studied, but as spiritual guidance to be contemplated and applied in one\'s own training. May this collection become a trusted reference for those who wish to understand more deeply how the ancient elders practised, what they realized, and what their experiences can teach those who follow the liberation path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

The voices of these elders have survived across the centuries. May we approach them with the seriousness they deserve, learn from their experiences with humility, and allow their words to challenge us wherever our understanding or practice remains incomplete. Above all, may their testimonies encourage us to persevere in the training and pursue its ultimate goal: liberation from worldly bondage and the realization of the Deathless.

*May the aspiration for liberation remain steadfast in all who walk this path, until all defilements are extinguished and the Deathless is realized.*

## Prologue: Vedagū and Mahāvīra: The Forgotten Titles of Gautama the Supreme Arahant

### Who Was the Vedagū?

Modern portrayals of Gautama the supreme Arahant often present him primarily as a critic of Brahmanical religion, a reformer rejecting the authority of the Vedas and overturning the spiritual foundations of ancient India. Over centuries, this simplified narrative became deeply embedded within both popular Buddhism and modern academic interpretation. As a result, many have come to imagine Gautama Buddha as somehow disconnected from the Vedic civilization into which he was born.

Yet the historical and textual reality is far more complex.

Before Gautama became fully liberated under the bodhi tree, Siddhattha Gautama was born into a highly developed spiritual culture already shaped by centuries of the hybridization of the immigrated Vedic tradition and the local Śramaṇa traditions. Ancient India was not intellectually primitive. On the contrary, it possessed sophisticated systems of memorization, oral transmission, metaphysics, ethics, meditation, grammar, astronomy, ritual science, and spiritual training. The world into which the future Gautama the supreme Arahant emerged was already saturated with sacred language, symbolic systems, and existential inquiry concerning suffering, rebirth, the soul-spirit, immortality, cosmic law, and liberation.

The young Gautama therefore did not begin as an outsider to this world. He inherited it. Within the early Pāli Canon itself such as the Therigatha and Theragatha, he is sometimes described by such titles now largely forgotten in modern teaching: *Vedagū* and *Vīra or Mahāvīra*.

The word *Vedagū* carries profound meaning. Derived from *veda* (sacred knowledge, wisdom) and *gū* (one who has gone beyond, penetrated, or mastered), the title may be understood as:

- one who has gone to the end of the Vedas,

- one who has penetrated sacred wisdom,

- one who has mastered true knowledge,

- or one who fully understands the deeper meaning of wisdom itself.

This title is enormously significant because it challenges the simplistic assumption by mainstream academia that Gautama Buddha merely rejected the Vedic world wholesale. On the contrary, the title *Vedagū* suggests deep familiarity with Brahmanic Veda traditions already existing within ancient India. Gautama Buddha was not ignorant of the Vedic civilization; he emerged from within it, mastered its intellectual and contemplative environment, and ultimately perfected it by transcending its limitations through direct realization.

This distinction is essential. To transcend something is not necessarily to deny it. One must first understand a system deeply before moving beyond it. Gautama Buddha's criticism was frequently directed not toward Vedic wisdom itself, but toward its degeneration of attaching to ritualism, caste pride, animal sacrifice, and external religiosity disconnected from genuine liberation. Again and again within the Nikāyas, Gautama Buddha emphasized liberation knowledge, disenchantment from the world, samādhi concentration cultivation, and liberation from the world.

Yet even while critiquing certain practices, he continued using the vocabulary, cosmology, symbolic language, and spiritual concerns inherited from the broader Indian world. Terms such as brahmacariya, kamma, saṃsāra, deva, Brahmā, yoga-like meditative absorption, rebirth, renunciation, and liberation all existed within the spiritual environment surrounding him. Gautama Buddha transformed and clarified these concepts through direct insight, but he did not coin or invent any specific terminology except anatta which in my view is a later distortion of his true teaching of soul-denial as mainstream academia claim nowadays.

Understanding this context is therefore indispensable for understanding Gautama Buddha himself. Who educated the young Gautama? What sacred texts did he encounter? What philosophical questions already existed within his civilization? What ideals of renunciation and transcendence shaped his imagination before he left palace life? What ancient contemplative currents prepared the intellectual atmosphere from which the Dhamma later emerged?

These questions are rarely explored deeply within modern Buddhist discourse, yet they are crucial for reconstructing the historical and spiritual environment that shaped the future Buddha's mind.

The title *Vedagū* opens an important doorway into this forgotten dimension. It suggests that Gautama Buddha's awakening did not emerge from intellectual void, but from profound engagement with the sacred inheritance of ancient India. His quest began within a civilization already wrestling with mortality, suffering, cosmic order, sacrifice, purification, and immortality. The existential shock of aging, sickness, and death did not occur in a vacuum; it unfolded within a sophisticated spiritual world already searching for transcendence.

This book therefore proceeds from a simple but important proposition: If we wish to better understand Gautama Buddha's world, his education, his language, his symbolic framework, and the spiritual environment that shaped his *dhamma*, then we must revisit the ancient texts that formed that civilizational foundation --- foremost among them, the Vedas.

The purpose of this work is not to collapse Gautama's teaching into Vedic religion, nor to erase the revolutionary dimensions of Gautama Buddha's realization. Rather, it is to recover forgotten continuities, deeper historical context, and overlooked layers of meaning obscured through centuries of sectarian separation and modern reductionism from mainstream academia.

The title *Vedagū* reminds us that Gautama Buddha was not merely a renunciant who rebelled his traditional background and established his unique doctrine, but one who penetrated beyond its outer forms in search of the highest truth.

To understand the Vedagū is therefore to better understand the origins of one of humanity's greatest spiritual quests: the quest to overcome suffering, aging, and death itself.

### Why Has This Title Vedagū Been Forgotten?

The disappearance of the title *Vedagū* from mainstream memory is not the result of a single rupture, but of a layered historical transition in how Gautama Buddha was remembered, transmitted, and later systematized by tradition. A title such as *Vedagū*---which carries the sense of "one who has mastered the Vedas" or "one who has gone through the Vedic corpus to its completion"---belongs to an early cultural horizon in which Gautama was still situated within a Vedic-educated intellectual world. Over time, that horizon was gradually replaced by different frameworks of identity, authority, and textual preservation.

One major factor is the **shift from oral cultural memory to canonical consolidation**. In the centuries following Gautama Buddha's passing, Buddhist communities increasingly organized his teachings into structured collections---the Nikāyas and later Abhidhamma systems---where emphasis moved toward doctrinal precision and soteriological function. Titles that reflected his formative intellectual background, especially those rooted in the Vedic educational milieu, naturally receded in prominence as the canon prioritized terms such as *Buddha*, *Bhagavā*, *Tathāgata*, and *Sammāsambuddha*, which express his awakened status rather than his prior training. And when we talked about *Buddha*, or *awakening*, it already has shifted into a later Mahayana mentality because the early layer of Gautama's training is not about awakening, but purification of worldly bondage of the mind-heart *Citta* and liberation of the *citta* (*citta-vimutti*) from the world. And we are living in this Buddhist era.

A second factor lies in the **reorientation of religious identity after Gautama Buddha's liberation**. Within Buddhist memory, the defining moment of Gautama's life is his awakening, or rather liberation/becoming an arahant, under the Bodhi tree. Everything prior to that event is reframed as preparatory, subordinate, or instrumental to liberation. In this interpretive architecture, Vedic mastery becomes background rather than identity. A designation like *Vedagū*, which highlights scholarly attainment within the Vedic domain, does not serve the same doctrinal function as titles that express transcendence beyond all knowledge systems.

A third factor emerges from the **gradual divergence between Vedic and Śramaṇa traditions**. As Brahmanical and Buddhist schools developed distinct institutional identities, terminology associated with Vedic learning tended to be absorbed, reinterpreted, or simply left outside Buddhist doctrinal vocabulary. This was not an abrupt rejection, but a long process of differentiation in which each tradition refined its own linguistic markers of authority. Within that process, *Vedagū* remained culturally intelligible but lost institutional reinforcement.

A fourth layer is the **later historiographical filtering introduced by manuscript preservation and scholastic transmission**. What survives today is shaped by centuries of selective copying, commentary traditions, and regional textual preferences. Terms that were not central to liturgical recitation or doctrinal debate were less likely to be preserved with equal consistency. Over time, memory stabilizes around a narrower set of canonical names, and peripheral designations fade from active usage even when they remain historically meaningful.

Finally, there is the **modern interpretive lens itself**, which often reconstructs Gautama Buddha primarily through doctrinal and philosophical categories rather than educational biography. Contemporary presentations---both academic and devotional---tend to emphasize the universal and transcendent dimensions of Gautama Buddha's dhamma. As a result, earlier identity markers connected to his Vedic training are rarely foregrounded, even when they may offer important insight into his intellectual formation.

Taken together, these processes not only indicate a loss of historical substance, such as the most important "three knowledges" (*tisso vijjā*) \[from the Vedic source about cosmology, cosmic mankind history including *Brahma*, and the meaning of liberation from the world and the deathless realm of Nibbāna\] was completely lost into oblivion and mystified into psychic powers, but also a transformation of emphasis \[such as into emptiness (*suññatā*) and non-self (*anatta*)\].

The title *Vedagū* belongs to an earlier layer of remembrance---one that situates Gautama within the living educational world of the Vedic tradition before his full emergence as Gautama Buddha. Its fading reflects how traditions refine their language to express what they consider most essential, while earlier descriptors gradually move into the background of cultural memory.

### What Education Did Gautama Receive?

Siddhattha Gautama's early education must be understood within the intellectual ecology of 6th--5th century BCE northern India, where **Vedic learning, royal instruction, and Śramaṇa yogic practices** existed side by side as overlapping but distinct streams of knowledge and conduct. His formation was not monastic in the later Buddhist sense; it was a **composite aristocratic education**, designed for a young prince of the Śākya clan.

From a historical standpoint, Siddhattha received training in the **core disciplines of kṣatriya education**: governance, martial skill, ethics of rulership, and the cultivation of discernment required for leadership. This included instruction in **statecraft (rāja-nīti)**, administrative reasoning, and the strategic evaluation of conflict and social order. Such training belonged to the broader educational culture of the ruling clans of the Gangetic plains, where political authority, ethical formation, and intellectual cultivation were closely intertwined.

Alongside this, he would have been exposed to the **Vedic knowledge system**, which at the time functioned not merely as a collection of ritual texts but as a comprehensive civilizational framework. This included knowledge of:

- **Chandas** --- metrical structures and recitation

- **Śikṣā** --- phonetics and precise articulation

- **Kalpa** --- ritual and procedural frameworks

- **Vedic cosmology and sacred knowledge**

- **The oral transmission and interpretation of Vedic teachings**

This intellectual background is important for understanding a title such as *Vedagū*: one who has **gone through, penetrated, or mastered the field of Vedic knowledge**. The significance of *Vedagū* therefore need not be reduced to that of a Brahmin/Brahmana ritual specialist. It can instead be understood within a broader culture in which *veda* represented a body of sacred knowledge concerning the structure of existence, divine order, human beings, and the possibility of transcendence.

The Vedic knowledge inherited by the early Śramaṇa traditions was itself vast and layered, which include the most famous *Muni* tradition. It should not be reduced to ritualism alone as mainstream academia have always been mis-portraying. Within the Vedic and early Upaniṣadic material are profound inquiries concerning **cosmology, consciousness, causality, the cosmic mankind, death, immortality, and what lies beyond the manifested world**. Gautama and other Śramaṇa teachers entered this already-developed intellectual environment and sought to clarify, verify, and go beyond it through direct realization.

This is particularly important when we consider the ancient meaning of **vijjā**---knowledge, science, or an organized field of knowing. The Vedic tradition preserved the idea of **Trayī Vidyā**, the Threefold Knowledge, associated with the three Vedas, Ṛg, Yajur, and Sāman. Within the deeper structure of this knowledge, one finds three complementary functions: knowing **what reality is (cosmology)**, knowing **how its processes operate in terms of the history of cosmic mankind race**, and knowing **how those processes are brought to resolution or completion- that is the liberation from the worldly entrapment particularly for the mankind race**.

This background helps illuminate the later Śramaṇa expression **tisso vijjā**, the **Three Knowledges**. Rather than treating *tisso vijjā* as an isolated technical invention appearing without an intellectual background, it can be understood as part of Gautama's transformation of an older Indian conception of complete knowledge. The inherited question was not merely, "What should one believe?" It was: **What is this existence? How does it operate? And how can one transcend its limitations?**

In this sense, the Three Knowledges can be understood as the culmination of the very kind of education Gautama had inherited. And the very same knowledges he would have imparted to the first five disciples right after the perfection of his attainment of Arahantship under the bodhi tree.

The **first knowledge** concerns the cosmology, the structure and boundaries of existence: **what things are, what the world is, and where the limits of the manifested cosmos lie**. This corresponds to the ancient cosmological inquiry into the nature of the universe, its realms, its inhabitants, and the relationship between the individual mankind and the greater cosmic order. The Vedic conception of the cosmic *Puruṣa*, for example, presents mankind in a cosmic dimension: humanity is not understood merely as an isolated terrestrial phenomenon but as participating in a greater cosmic mankind race that extends beyond the visible world. The cosmic Puruṣa both permeates the manifested realm and extends beyond it.

This first knowledge therefore reaches beyond ordinary worldly education. It concerns **cosmic knowledge**---the knowledge of *loka*, its structure, its boundaries, and the distinction between what belongs to the manifested universe and what lies beyond it. The ancient inquiry into *sat* and *asat*, *mṛtyu* and *amṛtam*, space and what lies beyond space, already points toward the fundamental question that would become decisive for Gautama: **Is there a reality beyond the jurisdiction of the world that under the universal law of aging and death?**

The **second knowledge** concerns the **operation of the cosmic and existential process for the cosmic mankind race, its source (*Brahma*), history and evolution, and destination or destinations**: how mankind become bound within the universe, how desire gives rise to action, how action produces results, and how the resulting process continues the cycle of birth and death. The older Vedic material contains an extensive investigation of this causal structure. Desire (*kāma*) leads to action (*karma*), action generates consequences, and generated existence remains within the sphere of the conditioned world with aging and death.

Here the question changes from **"What is the world?"** to **"How does the world continue to operate?"** The universe is not merely a collection of objects; it is an ordered process governed by causality. Attraction, aversion, craving, ignorance, action, and result bind conscious beings to the continuing wheel of worldly existence. The ancient image of the cosmic wheel therefore becomes highly significant: existence is structured, conditioned, and maintained by forces that continually reproduce worldly experience.

The **third knowledge** concerns **resolution, completion, and liberation**. It asks not merely how to understand the world but how to go beyond its binding conditions. This is the deepest point at which Gautama's education turns from knowledge *about* existence to direct knowledge of **release from existence within the world**.

The ancient aspiration, *asato mā sad gamaya, tamaso mā jyotir gamaya, mṛtyor mā amṛtaṃ gamaya*---from the unreal lead me to the real, from darkness to the light, from death to the Deathless---expresses this movement toward transcendence. But Gautama takes the question further. The higher heavenly realms, although vastly superior to ordinary human existence, still belong to the cosmic order. They remain within *loka*. Even celestial existence is therefore not yet the final freedom.

This distinction is essential to understanding Gautama's unique accomplishment. **Liberation is not merely movement from a lower realm to a higher realm within the universe as the ascension teaching we are familiar about.** It is the complete release of the citta from identification with the conditioned world (*citta-vimutti*) and the final transcendence of *loka* itself. The purpose of purification is therefore not simply to obtain a better rebirth, a heavenly existence, or a refined state of consciousness, though they can be the step-stone, but to reach the **Deathless realm (dhātu) of Nibbāna**, beyond the jurisdiction of the universe and beyond the laws of decay and death that govern conditioned existence.

Seen in this light, the **Three Knowledges (*tisso vijjā*)** represent a complete science of liberation: **First: know the cosmos.** Know what *loka* is, its structure, its realms, its inhabitants, and its boundaries. **Second: know the mechanism.** Know how craving, action, causality, birth, death, and continued existence operate within that cosmic structure. **Third: know the way beyond.** Know how the citta is purified and released from the mechanisms of *loka*, and how the practitioner reaches the **Deathless beyond the world**.

This gives a deeper meaning to Gautama's intellectual formation. He did not begin with an empty mind and invent an entirely new vocabulary for existence. He inherited an extraordinarily old Indian investigation into **cosmology, cosmic mankind, causality, consciousness, death, immortality, and transcendence**. He encountered the questions preserved in the Vedic and Śramaṇa traditions and then subjected them to the highest possible test: **direct knowledge and attainment**.

The difference between inherited knowledge and Gautama's awakening is therefore not simply that he learned more doctrines. It is that he **completed the inquiry through direct attainment**. Knowledge of the cosmos by itself does not liberate. Knowledge of causality by itself does not liberate. Even knowledge of higher celestial states does not liberate. Liberation requires the final knowledge and conduct of **how to become completely free from the conditioned world and attain the Deathless**.

This also gives greater significance to *Vedagū*. The term can be understood as indicating completion or mastery within the field of Vedic knowledge, whereas the later realization of the Three Knowledges represents a decisive movement beyond knowledge as inherited learning into **knowledge verified directly by the liberated citta**.

Importantly, this does not mean that Gautama simply discarded the Vedic inheritance. Rather, he **entered into the deepest questions already present within ancient Indian civilization and carried them to their ultimate existential conclusion**. Where earlier traditions investigated the cosmos, he investigated the possibility of transcending it. Where they spoke of *amṛtam*, he sought the truly Deathless. Where they described higher worlds, he sought the reality beyond *loka*. Where they investigated the cosmic mankind, he investigated the destiny of the individual citta and its possibility of complete liberation.

The education of Siddhattha Gautama can therefore be understood as a progression from **civilizational knowledge to direct liberating knowledge**: **Vedic knowledge → Kṣatriya formation → Śramaṇa inquiry → Three Knowledges → direct realization → Vimutti.**

In the broader treatment of the *Bhagavad Gītā* and the ancient Indian intellectual background, this becomes especially significant. The *Gītā*, the Vedic corpus, the early Upaniṣadic material, and the Śramaṇa traditions all participate in a vast Indian inquiry into **action, consciousness, cosmic existence, identity, death, and liberation**. Gautama stands within that civilizational continuum, but his decisive contribution is to take the question of liberation to its final point: **not accommodation within the universe, but complete release from *loka* and entry into the Deathless realm of Nibbāna.**

This is why the Three Knowledges are so important for understanding what Gautama actually became. They are not merely three supernatural abilities later Buddhists mystified it, out of pride and vanity, and added to an already completed religious career. They represent the completion of the ancient Indian quest for knowledge: **to know what exists, to know how existence operates, and to know the way completely beyond it.**

### What Spiritual World Shaped Him Before Renunciation?

Before Siddhattha Gautama's renunciation, he stood inside a **dense and highly developed spiritual civilization** that extended far beyond ritual religion. Ancient India already contained multiple, overlapping traditions of **direct spiritual experience, inner purification, renunciation, meditation, samādhi, tapas (primal spiritual force), and liberation**. These traditions did not all use the same terminology, follow identical disciplines, or pursue precisely the same destination, but they shared a fundamental recognition: **ordinary human existence is not the highest possible condition of consciousness**.

The spiritual world surrounding Siddhattha therefore included much more than a single Vedic or yogic system. It included the ancient **Ṛṣi tradition, Muni tradition, forest-dweller traditions, Yoga and Samādhi disciplines, Brahmacariya, tapas, wandering Samaṇas, Jain ascetics, and other renunciant practitioners** who had withdrawn from ordinary society to investigate the deepest possibilities of human consciousness.

This was a civilization of **experiential transcendence and ascension to a higher existence**.

The central question was not merely what a person should believe, but what a human being could **directly know, experience, purify, and ultimately transcend** through disciplined transformation of the citta.

**(1). The Ṛṣi Tradition: Seers of Direct Vision**

At one of the deepest foundations stands the **ṛṣi tradition**---the culture of the seers. The ṛṣi was not simply a scholar or theologian. The ṛṣi represented a human being whose consciousness had been refined sufficiently to **see, perceive, and directly know realities beyond ordinary sensory cognition**. The essential movement was from ordinary perception toward **direct vision (*darśana*)**:

- perception becomes inwardly stabilized;

- ordinary sensory limitation is transcended;

- subtle realities become accessible to refined consciousness;

- knowledge arises through direct seeing rather than ordinary conceptual speculation.

The Ṛṣi tradition therefore represents one of the oldest Indian memories of **human beings who could cross the ordinary boundary of human cognition**.

The forest seer was particularly important. The forest was not merely a place of physical isolation. It became a spiritual laboratory in which the practitioner could withdraw from society, reduce worldly stimulation, cultivate tapas the primal spiritual force, stabilize consciousness, and investigate the deeper structure of existence.

The ancient forest tradition therefore created an environment in which **Vedic knowledge and direct meditative experience could meet**. The seer did not merely preserve sacred knowledge intellectually. He sought to **verify the deeper meaning of that knowledge through transformed consciousness**.

**(2). The Muni Tradition: Silence, Wandering, and Inner Transcendence**

Alongside the ṛṣi tradition---and deeply connected with it---stands the **Muni tradition**.

This tradition is especially important for understanding the spiritual environment into which Siddhattha was born because the Muni represents a figure who has already stepped away from ordinary social identity and turned consciousness inward.

The ancient **Keśin/Muni tradition** preserves the image of extraordinary wandering practitioners who lived outside ordinary social structures, practiced silence, cultivated breath and inner energy, and sought states of consciousness beyond ordinary human limitation. The Muni is therefore not simply a "silent person." **Mauna is an inner discipline.** Silence becomes a means of withdrawing consciousness from worldly proliferation so that direct knowledge can arise. The Muni tradition emphasizes:

- **mauna** --- profound inner silence;

- wandering outside conventional social structures;

- physical and mental seclusion;

- control of sensory activity;

- breath and vital-force cultivation;

- celibacy and renunciation;

- tapas and inner spiritual force;

- deep contemplation and concentration;

- direct knowledge of realities beyond ordinary perception.

The image of the Muni is especially significant because it shows that the **renunciant ideal existed within ancient Indian civilization long before Siddhattha's own renunciation**. The Muni had already stepped outside the ordinary identity of householder, clan member, social rank, and material possession. He had entered a different mode of existence in which the primary field of work was the **citta itself**. This is why the later appearance of **Sākyamuni** is so meaningful. Siddhattha Gautama became not merely a renunciant but **the Muni of the Śākya clan**---a continuation and culmination of an ancient Indian ideal of the solitary, inwardly awakened sage.

In the early Pāḷi tradition, the Muni is characterized by withdrawal from worldly association, freedom from sensory attachment, inner stillness, direct knowledge, and liberation. The Muni is one who becomes **unshaken by the world rather than controlled by it**. One famous example is the founding father of India, Bharata, whose name became the official name of Indian continent, is indeed student of a Muni and whose throne is ensured by him. Thus, the Muni tradition forms an important bridge between the ancient Ṛṣi world and the Samaṇa world.

**(3). The Forest Tradition: Leaving Society to Transform Consciousness**

The **forest (*arañña*)** was another major component of this spiritual civilization. The movement into the forest represented more than geographical relocation. It was a deliberate withdrawal from the structures that continually reinforce worldly identity.

Village and household life are filled with: family identity, social recognition, possessions, status, sensual stimulation, competition, obligation and collective mental activity.

The forest provided the opposite environment. Here the practitioner could become **physically separated from collective consciousness** and turn attention toward the purification of the inner being. This principle appears across the ancient Indian renunciant traditions. The forest became the natural environment of the **ṛṣi, muni, yogin, tapasvin, and samaṇa**. In this sense, the forest tradition should not be treated as a marginal development. It was one of the great laboratories of ancient Indian spiritual civilization.

The practitioner entered the forest to discover whether the truths transmitted through sacred knowledge could actually be **realized within consciousness**. This creates an important continuity: **Vedic knowledge → forest seership → Muni discipline → Yoga and Samādhi → renunciation → direct liberation.** Siddhattha entered this world when it was already mature.

**(4). Yoga: The Technology of Consciousness Transformation**

Within this broader environment existed a wide spectrum of **yogic disciplines**. Yoga should not be reduced to the modern meaning of physical postures. In the ancient spiritual context, Yoga represented disciplined methods for **union with the unmanifested force, controlling, stabilizing, and transforming consciousness**.

These practices could include: breath regulation, sensory withdrawal, concentration, mental fixation, bodily discipline, celibacy, withdrawal from worldly stimulation, contemplation, meditation, deep absorption and more.

The underlying discovery was profound: **human consciousness is trainable.** The ordinary condition of the citta is not necessarily its highest condition. Through systematic discipline, consciousness can become increasingly stable, subtle, powerful, and independent of ordinary sensory activity. The yogic practitioner therefore did not merely think about transcendence. He **trained consciousness to enter it**.

**(5). Samādhi: Entering Realities Beyond Ordinary Consciousness**

At the higher end of this discipline stood **Samādhi**. Samādhi represents the stabilization and unification of consciousness to a degree far beyond ordinary distracted awareness. As sensory activity becomes progressively quiet, consciousness can enter increasingly refined states.

This spiritual technology allowed practitioners to investigate realms and modes of consciousness inaccessible through ordinary sensory experience. The significance of Samādhi in the ancient Indian world is therefore enormous. It established an experiential basis for the belief that: **the visible human condition is not the entire range of existence.**

Higher states of consciousness could correspond to higher planes of existence, including the **Brahma realms** and other refined dimensions of *loka*. Yet this would eventually become one of the most important distinctions in Gautama's path. **A higher realm is still a realm within loka.** A higher state of consciousness is not automatically final liberation. The practitioner could ascend through increasingly refined states and still remain within conditioned existence.

**(6). Tapas: The Spiritual Force of Purification**

Another major dimension of this ancient world was **tapas**. Tapas should not be reduced to physical self-torture or just austere fasting as the mainstream academia is mis-portraying. In the deeper spiritual sense, tapas represents the **primordial spiritual force directly from the soul-spirit ātmā, generated through disciplined practice**, capable of purifying consciousness and burning away defilements. The ancient practitioner cultivated this force through: restraint, celibacy, solitude, meditation, sensory control, vigilance, disciplined living, and sustained concentration.

Tapas therefore belongs to the same larger technology of transformation as Yoga and Samādhi. It is the **internal fire of spiritual practice**.

Gautama did not reject tapas at all as modern mainstream academia always wrongly claim in every text book on Buddhism. His path clearly adopts genuine spiritual exertion by distinguishing from useless body torment. The purpose of spiritual exertion is the purification of the citta and the destruction of the forces that keep consciousness bound to the world.

Thus **tapas, Samādhi, and Paññā** eventually become integrated into the liberation path rather than treated as separate phenomena.

**(7). Brahmacariya: The Renunciant Life**

Another important element of this spiritual civilization was **Brahmacariya**, the holy celibate and discipline life. Brahmacariya represented far more than sexual restraint alone. It expressed a complete reorientation of life toward spiritual purification.

The practitioner deliberately abandoned the ordinary cycle of: **sensory pleasure → attachment → worldly identity → action → consequence → continued existence.** Instead, the practitioner cultivated: **Sīla → Brahmacariya → Samādhi → Paññā → purification → liberation.**

Celibacy, simplicity, restraint, solitude, and disciplined consciousness were therefore not isolated moral practices. They formed a **technology for freeing the citta from worldly conditioning**.

This is why renunciation could become such a powerful force in ancient India. The practitioner was not merely leaving society or renouncing all actions. He was attempting to **leave behind the consciousness produced by society**.

**(8). The Samaṇa Tradition: Active Spiritual Exertion**

Alongside the Muni and forest traditions stood the wider **Samaṇa (Śramaṇa) tradition**, which is exactly what it means by the modern word shaman. It represents not only a group of people or ethnicity, but also culture and spirituality.

The Samaṇa represents the practitioner who undertakes deliberate spiritual exertion in order to overcome worldly bondage. The word itself carries the sense of **exertion, striving, stilling, and pacification**. Within the Samaṇa world, practitioners experimented with: severe restraint, fasting and dietary discipline, celibacy, solitude, meditation, tapas, sensory restraint, concentration, investigation of kamma, rebirth and saṃsāra and the possibility of liberation.

The Samaṇa tradition therefore added another dimension to the ancient spiritual field: **zealous (*appamāda*) practical experimentation**. The question was not merely whether liberation existed. The practitioner had to **practice and discover whether liberation could actually be attained**.

This world included different lineages and different conclusions. Jain ascetics, Ājīvika practitioners, and other wandering renunciants all participated in the larger culture of radical spiritual experimentation, although their doctrines and methods were not identical.

The important point for understanding Siddhattha is that **renunciation, wandering, celibacy, tapas, meditation, and liberation were already living possibilities in the world around him**.

**(9). Jain and Other Ascetic Traditions**

The Jain tradition represents another major expression of this renunciant civilization. The **Jain muni** embodied an extraordinarily rigorous discipline of non-possession, restraint, celibacy, non-harming, sensory control, and tapas. Jain spiritual practice also developed highly systematic approaches to the purification of the individual soul and the elimination of karmic bondage.

The similarities with other renunciant traditions are striking: leaving household life, abandoning possessions, celibacy, wandering, solitude, restraint, vigilance, tapas, purification, and liberation from worldly rebirth. At the same time, the Jain path developed its own distinctive understanding of the soul, karma, bondage, and liberation. This diversity is important. Siddhattha was not entering a world with only **one spiritual path**. He was entering a civilization in which different practitioners were already asking the same enormous questions from different directions:

**What is the human being? What is consciousness? Why are beings bound to the world? What is the origin of suffering? What survives death? Can rebirth be ended? Is there a reality beyond the world? And can a human being directly realize it?** These were living questions.

**(10). Brahma, Devas, and the Higher Cosmic Realms**

The spiritual world surrounding Siddhattha also included an elaborate conception of **devas, Brahma, higher realms, and cosmic existence**. Ancient Indian spiritual knowledge did not limit reality to the physical human world. The cosmos was understood as containing multiple levels of existence, from coarse sensory existence to increasingly refined realms. At the highest levels of the Rūpa realm were the **Brahma worlds**, associated with extraordinarily refined consciousness and existence.

This cosmology was not merely mythology in the framework of the contemplative traditions. Higher states of Samādhi were understood to correspond to higher modes of existence. Thus the ancient practitioner could conceive of a genuine **continuum of consciousness**: **ordinary human existence → refined human consciousness → Brahma consciousness → higher cosmic states → and ultimately the possibility of transcendence beyond loka.**

This distinction is fundamental to understanding Gautama. The higher cosmic realms are exalted, but they are still **within the universe**. They are still bound by the universal law of decay and dissolution. They are not the final Deathless. Therefore, even the highest achievement available through the existing spiritual technologies could still leave the practitioner within the cosmic structure of *loka*.

**(11). The Divine-Human Continuum**

Across these traditions runs a powerful ancient intuition: **human existence is not the final boundary of consciousness.** The ṛṣi, muni, yogin, tapasvin, samaṇa, and Brahmacariya practitioner all represent different expressions of the same larger possibility---the transformation of the human being beyond ordinary worldly limitation.

The human being could cultivate consciousness until: sensory limitation was weakened, the citta became extraordinarily stable, higher states became accessible, direct knowledge arose, attachment to worldly existence diminished, consciousness became aligned with higher realities and liberation became an actual possibility.

This created a **continuum between human and divine modes of existence**. Yet Gautama would ultimately distinguish between **ascending within that continuum** and **escaping the entire conditioned continuum**. That distinction is crucial.

**(12). The Spiritual World Siddhattha Inherited**

Siddhattha therefore did not grow up in a spiritually empty civilization. He inherited a vast field of accumulated experimentation: **Ṛṣis** had cultivated direct vision. **Munis** had cultivated silence, solitude, and inner transcendence. **Forest seers** had withdrawn from society to verify sacred knowledge through direct experience. **Yogins** had developed techniques for transforming consciousness. **Samādhi practitioners** had entered refined states beyond ordinary sensory consciousness. **Tapasvins** had cultivated spiritual force for purification. **Brahmacārins and renunciants** had dedicated their lives to the holy life. **Samaṇas** had wandered beyond conventional society in search of liberation. **Jain munis and other ascetics** had developed rigorous systems of restraint, non-possession, tapas, and release. **Brahma-oriented practitioners** had sought higher cosmic existence and communion with the divine.

All of these streams formed part of the spiritual environment into which Siddhattha was born. They were not necessarily one system, and they did not all teach the same final destination. But together they created an extraordinarily mature **civilization of spiritual experimentation**.

**(13). What Siddhattha Discovered Beyond Them**

What distinguishes Siddhattha is therefore not that he discovered meditation, renunciation, Yoga, Samādhi, tapas, Brahmacariya, or the existence of higher realms. He entered a world in which these were already known. His unique accomplishment was to **follow these ancient technologies of transcendence to their ultimate limit**.

He discovered that even the highest conditioned existential states remain within the structure of becoming. Even the most exalted existence that almost all renunciates practice to ascend to, like his two former teachers, the arūpa realm existence remains within *loka*. Even refined consciousness can remain conditioned. Even extraordinary spiritual attainment does not automatically constitute final liberation. This was the critical threshold.

Siddhattha did not simply reject the spiritual world that had shaped him. He **passed through it**. And he discovered the path to the Deathless is through the culmination of the rūpa realm. This is called the middle way because it is not through the lowest, coarse and vulgar kāma realm, not through the highest and exalted arūpa realm where individuality was thinned out, but the middle rūpa realm of the Brahma abodes.

He thus took the ancient disciplines of renunciation, Samādhi, tapas, Brahmacariya, and direct knowledge and brought them into a systematic training path of **Sīla → Samādhi / Jhāna → Paññā → Nibbidā → Virāga → Vimutti**. The purpose was no longer merely to ascend within the universe. It was to **become completely free from the universe**.

**(14). From the Ancient Spiritual World to the Ancient Path**

This gives a deeper meaning to Gautama's later identification with the **ancient path (*purāṇa-magga / purāṇa-mārga*)**. He did not create spiritual civilization from nothing. He inherited the ancient Indian memory of the ṛṣis, Munis, forest renunciants, yogins, Brahmacārins, tapasvins, Samaṇas, and contemplatives who had already turned away from ordinary worldly existence and sought direct knowledge.

But Gautama brought this inheritance to its **highest operational completion**. He distinguished the higher from the ultimate. He distinguished **Samādhi from Vimutti**. He distinguished **higher loka from Nibbāna-dhātu**. He distinguished **refined existence from complete liberation**. And he established the path by which the citta could be purified from rāga, dosa, and moha, cease its attachment to conditioned existence, and ultimately transcend the jurisdiction of *loka*. Thus, the spiritual world that shaped Siddhattha before renunciation was not a single religion or school.

It was a **vast Indian civilization of seers, Munis, yogins, forest practitioners, Brahmacārins, tapasvins, Samaṇas, and contemplatives**, all exploring different dimensions of the same great human possibility: **that consciousness can be transformed, that ordinary worldly existence can be transcended, and that the human being may seek a reality beyond death.**

Siddhattha Gautama entered this ancient field as a seeker. He left it as **Gautama the Supreme Arahant**, having carried the ancient quest beyond the highest conditioned realms and opened the direct path to complete liberation from *loka* and entry into the **Deathless realm of Nibbāna**.

### The Epithet Vīra (Mahāvīra) for Gautama

In the study of early Pāli canonical literature, the epithets assigned to Gautama Buddha provide crucial historical and philological evidence regarding the cultural milieu of Greater Magadha in the 6th--5th centuries BCE. Among the titles frequently invoked by his direct disciples---such as *Tathāgata*, *Arahant*, *Sugato*, and *Sammāsambuddha*---the term ***Vīra*** (\"Hero\" or \"Valiant One\") and its compound ***Mahāvīra*** (\"Great Hero\") hold a unique place.

Far from being isolated literary flourishes, these terms reflect the shared spiritual, ascetic, and linguistic matrix of the ancient Śramaṇa spirituality. In particular, *Mahāvīra* is historically recognized as the primary title of Vardhamāna, the 24th Tīrthaṅkara of Jainism. The frequent adoption of *Vīra* and *Mahāvīra* by Gautama's early disciples highlights how the early Saṅgha actively utilized the established vocabulary of Greater Magadha to articulate Gautama's spiritual triumph over internal defilements (*rāga*, *dosa*, *moha*) and the forces of Māra.

**Canonical Evidence: Examples from the Pāli Canon**

The usage of *Vīra* and *Mahāvīra* as epithets for Gautama Buddha appears across multiple early Pāli collections, most notably in the *Theragāthā*, *Therīgāthā*, *Sutta Nipāta*, and *Dhammapada*.

**(1). Theragāthā 1.47 (*Ujjaya Thera*)**

In *Theragāthā* 1.47, Venerable Ujjaya Thera utters a verse of homage directly addressing Gautama Buddha as *Vīra*: *Namo te buddha vīratthu, vippamuttosi sabbadhi; Tuyhāpadāne viharaṁ, viharāmi anāsavo ti.*

\"Homage to you, O Buddha, may homage be to you, O Hero (*vīra*)! You are fully liberated from everything in all respects; living following your footsteps, I live without defilement (*anāsavo*)!\"

As noted in textual commentary: \"The title Vīra (\'Hero\' or \'Valiant One\') was a widely recognized term across ancient Indian Śramaṇa traditions for a spiritual conqueror. In Jainism, it became the primary name of the 24th Tīrthaṅkara (Mahāvīra---\'Great Hero\'). In early Pāli literature, Gautama Buddha is frequently addressed as Vīra because spiritual victory in the Śramaṇa traditions was understood not as conquering external kingdoms, but as overcoming internal defilements\...\"

**(2). Theragāthā 1.8 (*Vīra Thera*)**

In *Theragāthā* 1.8, the elder bhikkhu who bears the very name *Vīra* uses the title to describe the spiritual victory of a true renunciate: *Yo duddamiyo damena danto, Vīro santusito vitiṇṇakaṅkho; Vijitāvī apetalomahaṁso, Vīro so parinibbuto ṭhitatto ti.*

*\"He who was difficult to tame, disciplined through self-control; a hero (vīro), fully content, having crossed over doubt; a conqueror (vijitāvī), fearless; that hero (vīro) is completely unbound (parinibbuto), centering life internally on the soul-spirit (attā / ātmā).\"*

**(3). Sutta Nipāta (The *Māgaṇḍiya Sutta* & *Salla Sutta*)**

In the *Sutta Nipāta*, one of the oldest strata of the Pāli Canon, Gautama Buddha is repeatedly called *Vīra* and *Mahāvīra* both by interlocutors and by his own direct disciples. In the *Māgaṇḍiya Sutta* (Sn 4.9 / v. 845), the Buddha is addressed as *Mahāvīra*: *\"Kathaṁdassī kubbidho sīlavā ti\... obhāsayasi mahāvīra\...\"*

\"Possessing what vision, what conduct\... do you shine forth, O Great Hero (Mahāvīra)?\"

In the *Salla Sutta* (Sn 3.8 / v. 587), the text declares: *\"Tasmā dhīrā na socanti, viditvā lokaniyyatiṁ; Sutāvato mahāvīra, na hi so socate naro.\"*

\"Therefore the wise do not grieve, knowing the nature of the world; having heard the teaching of the Great Hero (Mahāvīra), that person does not sorrow.\"

**(4). Therīgāthā (Verses of the Elder Bhikkhunīs)**

The elder bhikkhunīs also frequently invoked *Vīra* and *Mahāvīra* when declaring their realization and offering veneration to Gautama. In *Therīgāthā* 1.7 (*Vīrā Therī*), the elder bhikkhunī connects her own spiritual name and practice with the heroics of the path: *Vīrā vīrehi dhammehi, khīṇāvesā jhāyikā; Dhāreti antimaṁ dehaṁ, jetvā māraṁ savāhanaṁ.*

*\"Vīrā, endowed with heroic qualities (vīrehi dhammehi), free from taints, absorbed in jhāna; she bears her final body, having conquered Māra and his army.\"*

Furthermore, in *Therīgāthā* 5.11 (*Sundarī Therī*), Sundarī praises Gautama using the explicit compound *Mahāvīra*: \"Vande taṁ mahāvīra, uttamamaporisaṁ\...\"

*\"I pay homage to you, O Great Hero (Mahāvīra), supreme among human beings\...\"*

**Historical Significance and Shared Śramaṇa Vocabulary - Greater Magadha Śramaṇa Milieu (Shared Technical Vocabulary)**

- **Jain Tradition (Nigaṇṭha Nātaputta / Vardhamāna Mahāvīra)**

  - **Primary Epithet:** Mahāvīra

  - **Primary Title:** Tīrthaṅkara

  - **Mendicant Term:** Bhikkhu

  - **Key Concept:** Tapas, Kevala

  - **Goal:** Mokṣa / Siddha

- **Early Gautama Saṅgha (Disciples of Gautama the Supreme Arahant)**

  - **Frequent Epithet:** Vīra / Mahāvīra

  - **Primary Title:** Tathāgata / Arahant

  - **Mendicant Term:** Bhikkhu

  - **Key Concept:** Tapas, Sīla, Samādhi and Paññā

  - **Goal:** Nibbāna-Dhātu

**(1). The Jain Origin and Chronology**

Vardhamāna Mahāvīra (Nigaṇṭha Nātaputta in Pāli texts) was an elder contemporary of Gautama Buddha in the region of Greater Magadha. In the Jain Āgamas (such as the *Ācārāṅga Sūtra* and *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra*), Vardhamāna is predominantly referred to as *Mahāvīra* (\"Great Hero\").

Because the Jain order established by Mahāvīra (and preceding traditions tracing back to Pārśvanātha) had already popularized *Mahāvīra* as the ideal label for an all-conquering ascetic, the term possessed established cultural currency across the Magadhan urban centers and forest hermitages.

**(2). The Semantic Shift: Conquest Over Internal Defilements**

In classical Brahmanical or Vedic usage, a *vīra* was a martial warrior, a chieftain, or a heroic protector of sacrificial boundaries. In the Śramaṇa movement, however, the concept of heroism was fundamentally re-engineered:

**External Conquest vs. Internal Victory:** Martial heroes (*vīra*) conquered neighboring kingdoms, physical armies, and material territory through weapons. The Śramaṇa hero (*vīra* / *mahāvīra*) conquered internal enemies---namely *rāga* (lust/attachment), *dosa* (fear/hatred/ill-will), and *moha* (delusion/ignorance).

**Conquest of Māra:** In the Pāli Canon, Gautama is termed *Vīra* specifically because he sat at the foot of the Bodhi tree and repelled the army of Māra (*Mārasena*) without physical weapons, relying entirely on *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, *Paññā*, and the internal power of *Tapas*.

**(3). The Broader Matrix of Shared Terminology**

The adoption of *Vīra* and *Mahāvīra* by Gautama's disciples was not an isolated borrowing, but part of a wide-ranging linguistic overlap of Greater Magadha. Both Jainism and Gautama Saṅgha shared lexicon:

**Vīra / Mahāvīra**: **Śramaṇa Context / Meaning:** Spiritual Hero / Great Victor over internal defilements. **Application in Early Texts:** Applied to Vardhamāna in Jainism; frequently applied to Gautama Buddha in Pāli Suttas.

**Arahant / Arihant**: **Śramaṇa Context / Meaning:** Worthy One / One who has slain internal enemies. **Application in Early Texts:** Central Jain title (*Arihant* in the Navkar Mantra); primary description of Gautama and his liberated disciples.

**Bhikkhu**: **Śramaṇa Context / Meaning:** Wandering alms-mendicant who has renounced property. **Application in Early Texts:** Term for Jain mendicants in Ardhamāgadhī Āgamas; term for Gautama's ordained disciples.

**Jina: Śramaṇa Context / Meaning:** Victor / Conqueror. **Application in Early Texts:** Epithet for Jain Tīrthaṅkaras; also used by Gautama Buddha (e.g., *Māradisā jinitā me* / \"I am a victor over Māra\").

**Tapas**: **Śramaṇa Context / Meaning:** Spiritual force / Internal energy that burns away defilements. **Application in Early Texts:** Central ascetic discipline in Jainism; utilized in the Pāli Canon (*ātāpī*) to describe intense meditative exertion.

**Nibbāna / Nirvāṇa**: **Śramaṇa Context / Meaning:** Unbinding / Complete quenching of karmic and defilement fires. **Application in Early Texts:** Final liberation in both traditions.

The presence of the titles *Vīra* and *Mahāvīra* throughout the *Theragāthā*, *Therīgāthā*, and *Sutta Nipāta* confirms that early disciples of Gautama Buddha actively used the highest spiritual honorifics of their era to praise their Teacher.

Originating within the Śramaṇa culture of Greater Magadha and prominently established by Vardhamāna Mahāvīra's tradition, the title *Vīra* signified the ultimate conqueror---one who had mastered the mind, broken the bonds of worldly existence (*bhava*), and cleared the path to the Deathless (*Nibbāna-Dhātu*). By addressing Gautama as *Vīra* and *Mahāvīra*, the early Pāli reciters declared that Gautama Buddha was the supreme spiritual hero of his age, having achieved complete conquest over the forces of Māra and the cycle of involuntary reincarnation.

## Arahanism and the Question of Liberation

### A Historic Declaration for the Restoration of the Liberation Path

The clarification of Arahanism presented in this book is intended to mark a significant moment in the continuing history of humanity's search for spiritual liberation. It is not merely the declaration of another religious identity or the establishment of another school of thought. It is a call to reconsider a fundamental question that deserves to stand at the centre of spiritual life: **What is the ultimate purpose of Gautama's teaching, and what path leads to complete liberation from worldly bondage?**

Arahanism identifies Arahantship, complete worldly renunciation, and the eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* as the central purpose of Gautama's liberation path. It maintains that spiritual practice must extend beyond the cultivation of virtue, the development of elevated states of consciousness, or the pursuit of spiritual ideals within the world. Its ultimate objective is complete liberation from worldly bondage.

This declaration also calls for a renewed examination of the historical development of Gautama's teaching. Across the centuries, different religious ideals, scriptural traditions, and interpretations of awakening (Buddhahood) have shaped what it means to follow his path. Arahanism questions whether these developments have obscured the original centrality of renunciation and Arahantship, particularly as the aspiration to Buddhahood and the Bodhisattva ideal assumed greater prominence in many Buddhist traditions. Through a historical and doctrinal inquiry, it seeks to reopen the question of how the liberation-centred teaching of Gautama came to be understood in such different ways.

At the heart of this inquiry lies a fundamental question concerning the nature of the human being: **Does complete liberation require the denial of the Soul, or does the prevailing interpretation of *anattā* require renewed examination?** Arahanism maintains that the human being possesses a true spiritual essence, which it identifies with *attā* or Ātman---the Soul. It challenges the categorical denial of the Soul found in prevailing interpretations of Gautama's teaching and argues that the rejection of the five aggregates as a permanent self does not, by itself, establish the nonexistence of the Soul. This position calls for careful examination of the early texts, the meaning of their terminology, and the philosophical assumptions through which they have been interpreted.

These questions are inseparable from the purpose of renunciation. If the nature of the human being and the meaning of liberation are misunderstood, the direction of spiritual practice may also be misunderstood. Arahanism therefore seeks to restore the systematic path of *Sīla, Samādhi, Paññā, Nibbidā, Virāga,* and *Vimutti* to the centre of religious life, guiding practitioners towards the complete eradication of worldly craving and attachment, fear and hatred, ignorance and delusion.

The significance of this declaration lies in its invitation to examine inherited assumptions rather than accept them automatically. It calls upon monks, nuns, Buddhist practitioners, and all sincere seekers of truth to reconsider the relationship between renunciation, Arahantship, *attā*, *anattā*, and the ultimate purpose of Gautama's teaching. Its intention is not simply to establish a different religious identity, but to renew the inquiry into what complete liberation from worldly bondage means and how it may be realized.

Whether this declaration will come to be recognized as a historic turning point cannot be determined by the declaration itself. Its significance must ultimately be measured by what it enables people to understand, practise, and realize. Yet its aspiration is clear: to reopen a path that Arahanism understands as the essential purpose of Gautama's teaching, challenge interpretations it believes have obscured that path, and make the pursuit of complete liberation from worldly bondage intelligible to future generations.

If this effort enables even one person to question inherited assumptions, investigate the truth sincerely, undertake the discipline of renunciation, and pursue Arahantship with unwavering determination, its significance will extend beyond the written word. It will have become a living contribution to humanity's continuing search for liberation.

**Arahanism --- The Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.**

### 1. Two Ages, Two Spiritual Goals: Arahantship and Buddhahood

Arahanism begins with a fundamental question: What was the original goal of Gautama's religious teaching? Was it to become an Arahant or a Buddha? Was it to train renunciants to become Arahants through complete liberation from worldly bondage, or was it to inspire lay people or Bodhisattva to become Buddhas by following the example of Gautama's supposed previous lives?

For Arahanism, this is not a minor distinction within Buddhism. It represents two different spiritual orientations, two understandings of religious practice, and two conceptions of the ultimate goal.

The **Age of Arahanism** represents the renunciant orientation of Gautama's original liberation teaching and his true Dhamma. Its central objective is Arahantship: the complete purification of citta from worldly bondage termed as rāga, dosa, and moha, and liberation from the world into Nibbāna-dhātu, the Deathless realm beyond the universe. Renunciation is central because the practitioner is not merely seeking to become a better person within the world, but to bring worldly bondage to an end.

The **Age of Buddhism**, as Arahanism understands this historical transformation, represents the growing prominence of Buddhahood and the Bodhisattva ideal as spiritual aspirations. The religious imagination turned increasingly toward the extraordinary figure of Gautama becoming a Buddha through an immense accumulation of wholesome deeds and merit across countless lives. The central question shifted from how one can follow the renunciant path to complete liberation from the world to how one can cultivate merit or wholesome qualities to attain Buddhahood in a remote future life, while in this very lifetime simply remaining a Lay Person and accumulating merit.

Arahanism identifies this change of spiritual ideal as a paradigm shift: **from Arahantship to Buddhahood, from renunciant liberation to a broader ideal of awakening, and from the direct pursuit of liberation from worldly bondage towards the accumulation of merit and the cultivation of exemplary virtues within the worldly life.**

### 2. The Fictional Jātaka Stories and the Changing Religious Imagination

To understand how this transformation may have taken place, Arahanism directs attention to the Jātaka stories, the traditional narratives concerning Gautama's supposed previous births.

The Jātakas have been invented to serve an important moral and educational purpose. Through stories of generosity, patience, truthfulness, compassion, self-sacrifice, and perseverance, they offer memorable examples of wholesome conduct. Their narrative form makes ethical teachings accessible to ordinary people who may not be prepared to undertake the demanding discipline of renunciation.

Yet from the perspective of Arahanism, an important distinction must be made between a story that teaches moral values and a historical account that establishes the actual stages of Gautama's spiritual development. Many Jātaka narratives are best approached as didactic religious literature rather than as independently verified biographies of Gautama's previous lives. Their value as moral instruction does not, by itself, establish the historical truth of every event they describe.

The difficulty arises when these narratives cease to function primarily as moral instruction and begin to shape the central understanding of the religious path itself. The stories of Gautama's supposed previous lives can become a model for religious aspiration: practitioners are encouraged to imitate the generosity, sacrifices, and wholesome deeds attributed to him, accumulating merit in the hope of advancing towards a future spiritual attainment.

In this development, the religious emphasis can move away from the question, *How do I end my bondage to the world?* towards a different question: *How do I accumulate merit and cultivate the qualities that may lead towards Buddhahood?*

This distinction is especially significant for lay followers. A person who cannot or does not undertake full renunciation can still participate in generosity, moral conduct, devotional practices, and the accumulation of merit. Such activities can provide a meaningful religious life without requiring the practitioner to organize the whole of existence around the renunciant pursuit of Arahantship. The Jātaka stories consequently offer a powerful moral framework through which laypeople can understand their religious participation.

The concern raised by Arahanism is not that generosity, compassion, or moral conduct are without value. They are important qualities. The concern is that **merit accumulation can become a substitute for liberation when wholesome deeds are treated as the central religious objective rather than as supports for the path leading to the complete ending of worldly bondage termed as rāga, dosa, and moha.**

A person may become generous, respected, compassionate, and morally accomplished while remaining bound to worldly existence. The cultivation of good qualities of a worldly oriented citta and the attainment of complete liberation of the citta from the world are therefore not identical. For Arahanism, the decisive question is whether a practice contributes to the progressive withdrawal of citta from worldly attachment and ultimately to Vimutti.

### 3. From the Jātaka Ideal to the Bodhisattva Ideal

Arahanism sees this change in religious imagination as an important part of the broader historical transformation through which the ideal of Buddhahood became increasingly prominent in Buddhist traditions.

The Jātaka narratives and the Bodhisattva ideal are seemingly not identical, and the historical emergence of Mahāyāna maybe cannot be reduced to a single cause. Nevertheless, Arahanism identifies a significant conceptual continuity between celebrating Gautama's supposed previous-life deeds and making the Bodhisattva's long cultivation of virtues and accumulation of merit a central model for religious aspiration.

Under this orientation, Gautama is understood not as the Supreme Arahant who became an renunciant and attained liberation from worldly bondage and then taught others the path to liberation, but as the exceptional Bodhisattva who pursued Buddhahood, awakening under the bodhi tree, through an immense course of previous lives. His supposed previous-life conduct becomes a model that practitioners may admire, emulate, or aspire to reproduce according to their own spiritual commitments.

Over time, the ideal of becoming a Buddha through the Bodhisattva path gained a central place in Mahāyāna traditions. In Arahanism's interpretation, this development represents a decisive reorientation of the religious goal. Arahantship, which Arahanism regards as the culmination of Gautama's renunciant liberation teaching, is no longer necessarily treated as the highest spiritual aspiration. Instead, Buddhahood becomes the supreme ideal, and the Bodhisattva path provides the framework for pursuing it.

The significance of this transformation extends beyond terminology. If Buddhahood becomes the governing ideal, the meaning of spiritual practice changes accordingly. The cultivation of virtues across lifetimes, the accumulation of merit, the aspiration to Buddhahood, and the Bodhisattva's commitment to the welfare of beings can take precedence over the exclusive renunciant pursuit of Arahantship.

For Arahanism, this is the crucial point: **the paradigm shift is not simply from one title to another, but from one understanding of the purpose of religious life to another.** The Arahant's path centres on the ending of worldly bondage in a single life time by a few years' rigorous yogic practice. The Bodhisattva ideal centres on the long course of numerous lifetimes towards Buddhahood. Although both traditions may value morality, meditation, compassion, and wisdom, they organize these qualities around different ultimate aspirations.
