### 4. From Liberation from Worldly Bondage to Worldly Awakening to Reduce Suffering

The difference between liberation from worldly bondage and worldly awakening to reduce sufferings in life, must therefore be examined carefully.

In Arahanism's understanding, Arahantship means the complete ending of the defilements that bind citta to worldly existence. It is not simply an elevated state of consciousness, a profound insight, an experience of unity, or an improvement in one's emotional condition. It is the culmination of the renunciant path and the attainment of complete liberation from worldly bondage.

The concept of Buddhahood, as understood across Buddhist traditions, has a different range of meanings. It commonly refers to the supreme awakening of a Buddha, who discovers the truth but chose to stay in the world to help others. In Mahāyāna, the Bodhisattva ideal places particular emphasis on pursuing Buddhahood for the benefit of all sentient beings. Arahanism's criticism concerns the historical truthfulness and doctrinal development in which this ideal becomes the governing objective and the renunciant pursuit of Arahantship is displaced from its central position.

This is why the distinction matters to contemporary practitioners. If the goal is understood primarily as awakening, the cultivation of insight, compassion, wisdom, and beneficial activity may be regarded as sufficient expressions of spiritual progress, even where complete renunciation is not undertaken. If the goal is Arahantship, however, the practitioner must confront a more demanding question: whether worldly attachment is actually being brought to an end.

Arahanism does not deny the value of wholesome conduct or the importance of helping others. It questions whether these activities, by themselves, fulfil the purpose of Gautama's liberation teaching. Generosity may accumulate merit; compassion may benefit others; meditation may produce concentration and insight. But the central test remains whether rāga, dosa, and moha are completely eradicated and worldly bondage is brought to an end.

**The difference is between cultivating goodness within worldly existence and completing the path that liberates one from worldly bondage.**

### 5. How a Paradigm Becomes an Inherited Assumption

A historical transformation does not need to be consciously chosen by every individual for its influence to endure, but unconsciously influenced by forces unimaginable by humans. Once a religious ideal becomes dominant, it shapes institutions, devotional practices, educational materials, public expectations, and the language through which people understand spiritual progress.

Many contemporary Buddhists inherit their understanding of Buddhism from the traditions in which they were raised or trained. They may assume that the Bodhisattva ideal, the accumulation of merit, lay religious participation, and the pursuit of Buddhahood have always occupied their present position within Buddhism. They may never have been asked to examine whether these priorities represent the same spiritual orientation as the renunciant pursuit of Arahantship.

Arahanism invites practitioners to reconsider these inherited assumptions. It asks whether the familiar religious ideal is necessarily identical to the original purpose of Gautama's teaching, and whether the distinction between Arahantship and Buddhahood has been adequately examined.

This inquiry is not an argument against generosity, ethical conduct, or compassion. It is an invitation to reconsider the hierarchy of spiritual objectives. Are these qualities being cultivated as foundations for complete liberation from worldly bondage, or have they become ends in themselves? Is renunciation understood as indispensable to the final goal, or as one religious option among many? Is the practitioner seeking to end worldly bondage, or aspiring towards a different conception of awakening?

For Arahanism, these are questions of fundamental importance because the goal determines the path. A practitioner cannot properly evaluate a religious discipline without first understanding what that discipline is intended to accomplish.

### 6. Arahanism as a Return to the Liberation Path

Arahanism presents itself as a return to the renunciant orientation that it understands as central to Gautama's original liberation teaching, and his true Dhamma. It calls for the restoration of Arahantship as the supreme objective of religious training, the recovery of renunciation as a defining commitment, and the purification of citta from worldly bondage through Sīla, Samādhi, Paññā, Nibbidā, Virāga, and Vimutti.

This return requires more than adopting a new name. It requires examining the assumptions that govern religious life, distinguishing moral improvement from complete liberation, and restoring the question of worldly bondage to the centre of spiritual practice.

The challenge is considerable. The dominant religious paradigm has been transmitted through centuries of tradition, devotion, education, and institutional life. People naturally tend to understand a religion through the categories they have inherited. A different paradigm may initially seem unfamiliar or even contradictory to what they have always been taught.

Yet this is precisely why the distinction must be made explicit. Arahanism asks practitioners to examine their spiritual destination before deciding which path to follow. It asks them to distinguish the accumulation of merit from the eradication of defilements, the cultivation of wholesome qualities from complete liberation from worldly bondage, and the aspiration to Buddhahood from the pursuit of Arahantship.

This question is also relevant to people in Canada and elsewhere who do not identify as Buddhists. They need not begin by accepting a religious label or inheriting the assumptions of an established tradition. They can begin with the fundamental question of what they seek from spiritual life: a more virtuous life within the world, an awakening understood according to a particular Buddhist tradition, or complete liberation from worldly bondage through the renunciant path.

Arahanism offers its answer through the teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant. Its purpose is to restore the Liberation Path as a living discipline and to train practitioners who seek the Deathless through the complete purification of citta.

The question that stands before every sincere seeker is therefore direct:

**Is your goal to become a Buddha, or to become an Arahant?**

Arahanism calls for this question to be examined without prejudice, inherited assumptions, or confusion between different spiritual objectives. It seeks to renew the Age of Arahanism---not by denying the historical existence or religious significance of the diverse Buddhist traditions, but by restoring Arahantship and complete liberation to the centre of its own religious mission.

### 7. Awakening (Buddhahood) Is Not Necessarily Liberation from The World

The word *awakening* can describe many kinds of spiritual realization. A person may awaken to the impermanent nature of experience, recognize the limitations of desire, understand the constructed character of ordinary perception, or experience a profound insight into the interconnectedness of existence. Such realizations can transform one\'s understanding of life and substantially reduce emotional suffering.

Yet the experience of awakening, by itself, does not establish that the worldly bondage has been lessened.

A person may possess profound wisdom while retaining worldly attachment. One may understand the futility of anger yet continue to experience aversion. One may recognize the limitations of ordinary desire while remaining bound to innate sexual craving. Even an extraordinary experience of consciousness such as the interconnectedness of existence will not necessary lessen the bondage of the individual to the collective, this may leave the fundamental relationship between the individual Citta and worldly existence unchanged.

**Insight is not necessarily purification, and awakening is not necessarily lead to liberation from the world.**

Within the Arahanist framework, awakening must be evaluated according to its actual consequences. What has been understood? What has been abandoned? Which bonds remain? Has the mind merely acquired a different perspective on existence, or has it been completely freed from the forces that bind it to worldly existence?

These questions establish the difference among a degree of awakening, right awakening or wrong awakening, and the attainment of Arahantship.

Arahanism does not deny that awakening can produce genuine wisdom or meaningful relief from emotional suffering. It challenges the assumption that most profound spiritual realization constitutes the same goal of Gautama\'s Liberation Path toward Arahantship.

The decisive criterion is not the intensity of an experience, the depth of a philosophical insight, or the extraordinary nature of a meditative state. It is the difference of the purpose, goal, or destination that the awakening or liberation is leading to.

### 8. Awakening (Buddhahood) and Freedom Within the World

In the Arahanist analysis, Buddhahood and Arahantship are distinguished by their respective implications for purification and worldly existence.

Buddhahood, understood broadly as awakening, represents the realization of wisdom that changes one\'s relationship with experience. The awakened person may see through ordinary assumptions, understand the causes of emotional suffering, and acquire greater freedom from habitual reactions. Such awakening can make life within the world substantially different.

The awakened person may experience less fear, less anger, less compulsive desire, and less distress in response to changing circumstances. The world may remain the same in its fundamental characteristics, yet the person\'s relationship with it can change profoundly.

This is a meaningful achievement. Nevertheless, the reduction of suffering does not automatically establish that the causes of continued worldly existence have been changed.

An awakened person may continue to participate in ordinary social life, maintain relationships, perform worldly responsibilities, and pursue activities within the existing order of existence. Even when such a person possesses genuine wisdom, the question of purification of the Citta (mind-heart) from the worldly bondage remains open.

Arahanism therefore distinguishes between becoming freer **within** the world and becoming completely liberated **from** worldly bondage.

The distinction is not simply between an ordinary person and an extraordinary person. It concerns the final destination of spiritual practice.

A person may possess greater wisdom than before, experience greater peace, and become less vulnerable to emotional disturbance while still completely remaining within the conditions of worldly existence. The improvement may be real, but the existential problem has not necessarily been changed.

For Arahanism, this is why awakening must not be confused with the complete attainment of Arahantship.

### 9. The Arahant: Complete Purification of the Citta and Severance from Worldly Bondage

Arahantship establishes a different standard.

An Arahant is not merely someone who understands the world more deeply, experiences profound meditative experience, or becomes less affected by suffering. Arahantship signifies the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha---the forces that sustain worldly bondage and worldly existence.

**Rāga** encompasses worldly craving, passion, and attachment. It draws the mind towards worldly objects, pleasures, identities, and forms of existence.

**Dosa** encompasses fear, anger, aversion, hostility, violence, hatred, and the impulse to release chaotic and destructive inclination in the form of resisting or opposing what is experienced as undesirable. It binds the Citta to the chaotic side of the universe.

**Moha** encompasses ignorance and delusion, particularly the failure to understand the path to be liberated from worldly bondage and the real existence of the deathless realm outside of the universe.

These three roots operate at different levels of experience, but together they sustain the Citta\'s entanglement with the world. As long as they remain, worldly bondage has not reached its complete end.

The Arahanist path therefore does not stop at reducing their influence. It directs the practitioner towards their complete eradication.

There is an essential difference between controlling desire and ending worldly craving, between anger management and eradicating its root, and between acquiring knowledge and eliminating the ignorance that sustains worldly bondage.

Arahantship is defined by the completion of this purification of the Citta from the defilements termed as **rāga, dosa, and moha**.

Thus, Arahanism recognizes **one standard of Arahantship: the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha**. There are different stages of training, different depths of concentration, and different degrees of understanding along the way. But partial purification is not partial Arahantship in the sense of a lesser attainment of Arahantship. Until the defining bonds have been completely eradicated, the goal has not been fully realized.

The Arahant\'s freedom is therefore not merely an improvement in psychological well-being. It concerns the fundamental relationship between the citta and worldly existence.

When the bonds that bind the citta to the world have been entirely severed, the Arahant has completed the work of liberation. The ultimate destination is Nibbāna-dhātu, the Deathless realm beyond the world governed by universal law of aging, death, decay, and dissolution.

This is the existential level of liberation that Arahanism places at the centre of the spiritual path.

### 10. Emotional Suffering and Existential Dukkha

To understand the difference between Buddhahood awakening and Arahantship escaping, it is necessary to distinguish two dimensions of suffering.

The first concerns the suffering experienced through feelings, emotions, perceptions, and reactions. The second concerns the more fundamental existential problem of continued bondage to the world.

The distinction can be expressed as **emotional dukkha and existential dukkha**.

Emotional dukkha arises through the ways in which experience affects the mind. Loss produces grief; frustration produces anger; uncertainty produces fear; desire produces agitation; and attachment makes change painful. Spiritual insight can transform this relationship with experience. A person who understands the causes of emotional suffering may become less reactive, less fearful, and less dependent on circumstances for peace.

This is one important consequence of awakening.

Existential dukkha concerns a deeper question: even if emotional suffering is substantially reduced, has the underlying bondage to worldly existence been brought to an end?

A person may experience peace while continuing to exist within a world characterized by instability, aging and death. The person may have learned to navigate these conditions with greater wisdom, but the conditions themselves remain.

From the Arahanist perspective, freedom from emotional disturbance and freedom from existential bondage are not interchangeable achievements.

The first concerns the experience of living within the world. The second concerns the complete liberation of the citta from the bonds that sustain worldly existence.

An awakened person may therefore experience a profound reduction in emotional suffering and completely attached to the worldly experience and existence. Arahantship, by contrast, requires the complete purification of the citta that ends the worldly bondage.

This distinction does not diminish the value of wisdom, compassion, or emotional freedom. It establishes their proper place within the larger question of liberation.

The ultimate goal is not merely to suffer less while remaining bound to the world. It is to bring that bondage to its complete end.

### 11. Why Worldly Renunciation Is Essential

If the ultimate objective is Arahantship, renunciation cannot be treated as an optional lifestyle preference. It becomes the practical foundation of the liberation project.

Household life is organized around participation in the world, the person's sensory contacts with the world is constantly active. It involves economic activity, possessions, family responsibilities, social relationships, personal ambitions, and the pursuit of material and sensual well-being. These activities continually expose the mind through six sense faculties to the external objects by which worldly attachment is sustained.

A person may cultivate wisdom while living as a lay practitioner. A layperson may observe precepts, lead an ethical lifestyle, meditate, study the Dhamma, and gain meaningful insight. Such training has value, and Arahanism recognizes the importance of progressive spiritual development.

However, lay practice is preparatory rather than a qualified form of the renunciant path to Arahantship. **Arahantship requires complete renunciation, undertaken through the monastic life of a Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī.**

Worldly renunciation is not merely the outward abandonment of a house. It is a systematic withdrawal of the citta from the structures, pursuits, and attachments that bind the citta to worldly existence.

The training begins with Sīla, develops through Samādhi, and matures through Paññā. As the practitioner deepens in discipline and concentration, wisdom increasingly exposes the limitations of worldly existence. This leads towards Nibbidā, disenchantment with the world; Virāga, the fading of worldly passion; and ultimately Vimutti, liberation from the world.

The progression is not simply a movement towards greater serenity. It is a movement away from worldly bondage and worldly existence.

The monastic life gives this purpose a definite form. Celibacy, sense restraint, simplicity, voluntary poverty, renunciation of personal possessions and commerce, and adherence to the Vinaya reduce participation in all worldly pursuits. These disciplines are not ends in themselves. They establish the conditions for stopping new defilements entering into the citta, and sustained purification of the existing defilements.

Worldly renunciation must eventually penetrate the existential state of the citta. An outwardly renunciant person who remains governed by worldly craving and attachment, fear and anger, and delusion has not completed the work. Conversely, meditation and intellectual understanding cannot replace the complete renunciation from the world that Arahanism regards as indispensable for Arahantship.

The purpose of the monastic path is therefore to bring the training to its ultimate conclusion: the complete severance of the mind with the world, through sensory control.

### 12. Arahantship Requires Complete Renunciation: Why a Layperson Cannot Become an Arahant

A fundamental principle of Arahanism is that **Arahantship is attainable only through complete renunciation of household life, as a Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī. A layperson cannot attain Arahantship while remaining a layperson.** This is not a choice concerning lifestyle, nor is it simply a preference for monastic discipline. It follows from the very definition of Arahantship as the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha and the total severance of worldly bondage.

To understand this principle, one must distinguish between approaching liberation and completing liberation. A lay practitioner may cultivate Sīla, develop Samādhi, deepen Paññā, and experience profound disenchantment with worldly existence. Through diligent practice, one may relinquish many worldly attachments, overcome substantial defilements, and approach the threshold of complete liberation. Yet progress towards Arahantship does not mean that the final attainment has already occurred.

The decisive question is whether the practitioner has completely severed the bonds connecting the citta to worldly existence.

**The Incompatibility Between Arahantship and Lay Identity**

Lay life is constituted through participation in the world. A layperson maintains a household identity, engages in economic and social relationships, and ordinarily remains involved in the activities through which worldly existence is sustained. The five sense faculties continue to encounter their respective worldly objects, while the structures of household life provide innumerable occasions for worldly involvement.

For Arahanism, the issue is not simply whether a layperson can behave ethically, meditate deeply, or remain relatively undisturbed by sensory experience. It is whether the person has fulfilled the complete renunciation required for Arahantship.

Arahantship signifies the definitive completion of purification. Rāga, dosa, and moha are entirely eradicated; the bonds that sustain worldly bondage are severed. This attainment cannot be reconciled with retaining the worldly identity and obligations of a householder, because complete liberation and continued participation in household existence represent fundamentally different and paradoxical conditions.

The distinction is therefore categorical. A practitioner may be highly developed while remaining a layperson, but that development does not constitute completed Arahantship. **One cannot simultaneously remain established in lay existence and claim to have completely severed the bonds of worldly existence as an Arahant.**

**Renunciation Is the Completion of the Transition**

This principle also clarifies the relationship between lay practice and monastic life.

The Five Precepts establish the ethical foundation of lay practice. The Eight Uposatha Precepts introduce a more deliberate withdrawal from sexual and sensual pleasures and ordinary worldly activities. Sustained observance can prepare a practitioner for the more comprehensive renunciation undertaken through Pabbajjā, novice ordination, and ultimately Upasampadā, full ordination as a Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī.

These stages should not be confused with one another. A lay practitioner who observes the Eight Precepts may be undertaking serious renunciant training, but remains a lay practitioner. A novice has renounced household life and entered a more comprehensive but not full renunciation of worldly bondage, therefore has not yet attained the status of a fully ordained monastic. Full ordination establishes the formal monastic commitment within which the complete training towards Arahantship is undertaken.

Within the Arahanist framework, the decisive transition must be completed before Arahantship can be attained. A practitioner who has developed sufficient wisdom and purification to approach the final attainment of Arahantship must relinquish lay identity and establish the fully ordained monastic identity. Renunciation is not an optional addition after liberation; it is an essential condition of the path\'s completion.

Arahanism declares a definite position on this question: **there is no lay Arahant.** A person may approach the culmination of the path while living as a lay practitioner, but the final attainment requires complete renunciation. The practitioner must leave household identity and establish the fully ordained monastic identity; only then can the path culminate in Arahantship.

This distinction is essential to the Arahanist understanding of the Dhamma. It prevents the highest attainment from being reduced to a private experience of insight that leaves the practitioner\'s fundamental relationship with worldly existence unchanged.

**Complete Liberation Requires a Complete Break**

The purpose of renunciation is not merely to reduce worldly distractions or create more favourable conditions for meditation. Its ultimate purpose is to sever the bonds that sustain worldly existence.

A layperson may experience tranquility while continuing to participate in the world. A practitioner may understand impermanence while retaining household responsibilities. One may overcome many forms of attachment while still living within the structures of lay existence. Such developments may represent genuine progress, but they must not be confused with the completion of liberation.

Arahantship demands a definitive conclusion. The eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha must be complete; the work of purification must reach its end; and the practitioner must have relinquished household existence through full monastic renunciation.

This is why Arahanism places the Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī at the centre of its liberation model. Monastic life is not simply one spiritual option among many equivalent lifestyles. It is the formal renunciant commitment through which the complete training towards Arahantship is undertaken.

**The layperson may prepare for liberation. The renunciant undertakes its complete realization. Arahantship belongs to the completion of renunciation, not to the continuation of lay life.**

### 13. Awakening Does Not Necessarily Leads to Liberation

A further distinction follows from this understanding: not every experience described as awakening necessarily leads towards liberation. Most of them do not.

A spiritual realization may reveal something true about the structure of worldly existence and experience while leaving the practitioner bound to worldly existence. A meditative state may be extraordinary without being connected to liberation. An insight may transform one\'s understanding and even remove the fear of death, but without contributing to or even cause to lose the drive to seek liberation from the world.

Arahanism therefore evaluates spiritual realization according to its direction and final consequence.

Consider the experience or doctrine of non-duality, which transcends the concept of good and bad, worldly and nirvana, in which the apparent distinction between the individual and the whole is understood to be ultimately unreal. For some practitioners, this may produce a profound sense of unity, oneness, freedom from ordinary self-concern, and relief from emotional conflict.

Yet from the Arahanist perspective, such a realization does not constitute the true goal for liberation from the world to seek deathless, eternal existence and ultimate peace and joy. When the apparent goal of non-duality is the dissolution of individuality into a universal source, the practitioner is moving towards absorption into a larger source of existence rather than liberation beyond the world.

The emotional consequences may be beneficial. The existential conclusion, however, remains questionable.

**A realization that reduces suffering is not necessarily a step toward the end of worldly bondage.**

Arahanism therefore distinguishes between a realization that loosens attachment to ordinary experience and one that completes the work of liberation. It also distinguishes between wisdom that genuinely advances the practitioner towards Arahantship and interpretations of awakening that, in the Arahanist assessment, lead away from that goal.

In this sense, the language of awakening cannot serve as proof of attainment. A person may sincerely believe that liberation has been achieved, yet the belief itself does not establish that rāga, dosa, and moha have been eradicated.

The criterion remains the actual completion of purification of the citta and its liberation from worldly bondage.

### 14. Different Stages of Awakening, Wrong Awakening vs. One Standard of Arahantship

Spiritual development is not uniform. Practitioners differ in their understanding, meditative experience, degree of detachment from worldly bondage, and freedom from emotional suffering. Insight may deepen gradually, and the effects of spiritual training may become evident at different stages.

Arahanism does not need to deny these differences in order to maintain a definite final goal.

There may be different degrees and kinds of awakening. Some realizations may be superficial, while others may bring profound changes in understanding and conduct. Some may substantially reduce emotional suffering; others may transform a person\'s entire conception of existence.

There may also be claims to Buddhahood that, when examined, is a wrong awakening that leads to either annihilation of individuality, or merge with the Source of universal consciousness. These are the opposite direction Arahantship is seeking to establish the reality of the attainment.

For Arahanism, however, Arahantship has a definitive criterion: the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha. Until that purification is complete, the practitioner remains on the path rather than having reached its goal.

The Arahanist standard is therefore exacting. Profound insight is not enough. Extraordinary meditation is only the means. Reduced emotional suffering is not yet part of the solution. Even a transformative experience of consciousness is not enough.

The final question is whether worldly bondage has been completely severed.

### 15. Arahanism and the Theravāda Tradition

Arahanism arises from within the Theravāda monastic tradition. It preserves the central importance of Sīla, Samādhi, Paññā, renunciation, celibacy, sensory control, and the eradication of the defilements. It also takes seriously the ideal of Arahantship as the completion of the liberation path.

Its distinctiveness lies in the way it places Arahantship at the centre of its religious identity and examines spiritual doctrines according to whether they lead to that end.

Arahanism does not regard most contemporary Buddhist interpretation as an expression of the same understanding of liberation. Where spiritual practice becomes primarily concerned with emotional well-being, philosophical insight, worldly harmony, or experiences of universal unity and oneness, Arahanism asks whether the original objective of complete liberation from the worldly bondage has been preserved.

It also re-examines interpretations of anattā, Nibbāna, and awakening that, in its understanding, risk obscuring the distinction between the worldly aggregates and the true destination of liberation. The purpose of this examination is not simply to introduce a different philosophy. It is to clarify what the practitioner is training to attain.

The central question is whether the Dhamma is being approached as a means of living more peacefully within the world or as a path towards the complete severance of worldly bondage.

These purposes may overlap at certain stages of practice, but they are not identical.

Arahanism seeks to restore the final objective to the centre of spiritual life. It regards renunciation not as a good but not indispensable, a vocation only for a small number of people, but rather as indispensable and the essential direction of a path whose goal is Arahantship.

### 16. The Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant

The distinction between Arahanism and broader understandings of Buddhism ultimately returns to the purpose of spiritual practice.

If the goal is awakening, a practitioner may seek insight, develop wisdom, reduce emotional suffering, and acquire greater freedom in the conduct of life. These can be meaningful developments.

If the goal is Arahantship, however, the standard is complete purification of the mind-heart citta. The practitioner must undertake the training that progressively eradicates rāga, dosa, and moha, severs the bonds connecting the citta to worldly existence, and culminates in Vimutti, liberation from the worldly bondage.

The path begins with Sīla, develops through Samādhi, and matures through Paññā. Through Nibbidā and Virāga, attachment to the world fades; through Vimutti, the work of liberation from the world reaches its completion.

This is the purpose of Brahmacariya: not merely to become a better person within the world, not merely to acquire wisdom about the world, and not merely to experience less suffering while remaining bound to worldly existence, but to undertake the complete training that leads beyond worldly bondage.

Arahanism therefore defines itself through its final objective.

It is **the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**, dedicated to the complete purification of the citta, the attainment of Arahantship, and the realization of Nibbāna-dhātu, the real Deathless realm beyond the world.

The decisive question is not simply whether one has awakened, but what that awakening has to bring about.

Has suffering become more manageable, or has worldly bondage been brought to its complete end?

Has the practitioner gained a new understanding of existence, or has the work of purification reached its final completion?

Has one become more at peace within the world, or has one fulfilled the ancient path towards liberation beyond it?

For Arahanism, the answer defines the destination.

**Awakening may be a beginning. Arahantship is the completion of the Liberation Path.**

### 17. From Arahantship to Buddhahood: The Paradigm Shift

The distinction between Arahanism and the broader Buddhist traditions is not merely a disagreement over terminology or a preference for one spiritual ideal over another. It concerns a fundamental transformation in the purpose of religious life: the movement from the renunciant\'s pursuit of Arahantship towards the Bodhisattva\'s aspiration to Buddhahood.

Arahanism identifies its foundation with Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, and the Liberation Path that leads to complete purification of the citta and and its severance of worldly bondage. The central objective is the purification of the citta from its defilements terms as rāga, dosa, and moha, culminating in Arahantship and the realization of Nibbāna-dhātu, the Deathless realm beyond the world.

Within this framework, renunciation is not a preliminary lifestyle choice that can be replaced by another spiritual ideal. It is the defining orientation of the path. The practitioner leaves household existence, undertakes the discipline of a Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī, and devotes life to the progressive purification that culminates in complete liberation from worldly bondage.

The emergence of the Bodhisattva (a lay person) ideal as the highest aspiration represents a different religious orientation. In Mahāyāna Buddhism, the practitioner aspires to Buddhahood and undertakes the Bodhisattva path for the benefit of sentient beings. The highest ideal is no longer the completion of Arahantship, but the attainment of Buddhahood through the Bodhisattva\'s extensive cultivation of wisdom and compassion.

From the Arahanist perspective, this constitutes a paradigm shift.

The governing question changes from how the practitioner can completely eradicate worldly bondage to how the practitioner can fulfil the aspiration to Buddhahood. The meaning of renunciation, the purpose of monastic discipline, the authority of the teachings, and the criteria of spiritual accomplishment are consequently interpreted within a different framework.

Arahanism regards this development as a degeneration of the original liberation teaching, the true dhamma paradigm: a displacement of the renunciant\'s final objective by a different ideal of spiritual attainment.

The term *degeneration* here expresses Arahanism\'s doctrinal judgment, not a claim that every Mahāyāna practitioner is morally inferior or incapable of wisdom and compassion. The criticism concerns the direction of the teaching itself. A religious tradition may preserve ethical discipline, meditation, scholarship, and sincere devotion while, in the Arahanist assessment, departing from the original purpose of liberation.

The decisive issue is therefore not whether a later tradition has developed sophisticated philosophies or inspired generations of practitioners. It is whether its governing ideal preserves the path to Arahantship as Arahanism understands Gautama\'s teaching.

**Arahanism seeks to reverse this paradigm shift: to restore Arahantship, complete renunciation, and liberation beyond worldly existence to the centre of spiritual life.**

### 18. The Renunciant and the Bodhisattva: Two Different Governing Commitments

Every religious path is defined by more than its outward practices. Its deeper identity is determined by the goal towards which those practices are directed.

A Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī undertaking Gautama\'s Liberation Path renounces household life in order to complete the purification of the citta from worldly bondage subjectively termed as defilements. Celibacy, sense restraint or control, simplicity, voluntary poverty, the relinquishment of personal economic activity, and adherence to the Vinaya are integrated into a comprehensive discipline directed towards Arahantship.

These practices are not independent virtues collected into a religious lifestyle. They belong to a unified undertaking: the severance of the bonds that bind the citta to worldly existence.

The Bodhisattva path establishes a different governing aspiration. In Mahāyāna, the Bodhisattva vows to attain Buddhahood for the benefit of sentient beings. This aspiration can be undertaken by lay practitioners and monastics alike. The defining distinction is therefore not the external status of the practitioner, but the ultimate commitment governing the religious life.

Arahanism raises a fundamental question about what happens when a person takes monastic precepts while adopting a Bodhisattva vow as the governing spiritual objective.

The person may wear robes, observe celibacy, follow a Vinaya, live in a monastery, and undertake rigorous ritual ceremonies. Yet the meaning of these disciplines depends upon the path they serve. If the governing commitment is to attain Buddhahood through the Bodhisattva path, rather than to complete the liberation from the world represented by Arahantship, the monastic life has been placed within a different spiritual framework.

This is the distinction Arahanism wishes to emphasize.

The criticism is not that Mahāyāna monastics are necessarily insincere or that their discipline is merely theatrical. Rather, it is that **the same outward monastic form does not necessarily represent the same ultimate religious commitment**.

Two practitioners may both be called Bhikkhu or Bhikṣu, may both wear robes, different kinds of robes, and may both observe extensive precepts. Nevertheless, if they understand the purpose of the religious life differently, their paths cannot be assumed to be identical.

Within Arahanism, monastic identity is inseparable from the liberation objective for which renunciation is undertaken. The defining commitment is not the pursuit of Buddhahood through a Bodhisattva vow, but the complete purification of the citta that culminates in Arahantship.

The question is not merely whether a person has renounced household life. It is what that renunciation is intended to accomplish.

### 19. Rise of the Bodhisattva Ideal and the Displacement of Arahantship

The elevation of the Bodhisattva ideal changes the hierarchy of spiritual attainment.

Within Arahanism, Arahantship represents the completion of the path. The practitioner must eradicate rāga, dosa, and moha entirely, sever the bonds of worldly existence, and fulfil the training directed towards the Deathless.

The Bodhisattva ideal, by contrast, places the aspiration to Buddhahood Awakening at the summit of spiritual practice and paradoxically placed it above Arahantship. Arahantship is consequently not treated as the ultimate objective within the Mahāyāna framework. The practitioner directs the path towards a different conception of supreme attainment.

From the Arahanist perspective, this is not a minor adjustment. It changes what counts as the highest spiritual accomplishment.

When Arahantship is the final goal, the practitioner measures progress by the abandonment of worldly bondage and the completion of purification. When Buddhahood becomes the supreme aspiration, progress is evaluated in relation to the Bodhisattva\'s journey towards Buddhahood, which is subjective and chaotic.

The difference has consequences for the interpretation of renunciation. If Buddhahood is established as the highest goal, monastic discipline can be understood as one optional component of the Bodhisattva path rather than as the definitive framework for completing Arahantship.

The Arahanist criticism is that this reverses the proper order of spiritual priorities. Renunciation is no longer governed by the immediate objective of complete liberation from worldly bondage. It is incorporated into a broader ideal whose final destination is defined differently.

Arahanism regards this as an obscuration of Gautama\'s true Dhamma of the Liberation Path.

This judgment does not require denying that Mahāyāna traditions cultivate compassion, generosity, wisdom, and disciplined practices. Nor does it require claiming that every Bodhisattva remains attached to sensual pleasure or household existence. The issue is the relationship between these qualities and the final objective.

Compassion is not itself Arahantship. Wisdom is not necessarily lead to purification. A profound aspiration to benefit sentient beings does not, by itself, establish that the bonds of worldly existence have been eradicated, or maybe is augmented.

For Arahanism, the decisive standard remains the complete ending of rāga, dosa, and moha. No alternative spiritual ideal can substitute for that accomplishment.

### 20. The Pāli Canon and the Question of Authenticity for Mahāyāna Sūtras

Arahanism takes the Pāli Canon as its scriptural foundation. Its understanding of Gautama\'s Liberation Path is grounded in those Nikāyas and Vinaya that preserve the renunciant discipline and the pursuit of liberation from the world.

The Pāli Canon presents a path of sense restraint, samādhi, wisdom, disenchantment, dispassion, and liberation from the world. It places worldly renunciation at the centre of the monastic life and identifies the eradication of the defilements as essential to the attainment of Arahantship.

Arahanism regards this liberation-centred orientation as the defining character of the true Dhamma from Gautama, the supreme Arahant it seeks to preserve.

Mahāyāna traditions rely on additional sūtras that are not part of the Theravāda Pāli Canon. These texts establish teachings, philosophical interpretations, and spiritual ideals that differ in significant respects from the scriptural framework adopted by Arahanism. And are obviously not historical accounts.

Arahanism does not recognize these additional sūtras as authentic words of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant. In its assessment, they represent later doctrinal developments that reconfigure the purpose of the spiritual practice, particularly through the elevation of the Bodhisattva ideal and the reinterpretation of emptiness, selfhood, and Buddhahood.

This is a central point of doctrinal separation.

If a teaching establishes a spiritual objective different from the complete liberation presented in the Pāli Canon, Arahanism does not regard the mere attribution of that teaching to Gautama as sufficient to establish its authenticity. The teaching must be examined according to its content, its relationship to the liberation path, and the spiritual destination it proposes.

This does not mean that the historical origins of every Mahāyāna text can be settled simply by comparing its contents with the Pāli Canon. Mahāyāna scriptures developed across different periods and communities, and their historical formation remains a subject of scholarly study. Arahanism\'s position is a doctrinal judgment: these texts do not constitute its authoritative foundation because their teachings and spiritual objectives do not, in its understanding, preserve Gautama\'s original Liberation Path.

The issue is therefore not the prestige of a scripture, the size of the community that accepts it, or the antiquity claimed for its teachings. The issue is whether the teaching leads to the liberation that Arahanism identifies with Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

### 21. Emptiness, Non-Duality, and the Question of Existential Liberation

Arahanism also distinguishes its understanding of liberation from the world from Mahāyāna interpretations of emptiness and non-duality that Arahanism considers incompatible with the complete severance of worldly bondage and attainment of Nibbāna-dhātu, the real deathless realm out of the universe into which a purified citta enters.

Some Mahāyāna philosophies, particularly those associated with Madhyamaka, use emptiness to challenge the idea that beings possess independent, intrinsic existence. Other Buddhist traditions explore non-dual awareness and the relationship between consciousness and experience in different ways.

Arahanism does not accept the assumption that such doctrines necessarily establish the same liberation taught by Gautama. It examines them according to a more fundamental question: what happens to the practitioner, and what is the ultimate destination of the path?

In the Arahanist understanding, the complete liberation of the citta from the worldly bondage cannot be equated with its dissolution into a universal source, its absorption into a cosmic consciousness, or the annihilation of individuality. These are not interchangeable descriptions of the Deathless, permanence, ultimate peace and joy for Gautama's goal of liberation: Nibbāna-dhātu.

Arahanism understands Nibbāna-dhātu as the real Deathless realm beyond the world, not a psychological state, or as a state of annihilation, or the merging of an individual into the source of the universe. The liberation sought is the complete severance of worldly bondage and the entry into that Deathless realm destination.

Consequently, a spiritual experience of unity or non-duality does not establish Arahantship merely because ordinary distinctions appear to disappear. Such an experience may alter perception, diminish self-concern, or provide substantial relief from emotional suffering. Yet its existential significance must be examined independently.

If a realization leads the practitioner to understand liberation as dissolution into an impersonal source, Arahanism regards that conclusion as fundamentally different from its own understanding of Nibbāna-dhātu.

The issue is not whether an experience feels profound. It is whether the teaching accurately identifies the destination of liberation.

Arahanism therefore refuses to treat all forms of emptiness, non-duality, awakening, and mystical unity as equivalent. Their meanings and consequences must be examined according to the criterion of complete liberation from the worldly bondage.

### 22. The Identity of the Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī Saṅgha: Inheriting Gautama's Dhamma

The identity of the Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī Saṅgha must be understood through the Dhamma that Gautama transmitted to his disciples and the ultimate goal for which they undertook renunciation. The Saṅgha is not defined merely by the wearing of robes, the observance of monastic regulations, or membership in an institution. Its spiritual identity rests upon its relationship to Gautama's Dhamma and its commitment to the path of liberation.

In the *Itivuttaka 4.100, Brāhmaṇa Dhamma Yāga Sutta*, Gautama declares: "*Bhikkhus, I am a brāhmaṇa,*\
*accessible to requests (dhamma), always with clean hands---nothing to hide, bearing the final body,*\
*a physician and surgeon for the supreme crossing-over. You are my breast-born sons (citta), born from my mouth, born of my Dhamma, formed by my Dhamma, heirs to my Dhamma, not heirs to material things."*

This declaration establishes a profound relationship between Gautama, his Dhamma, and those who have entered the renunciant life under his teaching. Gautama identifies his disciples as those born of his Dhamma, formed by his Dhamma, and destined to inherit his Dhamma rather than material possessions. Their inheritance is therefore spiritual: they are to receive, practise, embody, and preserve the teaching through which liberation is realized.

The distinction between inheriting Dhamma and inheriting material things is particularly significant for the renunciant. One who leaves household life relinquishes the ordinary pursuits of possession, wealth, status, and worldly advancement. The purpose of this relinquishment is not simply to adopt a different social identity or to belong to a religious institution. It is to dedicate one's life to the Dhamma and to the complete liberation for which the renunciant training exists.

To be formed by Gautama's Dhamma is to undergo a transformation through practice. The renunciant undertakes Sīla, develops Samādhi, cultivates Paññā, and progresses through Nibbidā and Virāga towards Vimutti. The training is not complete merely because one has received ordination or acquired scriptural knowledge. Its fulfilment lies in the purification of the citta from rāga, dosa, and moha, and in the realization of the goal of liberation from worldly bondage.

From the perspective of Arahanism, this inheritance must be understood in relation to the ultimate purpose of Gautama's teaching: Arahantship and complete liberation from worldly bondage. The Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī Saṅgha inherit Gautama's Dhamma fully by preserving and practising the Liberation Path that leads to this goal. The robes and the Vinaya provide the outward form and discipline of renunciation; the Dhamma provides its direction, purpose, and fulfilment.

This principle also provides the basis for Arahanism's distinction between outward monastic identity and governing spiritual commitment. Today's wider Buddhist traditions may share robes, ordination forms, and elements of monastic discipline while differing in their understanding of the highest spiritual goal. Where Arahantship is displaced by a different ideal as the governing purpose of religious life, Arahanism regards this as a fundamental departure from its understanding of Gautama's original Liberation Path. The issue is not simply whether practitioners sincerely follow a religious tradition, but whether the tradition preserves the goal and path that Arahanism identifies as the heart of Gautama's Dhamma.

Arahanism therefore does not regard institutional continuity or outward resemblance as sufficient proof of fidelity to the original teaching. The decisive question is what the Saṅgha inherits, preserves, and practises. If Gautama's disciples are his Dhamma-born sons and daughters, formed by his Dhamma and heirs to it, then the responsibility of the renunciant community is to protect the integrity of that inheritance and to transmit it through practice, instruction, and example.

For Arahanism, this responsibility means restoring renunciation and Arahantship to the centre of spiritual training. The Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī Saṅgha must be understood not merely as a religious institution that perpetuates its own existence, but as a community dedicated to the realization and transmission of Gautama's Liberation Path. Its highest purpose is not the accumulation of material or institutional achievements, nor the pursuit of spiritual ideals detached from complete liberation from the worldly bondage, but the fulfilment of the training through which the practitioner reaches Vimutti.

To inherit Gautama's Dhamma is therefore to accept the responsibility of living according to it, practising it to its culmination, and transmitting it without abandoning its essential purpose. In this understanding of Arahanism, the true inheritance of the Saṅgha is the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant: the path of worldly renunciation, purification of the citta, Arahantship, and realization of the Deathless realm.

### 23. Reversing the Paradigm: The Mission of Arahanism

Arahanism presents itself as a movement to reverse what it understands as a historical displacement of the original liberation objective.

In its interpretation, the elevation of the Bodhisattva ideal and Buddhahood above Arahantship changed the governing purpose of today's wider Buddhist spiritual practice. Renunciation was incorporated into a different framework of aspiration, and the highest ideal was redefined.

Arahanism seeks to restore the opposite order.

Arahantship must again stand at the centre of the path. Complete renunciation must be understood in relation to the eradication of worldly bondage. Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā must be cultivated as stages of a comprehensive training whose completion is Vimutti, liberation from worldly bondage.

This restoration requires a re-examination of the teachings accepted as authoritative, the ideals presented to practitioners, and the meaning assigned to awakening. It also requires renewed recognition of the Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī as full-time renunciants whose lives are dedicated to the completion of liberation from the world.

Arahanism therefore does not seek merely to establish another interpretation alongside many others while leaving their respective objectives undifferentiated. It seeks to make a clear claim about the spiritual destination that it upholds.

The movement\'s purpose is to recover the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, as a distinct religious commitment. It challenges teachings that, in its assessment, obscure the distinction between awakening within the world and complete liberation beyond worldly bondage.

The standard remains uncompromising: rāga, dosa, and moha must be eradicated completely. The practitioner must fulfil the renunciant discipline. The final goal is not merely a transformed understanding of existence, but liberation from the bonds that sustain it.

This is the paradigm Arahanism seeks to restore.

### 24. A Historical Timeline of the Paradigm Shift from the Age of Arahanism to the Age of Buddhism

**1). The Central Question: What Happened to the Liberation Path?**

How did a spiritual tradition centred on renunciation and the complete liberation of the individual come to encompass so many different ideals, practices, philosophical systems, and understandings of spiritual attainment? How did the pursuit of Arahantship, which Arahanism identifies as the defining purpose of Gautama's teaching, become overshadowed by the pursuit of Buddhahood and the Bodhisattva ideal?

These questions cannot be answered merely by comparing doctrines as they exist today. They require us to examine the historical development of the traditions, the transmission of their scriptures, and the gradual transformation of their spiritual aspirations.

Arahanism proposes that the history of this transformation can be understood as a paradigm shift: from an age in which renunciant liberation stood at the centre of Gautama's teaching to an age in which a broader and increasingly diverse conception of Buddhism became dominant.

The term *Arahanism*, as used here, is a name for the liberation-centred path that Arahanism understands as the essential purpose of Gautama's teaching. It does not imply that the early renunciants historically organized themselves under this name. Rather, it identifies the defining orientation of their path: renunciation of worldly bondage and the attainment of Arahantship.

The following timeline sets out the historical framework for this interpretation. Some dates derive from traditional Buddhist chronicles; others are approximate historical reconstructions. The argument that these developments constitute a transition from the Age of Arahanism to the Age of Buddhism is the interpretation advanced by Arahanism.

**2). The Age of Renunciant Liberation: Gautama and the Brahmanical and Śramaṇa Traditions**

**Approximately the fifth to sixth centuries BCE --- Gautama's inheritance and reformulation of ancient Indian spiritual traditions**

Gautama emerged within a rich and diverse spiritual landscape shaped by both Brahmanical and Śramaṇa traditions. To understand the significance of his teaching, it is necessary to recognize this broader inheritance rather than portray him simply as a figure who rejected the religious world from which he emerged.

The Brahmanical tradition preserved an extensive heritage of sacred knowledge, ritual, philosophical inquiry, and reflection on the nature of ultimate reality. Alongside it, the Śramaṇa traditions cultivated renunciation, ascetic discipline, meditation, and the pursuit of liberation from the conditions of ordinary existence. These traditions were neither identical nor entirely separate. They developed in interaction, sometimes sharing questions and practices while differing profoundly in their answers.

Gautama's teaching arose within this wider environment. Arahanism understands his achievement as a profound reformulation of both inheritances: the integration of spiritual knowledge with the discipline of renunciation and samādhi, directed towards a definitive realization of liberation.

**Gautama as a Reformer of the Brahmanical Tradition**

Gautama's criticisms of Brahmins and Brahmanical practices should not automatically be interpreted as a rejection of the entire Brahmanical heritage. In the early Buddhist texts, his criticism often targets particular claims to spiritual authority, ritual practices, inherited status, and the conduct of those who claimed Brahminical distinction without possessing the qualities that would justify it.

The distinction matters. To criticize the degeneration of a tradition is not necessarily to deny everything that tradition has preserved. A reformer may challenge those who have misunderstood, misused, or failed to embody their inheritance while continuing to recognize the significance of the questions and truths that originally animated it.

Arahanism interprets Gautama in this light. His criticism of Brahmanical religion should be understood in relation to his pursuit of genuine spiritual realization, not reduced to a simple rejection of everything associated with the Vedas or the Brahmanical world.

The Pāli epithet *vedagū*, associated with profound knowledge or mastery on the Vedas, is particularly relevant to this inquiry. Its significance deserves careful examination when considering how Gautama positioned himself in relation to the language and concepts of his religious environment. For Arahanism, it invites us to reconsider the wrong portrayals of Gautama as wholly detached from the Brahmanical intellectual and spiritual inheritance.

Arahanism also maintains that Gautama did not deny the existence of the Ātman or Soul. Arahanism interprets his teaching on *anattā* as a rejection of identifying the true Self/ Soul with the five aggregates and the worldly ego, rather than as a declaration that no Soul exists for humans. This is a major point of disagreement between Arahanism and many contemporary academic and Buddhist interpretations. It therefore requires direct engagement with the relevant early texts and their competing interpretations.

The central Arahanist argument is that Gautama's relationship with the Brahmanical tradition must be investigated rather than predetermined by the assumption that he rejected its entire spiritual inheritance and the existence of the Ātman or Soul.

**Gautama and the Śramaṇa Tradition of Renunciation**

The Śramaṇa traditions provide another essential context for understanding Gautama's path. Ancient India was home to renunciants who left household life in pursuit of spiritual freedom. Through ascetic discipline, meditation, sensory restraint, and philosophical inquiry, they sought to overcome the limitations of human existence.

The Jain tradition and other renunciant communities developed their own teachings and disciplines within this broader environment. Their doctrines may differ from Gautama's to some degree, but their presence demonstrates that renunciation and liberation were central concerns in the spiritual landscape of ancient India.

Gautama did not merely reproduce every practice or doctrine he encountered. His path involved critical examination, disciplined cultivation, and realization. The significance of his teaching lies in the distinctive way he organized the spiritual life around the attainment of liberation.

For Arahanism, *samādhi* and renunciant discipline are essential to understanding this achievement. Knowledge cannot be separated from the transformation of the practitioner, and spiritual inquiry cannot be fulfilled merely by possessing ideas about or awakening to certain reality. The practitioner must undertake the discipline through which worldly attachment is abandoned, the mind-heart is purified, and liberation is realized.

The Śramaṇa inheritance thus provides the context for Gautama's emphasis on worldly renunciation, while his engagement with the broader Brahmanical heritage opens further questions about knowledge and truth, ultimate reality, and the meaning of spiritual attainment.

**The Synthesis: A Distinctive Path to Liberation**

Arahanism understands Gautama as a reformer who brought these inheritances to a new height. He did not simply preserve an existing Brahmanical system, nor did he merely reproduce the practices of the Śramaṇa communities. He established a distinctive path in which renunciation, sense restraint, samādhi, and liberating wisdom were directed towards the attainment of Arahantship.

This interpretation challenges the familiar picture of Buddhism promoted by mainstream academia as a movement defined principally by its rejection of Brahmanical thought. Arahanism instead asks whether Gautama's achievement is better understood through the transformation of inherited spiritual resources into a path with a clearly defined liberating purpose.

The distinction is fundamental. Gautama's greatness, in the Arahanist understanding, lies not in the creation of a new religious identity for its own sake, but in his realization and teaching of a path that leads beyond worldly bondage.

His teaching must therefore be examined in relation to both its historical inheritance and its ultimate objective. The question is not simply which earlier tradition he accepted or rejected. It is how he distinguished genuine spiritual realization from inherited knowledge, ritual, and practices that fail to bring liberation.

For Arahanism, this is the defining achievement of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant: the reformulation of the spiritual quest into a path of complete renunciation and liberation.

The history that follows must be understood against this background. If Gautama's teaching emerged through a distinctive engagement with the Brahmanical and Śramaṇa traditions, then the subsequent development of Buddhism must be evaluated by asking whether this liberation-centred purpose remained intact, or whether it gradually became obscured as new interpretations and spiritual ideals gained prominence.

**3). The Five-Hundred-Year Prophecy: A Warning About the Future of the True Dhamma**

**Traditionally attributed to Gautama in the Vinaya**

The Theravada Pali Canon Vinaya contains a prediction associated with the establishment of the Bhikkhunī Saṅgha: that the duration of Gautama's True Dhamma would last only five hundred years. Versions of this prediction occur in several other Vinaya traditions as well, although its interpretation in the commentary may differ, some commentors may think it should be 5000 years instead for example.

Within the traditional framework, this prediction invites reflection on the vulnerability of a spiritual teaching as it passes from one generation to another.

A teaching may survive institutionally while its original purpose becomes increasingly obscured and difficult to recognize. Its texts may continue to be recited, its monasteries may remain active, and its followers may sincerely believe that they preserve the tradition. Yet the question remains: does the living practice retain the same orientation towards liberation from the world that defined the teaching at its beginning?

Arahanism interprets the five-hundred-year prophecy as a historical fact and a warning that the continued existence of a religious institution does not necessarily guarantee the continued primacy of its founding purpose.

The distinction is crucial. The disappearance or decline of the True Dhamma need not be understood simply as the physical disappearance of monks, monasteries, scriptures, or religious communities. From the perspective of Arahanism, the more fundamental danger is that the path to complete liberation from the world may become obscured even while the name and outward forms of the tradition endure.

The five-hundred-year period therefore provides an important traditional reference point for the historical inquiry that follows. It should not, however, be treated as independently verified proof that the teaching disappeared at a precise date.

The question is not merely how long a tradition survives. It is whether the purpose for which it was established remains at its centre.

**4). The Third Buddhist Council: A Traditional Turning Point**

**Approximately 250 BCE --- The reign of King Asoka**

According to the Theravāda chronicles, a Third Buddhist Council was convened at Pāṭaliputta during the reign of King Asoka, traditionally dated to approximately 250 BCE. The account associates the council with the elder Moggaliputta Tissa and describes efforts to establish doctrinal boundaries, address disputed teachings, and reaffirm the community's understanding of the Dhamma. The chronicles also connect the council with the dispatch of missions to other regions, including Sri Lanka.

Asoka's inscriptions demonstrate his involvement to support the Saṅgha to conduct the council meeting trying to unify the Dhamma and Vinaya, the Theravāda chronicles give much details of this historical event. Many other traditions may have forgotten this history, but luckily the Theravāda lineage remembers its own history.

It presents a moment when the growing religious community now become national wide of the whole northern Indian continent faced the problem of distinguishing teachings regarded as authentic from teachings regarded as incompatible with its understanding of the Dhamma. It also portrays the state's patronage as giving the Saṅgha greater public recognition and enabling the transmission of its teachings beyond its original centres and on a world wide stage.

Arahanism treats this period as an important point of departure for examining the subsequent institutional history of the tradition. It does not require us to assume that every event in the traditional account can be independently verified, nor that a single council definitively settled every question of doctrine.

The deeper issue is the relationship between the preservation of a teaching and the interpretation of that teaching.

Gautama's teaching was transmitted orally, interpreted by successive generations, and preserved within communities that developed under different historical circumstances, carried on by people with diverse spiritual and cultural background. In such a process, questions inevitably arise concerning the relationship between inherited formulations and the purpose they are intended to serve.

Arahanism uses a criterion directly came from Gautama own instruction for addressing these questions: teachings must be examined according to the direction in which they lead the practitioner. Do they lead towards the abandonment of worldly craving and attachment, the eradication of defilements, and complete liberation? Or do they primarily encourage continued involvement in worldly existence, even when that involvement is expressed in religious or spiritual language?

This criterion is central to Arahanism's understanding of the True Dhamma.

**5). The Pāli Canon: Preservation, Development, and the Question of Authenticity**

**From the early centuries after Gautama to the first century BCE**

The teachings attributed to Gautama were transmitted orally for generations before the Pāli Canon was committed to writing, according to the Theravāda tradition, in Sri Lanka around 30 BCE.

Traditional accounts associate this undertaking with 12 years of famine, decades of political instability including civil wars and invasions from southern Indian continent, and thus caused danger to the continuity of monastic learning. According the Theravada accounts, after decades of war (termed as hundred of years of war) and years of famine, it is difficult to find monks can recite big portions of the Pali Canon. The decision to write down the texts was thus decided as a means of protecting the teachings against the risk that oral transmission might fail.

The preservation of the Pāli Canon was therefore a major development in the history of Theravāda Buddhism. It provided a durable textual foundation for the tradition and helped establish the collection that became authoritative across much of Sri Lanka and mainland Southeast Asia.

Yet the act of preservation also raises a question that remains important to Arahanism: how should we understand the relationship between the surviving collection and the original teaching?

The existence of a canon does not, by itself, establish that every text within it originated directly with Gautama or that every passage expresses an identical conception of liberation. The collection contains different genres, styles of exposition, and presentations of spiritual practice. Its texts must therefore be read with attention to their context, purpose, and relationship to the central path.

Arahanism does not regard the Pāli Canon as dispensable. On the contrary, it recognizes the Canon as indispensable to the study of Gautama's original teaching. Its concern is that the Canon should not be treated as though every element within it necessarily carries the same historical status or serves the same spiritual purpose.

The question is not whether the Canon should be preserved. It is how its contents should be understood and evaluated.

For Arahanism, the decisive criterion remains liberation. Teachings that guide practitioners towards worldly renunciation, the purification of citta, the eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha, and the attainment of Arahantship stand at the centre of the inquiry. Other materials must be considered in relation to that purpose rather than automatically treated as equivalent expressions of it.

This approach invites a distinction between the preservation of a textual collection and the preservation of the governing principle of liberation from the world.

**6). The Jātakas: Moral Instruction and the Changing Image of Gautama**

**Evidence of circulation by the third century BCE; development over a longer period**

The Jātaka tradition became an important part of Theravada Pali Canon and Buddhist literature. These narratives concern Gautama's supposed former births and are widely associated with moral instruction, generosity, sacrifice, patience, and other virtues. Stories identified as Jātakas were represented in early Buddhist art, while the collection preserved in the Pāli Canon comprises hundreds of verses associated with former-birth narratives. The prose commentary familiar to many readers is a distinct textual layer whose development cannot simply be assigned to Gautama's lifetime.

Arahanism considers the spiritual implications of this fictional literary completely negative. It is untrue, and cause the paradigm shift of the spiritual focus from renunciant liberation toward lay people's worldly merit hunting cravings, and eventually give birth to Mahayana counter movement which replaced the renunciants focused world transcendence liberation path.

When Gautama is presented through stories of his supposed former lives, readers encounter a figure whose long spiritual journey includes acts of generosity, extraordinary sacrifice, moral excellence, and the accumulation of virtues across many existences. These narratives can provide ethical instruction and inspire admiration. But they can also shape the way people understand the purpose of spiritual practice. And most importantly they are not facts and truth.

A reader who identifies with these stories may come to regard the accumulation of merit and the perfection of virtues over successive lives as the principal model of spiritual achievement. The question of complete renunciation and the attainment of Arahantship may consequently recede from view.

This is the critical point for Arahanism.

The issue is not whether moral virtues are valuable. Nor is it whether stories can communicate ethical lessons. The issue is whether a particular model of spiritual aspiration directs people towards the end of worldly bondage or encourages them to understand religious life primarily as the accumulation of merits within the world of repeated existence.

Arahanism interprets the growing prominence of former-birth narratives and, later, the Bodhisattva ideal as part of a broader transformation in the imagination of Buddhist practice. The figure of Gautama could increasingly be contemplated not only as the renunciant who attained liberation and taught others to do likewise, but also as a future Buddha whose long career of preparation could become a model for spiritual aspiration.

This development of course should not be reduced to a single date or claimed to have originated exclusively in the Jātakas. There could be manipulating forces behind as well. The relationship between Jātaka literature, the Bodhisattva ideal, and the emergence of Mahāyāna is not only as we see on the surface. Nevertheless, Arahanism sees in this literary tradition an important example of how the emphasis of a religious tradition can shift without the abandonment of its older texts.

A tradition may preserve narratives associated with liberation from the world while gradually directing popular attention, or collective consciousness, towards a different ideal.

**7). The Bhagavad-Gītā and the Wider Renunciant Question**

**A parallel witness to ancient Indian concerns about liberation**

The second chapter of the *Bhagavad-Gītā* presents a sustained discussion of renunciants based liberation from the world, with topics such as wisdom, disciplined action, equanimity, freedom from desire, and the restraint of the senses. This chapter is estimated to be inserted into the *Gītā* around 200 BCE and thus provides a valuable window to investigate how the renunciation based liberation path was perceived by the general populace.

The other chapters of *Bhagavad-Gītā* the presents other religious practices and philosophical framework, including Karma-yoga, represent the emerging Mahayana movement and their practices, as a wider ancient Indian spiritual movement about spiritual freedom.

The Jain tradition and other śramaṇa communities likewise emphasize renunciation, discipline, and liberation. Their doctrines may not be identical to those of Gautama, but their presence helps to understand the wider renunciants based world transcendence liberation path, instead of the later lay people centered merit accumulation rituals as we see in Mahayana. Jainism traditionally always questioned the ordinary household life and pursued release through rigorous spiritual practice.

For Arahanism, this wider context matters because it prevents the history of renunciant liberation from being understood solely through the later institutional boundaries of Buddhism.

The search for freedom from worldly attachment, the cultivation of sense control, withdrawal from the world, and the aspiration to transcend human existence were not concerns that belonged exclusively to one later Buddhist denomination. They were part of a broader religious environment in which Gautama's distinctive teaching took shape.

The chapters of the *Bhagavad-Gītā,* estimated to be compiled during a period of 200 BCE and 200 AD, is therefore a valuable windows to see how renunciants based world transcendence liberation path was challenged by a broader spiritual movement with lay people centered spirituality.

**8). The Missions to Sri Lanka and the Formation of the Theravāda Tradition**

**Traditionally dated around 250 BCE after the 3^rd^ council meeting during King Asoka's reign**

The Theravāda chronicles associate the reign of King Asoka with missions that carried the teachings of the Saṅgha beyond the Indian heartland. Among the most significant was the mission to Sri Lanka traditionally associated with the elder Mahinda (Pali speaking).

According to this account, the establishment of the monastic tradition in Sri Lanka became a decisive stage in the transmission of the lineage that would later be known as Theravāda. Sri Lanka subsequently became a major centre for the preservation and study of Pāli literature, and its monastic tradition played an important role in the transmission of the Canon to later generations as we see today.

The importance of this history should not be underestimated. The preservation of the Pāli Canon in Sri Lanka and its subsequent transmission to regions including Myanmar and Thailand helped sustain a major living tradition of monastic discipline, textual study, and meditation.

From the perspective of Arahanism, however, the preservation of a lineage and the preservation of the original purpose of a teaching remain related but distinct questions.

A lineage can preserve scriptures, ordination, and institutions across centuries. Whether its living interpretation consistently gives primacy to complete liberation from the world is a further question---one that must be examined through its teachings and practices.

Arahanism does not need to deny the historical importance of Theravāda to make this distinction. Rather, it seeks to recover what it regards as the liberation-centred purpose that the tradition was established to preserve.

**9). The Emergence of Mahāyāna: A Broader Conception of the Spiritual Ideal**

**Around the beginning of the Common Era, with further developments over subsequent centuries**

Although the Jātaka literature, developed from about 300 BCE and onward, can be treated as the source of Mahāyāna. The emergence of Mahāyāna was a gradual process rather than a single historical event. Early Mahāyāna texts formally appeared in written texts around the turn of the Common Era, and the movement further developed over subsequent centuries, with the growing prominence of the Bodhisattva ideal (influenced by the Jātaka literature) becomes a significant development in the history of Buddhist thought.

In Mahāyāna traditions, the Bodhisattva aspiration became central to a vision of spiritual practice directed towards Buddhahood (which unlike Arahant, a lay person can achieve, how convenient!) and, in many formulations, the liberation and welfare of all sentient beings. This represented a different ordering of spiritual goals from the one Arahanism places at the centre of Gautama's teaching.

Arahanism interprets this development as a paradigm shift.
