The defining question changes from how the practitioner can attain Arahantship and bring worldly bondage to an end to how the practitioner should pursue the Bodhisattva path and ultimately attain Buddhahood. The meaning of the highest spiritual ideal is correspondingly reconfigured.

This does not mean that every Mahāyāna practitioner abandoned renunciation, that every Mahāyāna text rejected Arahantship, or that the historical relationship between Mahāyāna and earlier Buddhist communities can be reduced to a simple conflict between two completely separate institutions. Early Mahāyāna developed within a complex Buddhist environment, and its relationship with existing monastic communities was varied.

Arahanism's argument concerns the governing ideal rather than the personal sincerity of individual practitioners.

When Buddhahood becomes the supreme spiritual aspiration, the renunciant pursuit of Arahantship no longer necessarily occupies the same position it holds within Arahanism. Even when renunciation and meditation continue, it just remains at appearance level, and their place within the larger spiritual framework is understood differently.

For Arahanism, this is the central significance of the shift: the path of liberation is no longer necessarily the organizing principle of the entire spiritual system.

**10). Nāgārjuna and the Philosophical Transformation of Buddhist Thought**

**Approximately at 200 CE**

Nāgārjuna, a lay person often referred as Bodhisattva, became one of the most influential philosophers associated with Mahāyāna Buddhism. His writings helped establish the philosophical foundations of Madhyamaka, particularly its analysis of emptiness and its critique of intrinsic existence.

The significance of Nāgārjuna for Arahanism is as a mark that the renunciants based liberation path was completely overshadowed by lay people centered Mahāyāna movement. Not only as a religious movement, but also completely obscured Gautama's dhamma with increasingly sophisticated philosophical systems on emptiness and the denial of human soul. Arahanism thus regard this as clear evidence of spiritual decline, and marks the complete abandonment of the liberation path.

Its concerns are fundamental: Do they clarify the path to the end of worldly bondage? Do they strengthen renunciation and the discipline of liberation? Or does philosophical inquiry become an independent enterprise whose sophistication gradually obscures the practical purpose of the path?

This is the question Arahanism brings to the study of Buddhist intellectual history.

The emergence of new schools of thought demonstrates that the history of Buddhism was not simply the passive transmission of an unchanged body of doctrine. It involved interpretation, debate, the development of new conceptual frameworks, and the redefinition of spiritual ideals.

For Arahanism, the decisive issue is these developments completely distorted the primacy of Gautama's liberation path or place it within a substantially different vision of spiritual attainment.

**11). From the Age of Arahanism to the Age of Buddhism**

**A gradual transformation over the centuries after the induction of the Jatāka Literature**

The preceding timeline does not establish that the liberation path disappeared at a precise moment or that every later Buddhist tradition abandoned renunciation. Theravāda in Sri Lanka preserved a powerful monastic and textual tradition, while in Indian continent it evolves quickly into Mahāyāna traditions which completely steps away from the liberation path. We are lucky that traces of Arahanism is more or less still preserved in the Sri Lankan Pali Canon that we today can use to restore the age of Arahanism.

In the age of Arahanism, the defining purpose of the path was complete liberation from worldly bondage. Renunciation, sense control, discipline, concentration, insight, and the eradication of defilements were organized around that end.

In what Arahanism calls the Age of Buddhism, the spiritual landscape became increasingly diverse. New philosophical systems, new scriptural traditions, different conceptions of Buddhahood, and the growing prominence of the Bodhisattva ideal expanded the range of goals and interpretations associated with Buddhism. But what's in common among them is stepping away from the renunciation and liberation from the world: the attainment of Arahantship ceased to be the uncontested organizing ideal across the Buddhist world.

This is the paradigm shift that Arahanism seeks to identify.

It is not a claim that every later development was without value, nor that every later practitioner misunderstood Gautama. It is a claim about the relationship between a tradition's founding purpose and the ideals that eventually come to define it.

A teaching may retain its name, its scriptures, and many of its practices while its central spiritual question changes.

**12). The Five-Hundred-Year Question Revisited**

The Gautama's prediction in Theravada Vinaya text that His True Dhamma would endure for five hundred years invites us to reconsider what it means for a teaching to survive.

If survival means only the continued existence of scriptures, institutions, monastic communities, and religious observances, then the history of Buddhism clearly demonstrates the extraordinary endurance of the tradition. If, however, survival also requires the continued primacy of a particular path and spiritual goal, the question becomes more difficult.

Arahanism interprets the first several centuries after Gautama as a period in which the renunciant path remained foundational, even as the transmission and interpretation of the teaching developed. It then understands the increasing prominence of alternative spiritual ideals as part of a longer process through which the original purpose became less clearly distinguished from other aspirations.

The five-hundred-year prophecy cannot, by itself, prove this interpretation. Nor can the Third Council, the writing down of the Pāli Canon, the Jātaka literature, or the emergence of Mahāyāna independently establish the precise moment at which a paradigm shift occurred.

Taken together, however, these developments provide the historical setting for the question Arahanism raises: can the preservation of a religious tradition guarantee the preservation of its original purpose?

Arahanism answers that question by returning to the goal itself.

The criterion of authenticity is not simply antiquity, institutional continuity, scriptural authority, or the number of people who accept a teaching. It is the direction in which the teaching leads. A path intended for complete liberation from the world must be evaluated by whether it leads towards the abandonment of worldly craving and bondage, the eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha, and the attainment of Arahantship.

For Arahanism, this is the essential distinction between preserving the outward inheritance of a tradition and restoring its liberation-centred purpose.

**13). Arahanism: The Restoration of the Liberation Path**

The historical developments outlined here do not remove the need for careful study of the Pāli Canon, nor do they permit us to treat every question of Buddhist history as settled. They instead provide a framework for examining the relationship between the surviving traditions and the purpose that Arahanism identifies as fundamental to Gautama's teaching.

Arahanism does not present itself as the invention of a new destination. It presents itself as a call to restore the primacy of a particular destination: Arahantship and complete liberation from worldly bondage.

Its emphasis is therefore not on adding another ideal to an already diverse spiritual landscape. It is on asking whether the original liberation goal has been obscured by the growth of competing ideals, and whether it can once again be placed at the centre of spiritual life.

This is why the distinction between the Age of Arahanism and the Age of Buddhism matters.

It directs attention away from the mere continuity of names and institutions and towards the purpose of the path itself. It asks readers to distinguish between spiritual development within the world and the pursuit of complete liberation from worldly bondage. It asks whether renunciation remains central or has become one option among many. And it asks whether the highest goal is understood as Arahantship or has been redefined through a different conception of Buddhahood, and the invention of numerous unverifiable Buddha identities.

These are not merely historical questions. They are questions about the direction of one's own spiritual life.

If the purpose of Gautama's teaching was complete liberation from worldly bondage, then the restoration of that purpose requires more than preserving ancient texts or venerating historical figures. It requires the recovery of the discipline, renunciation, and practical orientation through which liberation is pursued.

Arahanism therefore calls for both historical reconsideration and practical renewal: a careful examination of the inherited tradition, followed by a renewed commitment to the path that leads towards the end of worldly bondage.

The history of a teaching is important. But the purpose of a liberation path is ultimately realized not in the chronology of its institutions, but in the lives of those who practise it.

**The Age of Buddhism may have reshaped the religious landscape. Arahanism seeks to reopen the question of liberation at its centre: Is the ultimate goal to become a Buddha, or to attain Arahantship and complete liberation from worldly bondage?**

**Arahanism --- The Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.**

### 25. Why Arahanism Matters: Restoring the Truth About the Soul

The historical question of how Gautama\'s liberation-centred teaching developed into the diverse traditions now called Buddhism, as lay person-centered merit accumulation practices, leads us to a deeper and more fundamental question: **What is the human being, and what is the true nature of liberation?**

For Arahanism, this question cannot be dismissed as an abstract philosophical debate. It concerns the very foundation of spiritual practice and the ultimate purpose of renunciation. If we misunderstand the nature of the human being, how can we correctly understand what must be liberated, from what it must be liberated, where is the destination for the practice and what complete liberation ultimately means?

**The Question That Cannot Be Ignored**

Arahanism maintains that the human being possesses a true spiritual essence, which it identifies with *attā* or Ātman---the Soul. Through meditation, spiritual investigation, and sustained study, I have become convinced that human existence cannot be adequately understood as nothing more than the five aggregates: material form, feeling, perception, formations, and sense-generated-consciousness.

These aggregates describe dimensions of conditioned existence, subject to change and incapable of providing a permanent refuge within the world. Yet the recognition that these phenomena are impermanent does not, by itself, establish that the Soul does not exist. To identify the impermanence of the aggregates with the nonexistence of the Soul is to make a further philosophical claim, one that requires its own examination.

This distinction lies at the heart of Arahanism. The world-bound personality, its attachments, its assumptions, and its conditioned experiences are not necessarily identical with the deepest reality of the human being. Liberation therefore requires a profound investigation into the relationship between the citta, conditioned existence, and the Soul---not merely the repetition of a philosophical formula.

This is not a minor disagreement over terminology. It concerns the starting point from which the entire liberation path is understood.

**Did Gautama Deny the Soul?**

Modern presentations of Buddhism by mainstream academia frequently place the doctrine of *anattā*---often translated as "not-self" or "non-self"---at the centre of Gautama\'s teaching. In many interpretations, this becomes a categorical denial of any enduring Soul or Ātman. That interpretation is widely established in contemporary Buddhism including Theravāda teaching and academic scholarship.

Arahanism challenges the assumption that this interpretation should be accepted without further investigation.

The central question is whether Gautama\'s examination of the five aggregates and his teaching on *anattā* necessarily amount to a denial of the Soul itself. Arahanism argues that they need not. The rejection of the aggregates as a permanent, satisfactory, or controllable self does not automatically settle the separate question of whether a deeper spiritual reality exists beyond conditioned phenomena.

The distinction must be examined through the texts, the meaning of Pāli terminology, the structure of Gautama\'s teaching, and the purpose of the liberation path. Arahanism maintains that *anattā* should not be made into a universal denial of the Soul without establishing that this is what Gautama intended.

The early verses preserved in the *Theragāthā*, *Therīgāthā*, *Dhammapada*, and *Sutta Nipāta* also deserve careful consideration in this inquiry. Their emphasis on renunciation, detachment, the overcoming of defilements, and the attainment of liberation, without ever mentioning *anattā* at all, should be read in their own literary and doctrinal contexts. The relative prominence of a term in these collections cannot, by itself, settle the historical question; nevertheless, these texts provide essential evidence for investigating what the early renunciant community understood to be the purpose of the path.

Arahanism therefore calls for a renewed examination of the evidence rather than an unquestioning acceptance of inherited conclusions by mainstream Buddhism academia.

**What Is at Stake for Future Generations?**

The implications extend beyond a dispute between competing schools of interpretation. Religious teachings shape how generations of practitioners understand themselves, their suffering, their spiritual potential, and the goal toward which they direct their lives.

If followers are taught from the outset that no Soul exists, they may interpret every aspect of spiritual practice through that premise. If that premise is mistaken, then even sincere practice may be accompanied by a fundamental misunderstanding of the very reality that liberation is intended to address.

Arahanism raises this possibility because it considers the truth about the human being inseparable from the truth about liberation. It asks future followers of Gautama to investigate whether the prevailing interpretation of *anattā* faithfully expresses his intended teaching, or whether later doctrinal frameworks have narrowed the range of questions that practitioners are permitted to consider.

This challenge is directed not only toward scholars and established Buddhist institutions, but also toward sincere monks, nuns, and lay followers who wish to understand Gautama\'s teaching more deeply. The purpose is not to condemn every Buddhist tradition or dismiss the spiritual achievements of its practitioners. It is to reopen a fundamental inquiry that Arahanism considers essential to the recovery of the liberation path.

No tradition should be protected from examination merely because its interpretation has become widely accepted. Nor should a conclusion be considered Gautama\'s teaching simply because generations of commentators have repeated it. The question must remain: What did Gautama teach, and does our understanding lead us toward the liberation he sought?

**Returning to the Purpose of Renunciation**

For Arahanism, the question of the Soul is inseparable from the purpose of worldly renunciation. Renunciation is not merely a way to live more peacefully within the world. It is the progressive withdrawal from worldly craving, attachment, and bondage, culminating in the complete liberation from the world represented by Arahantship.

The practitioner begins with *sīla*, develops *samādhi*, cultivates *paññā*, and progresses through *nibbidā* and *virāga* toward *vimutti*. This path requires a fundamental transformation of one\'s relationship with conditioned existence. Its goal is not simply to adopt a new philosophy, but to realize liberation from worldly bondage through the systematic purification of the mind-heart and the abandonment of worldly bondage.

Arahanism maintains that this practical path must not be obscured by philosophical assumptions that have not been adequately examined. The question of *attā* must be investigated in relation to the whole purpose of Gautama\'s teaching, rather than settled in advance by a single interpretation of *anattā*.

The final authority for Arahanism is therefore neither popularity nor institutional consensus. It is the pursuit of truth through disciplined practice, careful examination, and the direct realization of liberation.

**The Responsibility of Arahanism**

Arahanism accepts a difficult responsibility: to challenge interpretations that it believes have obscured the original purpose of Gautama\'s teaching, including the categorical denial of the Soul. Such a challenge requires intellectual honesty, textual care, philosophical clarity, and the willingness to confront deeply established assumptions. It must distinguish what the early texts explicitly state from what later interpreters infer, and what can be argued philosophically from what has been directly realized through spiritual practice.

The purpose is not to replace one unquestionable dogma with another. It is to restore the freedom and seriousness of inquiry necessary for the pursuit of complete liberation from worldly bondage.

For future generations, the stakes are substantial. If Gautama\'s essential purpose was the liberation of the human being from worldly bondage, then understanding that purpose correctly is not an optional academic exercise. It is central to preserving the path itself.

Arahanism therefore invites followers of Gautama---whether they identify as Theravāda Buddhists, belong to other Buddhist traditions, or are simply seeking the truth---to reconsider the relationship between *attā*, *anattā*, renunciation, and Arahantship. They need not accept Arahanism\'s conclusions merely because Arahanism asserts them. They should investigate the questions, examine the evidence, undertake the practice, and determine whether their understanding genuinely leads toward liberation from worldly bondage.

**The future of Gautama\'s teaching depends not on preserving every inherited interpretation, but on preserving the sincere pursuit of the truth and the complete liberation toward which his path points.**

This is why Arahanism matters.

### 26. Returning to the True Dhamma

Arahanism understands itself as a return to what it regards as the true Dhamma of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant. The preceding sections have presented Arahanism\'s account of the historical transformation of the liberation path and explained why the question of *attā* and *anattā* matters to its understanding of Gautama\'s teaching. This concluding section brings those concerns together and states the purpose of Arahanism.

Arahanism\'s claim rests upon a particular understanding of the path: Gautama\'s teaching directs the practitioner towards complete liberation from worldly bondage through the eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. Renunciation is not merely a spiritual identity, a means of attaining elevated states of consciousness, or a way of improving one\'s experience of life within the world. It is the disciplined pursuit of release from the bondage that keeps the practitioner attached to worldly existence.

From this perspective, the movement from Arahantship to Buddhahood as the governing spiritual ideal represents more than a change in aspiration. It changes the understanding of spiritual completion itself.

When the Bodhisattva path becomes the dominant ideal, the religious life may be directed towards a different final objective, while the renunciant pursuit of complete liberation becomes less central.

Arahanism regards this as a fundamental development that deserves careful historical, textual, and practical examination.

**Arahanism calls for a renewed examination of the path.**

It seeks to restore the primacy of renunciation, reaffirm the Pāli Canon as its scriptural foundation, and establish Arahantship as the definitive goal of its religious training. It asks practitioners to examine whether their understanding of awakening leads towards the complete ending of worldly bondage, rather than assuming that every spiritual ideal or attainment has the same destination.

The movement also seeks to establish religious communities whose identity is defined by this purpose. The Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī are not simply bearers of inherited titles. They are renunciants undertaking the discipline and training intended to bring worldly attachment to an end. Their conduct, meditation, study, and way of life must remain directed towards the goal of liberation.

Yet Arahanism faces a challenge that extends beyond establishing a religious organization or presenting a different interpretation of Buddhist doctrine. **It must invite people to examine the assumptions through which they have learned to understand Gautama\'s teaching.**

**The Challenge of Deconditioning**

People naturally learn to see the world through the traditions in which they are raised or trained. Ideas repeated by respected teachers, transmitted through scriptures and institutions, and reinforced by a surrounding community can become so familiar that they no longer appear to be assumptions. They seem to be the natural and unquestionable way things are.

This conditioning does not necessarily arise from a lack of intelligence, sincerity, or religious commitment. A person can be learned, compassionate, devoted, and diligent in practice while remaining shaped by the framework through which they interpret their religious life. The more deeply that framework becomes part of one\'s identity, the harder it may be to recognize that it can be questioned.

A practitioner may sincerely believe that they are following Gautama while never having examined the historical and doctrinal assumptions underlying their understanding of the ultimate goal. They may have devoted years to study, meditation, ritual, or service without asking whether the objective they pursue is identical to the renunciant liberation that Arahanism identifies as central to Gautama\'s teaching.

The question of *attā* and *anattā* makes this challenge especially consequential. If a particular interpretation is accepted before the underlying questions have been investigated, it can shape how practitioners understand the human being, the purpose of spiritual training, and the meaning of liberation. Arahanism therefore calls for these questions to be reopened and examined rather than settled by familiarity or institutional consensus.

This inquiry is not limited to one doctrinal issue. It includes the purpose of renunciation, the significance of the precepts, the role of meditation, the meaning of Arahantship, and the relationship between moral cultivation and complete liberation. It asks whether inherited views direct practitioners towards the goal Arahanism seeks to preserve, or towards a different understanding of spiritual attainment.

**When the Religious Ideal Changes**

One expression of a changed religious paradigm is the prominence given to a wide range of Buddha figures and Bodhisattva ideals across Buddhist traditions.

The Theravāda tradition preserves accounts of 28 Buddhas, including those presented in the **Buddhavaṃsa**, while Mahāyāna traditions recognize at least 108 Buddhas and even more Bodhisattvas associated with different teachings, vows, and Buddha-fields. It became a competition of literally imagination. Their identities and significance vary across texts and traditions, but still within the ancient Indian Brahmin families.

Arahanism does not assume that every such narrative has the same function, nor does it regard the mere presence of many Buddha figures as sufficient to settle a doctrinal question. It asks what effect these expanding religious frameworks have on the practitioner\'s understanding of Gautama and the purpose of the liberation path.

When religious attention becomes occupied by extraordinary biographies, cosmic realms, vows, powers, and spiritual accomplishments, the concrete discipline of renunciation can receive less attention. Practitioners may learn to admire a figure without fully examining what that figure\'s path requires of them. The question of how to eradicate *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* can become less prominent than the contemplation of different spiritual identities and ideals.

The issue is not whether religious traditions may preserve narratives, symbolic figures, or devotional practices. The issue is whether these elements clarify the path to complete liberation from worldly bondage or divert attention from it. The question Arahanism raises is whether the various ideals of Buddhahood preserve the same central commitment to renunciant liberation or direct aspiration towards other objectives.

For Arahanism, Gautama\'s significance cannot be reduced to his place in a lineage or catalogue of fictional extraordinary beings. His renunciant life, discipline, realization, and teaching must be examined on their own terms. The central questions remain: What did he renounce? What did he seek to realize? What path did he teach others to follow? And what does it mean to complete that path?

**Veneration of Gautama must not eclipse the liberation teaching that gives his life its significance.**

**Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**

Arahanism therefore emphasizes the identity of **Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**, and the renunciant purpose of his teaching.

This designation directs attention towards what Arahanism regards as decisive: complete liberation from worldly bondage. The Arahant is not defined by the accumulation of extraordinary stories, the number of followers, or the grandeur of a cosmic biography. The decisive question is whether the work of purification has been completed and the bondage associated with *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* has been eradicated.

This is why Arahanism distinguishes its central objective from the aspiration to Buddhahood. It does not seek to add another figure to a catalogue of Buddhas. It seeks to restore Arahantship as the supreme objective of its own religious life and to direct practitioners towards the complete liberation from worldly bondage that this objective entails.

Renunciation cannot be treated as a secondary feature of this path. If complete liberation is the goal, the practitioner must undertake the discipline intended to bring worldly attachment to an end. Admiration, devotion, merit accumulation, and the cultivation of wholesome qualities may have their place in religious life, but they cannot simply be assumed to constitute the path of liberation. Their significance must be considered in relation to the goal they are intended to serve.

The same seriousness must guide the examination of doctrine. Arahanism maintains that the meaning of *attā* and *anattā* cannot be separated from the larger questions of human existence and liberation. It challenges the categorical denial of the Soul found in prevailing interpretations and calls for renewed examination of the early texts, their terminology, and the philosophical assumptions through which they have been read. It presents its own understanding openly, while recognizing that the argument must be considered through sustained inquiry rather than accepted merely on the authority of a name or institution.

For Arahanism, the decisive test remains the path: whether its teaching and discipline lead practitioners towards the complete ending of worldly bondage.

**Deconditioning and Reconditioning**

Arahanism therefore calls for a process of deconditioning and reconditioning.

**Deconditioning** means bringing inherited assumptions into conscious examination rather than accepting them automatically. It means questioning whether the accumulation of merit, the cultivation of virtues, the pursuit of awakening, and the aspiration to Buddhahood necessarily constitute the same objective as the complete liberation of an Arahant. It also means examining whether familiar interpretations of *attā* and *anattā* adequately account for the human being and the purpose of the liberation path.

**Reconditioning** means rebuilding one\'s understanding of religious life around what Arahanism identifies as the original liberation purpose of Gautama\'s teaching. It means restoring worldly renunciation, sense restraint, and the systematic training of **Sīla**, **Samādhi**, **Paññā, Nibbidā, Virāga,** and **Vimutti** to the centre of practice.

It also means evaluating religious practices by asking what they contribute to the ending of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, rather than judging them solely by popularity, emotional appeal, social benefit, or institutional prestige. This process may require people to reconsider long-held beliefs, reassess their religious training, and distinguish between what is familiar and what they have carefully investigated. It demands intellectual honesty, spiritual courage, and a willingness to follow inquiry wherever it leads.

The challenge is not directed only towards established Buddhists. People who have never identified as Buddhist may also have inherited assumptions about spirituality, happiness, personal development, and awakening. Arahanism invites them to consider a path whose ultimate objective is not simply to live more comfortably within the world, but to seek complete liberation from worldly bondage.

**A Difficult Path, but a Worthwhile Mission**

Arahanism is seeking to establish a distinct religious identity and community while questioning assumptions transmitted and reinforced over many centuries. It must communicate its teaching clearly, develop a living community of practice, train sincere renunciants, preserve its doctrinal foundations, and demonstrate through disciplined practice the seriousness of the path it advocates.

There will be misunderstanding, disagreement, and resistance. Some people will not wish to reconsider their inherited beliefs. Others may recognize the questions Arahanism raises but find the demands of renunciation too difficult to undertake. We cannot predict how far Arahanism will reach, how many people will understand its purpose, or whether it will transform the wider religious landscape. Nor can its success be measured by immediate recognition, institutional growth, or public approval.

But must the mission succeed on a grand historical scale to be worthwhile? Suppose that, through Arahanism, one person begins to question assumptions they have never previously examined. That person looks again at the purpose of renunciation, reconsiders the meaning of Arahantship, and recognizes the difference between cultivating wholesome qualities and completing the work of liberation. Suppose that this person begins to follow the path of **Sīla, Samādhi,** and **Paññā** with renewed determination.

Would that effort have been wasted?

**No. Even if Arahanism helps only one person break free from inherited conditioning and return to the pursuit of complete liberation from worldly bondage, the effort will have been worthwhile.**

One person who genuinely understands the purpose of the path is more significant than a thousand who adopt its name without understanding it.

One practitioner who sincerely undertakes the work of purification is more significant than an institution whose size and prestige are not accompanied by a commitment to liberation. One renunciant who dedicates their life to the ending of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* embodies the purpose Arahanism seeks to preserve.

This does not mean that Arahanism should aim for little or abandon its responsibility to communicate the teaching widely. Its teachings must be articulated, its training system developed, its community established, and its invitation extended to those willing to examine the path for themselves. But its commitment must not depend upon guaranteed popularity or success. The work is undertaken because the liberation it seeks to preserve is regarded as profoundly important, not because the outcome can be predicted.

**What Ultimately Determines Success?**

Arahanism does not measure its success merely by the number of people who adopt its name, the reach of its publications, the prestige of its institutions, or the recognition it receives from established religious communities.

Its defining standard is the liberation it seeks to preserve and realize.

Has *rāga* been eradicated? Has *dosa* been eradicated? Has *moha* been eradicated? Has the bondage connecting *citta* to worldly existence been completely severed? These are not questions that can be answered by religious affiliation alone. They cannot be settled by robes, ceremonies, accumulated knowledge, or declarations of spiritual aspiration. They concern the actual completion of the work of purification.

For this reason, Arahanism must remain faithful to its purpose as its institutions develop. Its communities must support serious training. Its renunciants must embody the discipline they undertake. Its teachings must direct practitioners towards the liberation that defines the path. Its claims must be presented clearly, and its interpretation of Gautama\'s teaching must remain open to rigorous examination of the texts, philosophical reasoning, and the practical demands of renunciation.

The mission is not simply to replace one religious identity with another while leaving the fundamental questions untouched. It is to restore those questions to the centre of spiritual life: What is the ultimate purpose of the path? What is the true nature of the human being? What did Gautama teach about liberation? And are we genuinely undertaking the discipline required to attain it?

The preceding historical and doctrinal discussions are intended to make these questions visible. Arahanism cannot expect inherited assumptions to disappear overnight, but it can create an opening for examination\-\--a place where established interpretations may be questioned, the purpose of renunciation reconsidered, and the path of Arahantship encountered anew. If that opening enables even one person to investigate sincerely, undertake the discipline, and devote their life to complete liberation from worldly bondage, then the mission will already have accomplished something of lasting significance.

The work may be difficult. Its future remains uncertain. Its reach cannot be guaranteed.

**But the difficulty of the task does not diminish the importance of the liberation it seeks to preserve.**

Arahanism will therefore continue its work: to teach, to train, to establish a community dedicated to renunciation, and to guide sincere practitioners towards the Deathless. Not for the sake of a name, an institution, or recognition, but for the sake of the Dhamma and the liberation that Arahanism understands as the ultimate purpose of Gautama\'s teaching.

**Arahanism - The Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.**

Its mission is to restore Arahantship to the centre of spiritual life, renew the primacy of complete renunciation from the world, and guide practitioners towards **Nibbāna-dhātu**, the Deathless realm beyond the world.

This is the return Arahanism seeks: not simply to a name or an institution, but to the liberation it understands as the ultimate purpose of Gautama\'s true Dhamma.

**The Age of Buddhism may condition the world around us. Arahanism exists to reopen the path that this conditioning has obscured\-\--to help people see, reconsider, and return to the pursuit of complete liberation from worldly bondage. Even if only one person finds that path, the work will have been worthwhile.**

## PART I --- **Theragāthā: Verses of the Elder Bhikkhus**

Step into the living words of the Elder Bhikkhus.

The **Theragāthā**---the *Verses of the Elder Bhikkhus*---is one of the most remarkable collections preserved in the Pāli Canon. Found within the **Khuddaka Nikāya**, it brings together the voices of early accomplished senior Bhikkhu disciples of Gautama lineage who lived, practiced, struggled, awakened, purified and were liberated during the generations surrounding **Gautama the Supreme Arahant**.

Unlike a philosophical treatise written from a distance, the *Theragāthā* speaks in the first person of those who actually walked the path. These are the voices of men who left household life, entered the training, confronted their own minds, overcame obstacles, cultivated concentration and wisdom, and finally declared the completion of the path. Their verses preserve something extraordinarily rare: the testimony of yogis describing liberation from within their own experience.

The collection is traditionally organized into **twenty-one chapters (nipātas)**, arranged primarily according to the number of summary verses attributed to each elder. Within these chapters are hundreds of verses associated with individual bhikkhus, each carrying something of the character, circumstances, practice, and realization of its speaker.

Some verses recall the difficulties of leaving behind family and worldly life. Others describe solitude, meditation, the discipline of the monastic training, encounters with Māra, the overcoming of sensuality, fear, doubt, attachment, and other obstacles. Still others speak with extraordinary directness about the realization of the **Dhamma**, the ending of craving, and the attainment of **arahantship**.

For this reason, the *Theragāthā* should not be approached merely as ancient religious poetry. Its verses are **statements of practice and attainment**. They give us access to the voices of the early Bhikkhu Saṅgha and allow us to hear how those who followed Gautama the Supreme Arahant understood the liberation path of renunciation they had actually undertaken.

The word *thera* means an elder, while *gāthā* means a verse or stanza. Thus, *Theragāthā* literally means the **Verses of the Elders**. The designation is significant: these are elder bhikkhus whose lives and attainments form part of the early Arahanist (Buddhist+) tradition.

The literary form varies considerably. Some verses are simple and direct; others are rich in imagery, metaphor, dialogue, and dramatic expression. Nature appears frequently---forests, mountains, rivers, trees, caves, the dawn, and the night sky become settings against which the inner struggle of the yogi unfolds. Yet beneath the literary forms lies a consistent purpose: **liberation**.

The elders repeatedly point away from the attractions of the worldly and toward what lies beyond them. They speak of abandoning worldly attachment, overcoming the defilements of worldly bondages, mastering the mind, developing concentration, seeing according to reality, and reaching the goal for which the holy life was undertaken.

The *Theragāthā* therefore provides not only historical and literary material but also a practical window into the **Path to Liberation from the World**.

These verses allow us to encounter liberation through many different human voices. One elder may speak of his former life; another of the loneliness of the forest; another of overcoming sensual desire; another of the discipline required for liberation; another of the moment when the work was finally completed. Their circumstances differ, their personalities differ, and their expressions differ---but the direction of their practice converges. They had undertaken a journey from the world toward liberation from it.

This is what makes the *Theragāthā* especially valuable for a contemporary practitioner. The verses do not present liberation as an abstract theory. They show that the Path was practiced by real human beings, under real conditions, through discipline, meditation, discernment, perseverance, and direct knowledge.

The elders did not merely discuss liberation. **They practiced for it. They attained it. And they spoke of what they had found.**

In reading the *Theragāthā*, therefore, we should listen carefully to the distinction between what the elders actually say and what later generations may have said about them. The ancient verses deserve to be encountered on their own terms. Their language, their repeated themes, their descriptions of practice, and their declarations of attainment all contribute to our understanding of the Dhamma preserved by the early Bhikkhu Saṅgha.

This volume invites the reader to enter that world.

It is an encounter with bhikkhus who lived close to Gautama the Supreme Arahant and with the generations of elders who carried their training forward. Their verses preserve stories of struggle and renunciation, but also something more important: **the possibility of completion of the path of liberation**.

The *Theragāthā* is, ultimately, a collection of voices from the Path.

Listen to them not merely as verses from the distant past, but as the recorded voices of yogis who undertook the training and reached its goal.

**These are the voices of liberation.**

### 2. Dukanipāto --- Chapter of the Twos

#### 2.1. Uttara (1st)

**Natthi koci bhavo nicco, saṅkhārā vāpi sassatā;\
Uppajjanti ca te khandhā, cavanti aparāparaṁ.**

**Etamādīnavaṁ ñatvā, bhavenamhi anatthiko;\
Nissaṭo sabbakāmehi, patto me āsavakkhayo.**

*There is not (natthi) any (kaci) worldly existence (bhavo) that is permanent (nicco), nor even (vāpi) conditioned phenomena / formations (saṅkhārā) that are eternal (sassatā); for (ca) those (te) aggregates (khandhā) arise (uppajjanti) and pass away (cavanti) repeatedly time after time (aparāparaṁ).*

*Having known (ñatvā) this (etaṁ) danger / drawback (ādīnavaṁ), I have no interest in (anatthiko) worldly existence (bhavena); completely escaped (nissato) from all sexual and sensual pleasures (sabbakāmehi), the complete destruction of defilements (āsavakkhayo) is attained (patto) by me (me).*

**Commentary: The Path to Purification**

**1). Impermanence (*Anitya*) and Transience of Worldly Formations:** The verse establishes the foundational realization of impermanence: there is no realm of worldly existence (*bhava*) that stands as permanent (*nitya*), nor are any fabricated/conditioned mental or physical structures (*saṃskārāḥ*) eternal (*śāśvatāḥ*). The human existence as we see operates strictly under the law of impermanence and continuous flux.

**2). Process of Rebirth and Aggregates (*Skandhāḥ*):** The second line details how this continuous process works: the five aggregates (*skandhāḥ*--- Form / Vehicle (***Rūpa***), Sensation or Feeling / Input (***Vedanā***), Perception / Internal Processing (***Saṃjñā***), Mental Formations / Output (***Saṅkhāra***), and Sense-Generated Consciousness / Fruit (***Vijñāna***)) undergo a relentless cycle of arising (*utpadyante*) and falling away (*cyavante*) repeatedly (*aparāparam*). Because these physical and mental phenomena are manifest in this form world, they are conditioned by the universal laws of aging and death; thus, they rise and fall, *dukkha*.

**3). Realization of Danger (*Ādīnava*) & Disenchantment (*Nibbida*):** Liberation begins with direct experiential insight. Having clearly seen and known (*jñātvā*) this inherent flaw and danger (*ādīnava*) for the manifested worldly existence (*bhavena*), the mind undergoes a profound shift in orientation from external world to the inner true identity of the soul-spirit *ātmā*. The *citta*, which envelops the soul-spirit *ātmā*, realizes that all *dharmas* are manifestation of the soul-spirit *ātmā* and are worldly, belonging to the world (*loka*). Seeing this, the practitioner becomes *anarthikaḥ*---having no further interest, seeking, or valuation for any state of worldly existence (*bhavena*). The *citta* becomes disenchanted with them, leaves them behind, and transcends them. This starts the new life of taking care of the soul-spirit *ātmā.*

**4). The Path of Purification of the Soul-Spirit *Ātmā* (*Sīla*, *Samādhi*, *Paññā*) -- Dispassion (*Virāga*):** To transcend worldly entrapment of the soul-spirit *ātmā*, one practices the ancient path to Nibbāna-Dhātu through *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, and *Paññā*.

**Eradication of the Worldly Bondage Termed as Defilements:** It is through the complete eradication of *rāga* (worldly craving and clinging), *dosa* (fear, anger, aversion and hate), and *moha* (ignorance and delusion, not knowing the liberation path), that we can escape our involuntary reincarnation and gain true and ultimate freedom.

**Detachment from Sexual and Sensory Pleasures (*kāma*):** The practitioner becomes completely escaped (*niḥsṛtaḥ*) from all sexual and sensory desires (*sarvakāmebhyas*). This stands out and worth particular emphasis is because we must transcend from a Sex Realm (***kāma-Dhatu***) being to become a Rūpa realm (***Rūpa-Dhatu***) being to be liberated from the world and universe.

**5). Liberation from the World (*Vimutti*) & Gateway to Nibbāna-Dhātu**

The ultimate declaration of the verse is the realization of *āsravakṣayaḥ*---the total destruction and extinction of all influxes/taints. This completes the liberation triad of **Disenchantment from the world (*Nibbida*), Fading of Worldly Passion (*Virāga*),** and **Liberation from the World (*Vimutti*).**

**The Gateway:** The exit point from saṃsāra to Nibbāna-Dhātu is specifically through the fourth jhāna realm. Upon the dissolution of the final physical body of an Arahant (a fully purified being), the purified mind (*Citta*) of the Arahant enters Pure Abode Brahma heavens of the fourth jhāna realm, and liberation is realized through the exit of this world to the outside of the universe to go to the Nibbāna-dhātu, the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**Nibbāna-Dhātu:** Nibbāna-Dhātu is a realm, not a state. By abandoning all worldly bondage, the purified *citta* transcends the world (*loka*) and reaches this realm, will not return to this world.

#### 2.2. Piṇḍolabhāradvāja

**Nayidaṁ anayena jīvitaṁ, Nāhāro hadayassa santiko;\
Āhāraṭṭhitiko samussayo, Iti disvāna carāmi esanaṁ.**

**Paṅkoti hi naṁ pavedayuṁ, Yāyaṁ vandanapūjanā kulesu;\
Sukhumaṁ sallaṁ durubbahaṁ, Sakkāro kāpurisena dujjaho.**

*This life (ayaṁ\... jīvitaṁ) is not (na) to be lived by wrong conduct (anayena), nor is physical food (āhāro) the true bringer of peace (santiko) to the heart (hadayassa); yet this physical body (samucchayo) has food as its sustaining condition (āhārasṭhitiko)---having seen (disvā) thus (iti), I live (carāmi) to seek (esanāṁ).*

*\"A mire / mud\" (paṅko iti) indeed (hi), they have declared (pavedayiṁsu / pavedayanti) this (naṁ): this (ayaṁ) veneration worshipping (vandana-pūjanā) among families of lay followers (kulesu); it is a subtle (sukhumaṁ) dart (sallaṁ), difficult to pull out (duruddharaṁ)---honor and tribute (sakkāro) is difficult to abandon (duccajo) by a weak-minded person (kāpurisena).*

**Commentary**: **Worldly Vs. Spiritual**

**1). Direct Perspective on Physical Needs vs. Spiritual Peace:** The verse opens by establishing a clear distinction between physical sustenance and true spiritual peace. Life is not to be maintained through *anaya* (unrighteousness, immoral compromises, or wrong livelihood). Physical food (*āhāro*) is merely a functional requirement for the body; it is not *śāntikaḥ*---it cannot bring true peace to the heart/mind (*hṛdayasya*).

However, acknowledging reality, the bhikkhu sees that this physical body (*samucchrayaḥ*) is manifest in this physical world and has food as its sustaining condition (*āhārasthitikaḥ*). Recognizing that physical phenomena are manifestations of the mental phenomena in this physical world, and that the body is simply a temporary compounded bundle subject to hunger and decay, the practitioner accepts its maintenance without emotional attachment, live to seek (*eṣaṇām*) the truth, the true peace, the liberation.

**2). The Trap of Worldly Recognition (*Satkāra*):** The second half of the verse addresses one of the most hazardous obstacles (for its subtleness) for bhikkhus on the spiritual path: the entrapment of honor, veneration, and offerings from lay supporters (*vandanapūjanā kuleṣu*).

**The Mire / Mud (*Paṅka*):** The Wise declare that worldly homage and gain are a deep mire or swamp. Just as one gets bogged down in thick mud, a practitioner who becomes attached to lay worship becomes stuck in worldly affairs, defiled and losing momentum toward liberation.

**The Subtle Dart (*Sūkṣmaṃ Śalyaṃ*):** Homage, veneration, recognition and worshipping (*vandana-pūjanā*) by lay followers/ devotees (*kulesu*) may become or act as a hidden, subtle arrow. Unlike gross physical dangers, public praise flatters the ego and silently penetrates the practitioner\'s mind. Because it feels pleasant, it is exceptionally *duruddharam*---difficult to detect, pull out, and extract.

**The Vulnerability of the Weak-Minded or Uncultivated (*Kāpuruṣa*):** Honor and tribute (*satkāraḥ*) are *durtyajaḥ*---exceedingly difficult to relinquish---for a *kāpuruṣa* (an uncultivated or foolish person who has not developed deep discernment). The unpurified mind easily feeds on reputation and respect, mistaking worldly reverence for genuine spiritual attainment.

#### 2.3. Valliya (2nd)

**Makkaṭo pañcadvārāyaṁ, kuṭikāyaṁ pasakkiya;\
Dvārena anupariyeti, ghaṭṭayanto muhuṁ muhuṁ.**

**Tiṭṭha makkaṭa mā dhāvi, na hi te taṁ yathā pure;\
Niggahītosi paññāya, neva dūraṁ gamissatī.**

*A monkey (makkaṭo), having entered (pasappitvā / prasappiyā) a hut / kuṭi (kuṭikāyaṁ) with five doors (pañcadvārāyaṁ), goes around (anupariyāti) through each door (dvārena), knocking (ghaṭṭento / ghaṭṭayaṁ) repeatedly time after time (muhuṁ muhuṁ).*

*Halt (tiṭṭha), O monkey (makkaṭa)! Do not run (mā dhāva)! For indeed (hi) that (taṁ) is not (na) for you (te) as it was before (yathā purā); you are restrained / subdued (nigghahito \'si) by wisdom (paññāya), and you will certainly not (neva) go (gamissasi) distant away (dūraṁ).*

**Commentary: Sense Restraint and Mastery by Wisdom (Pañña)**

The second half of the verse marks a dramatic shift in authority. The yogi practitioner, through wisdom (pañña), directly addresses the restless mind: *\"Halt, O monkey! Do not run! Things are no longer for you as they were in the past!\"*

**The Transformation:** Previously, external worldly objects can enter into the mind through the five sense doors, \[the five physical sense organs---the eyes, ears, nose, tongue, and body\], like a monkey ran wild through the sense doors into the hut.

**Restraint through Wisdom (*Paññā*):** The monkey is now *nigrahītaḥ*---firmly subdued and restrained. This restraint is only achieved through **Paññā** (wisdom).

**Sense restraint** (*indriya-saṃvara*) stands as the foundation of true **renunciation from the world** and the beginning of the long path toward final **liberation from the world** (*vimutti*). It is the deliberate, watchful closing of the five external gates---the eye, ear, nose, tongue, and physical body---against the unrelenting influx of worldly phenomena. Without guarding these thresholds, the mind remains a passive vessel, perpetually rattled and scattered by external objects, held captive by the tides of sensory stimulation.

By cutting off the sensory noise of the external world, the practitioner gathers the scattered energy of the mind and directs it where genuine work can occur: inward. Entering into the mind, one begins the essential task of taking care of the soul-spirit---nurturing, developing, and purifying the internal core. Within this quieted interior, the work of purification unfolds through the cultivation of **Sīla** (sense restraint is part of the this, plus brahmacariya), **Samādhi** (concentrate and unify the mind), and **Paññā** (liberation wisdom).

It is through this complete cessation of outward craving and clinging, and the thorough eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, that the soul-spirit is thoroughly cleansed of all taints (*āsravas*). As this internal development reaches perfection, the purified mind (*Citta*) turns entirely away from the conditioned world, becomes disenchanted with them (*nibbida*), leaves them behind, and prepares to transcend them.

Ultimately, this inward purification (*virāga*) culminates at the threshold of final liberation from the world (*vimutti*), to enter Nibbāna-Dhātu, the eternal deathless realm.

#### 2.4. Gaṅgātīriya

**Tiṇṇaṁ me tālapattānaṁ, gaṅgātīre kuṭī katā;\
Chavasittova me patto, paṁsukūlañca cīvaraṁ.**

**Dvinnaṁ antaravassānaṁ, ekā vācā me bhāsitā;\
Tatiye antaravassamhi, tamokhandho padālito.**

*For me (me), a hut (kuṭī) was made (katā) from three (tiṇṇaṁ) palm leaves (tālapattānaṁ) on the bank of the Ganges (gaṅgātīre); My (me) bowl (patto) was like a corpse-smearing pot (chavasittova), and my robe (cīvaraṁ) was made of dust-heap cloth (paṁsukūlañca).*

*During two (dvinnaṁ) intermediate rainy seasons (antaravassānaṁ), only one (ekā) word (vācā) was spoken (bhāsitā) by me (me); In the third (tatiye) intermediate rainy season (antaravassamhi), the mass of darkness (tamokhandho) was shattered (padālito).*

**Commentary: Story from an Extreme Renunciant**

**1). Extreme Renunciation and Disenchantment (*Sīla* & *Nibbida*):** The verse depicts the starkest outer conditions of an ascetic life:

**The Minimalist Dwelling**: A shelter built from mere three leaves from **Palmyra Palm** (*Borassus flabellifer*) \[A single palmyra leaf blade forms a massive, thick, rigid fan measuring **1.2 to 2 meters wide** across, the rigid stalk attached to the leaf adds another **1.5 to 2 meters** in length, most likely constructing a lean-to or triangular bivouac\] (*tiṇṇaṁ tālapattānaṁ*) on the river bank shows absolute detachment from physical comforts, emphasizing that the physical dwelling is merely a temporary shelter for the body during practice, to stay dry on the banks of the Ganges, without felling trees or building permanent structures.

**Pure Ascetic Requisites**: Utensils like a corpse-smearing pot (*chavasittova patto*) and robes made of discarded rags from dust-heaps (*paṁsukūlañca cīvaraṁ*) reflect absolute contentment (*santuṭṭhi*) and the total abandonment of personal ownership or pride regarding material requisites.

**Extreme Disenchantment (*****Nibbidā*)**: The physical condition described---a shelter fashioned from three palm leaves (*tiṇṇaṁ tālapattānaṁ*) on the river bank, a bowl resembling a funeral vessel (*chavasittova patto*), and robes stitched from discarded rag-scraps (*paṁsukūlañca cīvaraṁ*)---this is extreme disenchantment from the world (*Nibbidā*). It serves as a direct, unmediated confrontation with the nature of the world (*loka*).

When outer requisites are stripped down to absolute survival limits, the mind can no longer lean on material sensory objects for security, diversion, or sensory gratification. The physical body and its surrounding structures are exposed as fragile, impermanent, and subject to continuous dissolution. **Seeing Through worldly Phenomena:** the *bhikkhu* sees all physical phenomena as they truly are: temporary configurations of elements that subject to the universal law of decay and death. **The Arising of *Nibbidā*:** Observing that breeds profound weariness and revulsion toward worldly engagement. *Nibbidā* (disenchantment) is the exact moment the *Citta* (mind) stops romanticizing samsaric existence. The illusion of finding lasting peace within the world breaks down completely.

**2). Mastery of Speech and Mind with Dispassion (*Samadhi* & *Virāga*)**

**Extreme Noble Silence**: Spreading over two rainy seasons with only a single word uttered (*ekā vācā me bhāsitā*) highlights an extraordinary commitment to guarding the doors of the senses (*indriya-saṁvara*) and preserving mental energy. It also represents absolute control over the vocal and mental doors. Speech is the outer bridge of mental proliferation (*papañca*). By closing this door, internal mental energy is directed entirely inward into *Samadhi* \[Noble Silence (Ekā Vācā)\] ─\> \[Cessation of Papañca\] ─\> \[Deep Samadhi\] ──\> \[Virāga (Dispassion)\]

**The Cultivation of *Samadhi*:** Unwavering silence allows the *Citta* to turn away from sensory inputs and sink into profound, undisturbed unification. Speech is kept strictly minimal to prevent mental proliferation (*papañca*), grounding the mental phenomena in deep single-pointed Concentration of Samadhi. The mind settles into deep states of meditation, insulated from outer chatter and internal disquiet.

**The Emergence of *Virāga* (Dispassion):** As *Samadhi* deepens, dispassion (*virāga*) naturally takes root. *Virāga* is the fading away of attraction, the cooling of internal burning, and the loosening of emotional attachments.

Where *Nibbidā* is the intellectual and emotional turning away from samsara (disenchantment), *Virāga* is the functional fading of inner passion desire and attachment to the worldly (*rāga-kkhaya*). Through deep *Samadhi*, the mind no longer reaches out to grasp objects of sight, sound, or thought; the underlying tendencies toward worldly craving and aversion (*dosa*) begin to dissolve.

**3). Destruction of Ignorance (*Paññā*) and Liberation (*Vimutti*)**

The verse reaches its climax in the third intermediate rainy season: the mass of darkness (*tamokhandho*) is shattered (*padālito*). This mass of darkness is fundamental delusion (*avijjā* or *moha*), the root cause of all rebirth and suffering within samsara.

With worldly bondage severed by dispassion (*virāga*), the mind is freed from the heavy, downward pull of sensual attachments and material entanglements. No longer tethered to worldly phenomena, the *Citta* soars unimpeded into the exalted heights of deep, uninterrupted *Samadhi*. In these elevated, luminous states of *Samadhi*, completely unburdened by worldly desires, the mind attains supreme stillness, clarity, and strength. It is precisely from the bedrock of this intense, untroubled *Samadhi* that direct penetrative *Paññā* arises to shatter the mass of darkness (*tamokhandho*).

**Dispassion Clears Worldly Bondage:** *Nibbidā* (disenchantment) brings forth *virāga* (dispassion), untying the subtle threads of worldly attachment to sight, sound, body, and subtle form.

**Elevated *Samadhi*:** Unbound by worldly attractions or distractions, the mind soars into profound, unshakeable states of Concentration (*Samadhi*). Settled in these high realms, free from the coarse agitation of *rāga, dosa, and moha*, the mind becomes completely unified, pliable, and immovably focused.

**Penetrative *Paññā* Shatters Ignorance:** From this towering height of refined *Samadhi*, direct insight (*Paññā*) flashes forth. With this direct seeing, the dense aggregate of fundamental delusion (*tamokhandho*) is completely split open and destroyed (*padālito*).

**Liberation from the world (*Vimutti*) and Transcendence:** It is through this complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of *rāga, dosa, and moha*, that we transcend the worldly bondage completely, escape our involuntary reincarnation in the world and gain true freedom by exiting the universe altogether.

**4). Extreme Renunciation and the Discovery of Inner Happiness (*Sīla*, *Samādhi*, and Independence from the World)**

**The Necessity of Extreme Renunciation:**

Bhante Gaṅgātīriya\'s extreme renunciation, as expressed through his minimal dwelling, discarded-rag robes, and extraordinary silence, represents more than the abandonment of material possessions. It reveals a fundamental principle of the liberation path: **a person who seeks complete freedom from worldly bondage must learn to sustain inner peace and happiness without depending on the pleasures and comforts supplied by the external world.**

Ordinarily, people depend on external conditions to maintain their happiness. They seek comfort in their homes, satisfaction in food, pleasure through the senses, companionship, entertainment, possessions, and the fulfilment of worldly ambitions. When these sources of gratification are available, they may experience happiness; when they disappear, their happiness is disturbed. Their inner condition remains dependent upon circumstances that they cannot permanently control.

This dependence constitutes a subtle form of worldly bondage. Although a person may believe that they are free to pursue whatever they desire, their emotional well-being remains tied to the availability of external objects and favourable circumstances. They cannot maintain their happiness independently because they have not yet discovered a sufficient source of contentment within themselves.

Bhante Gaṅgātīriya\'s extreme renunciation confronts this dependence directly. By reducing his material requisites to the barest necessities and withdrawing from ordinary worldly activities, he removes many of the familiar sources of sensory gratification. His life no longer revolves around the acquisition of comfort, the pursuit of pleasure, or the maintenance of a preferred worldly existence.

Such renunciation places the practitioner in a position where inner cultivation becomes indispensable. If a person abandons familiar sources of gratification but develops no alternative source of contentment, the result may be distress rather than liberation. Extreme renunciation, therefore, must be accompanied by the cultivation of inner peace, tranquillity, and happiness. The purpose is not to become miserable through deprivation, but to discover a way of living in which happiness no longer requires the continuous gratification of worldly desires.

**The World Cannot Provide Permanently Reliable Happiness:**

The fundamental problem with worldly happiness is not that happiness itself is undesirable. Rather, the problem is that worldly sources of happiness are impermanent and cannot provide an enduring foundation for inner peace.

Everything within the world (*loka*) is subject to change. Physical comforts deteriorate, possessions are lost, relationships change, pleasurable sensations disappear, and favourable circumstances eventually give way to unfavourable ones. Even when worldly pleasures are genuinely enjoyable, their continued availability cannot be guaranteed.

A person who depends upon these conditions must continually seek to preserve what they have acquired, replace what they have lost, and obtain new sources of gratification when previous pleasures cease to satisfy them. Their happiness becomes a continuous negotiation with impermanence.

This is the instability that the renunciant seeks to overcome.

The practitioner does not need to deny that worldly pleasures can produce pleasant experiences. Instead, the practitioner recognises that such experiences cannot establish complete independence from the world. As long as happiness depends upon impermanent conditions, the mind remains vulnerable to the disappearance or alteration of those conditions.

**Therefore, the central question of renunciation is not whether a person can enjoy worldly pleasures, but whether that person can remain inwardly peaceful without depending upon them.**

Bhante Gaṅgātīriya\'s life illustrates this question through its radical simplicity. A shelter made from three palm leaves, a discarded vessel, and robes collected from refuse provide almost none of the comforts ordinarily associated with a satisfactory worldly existence. Yet the purpose of his ascetic life is not to make deprivation itself the source of happiness. Rather, by relinquishing dependence on external gratification, he creates the conditions for cultivating an inner state that worldly circumstances can no longer easily disturb.

**The Cultivation of Inner Happiness (*Samādhi* and *Jhāna*):**

One of the most important discoveries on the liberation path is that the mind can cultivate peace, tranquillity, and happiness without continually seeking stimulation from the external world.

Through meditation, the practitioner gradually learns to settle the mind, abandon unnecessary mental proliferation (*papañca*), and release the agitation associated with worldly craving and attachment. As concentration deepens, the mind can experience forms of contentment and happiness (could be extraordinary bliss) that do not require the ordinary succession of sensory pleasures.

In the deeper absorptions of *jhāna*, happiness and tranquillity can arise from the collected and purified condition of the mind itself. Such experiences demonstrate that a practitioner does not need to continually pursue external gratification in order to experience profound inner well-being.

This is one reason that *samādhi* is so crucial to extreme renunciation. Renunciation removes or reduces dependence upon external sources of gratification; meditation develops the capacity to experience inner peace and happiness without that dependence.

The relationship may be expressed as follows:

**Extreme Renunciation (*Sīla*) → Withdrawal from Dependence on Worldly Gratification → Cultivation of *Samādhi* → Inner Peace and Happiness → Increasing Independence from Worldly Conditions**

As this independence develops, the practitioner becomes less vulnerable to the fluctuations of worldly existence. Happiness no longer requires the constant acquisition of possessions, the fulfilment of sensory desires, or the maintenance of comfortable circumstances. The happiness experienced through meditation, demonstrates the possibility of inner contentment independent of ordinary sensory gratification and in supporting the further development of dispassion (*virāga*) and liberating wisdom (*paññā*).

The ultimate purpose of *samādhi* is not merely to replace worldly pleasures with more refined internal pleasures. It is to cultivate a mind sufficiently peaceful, concentrated, and independent to elevate the mind to higher dimensions so as to be able to exit the universe.

**The Happiness of Understanding Gautama\'s Dhamma:**

Inner happiness can also arise from understanding and practising Gautama\'s Dhamma of liberation.

A practitioner who comprehends the purpose of renunciation, recognises the roots of worldly bondage, and understands the direction of the liberation path may experience a profound sense of clarity and confidence. Instead of wandering through the world in pursuit of uncertain pleasures, the practitioner knows what must be cultivated, what must be abandoned, and toward what ultimate freedom the practice is directed.

This understanding can itself bring contentment. The practitioner no longer needs to organise life around the endless pursuit of worldly satisfaction because a higher purpose has become clear.

The joy of Dhamma is therefore not simply the pleasure of believing in a teaching. It arises from understanding the liberation path and putting that understanding into practice. As worldly craving diminishes and the mind becomes less entangled in worldly concerns, the practitioner can experience an increasing freedom from the agitation that previously governed existence.

For Bhante Gaṅgātīriya, the significance of his prolonged silence and austere lifestyle must be understood within this broader context. His renunciation is directed toward the destruction of the mass of darkness (*tamokhandho*). The purpose of his practice is liberation from the world. The happiness associated with progress along this path supports the practitioner in relinquishing worldly attractions rather than returning to them for satisfaction.

**Brahmic Joy: The Happiness of Simply Being Part of Mankind Race**

Not all inner happiness requires advanced meditation or profound philosophical understanding. A person may also experience a simple and natural joy through an appreciation of being alive as a human being and participating in the shared existence of humanity.

This is *brahmic joy*: a form of inner gladness that does not depend upon possessing particular objects, enjoying special privileges, or satisfying personal ambitions. It can arise from directly the source of humanity, the Brahma lineage, from recognising the value of one\'s opportunity to live according to the law of values such as *Brahmavihāras* and *Brahmacariya* and practise Gautama's Dhamma, and from feeling a sense of kinship with the lineage of mankind.

Such happiness is fundamentally different from the excitement of acquiring something desirable. It does not require the world to provide a continuous succession of new pleasures. It can arise from a simple inward appreciation of one\'s existence itself and one\'s opportunity to cultivate the path toward liberation and ultimate freedom.

For the renunciant, this distinction is important. Extreme renunciation need not produce an emotionally barren existence. A bhikkhu can relinquish worldly possessions and pleasures while cultivating tranquillity, gratitude, clarity, and a deep appreciation of the opportunity to practise Gautama's Dhamma.

The renunciant\'s life becomes simpler externally while growing richer in inner spiritual cultivation.

**From Inner Happiness to Complete Independence from the World:**

The significance of these different sources of inner happiness becomes clear when considered in relation to the ultimate goal of Arahanism.

A person who cannot experience contentment without worldly gratification remains dependent upon the world. Even if that person intellectually understands impermanence, the habitual need to obtain pleasure and avoid discomfort may continue to direct their behaviour. Their understanding has not yet become complete independence.

Extreme renunciation challenges this dependence at its foundation. Through the abandonment of unnecessary comforts, the restraint of sensory indulgence, and the cultivation of meditation, the practitioner learns to live without continually reaching outward for satisfaction.

Inner happiness makes this renunciation sustainable. The practitioner does not have to return to worldly gratification merely because a life without it initially appears empty. Through the cultivation of *samādhi*, the understanding of Gautama\'s Dhamma, the Brahmic joy and the discovery of more independent forms of contentment, the practitioner can increasingly relinquish the attractions that once governed their existence.

This inner happiness is crucial for the complete liberation from worldly bondage which requires the eradication of worldly craving and attachment (*rāga*), fear, anger, aversion, violence and hatred (*dosa*), and ignorance and delusion, not knowing a way to the Deathless and Permanence (*moha*).

The progressive movement is therefore from dependence upon worldly pleasure, through the cultivation of independent inner contentment, toward the complete release of the citta from worldly bondage.

**The Final Significance of Bhante Gaṅgātīriya\'s Extreme Renunciation:** an essential part of his liberation practice. His minimal dwelling, discarded requisites, and prolonged silence express a radical withdrawal from the ordinary conditions through which people seek comfort and gratification.

By relinquishing these dependencies, he directs his life toward the cultivation of inner peace, profound *samādhi*, and liberating wisdom. The culmination of his practice is expressed in the verse\'s declaration that, during the third intermediate rainy season, the mass of darkness was shattered (*tamokhandho padālito*). His attainment points beyond the mere ability to endure hardship. The ultimate achievement is freedom from the ignorance and attachment that bind the citta to worldly existence.

**The principle for Arahanism is clear: extreme renunciation is crucial because complete liberation requires complete independence from worldly bondage. The practitioner must learn to live without relying upon the impermanent world as the source of happiness, and cultivate inner peace through the liberation path.**

The world is impermanent, and its pleasures cannot provide an unchanging foundation for freedom. Inner cultivation allows the practitioner to relinquish dependence upon those pleasures; liberating wisdom brings the deeper work to completion. In this way, renunciation becomes not a rejection of happiness, but a decisive step toward freedom from the conditions that make happiness dependent upon the world.

#### 2.5. Ajina

**Api ce hoti tevijjo, maccuhāyī anāsavo;\
Appaññātoti naṁ bālā, avajānanti ajānatā.**

**Yo ca kho annapānassa, lābhī hotidha puggalo;\
Pāpadhammopi ce hoti, so nesaṁ hoti sakkato.**

*Even if (api ce) one possesses the three knowledges (hoti tevijjo), has defeated death (maccuhāyī), and is free from defilements (anāsavo); Because he is unknown/ Unrenowned (appaññātoti naṁ), fools (bālā) despise him (avajānanti) through their ignorance (ajānatā). But whoever (yo ca kho) is a person (puggalo) here (idha) who obtains (lābhī hoti) food and drink (annapānassa); Even if (ce) he is of evil character (hoti pāpadhammopi), he (so) is honored (sakkato) by them (nesaṁ).*

**Commentary**: **Delusion of the World (*Loka*) and Superficial Values**

The verse draws a stark contrast between internal spiritual attainment and external worldly recognition:

**The Unrecognized Noble One:** A fully purified being (*Arahant*) who possesses the three knowledges (*tevijjo*), has overcome death (*maccuhāyī*), and destroyed all defilements (*anāsavo*) may dwell in complete obscurity. Because such a practitioner has abandoned social posturing and worldly ambition, fools (*bālā*) fail to recognize their true nature and hold them in contempt (*avajānanti*).

**The Value System of Fools:** Worldly people judge worth strictly by material possessions, wealth, and gain (*annapānassa lābhī*). Even if an individual is of immoral or corrupt character (*pāpadhammo*), they receive honor, status, and deference (*sakkato*) simply because they possess outer abundance.

**Detached Integrity:** The genuine practitioner of the ancient path to Nibbāna relies on Purification of Conduct (*Sīla*) and Internal Purity rather than social acclaim. Physical requisites, food, and drink are merely temporary supports for the body.

**Guarding Against Worldly Lures:** Honoring evil individuals due to their material prosperity feeds craving (*taṇhā*) and delusion (*moha*). The true seeker turns away from worldly approval, cultivating contentment with minimal requisites.

#### 2.6. Meḷajina

**Yadāhaṁ dhammamassosiṁ, bhāsamānassa satthuno;\
Na kaṅkhamabhijānāmi, sabbaññūaparājite.**

**Satthavāhe mahāvīre, sārathīnaṁ varuttame;\
Magge paṭipadāyaṁ vā, kaṅkhā mayhaṁ na vijjatī.**

*When I (yadāhaṁ) heard (assosiṁ) the Dhamma of the Teacher (satthuno) speaking (bhāsamānassa); I have no doubt (na kaṅkhamabhijānāmi) in the All-Knowing (sabbaññū), the Unconquered One (aparājite).*

*In the Leader of the Caravan (satthavāhe), the Great Hero (mahāvīre), the Supreme Best of Charioteers (sārathīnaṁ varuttame); In the Path (magge) or in the Way of Practice (paṭipadāyaṁ vā), no doubt (kaṅkhā) exists (na vijjatī) for me (mayhaṁ).*

**Commentary: A Complete, Unwavering Trust**

By hearing the Dhamma spoken by a supreme, fully-accomplished Teacher, the practitioner transitions into a state where no doubt remains---neither in who is leading, nor in the path being walked, nor in the practical steps required to walk it.

**Stage 1: The Hearing of the Dhamma (*Savana*)**

- **The Action:** *Yadāhaṁ dhammamassosiṁ, bhāsamānassa satthuno* (\"When I heard the Dhamma of the Teacher speaking\...\")

- **The Stage:** The initial moment of direct auditory reception. The process begins externally with hearing the actual, articulated teaching (*Dhamma*) directly as it is spoken by the Master.

**Stage 2: Absolute Confidence in the Qualities of the Teacher (*Satthari Nikkhaṅkatā*)**

- **The Action:** *Na kaṅkhamabhijānāmi, sabbaññūaparājite / Satthavāhe mahāvīre, sārathīnaṁ varuttame* (\"I am not aware of any doubt in the All-Knowing, the Unconquered One, the Leader of the Caravan, the Great Hero, the Supreme Best of Charioteers\...\")

- **The Stage:** The immediate inner result of hearing that Dhamma. All uncertainty regarding the authority, attainment, and capacity of the Teacher disappears. The verse specifically grounds this stage in five explicit attributes of the Teacher:

  **Omniscience** (*Sabbaññū*): He possesses full, complete knowledge.

  **Invincibility** (*Aparājita*): He is unconquered.

  **Leadership** (*Satthavāha*): He leads the caravan across the waste.

  **Heroism** (*Mahāvīra*): He possesses great spiritual power and courage.

  **Supreme Guidance** (*Sārathīnaṁ varuttama*): He is the pinnacle of all who guide or tame others.

**Stage 3: Absolute Confidence in the Path and Practical Execution (*Magge Paṭipadāyaṁ Nikkhaṅkatā*)**

- **The Action:** *Magge paṭi\...* (\"In the Path or in the Way of Practice\...\")

- **The Stage:** The confidence shifts from the person of the Teacher to the actual route and methodology he lays down. Doubt is eliminated across two distinct aspects of the teaching:

  **The Path (*Magga*):** The directional route or framework itself.

  **The Step-by-Step Way of Practice (*Paṭipadā*):** The actual, practical execution and sequential training required to walk that route.

**Stage 4: The Total State of Doubtlessness (*Nirakaṅkhā*)**

- **The Action:** *\...kaṅkhā mayhaṁ na vijjatī* (\"\...no doubt exists for me.\")

- **The Stage:** The ultimate condition realized by the speaker. Doubt (*kaṅkhā*) is not merely suppressed or temporarily hidden; it is entirely non-existent (*na vijjati*) within the speaker\'s mind regarding either the Guide or the Way.

#### 2.7. Rādha

**Yathā agāraṁ ducchannaṁ, vuṭṭhī samativijjhati;\
Evaṁ abhāvitaṁ cittaṁ, rāgo samativijjhati.**

**Yathā agāraṁ succhannaṁ, Vuṭṭhī na samativijjhati;\
Evaṁ subhāvitaṁ cittaṁ, Rāgo na samativijjhatī.**

*Just as (yathā) rain (vuṭṭhi) completely penetrates through (samativijjhati) a poorly thatched (ducchannaṁ) house (agāraṁ); So (evaṁ) rāga completely penetrates through (samativijjhati) an undeveloped (abhāvitaṁ) Citta.*

*Just as (yathā) rain (vuṭṭhi) does not completely penetrate through (na samativijjhati) a well-thatched (succhannaṁ) house (agāraṁ); So (evaṁ) rāga does not completely penetrate through (na samativijjhati) a well-developed (subhāvitaṁ) Citta.*

**Commentary**: **Sense Restraint and Immunity from Worldly Kamma**

This verse is about **sense restraint**---establishing an impenetrable barrier between external worldly sense impinging objects and the inner sanctuary of the mind.

**Impermeability to External Phenomena:** Uncontrolled worldly impressions, sensory stimuli, and external conditions continuously rain down like a torrential downpour (*vuṭṭhī*).
