Without sense restraint, these forces breach the perimeter, causing the mind to react and being defiled with *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

**Shielding from Defilement and Worldly Kamma:** When sense restraint is fully established, external worldly things cannot penetrate through (*na samativijjhati*) into the mind. Consequently, external conditions are barred from generating new defilements, ensuring that the mind remains untouched and undefiled by worldly kamma.

**Cultivation of the Mind Through Disenchantment (*nibbidā*)**

To transform an *abhāvitaṁ cittaṁ* into a *subhāvitaṁ cittaṁ*, the core process of mental cultivation lies in developing **disenchantment from the world**.

- **Severing the Fuel of Attachment:** The mind naturally allows rāga to pierce through when it continues to see delight, permanence, or value in worldly phenomena. By cultivating deep disenchantment, the Citta recognizes that all external worldly phenomena subject it to friction and instability, or *dukkha*.

- **Transcendence and liberation from the world:** As the *Citta* becomes disenchanted with the external world, it withdraws its interest, leaves worldly phenomena behind, and transcends them. Without the drag of worldly bondage, the *Citta* soars into *Samadhi*, concentrated and unified, entering higher dimensional realms. From this untrammeled *Samadhi* arises *Paññā*---shattering ignorance---which leads directly to Dispassion (*Virāga*), where accumulated and latent worldly passion kamma seeds are burnt out by the primordial force of *tapas*, leading to ultimate Liberation from the world (*Vimutti*). This sequence closes every gap through which *rāga* could leak, turning the *Citta* into an unshakeable, fully fortified refuge that no worldly force can breach.

#### 2.8. Surādha

**Khīṇā hi mayhaṁ jāti, vusitaṁ jinasāsanaṁ;\
Pahīno jālasaṅkhāto, bhavanetti samūhatā.**

**Yassatthāya pabbajito, agārasmānagāriyaṁ;\
So me attho anuppatto, sabbasaṁyojanakkhayo.**

*Exhausted indeed (khīṇā hi) is my (mayhaṁ) birth (jāti), lived out (vusitaṁ) is the Victor\'s teaching (jinasāsanaṁ); Relinquished (pahīno) is that which is called the net (jālasaṅkhāto), the conduit to worldly existence (bhavanetti) is completely uprooted (samūhatā).*

*For the sake of which purpose (yassatthāya) one became a renunciant (pabbajito), from a household life (agārasmā) to homelessness (anagāriyaṁ); That (so) purpose (attho) has been attained by me (me anuppatto): the destruction of all fetters (sabba-saṁyojana-kkhayo).*

**Commentary**: **The Proclamation of Complete Liberation (*Arahatta-phala*)**

These verses record the solemn, definitive declaration (*aññā*) of a fully purified Arahant who has brought the holy life (*Brahmacariya*) to absolute completion. The phrasing directly mirrors the ancient formula marking the end of involuntary wandering through the round of rebirths.

- **Exhaustion of Worldly Birth (*Khīṇā jāti*):** Birth (*jāti*) in this world is a manifestation of the unmanifest essence \[traditionally called a soul or spirit but these terms contain subtle difference\] of a human being; now its generative mechanism---the accumulation of worldly passion, ignorance, and kamma seeds---has been entirely consumed. The engine of renewal of worldly existence (*bhāva*) is drained of fuel.

- **Fulfillment of Gautama's Dispensation (*Vusitaṁ jinasāsanaṁ*):** The training under the Victor (*Jina*)/ Gautama is not an ongoing, unending process, but a definitive path with a clear terminus. The instruction has been fully lived out, leaving nothing further to be performed for the realization of ultimate freedom.

**1). Severing the Net and Uprooting the Conduit (*Jāla* and *Bhavanetti*)**

The second line details the exact mechanism by which the Citta achieves immunity from further worldly entanglement, which stops the renewal of worldly existence (*bhāva*):

- **Relinquishing the Ensnaring Web (*Jālasaṅkhāto Pahīno*):** Worldly craving (*taṇhā*) acts as a multi-dimensional net (*jāla*) that entangles the *Citta* within this world causing the renewal of worldly existence (*bhāva*). By severing worldly craving and attachments through disenchantment (*nibbida*) and dispassion (*virāga*), the net collapses, and the *Citta* ceases to be bound by external worldly objects.

- **Uprooting the Conduit to Worldly Existence (*Bhavanetti Samūhatā*):** The term *bhavanetti* literally denotes the \"lead rope\" or \"conduit\" that drags the *Citta* into renewed worldly becoming (*bhava*) or manifestation, across various realms. Through the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, this conduit is utterly uprooted (*samūhatā*) at its source.

**2). Fulfillment of the Spiritual Quest (*Sabba-saṁyojana-kkhayo*)**

The final lines reflect on the initial movement of leaving the household life (*agārasmā anagāriyaṁ*) to embark upon the ancient path:

- **Realization of the Ultimate Goal (*So me attho anuppatto*):** The decision to leave the world was not an aimless withdrawal, but a targeted pursuit of complete severance of the *Citta* from the worldly entanglement, which is the foundation for the goal of liberation from the world (*vimutti*). That singular objective has been completely attained.

- **Destruction of All Worldly Bondage or Fetters (*Sabba-saṁyojana-kkhayo*):** The highest goal (*so attho*) is explicitly defined as the total destruction of all ten worldly fetters (*saṁyojana*) that bind the Citta to the world (*loka*). With every fetter shattered by Paññā and every latent passion kamma seed burnt out by *tapas*, the Citta stands thoroughly unbound to the world, completelt free, liberated, unshakeable, and completely clear of all worldly conditionality. The *Citta* is ready to jump out of the universe.

**3). Bondage in the Pali Canon**

The concepts of bondage and being bonded means the deep psychological and cosmological mechanisms that bind the **Citta** (mind-heart) to **saṃsāric worldly existence (bhāva)** and the world (**loka**). The state of being bonded is defined as being trapped in involuntary reincarnation, subject to birth, aging, sickness, and death.

**(1). The Primary Classifications of Bondage**

The Pali Canon categorizes bondage under several precise technical frameworks:

**A. The Ten Fetters (*Samyojana*)**

The *Samyojanas* are the primary bonds that tie an untrained worldly person (*puthujjana*) to the wheel of rebirth (*samsāra*). They are divided into two sets:

**Five Lower Fetters (*Orambhāgiya-saṁyojana*)** --- Bind the Citta to the lower realms of existence (sensual sphere):

> **Sakkāya-diṭṭhi:** Real/True (**Sa**) Body (**kāya**) view (**diṭṭhi**) (believing the body is true, or ultimately real).
>
> **Vicikicchā:** Skeptical doubt regarding the ultimate deathless reality and the validity for the ancient path of liberation from the world.
>
> **Sīlabbata-parāmāsa:** Wrong, misapprehension, blind attachment to (**parāmāsa**) the vow / observance (**bata/vata**) of **Sīla**: merit hunter or overly fear for observance-failures, abused by the Jataka stories beyond moral teaching, not knowing Sīla is purely a training place for gradual withdrawal from the world.
>
> **Kāma-chanda:** Sexual and sensual desire / craving for its stimulants.
>
> **Byāpāda:** fear, anger, ill-will, resentment, aversion and hate (*dosa*).

**Five Higher Fetters (*Uddhambhāgiya-saṁyojana*)** --- Bind the Citta to the higher rupa and arupa realms:

> **Rūparāga:** attachment to *rūpa* realm (*rūpa-dhatu*).
>
> **Arūparāga:** attachment to *arūpa* realm (*arūpa-dhatu*).
>
> **Māna:** Ego, conceit, worldly pride.
>
> **Uddhacca:** Restlessness, mental agitation.
>
> **Avijjā:** Ignorance of the three knowledges (**tisso** **vijjā**): 1) knowing what reality is (cosmology), 2) knowing how the universe operates across the history of the cosmic mankind race---including the Brahma source---and 3) knowing how these processes are brought to final resolution or completion: that is, liberation from worldly entrapment, specifically for the mankind race, into the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**B. The Three Root Fires / Poisonous Bonds:** At the core of all bondage to the world lie the three roots of defilements:

- **Rāga:** Sexual craving, lust, passion, worldly attachment.

- **Dosa:** Fear, anger, violence, hatred, ill-will.

- **Moha:** Ignorance and delusion, not knowing the path to liberation.

**C. The Four Bondages / Ties (*Yoga* & *Gantha*)**

The Suttas often use the word **Yoga** (literally \"yoke\" or \"bond\") and **Kāyaganthi** (\"bodily tie/knot\"):

a.  **Kāmayoga:** The bond of sexuality and sensuality.

b.  **Bhavayoga:** The bond of worldly existence/becoming/manifestation.

c.  **Diṭṭhiyoga:** The bond of wrong views and distorted understanding: such as the wrong-awakening to non-dual knowledge or non-dual reality, which leads to dissolution back into the source of the universe; and the wrong understanding of *Anattā* as \"no self\" or \"no soul,\" or the reductionist view that non-self implies the five aggregates constitute the entirety of human existence.

d.  **Avijjāyoga:** The bond of ignorance, not knowing the three knowledges: not knowing the cosmology, true nature or laws of the world. Not knowing the conflict between the civilizational laws and values of cosmic mankind race (from Brahma), and the natural way of the universal laws (from the source of universe). Not knowing what it truly means to be liberated from the world and universe.

**(2). The State of Being Bonded (*Baddha*)**

In the Pali Suttas, an individual who is bonded (*baddha*) is described through vivid structural and existential characteristics:

**I. Subjection to Māra's Snares (*Māra-pāsa*)**

**Māra** is the **personification** of **Death**, the universal law of decay and dissolution. The ancient Vedic metaphor of Varuṇa's noose (*pāśa*) evolves in the Theravada Pali Suttas into **Māra's snare** (*Māra-pāsa*). Anyone bound by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* is caught in Māra\'s net: *Kāmesu giddhā pasutā pamuttā, Anayaṃ samāpannā na hi jānamānā; Nandirāgabaddhā mārapāsena baddhā, Na te pamuccanti mārabandhanā.*

Greedy for sexual and sensual pleasures (kāmesu giddhā), intent upon (pasutā) and immersed in them (pamuttā), Fallen into ruin (anayaṃ samāpannā), truly without knowing (na hi jānamānā); Bound by delight in worldly craving and attachment (nandi-rāga-baddhā), bound (baddhā) by Māra\'s snare (māra-pāsena), They are not (na te) released (pamuccanti) from Māra\'s fetter (māra-bandhanā). (Sutta Nipāta 4.13)

**II. Misapprehension of *Anattā***

The state of worldly bondage is fundamentally perpetuated by a double-sided misunderstanding of *Anattā*, further amplified in the modern era by secular, psychological mistranslations:

a)  **The Modern Translational Distortion (\"No-Self\" as Psychological Reductionism):**

Under the influence of 20th-century secular academia and Western psychology, the Pāḷi term *attā* has been flattened into the abstract psychological concept of \"self\" or \"ego identity.\" This mistranslation distorts *Anattā* into the secular doctrine of \"no-self,\" misleading seekers into believing that ancient spiritual realization is merely a psychological theory claiming human beings possess no soul-spirit (*attā*) or spiritual essence---reducing the entirety of human existence to a temporary, mechanical cluster of five physical and cognitive aggregates (*pañcakkhandha*) that completely disintegrates upon physical death.

b)  **The Materialist/Annihilationist Fallacy (*Ucchedadiṭṭhi*):**

Building upon this translational mistake, this reductionist view assumes that because the five aggregates are *Anattā*, there is no transcendent spiritual entity whatsoever. To justify this claim, proponents of secular reductionism attempt to anchor their \"no-soul\" theory in the teaching of Conditioned Dependability (*Paṭiccasamuppāda*). They argue that human existence is nothing more than a closed, self-contained loop of interdependent mental and physical phenomena---an empty stream of cause and effect where \"impersonal processes give rise to impersonal processes,\" with no underlying soul-spirit (*attā*) or enduring essence behind them. From this premise, they assert that liberation is simply the mechanical halting of this continuous chain of becoming, treating ultimate liberation as total annihilation (*ucchedavāda*)---the exact materialist error explicitly rejected throughout the Pāḷi Canon.

**The True Meaning of Dependent Origination (*Paṭiccasamuppāda*)**

This reductionist interpretation fundamentally misconstrues the purpose and scope of Dependent Origination:

i.  **A Law of the World (*Loka*), Not the Boundless Citta:**

*Paṭiccasamuppāda* does not describe the absolute nature of reality or the totality of existence. Rather, it specifically maps the causal architecture of **worldly bondage, friction, and involuntary reincarnation** (*dukkha*). It demonstrates precisely how the Citta, when blinded by ignorance (*avijjā*), becomes entangled in the worldly apparatus of cause and effect through worldly craving (*taṇhā*) and worldly clinging (*upādāna*).

ii. **Diagnosing the Entrapment Mechanism:**

The twelve links of *Paṭiccasamuppāda* are not an ontological proof that \"nothing exists except conditions.\" Instead, they serve as a diagnostic blueprint showing how the Citta generates worldly becoming (*bhava*) and worldly birth (*jāti*). It shows that as long as the Citta identifies with conditioned phenomena (by the universal law of decay and dissolution), it remains trapped in this samsāric worldly existence.

iii. **Conditioned Cessation vs. Absolute Annihilation:**

When the teaching speaks of the cessation of Dependent Origination (*avijjāyatveva asesavirāganirodhā saṅkhāranirodho\...*), it does not mean the annihilation of a living being or the destruction of the soul-spirit. It signifies the **complete collapse of the worldly causal chain**.

By shattering ignorance through Paññā and burning out latent passion kamma seeds through the primordial force of *tapas*, the Citta severs the fuel of worldly becoming. It drops the five aggregates---recognizing them as worldly burdens belonging strictly to the laws of cause and effect of the world (*loka*)---withdraws its interest, leaves worldly phenomena behind, and transcends them. Rather than dissolving into non-existence, the purified, untrammeled Citta is freed from the closed circuit of conditionality altogether, realizing its true, unshakeable sanctuary in the deathless realm of *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

c)  **The Pseudo-Monistic/Non-Dual Entrapment:**

On the opposite extreme, pseudo-awakening to a non-dual view mistakes the non-duality for the divine, leading to dissolution back into the conscious source of the universe rather than true transcendence out of *saṁsāra*.

To truly understand *Anattā* according to the early Pāḷi Canon is not to proclaim the secular slogan \"there is no soul.\" Rather, *Anattā* serves as a rigorous analytical tool of **disenchantment and dis-identification**. It explicitly targets the five world-bound aggregates (*rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, viññāṇa*) to declare:

*\"This is not mine; I am not this; this is not my soul-spirit.\"*

The fundamental error lies in mistaking the *objects being discarded* (the five aggregates) for the *totality of what exists*. Those bound by this view fail to comprehend that the true spiritual essence (*attā*)---the purified, untrammeled Citta---is entirely beyond, independent of, and uncontained by the five worldly aggregates.

**Textual Proofs & Citations from the Pāḷi Canon**

**The Triad Formula of Dis-identification (*Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta*, SN 22.59 / Vin I 13):** In this sutta, *Anattā* is defined not as a categorical denial of spiritual reality, but as a systematic methodology to dis-identify from conditioned, worldly phenomena: *\"Rūpaṁ\... vedanā\... saññā\... saṅkhārā\... viññāṇaṁ anattā. Taṁ kiṁ maññatha, bhikkhave, rūpaṁ niccaṁ vā aniccaṁ vā\'ti? --- Aniccaṁ, bhante. Yaṁ panāniccaṁ dukkhaṁ vā taṁ sukhaṁ vā\'ti? --- Dukkhaṁ, bhante. Yaṁ panāniccaṁ dukkhaṁ vipariṇāmadhammaṁ, kallaṁ nu taṁ samanupassituṁ: \'Etaṁ mama, eso\'hamasmi, eso me attā\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, bhante.*

*"Form\... feeling\... perception\... mental formations\... consciousness is not the soul-spirit (anattā). What do you think, monks, is form permanent or impermanent?" --- "Impermanent, Venerable Sir." --- "Is that which is impermanent suffering or happiness?" --- "Suffering, Venerable Sir." --- "Is it fitting then to regard that which is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change as: \'This is mine, this I am, this is my soul-spirit\'?" --- "Definitely not, Venerable Sir."*

**Analysis:** The discourse does not state \"there is no soul-spirit.\" It rigorously instructs the seeker to examine each of the five aggregates, recognize their impermanent (*anicca*) and unsatisfying (*dukkha*) nature, and systematically reject them as \"not my soul-spirit\" (*na me so attā*).

**The Five Aggregates Are Not the Whole Being (*Yamaka Sutta*, SN 22.85):** In SN 22.85, bhikkhu Yamaka holds the harmful, reductionist view (*pāpakaṁ diṭṭhigataṁ*) that a liberated being is completely annihilated at death because human existence consists *only* of the five aggregates that break apart. Venerable Sāriputta corrects this exact misunderstanding: *\"Taṁ kiṁ maññasi, āvuso yamaka, rūpaṁ tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso. Vedanā\... Saññā\... Saṅkhārā\... Viññāṇaṁ tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso. Aññatra rūpā\... vedanāya\... saññāya\... saṅkhārehi\... viññāṇā tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso.*

*"What do you think, friend Yamaka: Do you regard physical form as the Tathāgata?" --- "No, friend." --- "Do you regard feeling\... perception\... choices\... consciousness as the Tathāgata?" --- "No, friend." --- "Do you regard the Tathāgata as existing apart from form, feeling, perception, choices, or consciousness?" --- "No, friend." --- "Since the Tathāgata cannot be pinned down or trapped as real and true even here in this very life within the five aggregates, is it proper for you to declare that a realized being is annihilated upon death?"*

**Analysis:** Sāriputta proves that equating a liberated being (*Tathāgata,* literally means having resolved to go there) strictly with the five aggregates---or assuming that nothing remains once the aggregates dissolve---is a fundamental delusion born of materialist reductionism.

**Rejection of Both \"No Soul-Spirit\" and \"Soul-Spirit is the Aggregates\" (*Sabbāsava Sutta*, MN 2):** In MN 2, the Buddha explicitly classifies the dogmatic assertion \"I have no soul-spirit\" (*natthi me attā*) as a thicket of views (*diṭṭhigahana*) and a fetter (*diṭṭhibandhana*) that locks a person into worldly bondage: *\"Tassa evaṁ ayoniso manasikaroto channaṁ diṭṭhīnaṁ aññatarā diṭṭhi uppajjati: \'Atthi me attā\'ti vā assa saccato thetato diṭṭhi uppajjati; \'Natthi me attā\'ti vā assa saccato thetato diṭṭhi uppajjati\... Ayaṁ vuccatābhikkhave, diṭṭhigahaṇaṁ diṭṭhikantāraṁ\... diṭṭhisaṁyojana saṁyutto.\"*

*"When one attends unwisely in this way, one of six views arises as true and established: The view \'I have a soul-spirit\' arises\... or the view \'I have no soul-spirit\' arises\... This is called a thicket of views, a wilderness of views, a contortion of views\... bound by the fetter of views."*

**Analysis:** The canonical text explicitly rejects the annihilationist/ materialist stance (\"I have no soul-spirit / there is no soul\") as an unwise, speculative trap. Denying spiritual reality entirely keeps the mind trapped within the worldly framework.

To uncover why the statement **\"I have a soul-spirit\" (*Atthi me attā*)** is classified as an unwisely (not touching the source/yoni) attended view (*ayoniso manasikāra*). It boils down to **who the \"I\" is** and **the direction of ownership**.

**The Trap of \"I Have a Soul-Spirit\" (*Atthi me attā*)**

When someone says **\"I have a soul-spirit,\"** the sentence grammatically and conceptually sets up a false subject-object relationship: **The False \"I\":** The \"I\" claiming ownership in this statement is actually the **ego/personality rooted in the physical reality and five aggregates** (*rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, viññāṇa*). **The False \"Object\":** It treats the true spiritual essence (*attā*) as a secondary possession---a \"thing\" that this worldly body-mind complex *owns* or *possesses* (like saying \"I have a car\" or \"I have a house\").

This is an inverted, upside-down understanding. It places the worldly manifestation in the driver\'s seat and treats the transcendent soul-spirit as an attribute or accessory belonging to the physical person.

**Correct Understanding: The Citta / Soul-Spirit as the True Subject**

To correct this inversion we need to place the true subject back where it belongs: **The True Subject:** **I am the soul-spirit (the purified Citta). The Manifestation / Entrapment:** **I have a temporary manifestation / vehicle in this physical reality (the five aggregates).**

Under this understanding: **The Physical/Mental Equipment is the Burden:** The physical body and cognitive machinery are physical/mental phenomena manifested in this physical world (*loka*). They are subject to cause, effect, decay, and death (*dukkha*). **The Identification Error:** Delusion occurs when the true subject (the Citta) misidentifies with its temporary manifestation, thinking: *\"This physical body/mind is me, this is mine, this is my self.\"* **The Trap:** Thinking \"I (this person) have a soul-spirit\" keeps you grounded in the physical identity. Recognizing \"I am the soul-spirit trapped in/manifesting through this temporary world-bound equipment\" restores the true scale of spiritual reality.

**How the Pāḷi Canon Frames This Correction**

This exact distinction is illustrated in the **Bhāra Sutta (SN 22.22)** through the analogy of the **Burden and the Bearer**: **The Burden (*Bhāra*):** The five aggregates---the physical body, feelings, perceptions, choices, and sensory consciousness---are the worldly burden. **The Bearer (*Bhārahāra*):** The individual spiritual entity (the soul-spirit / Citta) is the bearer carrying the burden.

The error of saying *\"I (the body/mind) have a soul-spirit\"* is equivalent to the **burden claiming it owns the bearer**. The correct realization is recognizing that the bearer (the Citta / soul-spirit) is currently carrying an external, heavy, worldly burden. Through sense restraint, disenchantment, Samadhi, and Paññā, the bearer simply **drops the burden** (*bhāranikkhepanaṁ*), severs all worldly attachment, and realizes ultimate liberation in *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

**The Analogy of Carrying the Worldly Load (*Bhāra Sutta*, SN 22.22)**

In SN 22.22, the Buddha explicitly separates the five aggregates from the true bearer (*bhārahāra*) who carries and eventually drops them: *\"Bhārā ha pañcakkhandhā, bhārahāro ca puggalo; Bhārādānaṁ dukkhaṁ loke, bhāranikkhepanaṁ sukhaṁ.\"*

*"The five aggregates are indeed the burden (bhāra); the individual bearer (bhārahāra) is the carrier of the burden. Taking up the burden is suffering in the world; laying down the burden is supreme bliss."*

**Analysis:** The five aggregates constitute the worldly burden (*bhāra*). The spiritual essence (the Citta / person) is the bearer (*bhārahāra*). Equating the bearer directly with the burden---or claiming the burden is all that exists---is the precise reductionist mistake that prevents the Citta from dropping the load, achieving dispassion (*virāga*), and realizing ultimate liberation (*Vimutti*) in *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

**III. Involuntary Reincarnation (*Saṃsāra*)**

Because of worldly craving (*taṇhā*) and clinging (*upādāna*), the Citta is perpetually yoked to new worldly existence. Upon the dissolution of one body, the Citta is propelled by its own worldly attachments into another womb, another world, and another cycle of birth and death.

**(3). The Nature of Liberation (*Vimutti*)**

Just as bondage is the internal tying of the Citta to the world through rāga (worldly craving and attachment), dosa (fear, anger, aversion, violence, and hate), and moha (ignorance and delusion, not knowing the ancient path to liberation from the world), liberation from the world (Vimutti) is the complete untying and unbinding from the world:

**Cessation of Worldly Clinging:** It is through the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha, that we can escape our involuntary reincarnation and gain true freedom.

**Transcendence of the World (Loka):** We are trapped in the world, resulting in involuntary reincarnation across this world and all worlds within the universe. We call the gravitational pull of the world the defilements of the Citta---namely rāga, dosa, and moha. The goal of our practice is to purify the Citta (sever all worldly bondage). The purified Citta will then become disenchanted with the world (nibbidā), fading off worldly passion (virāga), and gain liberation from the world (vimutta).

**The Exit Threshold:** The exit point from saṃsāric worlds to the deathless realm of Nibbāna is specifically through the Pure Abodes Brahma heavens. Upon the dissolution of the final physical body of an Arahant (a fully purified being), the purified Citta of the Arahant exit this world and the universe to go to the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**The Unconditioned Realm (*Asaṅkhata-Dhātu*), Deathless (Amata), Pure Wholesomeness (Sugata) with Ultimate Peace (Santi)**

Everything in the world is conditioned (*saṅkhata*) by the universal laws of the universe which contains billions of worlds---including the laws of decay and dissolution. Nibbāna-Dhātu is a realm that is completely unconditioned (*asaṅkhata*) by the laws we observe in this universe because it is outside of the jurisdiction of this universe. That is why mankind in the realm of Nibbāna has no death, they can enjoy permanent blissful life, ultimate Peace (Santi), and without sufferings as we experienced here in this world.

In short, the liberated life in the realm of Nibbāna is not nothingness, annihilation or empty voidness; it is a new life with the perfect freedom. In the Pāli Canon, specifically within the **Asaṅkhata Saṁyutta** (SN 43.1--44), the Buddha delivers a series of short discourses outlining the **epithets, characteristics, and synonyms of Nibbāna**. This definitive canonical list contains **35 characteristics** describing what the realm of Nibbāna is like including:

Asaṅkhata (unconditioned) ananta (infinite) anāsava (taintless, undefiled), Sacca (truth, real) pāra (beyond, the other shore) nipuṇa (perfect) sududdasa (hard to be seen); Ajajjara (un-aging, undecaying) dhuva (permament, constant) apalokita (undisintegrating), Anidassana (non-manifestive) nippapa (free from proliferation/diffuseness) santa (calmed, peace).

Amata (deathless) paṇīta (sublime, excellent) siva (auspicious) khema (secure, safe), Taṇhākkhaya (destruction of craving) acchariya (wondrous, extraordinary) abbhuta (un-precedented, marvellous); Anītika (free from harm/ plague) anītikadhamma (of a nature free from harm/ plague), Nibbāna (unbinding) sugata (pure wholesomeness) desita (shown, pointed out).

Abyābajjha (without oppression, un-afflicted) virāga (dispassion), Suddhi (purity, cleanness) mutti (release, unbinding) anālaya (un-clinging, free from attachment); Dīpa (island) leṇa (cave) tāṇa (guard, protection), saraṇa (refuge, shelter) parāyana (final destination, ultimate goal).

#### 2.9. Gautama (1st)

**Sukhaṁ supanti munayo, ye itthīsu na bajjhare;\
Sadā ve rakkhitabbāsu, yāsu saccaṁ sudullabhaṁ.**

**Vadhaṁ carimha te kāma, anaṇā dāni te mayaṁ;\
Gacchāma dāni nibbānaṁ, yattha gantvā na socatī.**

*The munis sleep (supanti) happily (sukhaṁ), who (ye) are not (na) bound/trapped (bajjhare) in women (itthīsu) --- who indeed (ve) must always (sadā) be guarded (rakkhitabbāsu), and in whom (yāsu) truth (saccaṁ) is very difficult to find (sudullabhaṁ).*

*We have endured (carimha) your execution/destruction (vadhaṁ), O sexual desire (kāma), but now (dāni) we (mayaṁ) are free from debt (anaṇā) to you (te); we go (gacchāma) now (dāni) to nibbāna, where (yattha) having gone (gantvā), one grieves no more (na socatī).*

**Commentary**: *Kāma* and the Path to Liberation

To understand why the sage addresses *kāma* as an executioner (*vadha*) whose debt has now been paid, we must trace the linguistic, philosophical, and cosmological trajectory of *kāma* through the progressive stages of cosmic manifestation.

**1). Ṛgveda: *Kāma* as the Primal Impulse of Manifestation/ Creation**

In the earliest Vedic layer (*Ṛgveda* 10.129.4, *Nāsadīya Sūkta*), *kāma* is introduced as the primordial cosmological force that initiates the entire unfolding of existence: *Kāmas tad agre samavartatādhi, manaso retaḥ prathamṁ yad āsīt* \"In the beginning, desire (*kāma*) arose; it was the first seed of the mind-will (*manas*).\"

Here, *kāma* is the original stir of cosmic manifestation---the binding gravity that draws spirit into form, non-manifestation into manifestation. In later Vedic texts (such as the *Atharvaveda*), *Kāmadeva* is personified as a cosmic deity embodying creative force, love, and sexual potency.

**2). Sensuality (*Kāma*) vs. Sexual Desire (*Methuna-rāga*)**

Understanding how *kāma* operates across layers of density requires distinguishing between its broader field and its condensed anchor:

- **Sensual Desire (*Kāma*):** The master category encompassing gratification across all five physical sense gateways---sights (*rūpa*), sounds (*sadda*), smells (*gandha*), tastes (*rasa*), and tactile textures (*phoṭṭhabba*). Beyond physical sex, *kāma* includes all sensory indulgence: fine foods, music, aesthetics, perfumes, and physical luxury/ comfort.

- **Sexual Desire (*Methuna-rāga*):** The most intense, highly condensed physical anchor of *kāma*. Sexual contact combines all five sense gateways simultaneously and is strictly tied to dense physical bodies.

In conclusion, **Sensuality (*kāma*)** includes sex, but it *also* includes craving for delicious food (taste), beautiful music (sound), aromatic scents (smell), and luxurious visual forms. **Sex (*methuna*)** is the apex of *body-contact* (*phoṭṭhabba*) and visual/emotional attraction. It is the most binding, intense expression of *kāma*.

**3).** **The Russian Nesting Doll (matryoshka) model of *Kāma***

The **Russian nesting doll (matryoshka)** model precisely captures how *kāma* operates across both our dense physical realm and the subtle architecture of the cosmos.

**(1). The Nesting Doll Structure: Layers of *Kāma***

In our physical world (*kāma-dhātu*), *kāma* is organized in concentric layers of density, where each outer layer houses, protects, and expresses the subtler layers inside:

Outmost Doll: Dense Physical /Gender Duality (Sexuality /Women /Men)

└─Doll 2: Physical Touch & Body Contact (Phoṭṭhabba)

└─Doll 3:Taste/Smell/Sound/Visual (Rasa/Gandha/Sadda/Rūpa)

└─ Doll 4: Subtle Manifestation Attraction / Form (Rūpa-Rāga)

└─ Innermost Seed: Primal Stir of Manifestation

**A. The Outermost Doll: Gender and Sexual Bondage (*Itthīsu / Purisesu*)**

- **Why the Verse Mentions Women (*Itthīsu*):** The verse focuses specifically on women (*ye itthīsu na bajjhare*) because **sexual entanglement and gender divide form the outermost, most visible, and most binding shell of the entire nesting doll.**

- **The External Anchor:** In the coarse physical world, the attraction between male and female (*itthi-bhāva* and *purisa-bhāva*) is the dense container that holds all other sensory desires together. It is the immediate, external trap where social obligations, emotional attachment, and physical reproduction take physical form.

**B. Peeling Back the Outer Shells**

When a practitioner begins to unwrap the layers:

a)  **Removing the Outermost Shell (Sexuality):** By stepping back from sexual involvement and gender attraction (*itthīsu na bajjhare*), one removes the coarsest, most troublesome outer layer.

b)  **Exposing the Inner Shells (Sensuality):** Once the outer sexual shell is removed, the inner dolls remain---desire for delicious tastes, pleasant sounds, visual beauty, aromas, and physical comforts.

c)  **Reaching the Subtle Form Layer (*Rūpa*):** As the practitioner deepens concentration (*samādhi*), even these five coarse sensory desires fall away (*vivicceva kāmehi*). The mind enters the Fine-Material Realm (*rūpa-dhātu*), where only the subtle inner core remains: attachment to luminous form, light, and so on.

**(2). Why the Verse Focuses Directly on the Outermost Doll**

The verse addresses the **outermost doll**---women/sexual entanglement---for three direct reasons:

**A. The Direct Point of Entry**

To dismantle a nesting doll, you cannot start from the innermost core while leaving the outer shell intact; **you must open the outermost shell first.**

- Sexual entanglement (*methuna*) is the heaviest, most binding knot in our sex realm/ *kāma-dhātu*. We are sex realm beings.

- By overcoming attachment to the opposite sex, the practitioner breaks open the outermost container of *kāma*.

**B. The Context of the Speaker**

The verses in the canonical collections are spoken by male practitioners/ munis (*munayo*). For a male practitioner living in that social context, female attachment represented the primary external embodiment of *kāma*. (Correspondingly, in verses spoken by female practitioners, male attachment (*purisesu*) is identified as the equivalent external shell).

**Worldly Relationships as False Refuge:** For a man, woman or family is not his true refuge, he will not find truth in woman or family, but delusion and death, because the world is governed by impermanence, and those we love remain subject to ageing, separation, and death. When one seeks truth and liberation through worldly relationships, one encounters delusion rather than deliverance, and death rather than the Deathless. **No worldly relationship can serve as true refuge from worldly existence.** The renunciant must therefore relinquish attachment to worldly bonds and seek the truth that leads beyond the cycle of birth and death.

**C. The Synthesis: Addressing the Master Executioner (*Kāma*)**

Notice how the verse transitions seamlessly between the outer shell and the underlying master force:

- Line 1--2 addresses the **outer shell**: being unbound by women (*ye itthīsu na bajjhare*).

- Line 3 addresses the **entire nesting doll itself**: addressing *Kāma* directly---*\"We have endured your execution, O sensual desire (kāma), but now we are completely free from debt to you.\"*

By shattering the outer sexual container, the practitioner gains the leverage needed to confront and dissolve the entire structure of *kāma*.

**4). Why debt (*anaṇā*)? Because we were born from *kāma.***

**(1). The Nature of the Debt (*Aṇa*) and Rebirth**

Sexual reproduction in this sex realm is most efficient way to generate new souls. The mankind presence in this physical sex realm serves a distinct purpose: sexual reproduction for generating and housing new soul-spirits. The descent of mankind into this dense, gendered realm functions to expand the population of mankind specific souls. Because most of mankind (soul-spirit) in this Sex realm were born because of ***kāma,*** this creates the fundamental debt owed to ***kāma***.

This also leads to the new soul-spirits trapped within this realm, these souls require liberating knowledge and spiritual practice to be released from this Sex realm at least. By transcending the sex realm, they can rejoin the cosmic community of mankind in the fine-material realm (*rūpa-dhātu*), the Brahma heavens. From there, the goal is to go beyond the *rūpa* realm and exit the universe to enter *Nibbāna-Dhātu*---the deathless realm that serves as the ultimate home for mankind.

This journey of liberation for us humans on earth begins through ordination in the Gautama's lineage as *bhikkhus* and *bhikkhunīs*, a sacred path only open to human beings, not other races.

After so many cyclic rebirth, we have paid back this debt by creating more lives under *kāma,* and we considered this debt is paid and we do not want to remain locked in an endless cyclic reincarnation here:

- **The Cycle of Servitude:** Beings bound by *kāma* continuously undergo *vadha*---execution, suffering, and subjection to the laws of death across physical generations.

- **Becoming Debtless (*Anaṇā*):** By severing attachment to *kāma* at its outermost sexual shell and cultivating the path of absolute purity (*Brahmacariya*), the practitioner renounces all lingering obligations to service to *kāma*. The mind (*citta*) owes nothing more and want to be free from *kāma* and the law of reproduction.

**(2). Ascending to Brahma Realms in *Rūpa-Dhātu***

Through the deliberate cultivation of **Sīla**, **Samadhi**, and **Paññā**, and the formal institution of Gautama's Bhikkhu and Bhikkhuni Sanghas, one can train the citta to sever sexual and sensual ties and live in Brahmacariya. By ascending to the Brahma heavens in *rūpa-dhātu*, the *citta* is freed from sexual divide, aligning with the Brahma standards of divine existence. This serves as a vital stepping stone toward total transcendence into **Nibbāna-Dhātu**. Through the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, the purified *citta* leaves behind all worldly manifestation, exiting the universe and enter **Nibbāna-Dhātu**---the deathless realm where grief (*soka*) ceases forever (*yattha gantvā na socatī*).

**5). Classical Indian Philosophy and *Kāma Sūtra***

In **Classical Indian Philosophy (Trivarga / Puruṣārtha)** and ***Kāma Sūtra*, h**uman life is traditionally divided into four goals: *Dharma* (Duty/Ethics), *Artha* (Wealth/Material Success), *Kāma* (Sexual Reproduction), *Mokṣa* (Liberation).

While Western popular culture views the *Kāma Sūtra* purely as a \"sex manual,\" classical scholars and the text itself cover a much broader scope. Vātsyāyana's *Kāma Sūtra* includes chapters on architecture, music, painting, food, perfumery, social etiquette, and gardening, alongside courtship and sexual union.

*Kāma* in that context means **the science and knowledge of all sensory pleasures and aesthetic arts**, of which sexual love (*erotism*) is the central chapter.

**6). Jain: *Kāma*, *Deva-Loka*, and the Graiveyaka/Anuttara Realms**

Jain philosophy provides an exceptionally technical, systematic breakdown of how desire operates across different realms of existence.

**Jain Cosmology & Deva-Loka**

(1). Bhavanavāsī, Vyantara, & Kalpavāsī (Lower/Middle Heavens)

\- Experience Kama (Sensual pleasure).

\- Engage in physical or subtle sexual gratification (by touch, sight, or mind).

(2). Kalpatīta Realms (Graiveyaka & Anuttara Heavens - Higher Form Realms)

\- ZERO Sex (Maithuna-saṁjñā completely suspended).

\- No gendered bodies; reached through austere restraint (Vrata) & concentration.

\- Still Bound BY: Subtle attachment to sensory form, self-manifestation, and sub-conscious karmic impression (Kāma-guna / Rūpa-rāga).

(3). Mokṣa (Siddha-Śilā)

\- Complete annihilation of all Karma (including Vedanīya and Mohanīya).

\- Beyond all universes.

Verses from ancient Jain texts (such as the *Tattvārtha Sūtra*, *Pravacanasāra*, and *Samayasāra*) that describe **Mokṣa** and the state of the liberated souls at **Siddha-Śilā** (the Abode of the Liberated, located at the apex of the universe), highlighting the complete annihilation of all karma and the transcendence beyond all universes.

**(1). From the *Tattvārtha Sūtra* (Chapter 10, Sūtra 4 & 5):** *\"Karmakṣayān-mokṣaḥ\",\"Tad-abhāve-bandh-hētuv-abhāvān-nirjarāyāṁ-ca-mahākarmakṣayāt-siddhaḥ\"*

*"Destruction (kṣayāt) of karma (karma) \[leads to\] liberation (mokṣaḥ)."*

*"Non-manifest/ non-rebirth (abhāve), is the non-manifest/ non-rise/ absence (abhāvāt) of bondage (bandha) as cause (hetu), and through shedding (nirjarāyāṁ), and from destruction (kṣayāt) of major (mahā) karma (karma),* *the perfected/fulfilled one / one who has accomplished \[the ultimate goal\]. (siddhaḥ)."*

**(2). From the *Samayasāra* by Acharya Kundakunda (Verse 167):** *\"Sabba-kamma-kkhayā-mokkho\...\"*

*"From destruction (kkhayā) of all (sabba) karma (kamma) \[arises\] liberation (mokkho). Completely released (vippamutto) from all (sabba) bondage (bandha), abiding (tiṭṭhati) in its own (sayam) pure (suddha) citta (cetta), reaches (paipajjadi) the supreme (parama) abode/foundaion (padam) at the summit/apex (śikhā) of the world (loka)."*

**(3). From the *Pravacanasāra* by Acharya Kundakunda (Book 1, Verse 64):** *\"Jido sabba-kammo\...\"*

*"Having conquered (jido) all (sabba) karma (kammo), attained (sampatto) infinite (ananta) knowledge (jñāna), infinite (ananta) vision (darśana), infinite (ananta) bliss (sukha), and infinite (ananta) potency/energy (vīrya), the perfected/fulfilled one (siddho) goes (gacchadi) upward (ūrdhva) to the summit (śikhā) of the world (loka) and remains (tiṭṭhati) there (tattha) always (saddā) in the supreme (para) untainted (nirañjana) foundation/realm (padam)."*

**(4). From Ancient Liturgical Praises (Siddha-Bhakti):** *\"Nirvikāraṁ niranjanaṁ nityam-akṣayam-avyāhataṁ, Siddha-kṣetram-adhigamya tiṣṭhanti te paraṁ padam.\"*

*"immutable (nirvikāraṁ), untainted (nirañjanaṁ), eternal (nityam), undecaying (akṣayam), \[and\] unhindered (avyāhataṁ) ---having high-attained (adhigamya) this abode (kṣetram) of the perfected ones (siddha), they (te) remain (tiṣṭhanti) in \[that\] supreme (paraṁ) foundation/abode (padam)."*

**A. The Four Types of Sexual Gratification in Jain Heavens**

Jain texts (such as the *Tattvārtha Sūtra* by Ācārya Umāsvāti) categorize heavenly beings (*Devas*) according to how they experience sexual/ sensual pleasure based on their level of elevation:

a)  **Physical Union (*Kāyapravārom*):** Lower celestial beings engage in physical union similar to humans.

b)  **Touch/Contact (*Sparśa*):** Higher beings satisfy sexual desire merely by physical touch.

c)  **Sight (*Rūpa*):** Even higher beings satisfy desire simply by looking upon beautiful celestial forms.

d)  **Mind/Thought (*Manas*):** Higher *Kalpavāsī* devas experience gratification purely through mental projection.

**B. Beyond Sex, But Still Bound by Subtle Sensuality**

In the highest Jain heavens---the **Kalpatīta** realms (comprising the *Graiveyaka* and *Anuttara* heavens, which directly correspond to the Fine-Material / *Rūpa* realms):

- **Sexuality (*Maithuna-Saṁjñā*) Ceases:** Beings here have completely suppressed sexual drive and gender identification. They do not possess female counterparts or sexual organs.

- **Subtle Sensual/Form Attachment Remains:** Jainism explicitly teaches that unless a soul (*jīva*) completely destroys *Mohanīya Karma* (deluding karma) through pure insight (*Samyak-Darśana*), **it still harbors subtle sensual attraction (*Kāma-pariṇām*) to its own luminous form (*rūpa*) and peaceful divine existence.**

Jain texts explicitly warn that ascetics can attain these elevated, sexless *Brahma* heavens purely through rigorous primordial spiritual force *tapas* (*bāhya-tapas*) and deep mental concentration---**yet fail to reach *Mokṣa*** because a subtle, lingering desire (*kāma*) for worldly existence, light, and sensory bliss remains intact within the soul.

**7). The Layered Condensation of the Cosmic Manifestation**

Cosmic manifestation functions as a process of continuous condensation and thickening---moving down from formless energy to fine matter, and finally into dense, gendered physical existence.

**Level 1: Unmanifest Reality (*Asat* / *Nirguṇa*):** The unconditioned source before any cosmic manifestation or stir begins.

**Level 2: The Primal Seed of *Kāma:*** The emergence of the root ***Kāma*** acts as the first subtle stir (*spandana*), initiating the downward pull of condensation.

**Level 3: Formless Manifestation (*Arūpa-Dhātu*):** energy, consciousness, and wave-like subtle vibrations. Shown as empty space, unbound by physical shape, form, or matter. It represents the highest, most refined layer of manifested existence.

**Level 4: Fine-Material Manifestation (*Rūpa-Dhātu*):** As the cosmic energy continues to condense, wave-like subtle force thickens into refined, luminous, radiant particles forms.

- **Absence of Sex:** At this layer, there are no gross physical bodies, no male or female distinctions (*itthi-purisa-bhāva*), no smells, no tastes, and no coarse sexual drive, with bliss (*sukha*).

- **Driving Impulse:** In Vedic terms, this layer is driven by subtle desire (*sūkṣma-kāma*); in early Pali Sutta terms, it is secluded from 5-sense sensuality (*vivicceva kāmehi*), yet bound by attachment to luminous form (*rūpa-rāga*).

**Level 5: Gross Sensual Manifestation (*Kāma-Dhātu*):** The final, densest condensation of matter, where light and energy settle into solid, coarse physical bodies operating through five physical sense doors.

- **Nucleus of Sex:** At this thickest layer of manifestation, gender divide emerges. Sexual union (*methuna*) serves as the primary reproduction mechanism and also binding mechanism of the entire *kāma-dhātu*.

**8). Settling the Debt (*Anaṇā*) and Ending the Execution (*Vadha*) of Rebirth**

In the line *\"Vadhaṁ carimha te kāma, anaṇā dāni te mayaṁ\"*, the *muni* addresses *kāma* as the master, and people serve *kāma* throughout endless rebirths in *saṃsāra* as reproduction instruments, undergoing continuous *vadha*---execution, destruction, and subjection to the laws of death. By eradicating *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, the *muni* (sage), thus becomes *anaṇā* (debtless) and no more serving *kāma*.

#### 2.10. Vasabha

**Pubbe hanati attānaṁ, pacchā hanati so pare;\
Suhataṁ hanti attānaṁ, vītaṁseneva pakkhimā.**

**Na brāhmaṇo bahivaṇṇo, anto vaṇṇo hi brāhmaṇo;\
Yasmiṁ pāpāni kammāni, sa ve kaṇho sujampatī.**

*First (pubbe) he ruins (hanati) his attā / soul-spirit (attānaṁ), subsequently (pacchā) ruins (hanati) his (so) further/ beyond / the next lives (pare); He ruins (hanti) his attā / soul-spirit (attānaṁ), thoroughly ruined (suhataṁ), like (iva) a bird-catcher (vītaṁsena) \[catching\] a bird (pakkhimā).*

*A brāhmaṇa is not (na) defined by outer appearance (bahivaṇṇo), indeed (hi) a brāhmaṇa is defined by inner (anto) beauty / appearance (vaṇṇo); He (sa) indeed (ve) is unwholesome / impure / dark (kaṇho), for in him (yasmiṁ) there are evil/unwholesome (pāpāni) deeds (kammāni), O lord of Sujā (sujampati).*

**Commentary: *Sujampati:*** O Lord of Sujā (an epithet used when addressing Sakka, the ruler of the Tāvatiṁsa deva realm).

The expression **Sujampati** (*Sujā* + *pati*) appears in the Pāḷi Canon (such as in the *Dhammapada* and associated commentaries/Jātaka tales) as a vocative epithet addressing **Sakka**, the ruler of the Tāvatiṁsa devas. Examining why a male deity is addressed through a compound naming his consort---literally translating to \"Lord of Sujā\"---reveals deep linguistic, mythological, and structural precedents across ancient Indo-Aryan literature.

**1). Linguistic and Grammatical Analysis of *Sujampati***

*Sujā* (proper noun, the name of the female consort) + *pati* (masculine noun meaning \"lord\", \"master\", \"husband\"). ***Pati***: In Sanskrit and Pāḷi grammar, the final member (*uttarapadartha-pradhāna*) determines the grammatical gender and core referent of the compound. Because *pati* is masculine, the entire compound is a masculine vocative noun referring directly to the male actor---Sakka---rather than a female entity.

**2). Precedents in the *Ṛgveda* and Ancient Vedic Literature**

The mechanism of defining a supreme male deity by his relationship to his female consort or divine counterpart has deep roots in the *Ṛgveda* and Vedic traditions:

- **Matronymics and Consort-Based Epithets**: In Vedic poetry, deities are frequently invoked not merely by their own names, but through relational compounds that establish their supreme sovereignty via their cosmic household (*dampatī*).

- **The Concept of *Pati* as Master/Lord**: In the *Ṛgveda*, terms like *Gāspati*, *Bhaspati*, or compounds ending in *-pati* designate the protector, ruler, or lord of a specific domain or household. Naming Indra/Sakka as the \"Lord of Sujā\" (*Sujampati*) mirrors Vedic naming conventions where a king or god\'s supreme status is anchored to his principal queen or shakti, reflecting household sovereignty elevated to a cosmic scale.

This naming convention is deeply embedded in the **earliest layers of the *Ṛgveda*** and represents an archaic structural feature of ancient Indo-Aryan mythology, rather than a later evolution. In the *Ṛgveda*, gods and their consorts are routinely defined through reciprocal, relational compounds.

- **The Paradigm of Indrānī**: The very name of Indra\'s primary Vedic consort, *Indrānī*, is formed by adding a feminine suffix directly to the god\'s name (*Indra* + *-ānī*), meaning \"the female counterpart or wife of Indra.\"

- **The Reverse Construction (*Śacīpati* / *Sujampati*)**: While *Indrānī* derives the goddess\'s name from the god, the inverse pattern---naming the supreme male deity after his consort\'s attributes or identity (such as *Śacīpati*, \"Lord of Śacī,\" or *Sujampati*, \"Lord of Sujā\")---is equally ancient. For example, *Śacī* appears in the *Ṛgveda* as a powerful consort/manifestation of power, and Indra is explicitly styled *Śacīpati* in Vedic hymns.

This practice stems from the foundational Vedic concept of the **dampatī** (the joint lords/rulers of the household or cosmic domain). In the early Vedic worldview, a male deity\'s sovereignty was not viewed as isolated or absolute; rather, it was structurally and ritually bound to his divine partner (*shakti* or consort). Defining a god through his relationship to his consort codifies his status as a householder-king (*pati*) anchored to a specific cosmic household.

In the earliest layers of the *Ṛgveda*, major male deities like Indra are predominantly portrayed with **one principal consort** who defines their station, though the poetic framework is fundamentally centered on the *dampatī* (the dual headship of the household).

- **Monogamous Focus in Early Hymns**: In the *Ṛgveda*, Indra\'s primary and most prominent consort is **Indrānī** (also known in later texts by attributes like *Śacī*). The hymns dedicated to her or referencing her relationship with Indra depict a singular, core pairing representing the divine household (*dampatī*).

- **The Later Puranic Expansion**: The multiplication of consorts, complex harems, and numerous secondary wives (such as alternative names or multiple companion goddesses) largely belongs to **later textual evolution**---developing through the later Brahmanas, the Epics (*Mahābhārata*, *Rāmāyaṇa*), and extensively in the Puranas.

Thus, the archaic Ṛgvedic model kept the divine pairing tight and relational, anchoring the sovereign male deity to a single primary queen-consort.

**3). Frequent And Normative Interaction with Śakra/Indra**

Both in the Pāḷi Nikāyas and in early Jain canonical texts (where Śakra/Indra plays a prominent role in the lives of the Tīrthaṅkaras), encounters, dialogues, and invocations involving this deity are frequent and normative.

In the ancient literary and cosmological environment shared by both traditions, the king of the deva group frequently visiting assemblies to listen to teachings, pay respects, or seek guidance. Consequently, addressing him by established traditional epithets like *Sujampati* in verse dialogs was a natural reflection of that shared cultural and narrative framework.

**Sakka is a Real, Empirical Presence, Not a Metaphor**: In the ancient Pāḷi Nikāyas, the *devatā* (deities) of the sense-sphere heavens (*kāmāvacara*), including Sakka as the ruler of the Tāvatiṁsa realm, were literal, living beings occupying higher planes of existence born from past wholesome actions (*kamma*). They frequently visited the human realm to listen to the Dhamma, ask questions, or pay homage to the Buddha and accomplished practitioners. Invoking or addressing Sakka was not a mythological abstraction, but as addressing an actual listening entity present in the assembly. Sakka himself is depicted in texts like the *Sakkapanha Sutta* as a student of the Buddha---one who still experiences anxiety, fear, and the limitations of conditioned existence. Invoking or teaching Sakka underscores the supreme authority of the dhamma; even the king of the devas must bow to seek liberation from the cycle of worldly realms. Such narratives historically served as a traditional way to frame profound doctrinal teachings within the broader, multi-layered cosmological landscape familiar to ancient audiences, but whether it is historical or not is highly questionable.

**(1): *Samyutta Nikāya* (SN 11.3 - *Paṭhama-dhaja-aggasutta* / The Banner Top)** In this verse exchange, the Buddha uses Sakka as an object lesson for the bhikkhus, showing that even the king of the devas, who is not free from *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, can still experience fear. Sakka and his deities are invoked and addressed directly within the verse framework:

\"If, bhikkhus, when you are in the battle-gorge, fear or terror or horripilation should arise, on that occasion you should look at the banner-top of Sakka, lord of the devas (*devānaṁ indassa*); for when you look at the banner-top of Sakka\... any fear, terror, or horripilation that you have will be abandoned.\"

**(2): *Digha Nikāya* (DN 21 - *Sakkapañha Sutta*)** Upon descending to the Indasīla cave to question the Buddha, Pañcasikha plays the lute and sings verses praising the Buddha and the Dhamma, invoking the heavenly assembly and acknowledging Sakka\'s leadership:

\"Then Pañcasikha the heavenly musician, embracing his lute\... sang these verses concerning the Buddha, the Arahants, and the path to peace, while Sakka, king of the devas, rejoiced in the assembly.\"

**(3): *Samyutta Nikāya* (SN 11.12 - *Sakalika Sutta* / The Splinter)** When the Buddha was suffering physical pain from a stone splinter, Sakka approaches and addresses him in verse to check on his well-being, demonstrating the celestial king\'s active presence and respectful inquiry toward the fully awakened being:

\"Are you in pain, Blessed One? Are you uncomfortable? How is it with you, O Sage?\" --- to which the Buddha responds regarding his unshakeable *citta*.

**Parallels in Jain Texts and Early Prakrit Literature**

**Shared Epic and Puranic Milieu**: In early Jain narrative literature and the Prakrit Āgamas, the cosmological figures shared with early Indian mythology---such as Indra (Sakka) and his court---frequently retain traditional epithets. Jain and Buddhist texts alike invoked epithets like *Sujampati*.

**(1): *Ācārāṅga Sūtra* (Book 2, Lecture 13)** In early Jain canonical verses detailing the miraculous life events of the Jinas (later known as the Mahāvīra), Śakra (Indra) is described descending with his celestial retinue to pay homage to the newly ordained Samaṇa / Śamana/ Samāna taking complete *dīkṣā* (renunciation) breaking worldly ties:

"Then (*tae ṇaṁ*) that (*se*) lord of the devas (*sakkindeve*)---beloved of the gods (*devāṇuppiya*!), supreme lord of devas (*devāhiḍhipati*)---having gathered (*samāgaṇie*) up to (*jāva*) that (*taṁhaṁ*), he (*se*) descends (*otharai*) from that heavenly world (*devalogāo*), having descended (*otharittā*)\..."

"..approaches (*uvaṭṭhei*) the presence (*antikam*) of the *samaṇa,* the blessed (*bhagavao*) Mahāvīra (*mahāvīraassa*), having approached (*uvaṭṭheittā*) bows his head (*sirasā vaṁdei*), having bowed (*vaṁdeittā*) circumambulates three times (*tikkhattuṁ āyakkhiṇāyaṁ karei*), having done so (*kareittā*)\..."

"\...praises (*vaṇṇēi*) \[him\] with supreme/elevated praise (*uḷāraṁ vaṇṇaṁ*), having praised (*uḷāraṁ vaṇṇaṁ vaṇṇeittā*) \[with\] joyful mind (*tuṭṭhe tuṭṭhaṁ, maṇe maṇaṁ*), true and unblemished (*anie ṇiyāḍam*), victorious (*jae jaaṁ*), he utters hymns of praise (*paṁsaṁsaim*), having praised \[him\] there (*taṭṭhaṁ vaṇṇeittā*)\..."

**(2): *Kalpasūtra* (Section on the Birth of Mahāvīra)** Canonical Prakrit stanzas within the *Kalpasūtra* record Śakra taking the infant Tīrthaṅkara and performing sacred rites, accompanied by direct stanzas of invocation:

"Then (*tae ṇaṁ*) that (*se*) lord of the devas (*sakkindeve*)---beloved of the gods (*devāṇuppiya*!), having arrived at such a thought/state (*evārūve ajjhuvagae*)\..."

"\...blesses (*āsisaim*) that (*tassa*) blessed one (*bhagavao*), having blessed \[him\] (*āsisittā*) thus spoke (*evaṁ vayāsī*):"

\"This (*esa ṇaṁ*) one is an Arhat (*arahā*), a Jina (*jiṇe*), an omniscient one (*kevalī*), a crosser/liberator (*tiṇṇe tāreie*), an awakened one (*buddhe*), an ascetic (*muṇḍe*), unsuppased (*aṇuttare*)\...\"

\"\...up to (*jāva*) completely abandoning all *dukkha* (*savvaduḥkha-ppaheṇaṁ paheṇai*), having crossed the ocean of *sāṁsāra* (*saṁsāra-maṇṇavaṁ tiṇṇe tiṇṇai*).\"

**(3): *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (Lecture 20 - *The Nirvana of Nami*)**

In dialogues between the royal sage Nami (*Nami-rājarṣi*) and Sakka/Indra (disguised as a Brahmana, who then reveals his celestial form with his hosts/chariot). In this canonical passage, Indra arrives to test King Nami, who has abandoned his kingdom, army, and worldly power (*bhava*) to become a solitary *samaṇa*. After Nami counters every argument regarding worldly duty, royal conquest, and celestial splendor, Indra yields, acknowledges that monastic renunciation surpasses heavenly reign, and pays direct homage to the sage who abides in total restraint (*saṁyama*).

"Then (*tae ṇaṁ*) that (*se*) lord of the devas (*sakkindeve*)---beloved of the gods (*devāṇuppiya!*)---having done so (*taha-m-eva*)\...""\...bows to the feet (*pāyāṇi vaṁdai*), having bowed (*vaṁdeittā ya*), he praises (*vaṇṇēi*) the royal sage (*se rāyaṁ*) with supreme beauty/praise (*aṇuttareṇa vaṇṇeṇa*):"

\"Ah, the detachment (*aho ṇiṇṇāṇayā*), O King (*rāyam*)! Ah, the fortitude (*aho dhiī*)! Ah, the restraint (*aho saṁyamo*)!\"

\"Ah, the supreme wisdom (*aho aṇuttaraṁ maṇṇamaṁ*)! Approached with supreme *sīla* (*sīleṇa aṇuttareṇam uveya-m-āṇam*)!\"

"The king of devas (*devindo*), surrounded by the host of devas (*devasiṁgha-m-ajjhagao*), having approached his feet (*pāya-m-uvaṭṭheittā*), \[praises\] the king (*rāyaṁ*) with supreme speech\..."

"\...saying \"Victory, victory!\" (*jae jae ti*), greatly delighted (*pahaṁṭho*), Indra together with the devas (*sa-devindo*) bows (*vaṁdai*)."

#### 2.11. Mahācunda

**Sussūsā sutavaddhanī, sutaṁ paññāya vaddhanaṁ;\
Paññāya atthaṁ jānāti, ñāto attho sukhāvaho.**

**Sevetha pantāni senāsanāni, Careyya saṁyojanavippamokkhaṁ;\
Sace ratiṁ nādhigaccheyya tattha, Saṅghe vase rakkhitatto satimā.**

*Attentive listening (sussūsā) increases learning (sutavaddhanī); What is heard (sutaṁ) is the growth (vaddhanaṁ) of Paññā; Through Paññā, one knows (jānāti) the true goal (atthaṁ); The goal (attho), once known (ñāto), leading to bliss (sukhāvaho).*

*One should resort to (sevetha) remote/secluded dwellings/lodgings (pantāni senāsanāni); One should practice/live (careyya) toward complete liberation from all fetters (saṁyojana vippamokkhaṁ); If (sace) one does not find (nādhigaccheyya) delight (ratiṁ) there (tattha); One should dwell (vase) within the Saṅgha (saṅghe), with the attā/soul-spirit guarded/ protected (rakkhitatto) and mindful (satimā).*

**Commentary: Attha (Goal, Purpose), Liberation from All Fetters, Remote Dwelling or Within the Saṅgha, Attā/ Soul-Spirit Guarded and Mindful**

**1). Attha (The Supreme Goal of complete liberation from all fetters)**

**Attha** (Sanskrit *artha*) **Etymological Root:** Derived from the Proto-Indo-European root ***er-*** / ***ṛ-*** (meaning \"to move toward,\" \"to reach,\" or \"to set in motion\"), giving rise to the Sanskrit/Pāḷi verbal root **√ar** or **√arth**.

**Primary Structural Meaning**: That toward which one moves; **the ultimate goal, purpose, or benefit**.

**In the Accusative Singular (atthaṁ)**: Functions grammatically either as the direct object (\"the true goal/benefit\") or adverbially (\"for the purpose of\" / \"for the sake of\").

*Attha* (Sanskrit *artha*) is not an abstract concept or mere intellectual definition. Rooted in **√ar** (\"to reach, to attain\"), it represents the objective reality toward which all effort (*brahmacariya*) is directed---the ultimate goal or benefit, which is **complete liberation from all fetters** (*saṁyojanavippamokkhaṁ*).

**2). Liberation from All Fetters (*Saṁyojana vippamokkha*)**

*saṁyojana* (binding chains or fetters) and *vippamokkha* (complete release).

The fetters (such as *sakkāyaditṭhi*, *vicikicchā*, *sīlabbata-parāmāsa*, and especially *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*) are the binding mechanisms that force the *citta* into involuntary reincarnation across the worldly realms (*bhava*).

*Vippamokkha* is the total destruction and eradication of these fetters, freeing the *citta* so that it may leave behind the world (*loka*) and transcend. This is the only goal for the whole life (*careyya*) of the practitioner.

**3). Remote Dwelling (*Pantāni Senāsanāni*) vs. Dwell within the Saṅgha (*Saṅghe Vase*)**: A Two-Fold Tactical Framework for The Practicing *Samaṇa* or *Bhikkhu*.

**Secluded Lodgings (*Pantāni Senāsanāni*)**: the core of the ancient practice methodology toward liberation from worldly bondage. The transition from remote dwelling (*pantāni senāsanāni*) to complete liberation (*vimutti*) is an empirical, step-by-step purification process designed to sever the *citta* from the worldly bondage.

**(1). Solitude (*Pantāni Senāsanāni*) as the Operational Environment**

**Cutting Off External Inputs**: Living in remote lodgings isolates the practitioner from sensory agitation (*kāmas*). By severing physical contact with the worldly environment, no new impressions trigger fresh *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* to enter and defile the *citta*.

**Inspecting the Mind**: In solitude, the external world drops away, leaving only the internal landscape. The practitioner observes the *citta* directly: *Do any latent worldly cravings, passions, thoughts, or ideas arise when external stimuli are removed?*

**(2). *Nibbidā*: Disenchantment with the World**

**Severing the Bond**: *Nibbidā* is the immediate result of quiet observation in solitude. the *citta* in solitude experiences total disenchantment.

**Turning Away**: The *citta* loses all taste for worldly existence (*bhava*). It recognizes that the world offers nothing of true value to hold onto, creating the necessary spiritual repulsion to break affection for the worldly.

**(3). *Virāga*: Fading of Worldly Passion through *Tapas***

**Exposing Latent Defilements**: Even after external inputs cease, innate, deep-seated *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* remain buried within the *citta*. As these latent thoughts and passions surface, they are exposed in the stillness of solitude.

**Purification via *Tapas***: This is where **tapas**---the primordial force of intense spiritual energy---is applied. Using *tapas*, the practitioner confronts these arising worldly passions directly, burning away and purifying the residual impurities inherent to the citta until all desire for the worldly completely fades (*virāga*).

**(4). *Vimutti*: Final Liberation from the World**

**Maturity of Practice**: Once the innate *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* are fully eradicated through *tapas*, no trace of worldly passion remains to bind or defile the mind. The *citta*, now fully purified, unburdened, and mature, achieves **vimutti**---absolute liberation from the world (*loka*), is ready to break free from all involuntary reincarnation (*bhava*) to transcend into the deathless reality.

- **Within the Saṅgha (*Saṅghe Vase*)**: A vital protective recourse. If the practitioner does not find delight (*ratiṁ*) or encounters mental weakness in total solitude, dwelling within the monastic community (*Saṅgha*) provides direct moral restraint, guidance, and spiritual protection, preventing regression. It may function as a stepstone to remote dwelling in solitude.

**4). Guarding the Soul-Spirit (*Rakkhita* + *Attā*) and Mindful (*Satimā*)**

Composed directly of **rakkhita** (guarded / protected) + **attā** (soul-spirit) alongside **satimā** (possessing mindfulness). It strictly denotes \"one whose *attā* (soul-spirit) is guarded/protected,\" rather than \"self-protected\" as mainstream academia distort its translation. \"self-protected\" in Pali and Sanskrit is "*sva-rakkhitatto*". This happens for all translation regarding "self + something". So, readers should always be careful for mistranslation and misinformation from mainstream academia with their own agenda of no-self/non-self/no-soul wrong teaching.

**Preserving the Reality of the *Attā***: The compound **rakkhitatto** preserves the actual reality of the **attā** (the soul-spirit inherent to the *citta*). The physical and mental phenomena (nama-rupa) of human manifested existence belong to this world (*loka*) and its laws of cause and effect and death, which is why the *citta* disenchants them, leaves them behind, and transcends; hence, the *attā* must be actively guarded (*rakkhita*) from being enticed or stained by them.

**Active Protection**: To be **rakkhitatto** means the *attā* (soul-spirit) is guarded and shielded from the corrupting influence of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. Combined with **satimā** (mindfulness), the guarded soul-spirit keeps the sensory doors closed against all worldly defilements, ensuring that the *citta* remains pure and steadfast on the ancient path toward the *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

#### 2.12. Jotidāsa

**Ye kho te veṭhamissena, nānattena ca kammunā;\
Manusse uparundhanti, pharusūpakkamā janā;\
Tepi tattheva kīranti, na hi kammaṁ panassati.**

**Yaṁ karoti naro kammaṁ, Kalyāṇaṁ yadi pāpakaṁ;\
Tassa tasseva dāyādo, Yaṁ yaṁ kammaṁ pakubbatī.**

*Those who (ye kho) indeed, by means of various (nānattena) fraudulent /deceitful (veṭhamissena) action (kammunā), oppress /obstruct /restrict (uparundhanti) human beings (manusse), people who are (janā) harsh /cruel /violent /ruthless means (pharusūpakkamā)---they too (te pi) are bound /trapped (kīranti) right there (tattha eva); for (hi) action (kammaṁ) does not (na) disappears /get lost (panassati).*

*Whatever (yaṁ) action (kammaṁ) a person (naro) performs (karoti), whether wholesome (kalyāṇaṁ) or (yadi) unwholesome (pāpakaṁ), of that exact action (tassa tassa eva) he becomes the heir (dāyādo), whatever (yaṁ yaṁ) action (kammaṁ) he continues to perform (pakubbati).*

**Commentary: The Doctrine of *Kamma***

This verse reveals the nature of action (*kamma*) and its fruits (*vipāka*) by the law of inescapable action & consequences. As in Upajjhatthana Sutta (*AN 5.57*), Kamma contains the fivefold formula of personal responsibility: *Kamma-sako\'mhi, kamma-dāyādo, kamma-yoni, kamma-bandhu, kamma-paṭisaraṇo.*

\"I am the owner of my actions, heir to my actions, born of my actions, bound to my actions, and have my actions as my refuge.\"

**Key Thematic Pillars of Intentional Action (*Kamma*)**

**(1) Ownership & No Loss (*Kamma-saka*):** Actions are one's sole true property and are never lost or destroyed (*na hi kammaṁ panassati*).

**(2) Inheritance & Consequence (*Kamma-dāyāda*):** One becomes the direct heir to the fruits (*vipāka*) of whatever action one performs, whether wholesome or unwholesome.

**(3) Origin & Kinship (*Kamma-yoni* & *Kamma-bandhu*): Origin (*Kamma-yoni*):** Action is the true womb, source, and birthplace of one\'s existence. **Kinship (*Kamma-bandhu*):** Action is one\'s true, inescapable relative and bound companion across lifetimes.

**(4) Refuge & Protection (*Kamma-paṭisaraṇa*):** One\'s own actions serve as the ultimate ground, shelter, and protection, with no external deity or outside force to rely upon.

**1). Inviolability and Non-Disappearance of Action (*Na hi kammaṁ panassati*)**

Actions are never lost or wiped out in the cosmic order (*na hi kammaṁ panassati*). Those who use fraudulent, deceptive means (*veṭhamissena*) or violent, harsh approaches (*pharusūpakkamā*) to oppress (*uparundhanti*) human beings (*manusse*) are directly bound by those very actions (*tepi tattheva kīranti*). Every volitional action driven by *rāga*, *dosa*, or *moha* creates an inexorable trajectory. The actor cannot escape or evade the exact fruits generated by their intentional deeds.

**2). Ownership (*Kamma-sako\'mhi*) & Inheritance (*Kamma-dāyādo*)**

To be *kamma-saka* means that one\'s intentional deeds are one\'s sole true property. While worldly possessions, physical structures, and mental aggregates are impermanent and belong to this world (*loka*), *kamma* remains attached to the continuum of the doer.

As the verse declares, *tassa tasseva dāyādo*: whatever action a person performs---whether wholesome (*kalyāṇaṁ*) or unwholesome (*pāpakaṁ*)---of that exact action one becomes the heir (*kamma-dāyādo*). One inherits the precise fruit (*vipāka*) corresponding to the seed sown.

**3). Womb / Origin (*Kamma-yoni*) & Kinship (*Kamma-bandhu*)**

**Matrix / Origin (*Kamma-yoni*):** The term *yoni* literally denotes the womb, source, or origin. *Kamma* is the true birthplace of a being\'s realm, conditions, and life experiences. Physical lineage and birthplace are merely secondary manifestations; intentional action is the real generator of existence within saṃsāra.

**Kinship (*Kamma-bandhu*):** The term *bandhu* denotes a relative, kinsman, or bound companion. External family and associates cannot accompany an individual past death or shield them from the results of their deeds. One\'s own actions (*kamma*) are one\'s true and persistent companions across births.

**4). Ultimate Refuge (*Kamma-paṭisaraṇo*)**

In the framework of *kamma-vipāka*, there is no external deity, divine intervention, or outside protector to rely upon. One\'s own wholesome intentional actions serve as the ultimate ground, protection, and shelter (*paṭisaraṇa*) that one carries forward. Wholesome deeds rooted in non-greed, non-hatred, and non-delusion shield the practitioner from lower realms of existence and lay the foundation for spiritual development.

While there is no external deity or divine intervention to erase or rewrite the fundamental fruit (*vipāka*) of past deeds, external conditions play a vital supportive role. External deity or divine intervention, family, friends, teachers, and a wholesome physical or social environment can provide inductive conditions (*upanissaya-paccaya*)---such as material support, safety, guidance, and encouragement---that help a person better cope with, navigate, and transform their circumstances while generating new wholesome actions.

**The Universal Law of Kammic Causality is Not Liberating Knowledge**

The knowledge of *kamma* is simply the understanding of a universal law of cause and effect---explaining how physical and mental phenomena in this universe operate, rise, fall, within this world (*loka*). Physical phenomena are manifestations of mental phenomena in this physical world, and under the laws of cause and effect, decay and death. There is nothing within the logic of kammic causality to impel the urge to transcend the cycle of *kamma* and its fruit. Understanding this universal law is irrelevant to achieving liberation from the universe and its laws of aging and death. Because the knowledge of *kamma* is not a liberation knowledge, it is solely about the mechanics of worldliness.

**Liberation Knowledge and the Ancient Path**

Liberating knowledge is fundamentally distinct: it is strictly about how to sever the *citta* from worldly bondage. This bondage is subjectively termed as the defilements (*kilesa*) of the citta as *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. Liberating knowledge provides the direct understanding of how to untie the knots and fetters that lock the *citta* inside this world, enabling it to disenchant these worldly phenomena, leave them behind, and transcend. While wholesome *kamma* creates favorable conditions within saṃsāra, the ultimate objective of *Brahmacariya* requires moving completely beyond *kamma* through this liberating knowledge. It is through the complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, that we can escape our involuntary reincarnation within the universe and gain true freedom.

#### 2.13. Heraññakāni

**Accayanti ahorattā, jīvitaṁ uparujjhati;\
Āyu khīyati maccānaṁ, kunnadīnaṁva odakaṁ.**

**Atha pāpāni kammāni, karaṁ bālo na bujjhati;\
Pacchāssa kaṭukaṁ hoti, vipāko hissa pāpako.**

*Days and nights (aho-rattā) pass by (accayanti); life (jīvitaṁ) ceases (uparujjhati). The lifespan (āyu) of mortals (maccānaṁ) is exhausted (khīyati), like the water (odakaṁ) of small rivers (kunnadīnaṁ + iva). Then (atha), while performing (karaṁ) evil deeds (pāpāni kammāni), the foolish (bālo) does not understand (na bujjhati). Afterwards (pacchā + assa), it becomes (hoti) bitter (kaṭukaṁ); indeed (hi + assa), the fruitage (vipāko) of that evil (pāpako).*

**1. Transiency and the Incessant Decay of Life:** The opening lines establish the relentless forward movement of time (*accayanti ahorattā*). Days and nights do not pause; they consume the vitality of existence. The lifespan (*āyu*) of mortals (*maccānaṁ*) continuously wastes away (*khīyati*) just as the water (*odakaṁ*) of small, shallow streams (*kunnadīnaṁva*) rapidly drains and dries up under the sun. This directly highlights the worldly nature of physical phenomena, under the universal law of decay and death, therefore *dukkha*.

**2. Ignorance and the Accumulation of Demerit:** Caught within this swift current of decay and death, the foolish person (*bālo*), governed by *moha* (ignorance and delusion) rather than wisdom (*paññā*), performs evil deeds (*pāpāni kammāni*).

**3. Karmic Maturation and the Inevitability of Suffering:** The verse concludes with a stark moral and causal reality: *Pacchāssa kaṭukaṁ hoti, vipāko hissa pāpako*. Evil (pāpako) actions yield bitter fruit. When the *vipāka* (fruitage, karmic result, consequence) ripens, it brings forth *dukkha* and harsh pain. To break free from this cycle of accumulating unwholesome *kamma* and facing bitter karmic maturation, one must turn to the *ancient path to the deathless realm of Nibbāna*.

#### 2.14. Somamitta

**Parittaṁ dārumāruyha, yathā sīde mahaṇṇave;\
Evaṁ kusītamāgamma, sādhujīvīpi sīdati;\
Tasmā taṁ parivajjeyya, kusītaṁ hīnavīriyaṁ.**

**Pavivittehi ariyehi, pahitattehi jhāyibhi;\
Niccaṁ āraddhavīriyehi, paṇḍitehi sahāvase.**

*Just as (yathā) a person, having climbed onto (āruyha) a piece of wood (dāruṁ), sinks (sīdati) in the great ocean (mah-aṇṇave), if it is small or inadequate (parittaṁ); Even so (evaṁ), coming into association with (āgamma) a lazy, sluggish person (kusītaṁ), even one who lives a virtuous and righteous life (sādhu-jīvī + api) sinks (sīdati).*

*Therefore (tasmā), one should avoid (parivajjeyya) him (taṁ)---the lazy one (kusītaṁ) of diminished or inferior spiritual energy (hīna-**vīriya**ṁ). Constantly (niccaṁ) associate with (sahāvase) the wise (paṇḍitehi), who are secluded and detached (pavivittehi), noble (ariyehi), resolute and energetic in mind (pahit-attehi), meditative and absorbed in jhāna (jhāyibhi), and steadfastly possessed of aroused, active energy (āraddha-vīriyehi).*

**1. The Peril of Spiritual Association with the Lazy:** The opening verses utilize a stark nautical metaphor: just as a person who attempts to cross the great ocean (*mah-aṇṇave*) using only a small, or inadequate piece of wood (*parittaṁ dāruṁ*) will inevitably sink (*sīdati*), so too does a person who associates with someone lacking spiritual drive (*kusītaṁ āgamma*). Even if an individual is naturally virtuous and strives to live a good life (*sādhu-jīvī api*), the dead weight of negative, sluggish, and unmotivated companionship drags them down into failure and moral stagnation.

**2. The Rejection of Indolence and Inferior Spiritual Energy**

Because association heavily influences the *citta*, the practitioner must ruthlessly filter their environment. The text commands: *Tasmā taṁ parivajjeyya, kusītaṁ hīnavīriyaṁ*. One must entirely avoid (*parivajjeyya*) the lazy person (*kusītaṁ*) whose spiritual energy is diminished (*hīna-**vīriya**ṁ*). Indolence, lack of *sīla*, and the absence of active *vīriya* (spiritual energy) suffocate spiritual progress. To remain mired in such company prevents the cultivation of *sīla*, *samadhi*, and *paññā*.

**3. Cultivating Noble Companionship and Aroused Energy**

Conversely, true progress on the *ancient path to Nibbāna* requires deliberate, elevated association (*sahāvase*). The practitioner is instructed to constantly dwell with those who embody the highest spiritual standards:

- **Pavivittehi**: Those who live secluded from worldly distractions.

- **Ariyehi**: The noble ones who have purified their minds.

- **Pahitattehi**: Those with resolute minds fully directed toward liberation.

- **Jhāyibhi**: Those deeply established in meditative absorption (*jhāna*).

- **Āraddhavīriyehi**: Those who possess continuously aroused, unflagging spiritual energy (*vīriya*).

- **Paṇḍitehi**: The truly wise.

By surrounding oneself with such sages, the mind is fortified against *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. It is through the complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, that we can escape our involuntary worldly reincarnation and gain true freedom.

#### 2.15. Sabbamitta

**Jano janamhi sambaddho, janamevassito jano;\
Jano janena heṭhīyati, heṭheti ca jano janaṁ.**

**Ko hi tassa janenattho, janena janitena vā;\
Janaṁ ohāya gacchaṁ taṁ, heṭhayitvā bahuṁ janan.**

*A person (jano) is bound (sambaddho) to people (janamhi); a person (jano) is dependent upon (assito) people (janam + eva + assito); A person (jano) is harmed and oppressed (heṭhīyati) by people (janena), and a person (jano) harms and oppresses (heṭheti) another person (janaṁ).*

*For (hi) what (ko) benefit (attho) does one have with that person (tassa janena), or with people brought into existence\[offspring/progeny\] (janena janitena vā)? Leaving behind (ohāya) the crowd of people (janaṁ), go (gacchaṁ), having harmed (heṭhayitvā) many people (bahuṁ janaṁ) \[previously in this lifetime and numerous past lifetimes\].*

**1. Social Entanglement and Mutual Oppression (*Dosa*)**

This verse provides an unvarnished examination of human social structures (*jana*). The text highlights the inescapable feedback loop of worldly attachment: ordinary people are bound to each other (*sambaddho*), depend on each other for validation and support (*assito*), and consequently enter into conflicts of mutual oppression (*heṭhīyati / heṭheti*), *Dosa*, and thus *dukkha*.

**2. The Futility of Worldly Relationships**
