The second stanza poses a sharp, analytical question: *Ko hi tassa janenattho, janena janitena vā?* What ultimate benefit or spiritual gain (*attha*) can ever come from clinging to other people or to offspring/progeny brought into existence (*janitena*)? When an individual remains entangled in social obligations, they are constantly driven by *rāga* (worldly craving and attachment), *dosa* (fear, anger, aversion, conflict, anger, and hatred), and *moha* (delusion and ignorance, not knowing the path to liberation from worldly bondage). Social ties keep the *citta* anchored in the repetitive cycle of *saṃsāra*, prioritizing temporary worldly relationships over absolute liberation from worldly bondage.

**3. The Necessity of Solitude and Renunciation**

To escape this cycle, the text advocates for total renunciation: *Janaṁ ohāya gacchaṁ*. One must leave behind the crowd (*janaṁ ohāya*) and step onto the solitary path. The phrase *heṭhayitvā bahuṁ janaṁ* deserves a full critical doctrinal commentary, with a clear distinction between the traditional *Aṭṭhakathā*, modern Pāḷi translations, the limitations of their interpretation, and the Arahanist understanding. Standard academic commentary and mainstream Pali text translation frameworks and modern Western philological translations like those by the Pali Text Society (PTS) all wrongly refers to the emotional shock or grief that worldly companions feel when a seeker abruptly cuts ties to pursue the holy life (*Brahmacariya*).

**Commentary: The Necessity of Leaving Worldly Human Entanglements**

**1. The Wrong Conventional Interpretation in Traditional Commentaries and Modern Pāḷi Scholarship**

The verses of *Theragāthā* 2.15 present a profound reflection on the nature of human relationships, mutual dependence, reciprocal harm, and the necessity of leaving worldly entanglements. The passage reads: *Jano janamhi sambaddho, janamevassito jano; Jano janena heṭhīyati, heṭheti ca jano janaṃ. Ko hi tassa janenattho, janena janitena vā; Janaṃ ohāya gacchaṃ taṃ, heṭhayitvā bahuṃ janaṃ.*

The conventional translation framework interprets the passage primarily as a description of people being bound to one another, depending upon one another, and causing harm to one another. Its final lines are generally rendered as an instruction to leave people who have harmed many others.

This interpretation has been influenced by two principal sources of textual authority: the traditional post-canonical commentaries (*Aṭṭhakathā*) and modern Western philological translations, particularly those associated with the Pali Text Society (PTS).

This stanza, preserved in Dhammapāla\'s *Paramatthadīpanī*, provides the traditional commentarial explanation. It interprets *jano* as foolish or unenlightened people, *sambaddho* as being bound by craving (*taṇhā*), and the reciprocal oppression described in the third line as arising through greed (*lobha*) and hatred (*dosa*).

The commentary also provides a narrative background. Sabbamitta witnesses the suffering of a mother deer and her trapped offspring, followed by the torture of a man captured by robbers. These events generate a sense of spiritual urgency (*saṃvega*), prompting him to recite the verses. The commentary subsequently explains the final lines through the abandonment of craving and hatred, and records that Sabbamitta attained arahantship.

Modern Western philological translations have generally retained this commentarial framework. K. R. Norman\'s translation for the PTS has been particularly influential. Later translations, including the SuttaCentral version by Bhikkhu Sujato and Jessica Walton, explicitly acknowledge their substantial reliance on Norman\'s work.

The SuttaCentral translation renders the conclusion: \"Go, leave these people, who\'ve hurt so many people.\"

This rendering treats the people who have caused harm as the principal reference of the concluding expression.

Although the traditional commentary and modern translations differ in their explanatory details, they share an interpretive tendency: the concluding instruction is explained through the abandonment of people associated with harmful relationships, or through the abandonment of the defilements responsible for those relationships.

Arahanism considers this approach insufficient and wrong interpretation because it does not adequately recognize the radical renunciant significance of the stanza. More importantly, the grammatical construction and the doctrinal direction of the verses require examination from the standpoint of complete renunciation.

**2. The Limitations of the Commentarial Interpretation**

The traditional *Aṭṭhakathā* provides an elaborate explanation of the stanza through craving, hatred, possessiveness, and the suffering arising from human relationships.

However, its interpretation introduces an explanatory framework that moves attention away from the direct instruction conveyed by the verses.

The commentary explains the final expression through the abandonment of the person, craving, and hatred. It then interprets the destination as a state free from oppression by these conditions.

Arahanism challenges the adequacy of this explanation.

The central issue is not simply whether craving and hatred should be abandoned. Their abandonment is unquestionably essential to liberation. The issue here is the relationship between the renunciant\'s departure from worldly existence and the cessation of reciprocal harm described in the stanza. A liberation from worldly bondage, not a liberation from harmful mental state.

The verses establish a direct progression:

1.  People are bound to one another.

2.  People depend upon one another.

3.  People are oppressed and harmed by one another.

4.  People themselves oppress and harm others.

5.  The value of continued human association and worldly relationships is questioned.

6.  The speaker declares the necessity of leaving people behind.

This progression concerns the condition of human existence itself, as a universal law of human existence. It is not merely an explanation of particular psychological defilements operating within otherwise independent social relationships.

The commentary\'s emphasis on craving and hatred does not adequately preserve this movement towards complete renunciation. By making the defilements the principal objects of abandonment, it risks reducing the instruction to an internal psychological process while leaving insufficiently examined the speaker\'s explicit departure from people or the human society and world.

From the Arahanist standpoint, this is a significant doctrinal reduction.

The renunciant path requires more than developing the ability to maintain peaceful relationships while continuing to participate in worldly human affairs. It requires a decisive withdrawal from household existence, worldly associations, and the conditions that perpetuate human entanglement.

**3. Human Relationships as a Cycle of Mutual Harm, this is a Natural Law**

The opening stanza identifies a fundamental characteristic of ordinary human existence: *Jano janamhi sambaddho, janamevassito jano.*

People are bound to people; people depend upon people.

The terms *sambaddho* and *assito* describe a condition of attachment, dependence, and entanglement. Human beings establish relationships through expectations, possessiveness, mutual reliance, family connections, social obligations, and worldly interests.

The following lines reveal the consequences: *Jano janena heṭhīyati, heṭheti ca jano janaṃ.*

People are harmed and oppressed by one another, and people themselves harm and oppress others.

The reciprocal structure is fundamental. Human beings are not merely occasional victims of isolated misconduct. They participate continuously in relationships in which one person\'s interests, expectations, attachments, and actions affect another.

The Arahanist understanding recognizes this as an inherent characteristic of worldly human entanglement, a natural cosmic law.

Even when individuals attempt to cultivate restraint and goodwill, continued participation in ordinary human relationships exposes them to competing interests, obligations, expectations, attachment, aversion, and interference. One person becomes dependent upon another; another becomes dependent upon the first. These relationships perpetuate the conditions in which people are repeatedly subjected to harm and become sources of harm themselves.

The stanza therefore raises a fundamental question: *Ko hi tassa janenattho, janena janitena vā?*

What benefit is there in such association, or in those brought into existence through human relationships?

This question challenges the value of continued worldly association from the standpoint of liberation from worldly bondage.

The concern is not simply that certain individuals behave badly. It is that ordinary human existence remains structured around attachment, dependence, and reciprocal entanglement.

For the renunciant who seeks the complete cessation of worldly bondage, continued participation in this condition cannot be treated as the ultimate objective.

**4. The Correct Understanding of the Final Lines**

The concluding expression is: *Janaṃ ohāya gacchaṃ taṃ, heṭhayitvā bahuṃ janaṃ.*

Arahanism understands this as an instruction to leave people, or the human society behind, having already harmed many people through participation in worldly human existence.

The grammatical relationship is essential. *Gacchaṃ* is a present participle of *gacchati*, indicating the person who is going or proceeding. *Heṭhayitvā* is an absolutive, expressing an action completed in relation to the principal verbal construction.

In the Arahanist reading, the person proceeding is also the understood subject of *heṭhayitvā*. The expression therefore concerns the person who has already participated in the harming of many people and who must now leave behind worldly human entanglements.

The meaning is not that the renunciant should identify other people as the sole source of harm and simply abandon them. Rather, the speaker recognizes his own participation in the cycle of reciprocal harm. Having already lived within this condition, he must not continue perpetuating it.

The instruction is therefore one of decisive renunciation:

Leave people behind. Having already participated in the harm that characterizes worldly human existence, proceed towards solitude and liberation.

This reading gives the concluding lines their full renunciant significance. It connects the recognition of reciprocal human harm directly with the decision to leave worldly association.

The speaker is not merely advising people to abandon particular troublesome relationships. He is expressing the necessity of renunciation from the entire condition of worldly human entanglement.

**5. The Necessity of Renunciation and Solitude**

The distinction between awakening while remaining among people and the complete renunciant pursuit of liberation from worldly bondage is fundamental to the Arahanist understanding.

The cultivation of wisdom, restraint, and wholesome conduct can occur while an individual remains within society. However, the complete renunciant path has a more radical objective.

A bhikkhu renounces household life, abandons ordinary social obligations, and dedicates himself to spiritual cultivation. Solitude provides the conditions for sustained practice, freedom from worldly distractions, and the progressive purification of consciousness. The purpose of renunciation is not merely to become a more agreeable participant in human society. It is to withdraw from worldly entanglements and pursue the complete eradication of the conditions responsible for continued bondage to the world.

This stanza must therefore be understood in relation to the renunciant ideal.

Its recognition of mutual dependence and reciprocal harm leads directly to the rejection of continued worldly association as the ultimate direction of life. The speaker questions the benefit of human relationships and declares the necessity of leaving people behind.

This is the movement from worldly entanglement towards renunciation.

The distinction is especially important because Gautama Buddha\'s liberation teaching places individual purification at its centre. Another person may provide instruction and demonstrate the path, but the purification of consciousness must be accomplished individually.

The renunciant must undertake this work through *sīla*, *samādhi*, and *paññā*, progressing towards *nibbidā*, *virāga*, and *vimutti*.

The ultimate objective is the eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, culminating in Nibbāna.

The stanza is therefore not merely a reflection on interpersonal conflict. It expresses the necessity of abandoning the worldly condition in which human beings remain bound to one another through reciprocal dependence and harm.

**6. The Arahanist Interpretation: Leaving Behind the Cycle of Human Harm**

Arahanism understands this verses as a direct expression of the renunciant\'s recognition of the fundamental predicament of worldly human existence.

People are bound to one another. They depend upon one another. They harm one another and are harmed in return. Their relationships perpetuate attachment, dependence, conflict, and continued worldly entanglement.

The speaker recognizes that he has already participated in this condition for too long. He questions the benefit of continuing such relationships and resolves to leave people behind. The concluding instruction must therefore be understood as a declaration of renunciation.

The person of renunciation does not leave because of hostility towards humanity, nor does he seek to punish or condemn other people. He leaves because continued participation in worldly human existence cannot fulfil the ultimate objective of liberation from worldly bondage.

He has already participated in the cycle of reciprocal harm. He must now cease perpetuating it and proceed towards solitude, spiritual cultivation, and liberation from worldly bondage.

This interpretation preserves the direct relationship between the opening recognition of human entanglement and the concluding act of departure. It also restores the central importance of renunciation within the liberation path from Gautama, the supreme Arahant. The verses are therefore understood as follows:

**People are bound to one another, and people depend upon one another. People are oppressed and harmed by one another, and people themselves oppress and harm others. What ultimate benefit is there in such human association, or in those brought forth through these relationships? Leave people behind and proceed. Having already participated in harming many people, do not continue perpetuating this condition. Go forth towards solitude and liberation.**

This is the Arahanist understanding of Theragāthā 2.15. The stanza expresses a fundamental principle of renunciant life: the recognition of worldly entanglement must lead to the abandonment of worldly entanglement. The purpose of renunciation is to cease participation in the conditions that perpetuate worldly bondage and to undertake the complete purification of one\'s own consciousness.

**The path to liberation from worldly bondage requires more than changing one\'s conduct within the world. It requires the courage to leave worldly entanglements behind and pursue their complete cessation.**

#### 2.16. Mahākāḷa

**Kāḷī itthī brahatī dhaṅkarūpā, Satthiñca bhetvā aparañca satthiṁ;\
Bāhañca bhetvā aparañca bāhaṁ, Sīsañca bhetvā dadhithālakaṁva;\
Esā nisinnā abhisandahitvā.**

**Yo ve avidvā upadhiṁ karoti, Punappunaṁ dukkhamupeti mando;\
Tasmā pajānaṁ upadhiṁ na kayirā, Māhaṁ puna bhinnasiro sayissan.**

*A dark female entity \[Yakkhiṇī or Rākṣasī of death and destruction\] (kāḷī itthī), monstrous in size (brahatī), with the appearance/form of a scavenger crow (dhaṅka-rūpā), having broken (bhetvā) one thigh (satthiṁ-ca) and also the other thigh (aparaṁ ca satthiṁ); Having broken (bhetvā) one arm (bāhañca) and also the other arm (aparañca bāhaṁ), and having broken (bhetvā) the head (sīsañca) like a bowl of curd (dadhi-thālakaṁ-iva); She (esā) sits (nisinnā), having caused to overflow or soaked \[in blood/fluids\] (abhisandahitvā).*

*Indeed (ve), the foolish person (mando), being devoid of true wisdom (avidvā), creates or accumulates upadhi \[substance, substrate of existence, clinging, acquisitions, fuel for rebirth\] (upadhiṁ karoti). Therefore (tasmā), one who clearly knows and understands (pajānaṁ) should not create upadhi (upadhiṁ na kayirā), \[thinking:\] \"May I not lie down again with my head broken (māhaṁ puna bhinna-siro sayissaṁ).\"*

**Commentary***: kāḷī itthī* and *upadhi*

In Pāli philology, **kāḷī** is an adjective used as a noun. It is the direct feminine form of the adjective **kāḷa** (meaning black, dark, or dark-complexioned).

**1. Etymology and Grammar of *Kāḷī***

- **Kāḷa** (masculine/neuter adj.) = black, dark.

- **Kāḷī** (feminine adj./noun) = a dark/black female, or a dark woman.

- In Sanskrit, *Kāla* (masculine) can mean \"time\" or \"death\" (and *Kālī* later became associated with a deity), but in **Pāli text and grammar**, *kāḷa* primarily denotes the color black/darkness. The Pāli word for death used throughout this very literature is *maccu*, *maraṇa*, or *anta*.

Because the noun modifying it in the verse is **itthī** (which explicitly means \"woman\" or \"female human/entity\"), **kāḷī itthī** translates literally as **\"a dark/black woman\"** or **\"a dark female entity\"**.

But this standard dictionary backed up mainstream academia translation is wrong. To determine what is **kāḷī itthī,** let's look at **upadhi** first**.**

To determine the background and origin of the term **upadhi** (found in the line *upadhiṁ karoti*), I have traced its linguistic roots across the three primary textual strata of ancient India: the early Vedic literature (specifically the *Ṛgveda*), the early Pāli Canon (*Tipiṭaka*), and early Ardhamāgadha based Jain texts (*Āgamas*).

Tracing *upadhi* across these three traditions reveals the exact environment from which this verse emerged:

**It Belongs to the Greater Shramana (*Samaṇa*) traditoins:** The Vedic tradition did not use *upadhi* as an ascetic term for rebirth fuel. The usage of *upadhi* (*uvahi*) as a fundamental obstacle to liberation was forged in the **northeast Indian Śramaṇa culture**.

In early Jain texts composed in Ardhamāgadha (such as the *Ācārāṅga Sutta* and *Sūtrakṛtāṅga*), the Pāli form *upadhi* corresponds directly to the Prakrit term **uvahi** (or *upadhi*):

**Concrete Possessions & Attachments (*Uvahi*):** In the oldest Jain monastic rules (e.g., *Ācārāṅga Sutta*), *uvahi* refers explicitly to **worldly possessions, clothes, bowls, or physical gear** that a monk carries. To be *niruvi* (Pāli: *nirupadhi*) meant literally \"without physical possessions/encumbrances.\"

**Karmic Substrate:** As Jain doctrine systematized, *uvahi* expanded to mean any physical or material attachment that acts as an \"addition\" or \"weight\" binding the soul (*jīva*) to matter (*pudgala*).

**Jain Conclusion:** The Jain tradition preserved both the original concrete meaning (physical items/possessions carried by a person) and the early Shamana meaning (material attachments that weigh down the soul).

**The Early Pāli Canon (*Tipiṭaka*)**

In the early Pāli Suttas (e.g., *Sutta Nipāta*, *Saṁyutta Nikāya*, *Ittivuttaka*), **upadhi** is adapted into a precise, central technical term:

**Substrate / Fuel for Rebirth:** Pāli texts synthesize the physical meaning (\"foundation/substrate\") with the mechanics of *kamma*. *Upadhi* defines the **underlying substrates of existence**---specifically the physical body, sensory attachments, defilements (*kilesa*), and volitional formations---that provide the fuel (*upādāna*) for *saṃsāra*.

**Direct Textual Usage:**

1.  *Upadhīnidānā hi dukkhā bhavanti* (\"Suffering has *upadhi* as its source/foundation\").

2.  *Sabcupadhipaṭinissaggo* (\"The total relinquishment and casting off of all substrates/upadhi\" = *Nibbāna*).

**The Verse Connection (*upadhiṁ karoti*):** In the verse *Yo ve avidvā upadhiṁ karoti*, creating *upadhi* means accumulating the physical and mental substrates (through *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*) that force the *citta* back into physical bodies in rebirth.

Evaluating the Jain canonical, Śramaṇa, and early Indian literary parallels for the figure of **Kāḷī** (*Kālī*)---alongside the structural imagery of a terrifying female figure breaking open bones and corpses---reveals a direct shared heritage within the ancient Śramaṇa milieu (the common cultural and linguistic matrix of early Buddhism and Jainism).

**1. The Figure of *Kāḷī* / *Kālī* in Early Jain Canonical Texts**

In the early Jain Ardhamāgadha Śvetāmbara Canon (*Āgamas*), the figure of **Kālī** (Prakrit: *Kālī* / *Kāliyā*) appears in two precise contexts that match the Pāli verse:

**A. As a Terrifying Female Ogre/Deity (*Yakkhiṇī* / *Vidyādevī*)**

In texts such as the *Nāyādhammakahāo* (the 6th Aṅga) and the *Sūriyapaṇṇatti*, as well as later early commentaries (*Nirukti* and *Cūrṇi*):

- **Kālī** and **Mahākālī** are classed among the fierce, dark-complexioned female Yakkha entities (*Yakkhiṇī* or *Rākṣasī*).

- In early Śramaṇa demonology, these female entities were associated with wilderness, battlefields, crematoria (*śmaśāna*), and scenes of bodily destruction.

- They are explicitly described as having terrifying, dark, or monstrous forms (*kālī*), consuming flesh, or haunting places of death---mirroring the Pāli description: *Kāḷī itthī brahatī dhaṅkarūpā* (\"a dark, huge entity having the appearance of a crow\").

**B. The Imagery of Crematoria and Corpse Destruction**

In early Jain ascetic texts (such as the *Ācārāṅga Sutta* and *Sūtrakṛtāṅga*), the physical body is systematically stripped of romance. Monks are instructed to meditate on the physical disintegration of bodies in burial sites where wild animals, crows (*dhaṅka* / *kāka*), and female spirits scavenge, crack open limbs, and pick at exposed marrow/curd-like brains (*dadhi-thālakaṁva*).

**2. The Shared Śramaṇa Vocabulary: *Kāḷī* + *Upadhi* (*Uvahi*)**

When looking at the two halves of the verse together, the convergence of **Kāḷī** (the terrifying visual of bodily destruction) and **Upadhi** (the substrate/fuel of rebirth) points directly to a shared Śramaṇa ascetic teaching:

a.  **Jain Parallel (*Uvahi*):** In Jainism, *uvahi* (*upadhi*) is the material substrate or weight that attaches to the soul (*jīva*), binding it to physical rebirth.

b.  **The Common Śramaṇa Lesson:** Both early Jain texts and early Pāli Suttas used the gruesome, physical reality of bodily destruction (*Kāḷī* breaking open limbs in a graveyard) to shatter bodily attachment (*upadhi*), and more that to end the fantasy of reincarnation to have another body.

c.  **The Logical Deduction:** If a person foolishly generates *upadhi* for another physical body, they force themselves back into inevitability of being crushed, broken, and shattered (*bhinna-siro*, with broken heads).

**3. Conclusion on Textual Provenance**

**This verse originated directly within the ancient Śramaṇa culture** shared by early *bhikkhus* and Jain *niganṭhas* in the Greater Magadha region. Both traditions utilized:

- The common linguistic term **upadhi / uvahi** to denote the fuel of rebirth.

- The terrifying physical figure of **Kāḷī**---a giant, dark, crow-like graveyard entity---as a direct object lesson of death and destruction of physical body to urge monks not to reincarnate to have another body.

Now we can conclude that the modern mainstream academia standard translation of **kāḷī itthī** as a standard human \"black woman\" is an anachronistic, mistranslated mistake that completely misses the textual and cultural context of early Indian demonology.

In the ancient Śramaṇa matrix (shared by early Pāli texts and Jain *Āgamas*), these terms never referred to human demographics. They described specific non-human, dark-complexioned predatory entities---specifically a **Yakkhiṇī** or **Rākṣasī**---associated with graveyards, wilderness, and the physical destruction of corpses.

Here is the corrected, precise textual breakdown incorporating ancient Indian demonology:

**Precise Rendering of the Terms**

- **kāḷī itthī**: **A dark female entity** (*Yakkhiṇī* / *Rākṣasī* of death and destruction)

- **brahatī**: Towering / gigantic in stature

- **dhaṅkarūpā**: Having the appearance/form of a scavenger crow

- **dadhithālakaṁva**: Smashed open like a bowl of curd (referring to brain matter exposed by a shattered skull)

- **abhisandahitvā**: Saturated / drenched in blood and bodily fluids

**Demonological Function:** The *Yakkhiṇī* / *Rākṣasī* (*kāḷī itthī*) is a literal, non-human figure of Indian demonology whose horrific action---smashing limbs and devouring corpses in the crematorium---serves as an objective, visceral visualization of what happens to physical bodies.

**The Causal Link to *Upadhi*:** The second stanza immediately ties this terrifying vision to the law of *kamma*. The body is doomed to be destroyed in gruesome ways, why have another reincarnation by accumulating *upadhi* (the material attachment for fueled the craving for another body).

**The Ascetic Goal:** Recognizing that all physical bodies in *saṃsāra* are subject to this violent dissolution, the practitioner stops creating *upadhi* by eliminating *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, allowing the *citta* to break free from involuntary rebirth entirely.

That cuts straight to the absolute core of the Śramaṇa teaching.

Translating *māhaṁ puna bhinnasiro sayissaṁ* (\"May I not lie down again with a shattered head\") simply as a call to \"shatter bodily attachment\": the romantic fantasy that rebirth will somehow yield a refined, better, or immortal physical form.

The verse explicitly shatters that illusion.

**Ending the Delusion of Reincarnation**

a.  **The Trap of *Upadhi*:** The fool (*mando*), blinded by *moha* (delusion), accumulates *upadhi*---the material substrates, fuel, and clinging that drive involuntary reincarnation---because they secretly crave continued physical existence. They imagine future lives in pleasant forms, ignoring the structural law of *saṃsāra*.

b.  **The Inevitable Brutality of Rebirth:** The text uses the gruesome image of the dark female entity (*kāḷī itthī* \[Yakkhiṇī/Rākṣasī\]) smashing limbs and cracking open skulls to prove what *every* physical body ultimately inherits. To take another body through rebirth is not a spiritual progression; it is a guarantee of returning to the slaughterhouse of physical existence to lie down once more with a crushed skull (*bhinnasiro*).

c.  **Complete Transcendence:** The goal of the holy life (*Brahmacariya*) is not to upgrade the physical form or achieve a higher worldly birth, but to **end the fantasy of physical reincarnation entirely**.

The entire purpose of forcing a *bhikkhu* to confront this graphic scene in the crematorium is to shatter the romantic, delusion-driven fantasy of reincarnation: **the delusion that taking another body in a future life is somehow desirable, safe, or noble.**

**The Renunciant\'s Absolute Goal: The Final Body -** For the *bhikkhu* walking the *ancient path to Nibbāna*, the defining mark of an *Arahant* is that their current physical body is their **final body** (*antima-sarīra*).

**The Fool (*mando*):** Governed by *moha*, the ignorant person creates *upadhi* (*upadhiṁ karoti*), secretly harboring the fantasy of acquiring another physical form, higher status, or heavenly body. They are dragged back into *saṃsāra* time and time again, only to have that new body smashed open (*bhetvā*) in the end.

**The Wise (*pajānaṁ*):** The true practitioner sees the graveyard *Yakkhiṇī* destroying the corpse and realizes: *Every physical body without exception ends in this exact destruction.* They resolve with absolute clarity: **\"May I never again create the fuel for another body; may I never again lie down in rebirth with a broken head.\"**

#### 2.17. Tissa (3rd)

**Bahū sapatte labhati, muṇḍo saṅghāṭipāruto;\
Lābhī annassa pānassa, vatthassa sayanassa ca.**

**Etamādīnavaṁ ñatvā, sakkāresu mahabbhayaṁ;\
Appalābho anavassuto, sato bhikkhu paribbaje.**

*Many (bahū) rivals and enemies (sapatte) receive or attract (labhati) the shaven-headed one (muṇḍo) clad in the outer robe (saṅghāṭi-pāruto), Who is a recipient (lābhī) of food (annassa), drink (pānassa), clothing (vatthassa), and lodging (sayanassa).*

*Having known (ñatvā) this danger (etaṁ ādīnavaṁ) and the great fear (mahab-bhayaṁ) present in gains, honors, and veneration (sakkāresu); With few gains (appa-lābho), unpolluted by desire (anavassuto), mindful (sato), the bhikkhu should wander forth (paribbaje).*

**Commentary**: Another interesting case

**Modern academic contextualization and sociological readings** of early Indian history argue that this verse addresses a subtle, dangerous hazard on the ascetic path: the material success and public veneration (*sakkāra*) that naturally follow a respected, shaven-headed mendicant (*muṇḍo saṅghāṭipāruto*).

When a *bhikkhu* becomes a major recipient (*lābhī*) of the four physical requisites---food (*anna*), drink (*pāna*), clothing (*vattha*), and lodging (*sayana*)---that worldly abundance attracts intense envy, competition, and hostile rivals (*bahū sapatte*). Both laypeople and inferior practitioners begin to compete for access, influence, and material share.

They interpret **sapatte** literally and physically to **external human rivals, adversaries, and hostile competitors**. This is how standard academic translators and critical editions handle the exact term in the Pāli Canon.

Modern philological dictionaries, academic translations, and historical commentaries translate **sapatte** (accusative plural of *sapatta*) literally as **\"rivals,\" \"enemies,\" or \"foes\"**---derived directly from the Vedic *sapatna* (co-wife, rival, or adversary).

**1. Lexical (Standard Dictionaries)**

- **The Pali Text Society (PTS) Pali-English Dictionary:** Defines *sapatta* as \"an enemy, adversary, rival.\"

- **The Digital Pāḷi Dictionary (DPD):** Defines *sapatta* explicitly as **\"enemy; foe; rival\"** (inflected as *sapatte* in the accusative plural).

**2. Academic Translation**

When modern academic translators (such as K.R. Norman in the *Elder\'s Verses / Theragāthā* translations for the PTS, or Bhikkhu Sujato in modern digital renderings) translate verses containing *sapatte*, they render it literally as external human competitors or enemies.

For instance, in the parallel contexts of monastic recruitment, wealth, and patronage rivalry in ancient India, academic historical commentaries (such as those by Uma Chakravarti, Gregory Schopen, or Richard Gombrich on early Buddhist social history) explicitly interpret terms like *sapatta* and *sakkāra* through a **sociological framework**: They argue that when a monk (*muṇḍo saṅghāṭipāruto*) becomes successful and receives abundant food, lodging, and robes (*lābhī annassa pānassa\...*), he incurs the jealousy of **other human ascetics and rival sects** (*sapatte*) who are competing for the same limited lay patronage in the Gangetic towns.

**Why This Modern Academic Read Is a Distortion**

- **The Academic/Sociological Error:** Academics treat the verse as a practical warning about **social politics and physical/reputational danger**---implying the monk should keep a low profile so other monks or rival teachers won\'t ruin his reputation or fight him.

This is absurd.

Mapping *sapatte* to external human competitors, inferior monastics, or rival sects is an **absurd reduction**. It treats a profound Śramaṇa text on the liberation of the mind as if it were a handbook on monastic politics or social survival in ancient India.

**Why the \"External Human Rival\" Reading Fails**

a)  **The Śramaṇa Horizon is Internal:** An Arahant or dedicated Śramaṇa training path is an uncompromising battle against the internal architecture of *saṃsāra*.

b)  **The True Adversaries (*Sapatte*):** In the true ascetic framework, the \"rivals\" and \"enemies\" (*sapatte*) that attack the practitioner are the **internal enemies of the mind**---the insidious surges of material and worldly craving (*taṇhā*), attachment, pride, and complacency that creep in when one is surrounded by comfortable food (*anna*), drink (*pāna*), soft robes (*vattha*), and luxurious shelter (*sayana*).

c)  **The Trap of Comfort:** Material requisites and public veneration (*sakkāra*) act as catalysts. They feed the internal enemies, lulling the mind into a false sense of security, corrupting the *citta* with *rāga* (worldly carving and attachment) and *moha* (ignorance and delusion), and accumulating *upadhi*---the material substrate and fuel for involuntary physical reincarnation.

**The Restored Śramaṇa Structural Translation**

With *sapatte* correctly identified as the internal enemies of craving and attachment born of worldly comfort, the verse reveals its true, seamless unity:

**The tonsured one, draped in the outer robe (muṇḍo saṅghāṭipāruto), who becomes a recipient of food, drink, clothing, and lodging (lābhī annassa pānassa, vatthassa sayanassa ca)--- attracts numerous internal enemies \[of craving and defilement\] (bahū sapatte labhati).**

**Having recognized this inherent danger (etamādīnavaṁ ñatvā), this great peril present in gains, honors, and veneration (sakkāresu mahabbhayaṁ); With minimal gains (appalābho), unpolluted by desire (anavassuto), mindful (sato), the *bhikkhu* should wander forth in solitude (paribbaje).**

**The Absolute Goal: Eradicating *Upadhi* for the Final Body**

When a practitioner mistakes external comfort for spiritual success, the internal enemies (*sapatte*) take root, causing them to generate *upadhi* (*upadhiṁ karoti*). If these internal enemies are allowed to corrupt the mind through *sakkāra*, the practitioner is dragged back into the physical matrix to take another body---guaranteeing that they will once again lie down in a graveyard with a shattered head (*bhinnasiro*). By seeing honors and comfortable requisites as a deadly internal trap:

- The *bhikkhu* becomes **appalābho** (content with absolute minimal requisites) to starve the internal enemies of material and worldly craving.

- The mind remains **anavassuto** (completely unpolluted and unleaking), thoroughly eradicating *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

- The practitioner ensures that this current body is their **final body** (*antima-sarīra*).

Upon the dissolution of the final physical body of an Arahant, liberation from worldly bondage is realized through the exit of this world to the *Nibbāna-dhātu*, the deathless realm of *Nibbāna,* only for the purified *citta* of the Arahants.

#### 2.18. Kimbila (2nd)

**Pācīnavaṁsadāyamhi, sakyaputtā sahāyakā;\
Pahāyānappake bhoge, uñchāpattāgate ratā.**

**Āraddhavīriyā pahitattā, Niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā;\
Ramanti dhammaratiyā, Hitvāna lokiyaṁ ratin.**

*In the East Bamboo Park (Pācīnavaṁsadāya), the Sakyan sons, spiritual companions (sakyaputtā sahāyakā), Having abandoned no small amount of wealth and possessions (pahāya anappake bhoge), take delight in food gathered bit by bit into the bowl (uñchā-pattāgate ratā). Aroused in spiritual energy (āraddha-vīriyā), intent toward the attā (soul-spirit) (pahita-attā), constantly possessing unshakeable, relentless striving (niccaṁ daḷha-parakkamā); They delight in the spiritual joy of the Dhamma (ramanti dhamma-ratiyā), having discarded all worldly delight (hitvāna lokiyaṁ ratin).*

**1. The Abandonment of Material Substrates (*Bhoga* & *Upadhi*)**

This verse captures the physical and mental posture of the Śramaṇa renunciants---the Sakyan scions (*sakyaputtā sahāyakā*) living in absolute seclusion in the East Bamboo Park. They discarded vast, immense worldly wealth (*pahāya anappake bhoge*). In the Śramaṇa matrix, *bhoga* (material wealth, status, physical comfort) is the primary fuel that generates **upadhi**---the material substrate and binding agent that anchors the *citta* to physical existence and forces involuntary reincarnation.

Instead of relying on stored provisions, financial security, or organized monastic estates, they delight strictly in **uñchāpattāgate**: food obtained by scraping, gleaning, and receiving whatever scraps fall anonymously into the alms bowl (*patta*). By stripping away all material security, they eradicate the physical anchors of *sakkāra* (honors and gains) and starve the internal enemies (*sapatte*) of craving and attachment.

**2. Worldly Delight (*Lokiya Rati*) vs. Dhamma Delight (*Dhamma-rati*)**

The verse establishes an absolute, binary divide between two mutually exclusive operational modes:

**Worldly Delight (*Lokiya Rati*):**

- **Operational Mode:** Focuses on sensory pleasures, physical bodies, and worldly status within the *loka*.

- **Underlying Process:** Driven by the operation of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

- **Ascetic Outcome:** Results in the accumulation of *upadhi* (material fuel for rebirth), leading directly to involuntary reincarnation and physical destruction again (*bhinnasiro* / shattered heads).

**Transcendent Dhamma Delight (*Dhamma Rati*):**

- **Operational Mode:** Begins with the complete abandonment of worldly delight (*hitvāna lokiyaṁ ratin*).

- **Underlying Process:** Fueled by aroused heroic spiritual energy (*āraddhavīriyā*) and unshakeable, relentless striving (*daḷhaparakkamā*).

- **Ascetic Outcome:** Leads to the total eradication of *upadhi*, securing the final body (*antima-sarīra*) and ending the cycle of rebirth.

The *bhikkhu* does not attempt to refine, sanctify, or enjoy worldly physical existence. Having recognized that all worldly delights (*lokiyaṁ ratin*) lead back to the slaughterhouse of *saṃsāra*, they discard them entirely (*hitvāna*).

**3. Heroic Ascetic Effort (*Āraddhavīriyā Pahitattā*)**

The Sakyan companions are characterized by three compounding terms of intense physical and mental exertion:

a)  **Āraddha-vīriyā**: Spiritual energy aroused to the highest pitch, leaving no room for sloth or complacency.

b)  **Pahit-attā**: Intent entirely set on the Soul-Spirit **attā**.

c)  **Niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā**: Unceasing, firm, unshakeable striving.

This level of effort is required because the goal of the renunciant is not peaceful worldly co-existence or psychological adjustment, but the complete termination of physical reincarnation.

#### 2.19. Nanda

**Ayoniso manasikārā, maṇḍanaṁ anuyuñjisaṁ;\
Uddhato capalo cāsiṁ, kāmarāgena aṭṭito.**

**Upāyakusalenāhaṁ, buddhenādiccabandhunā;\
Yoniso paṭipajjitvā, bhave cittaṁ udabbahin**

*When mind (manas) is not directed to the Source (ayoniso manasikārā), I indulged in bodily adornment (maṇḍanaṁ anuyuñjisaṁ); I was restless (uddhato), unsteady (capalo), and afflicted (aṭṭito) by attachment to sexual and sensual craving (kāma-rāga).*

*By the Skilled in Means (upāya-kusala), the Awakened One (buddhena), Kinsman of the Sun god (ādicca-bandhu); Having directed the mind (manas) to the root Source Yoni (yoniso paṭipajjitvā), I uprooted the citta (mind-heart) from worldly manifestation / worldly becoming (bhave cittaṁ udabbahiṁ).*

**Commentary**: *yoniso + ādicca-bandhu*

**Yoniso** is not a superficial catchword or a mild synonym for "Wise" or \"sensible\" or \"careful\" as we see in the standard mainstream academia's interpretation. It actually cuts to the absolute ontological root of existence.

Tracing the etymology back through the earliest strata of the *Ṛg Veda* reveals why this term carries the explosive force required to shatter the wheel of reincarnation.

**1. Etymological Root: *Yoni* in the *Ṛg Veda***

- **The Root:** Derived from the Indo-Iranian and Proto-Indo-European root **\*yónh₂-** (connected to generation, begetting, and birth).

- **Primary Vedic Meaning:** In the *Ṛg Veda*, **yoni** (योनि) literally and fundamentally means **the womb, the place of origin, the matrix, the nest, the fountainhead, or the generative source** from which anything is born, manifested, or brought forth.

- **Application:** In the oldest Vedic hymns, *yoni* denotes the primal root source or womb---the literal source from which forms, worlds, and lives emerge into *bhava* (worldly becoming or manifestation).

*Ṛg Veda* 3.29.2: ***yoniḥ ta āśayo kṛto, yathā vaso ratna-dhānam:** **etām ā-vaha: yoniḥ eṣā, yaḥ ta āśayo kṛto, yataḥ jāto arocathāḥ.***

This (eṣā) is your (te) true place of origin /matrix (yoniḥ). A place of rest and dwelling (āśayo) has been prepared (kṛto) for you, like (yathā) a container of precious treasures (ratna-dhānam), O dwelling light (vaso, *dwelling one, radiant lord, an epithet for **Agni,** the Divine Fire, while also functioning in the plural (**Vasavaḥ**) as a major class of elemental deities*). Bring her forth (etām ā-vaha)! This (eṣā) is the original womb-source (yoniḥ), which (yaḥ) was made (kṛto) your (te) resting place (āśayo), from which (yataḥ) having been born (jāto), you shone forth (arocathāḥ).

In the *Ṛg Veda*, **yoni** is not an abstract metaphor; it is the concrete, generative origin from which fire (*Agni*) or divine force is drawn forth into manifestation.

The text describes the ritual extraction of Agni from the wooden matrix (*araṇī*). The lower wood block is explicitly named the **yoni**---the physical womb and generative source. The verse asserts that Agni cannot shine (*arocathāḥ*) until he emerges directly from his true **yoni** (*yataḥ jāto*).

This establishes the foundational Vedic definition: **yoni** is the hidden, underlying source-matrix where an entity resides before it emerges into manifest existence.

When this Vedic framework was inherited by Śramaṇa practitioners, the adverbial form **yoniso** (*yoni* + suffix *-śas*, meaning \"by way of the source\" or \"from the root matrix\") retained this exact structural logic: to trace a phenomenon **yoniso** is to strip away its surface manifestation and track it directly back into its generative womb (*yoni*).

By applying **yoniso manasikāra** (root-level, causal insight into the womb-matrix of all physical phenomena), the practitioner sees that *all* worldly forms of birth---whether human (*manussa*), animal, or divine---are impermanent, manifest within the world (*loka*), and governed by the universal law of decay and death.

By penetrating the *yoni* down to its root, the ascetic completely extinguishes the accumulation of *upadhi*, ensuring that the current physical body is their **final body** (*antima-sarīra*), and extracts the *citta* entirely out of the universe into the unconditioned and deathless **Nibbāna-dhātu**.

Therefore, when the texts speak of **yoniso manasikāra**, they do not mean \"thinking positively, wisely or appropriately\" or \"using common sense.\" They mean:

**\"Directing the mind straight to the womb-source, tracking every phenomenon back to its generative root and structural cause.\"**

**Let's look at Early Jain Śramaṇa Parallel (*Ācārāṅga Sūtra*):**

*Ācārāṅga Sūtra* 1.1.5 (Sanskritized): *yāṃ ca yonim anupariyatamānaḥ, karma-samudaya-mūlaṃ yonikam: yoni-parijñāṁ parijānāti.*

And whichever (yām) original womb-source (*yonim*) \[one\] continuously wanders and revolves around (*anupariyatamānaḥ*), that very womb-matrix (*yonikam*) is the root (*mūlam*) of the accumulation of karma (*karma-samudaya*). \[He/the ascetic\] fully comprehends (*parijānāti*) the thorough direct knowledge of the womb-source (*yoni-parijñām*).

In early Jain Śramaṇa literature---composed primarily in Ardhamāgadhī and Śaurasenī Prakrit---the term *yoni* (Prakrit: *joṇi*) and its diagnostic applications (*joṇi-pariṇṇā* / *yoni-parijñā*, mirroring Pāli *yoniso*) serve as the foundational mechanical entry point for ending reincarnation.

In the Jain matrix, a **yoni** is not merely an anatomical organ; it is a specific **physico-karmic seedbed or nucleus of material manifestation** (traditionally categorized into 8.4 million *joṇi-ppamāṇa* across the universe) where a sentient entity (*jīva*) is bound by karmic matter (*kamma-puggala*) to take physical form.

To achieve liberation (*mokṣa*), the ascetic must execute **Yoni-Parijñā** (the thorough, radical penetration and dismantling of the womb-matrix).

Here are more verses from the oldest strata of Jain Āgamas:

**1. *Ācārāṅga Sūtra* (*Āyāraṁga Sutta*) 1.1.2 (Sanskritized):** *yo yoniṁ sarvaśo jñāna-parijñayā parijānāti, sa bhavati viṣṭhā-vinaya-paravasa-laṅghana-parāṅmukhaḥ.*

This verse establishes the direct link between ignorance of the womb-matrix (*yoni*) and continuous physical destruction.

Whoever (yo) thoroughly comprehends (parijānāti) the womb-source (yoniṁ) in all its aspects (sarvaśo) through direct discerning knowledge (jñāna-parijñayā), he (sa) becomes averse (parāṅmukhaḥ) to remaining subject to foreign force (paravasa), suffering physical degradation (viṣṭhā), and being bound down (vinaya).

**2. *Sūtraktāṅga Sūtra* (*Sūyagaḍaṁga*) 1.1.1.5:** *yoniṃ-anupraviṣṭānām, na vigacched a-paśyakaḥ; karmaṇām-upasaṅgāt, mucyate bandhanād uta.*

This text addresses the mechanics of how the soul (*jīva*) becomes drawn into a *yoni* through ignorance, generating the material fuel for rebirth.

For those who have entered into (anupraviṣṭānām) the womb-matrix (yoniṃ), the blind one who does not see (a-paśyakaḥ) cannot escape (na vigacchet). Only by dissolving the attachment to (upasaṅgāt) karma-particles (karmaṇām) is one released (mucyate) from the prison of bondage (bandhanāt).

**3. *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (*Uttarajjhayaṇa*) 29.11:** *yoni-parijñayā nūnaṁ jīvaḥ, yoni-saṁsṛtiṃ chindati; yoni-saṁsṛti-chinnānām, tathā-pākaṁ niyacchati.*

This explicit verse details the direct causal chain connecting **Yoni-Parijñā** (the thorough knowledge of the womb-matrix) to the complete severance of reincarnation (*bhava*).

By means of the radical discernment of the womb-source (yoni-parijñayā), the soul (jīvaḥ) certainly (nūnaṁ) cuts off (chindati) the continuous cycle of entering womb-matrices (yoni-saṁsṛtiṃ). For those who have severed (chinnānām) the cycle of entering womb-matrices (yoni-saṁsṛti), \[the soul\] attains (niyacchati) the ultimate state of mature liberation (tathā-pākaṁ).

**The Contrast: *Ayoniso* vs. *Yoniso***

The profound opposition between *ayoniso* and *yoniso* maps directly onto the mechanics of bondage and liberation:

**Ayoniso (Not from the source):**

- Observes the physical body as an end in itself.

- Leads directly to bodily ornamentation (*maṇḍanaṁ*), attachment to sexual and sensual craving (*kāma-rāga*), and the accumulation of *upadhi* (the womb-fuel for rebirth).

**Yoniso (Tracing back to the womb-source):**

- Penetrates beneath the surface of physical phenomena.

- Recognizes that physical bodies are temporary worldly manifestations born of cause, effect, and decay---belonging entirely to the world (*loka*).

- Uproots the *citta* from worldly becoming (*bhava*) to secure the final body (*antima-sarīra*).

**Why Yoniso Leads Directly to Liberation**

When a practitioner applies yoniso manasikāra, they refuse to look at things superficially. They look into the \"womb\" (yoni) of dukkha and see that every physical form, every material desire, and every worldly honor is rooted in the conditioned matrix of saṃsāra.

By tracing phenomena back to this root source with radical clarity, the citta undergoes complete disenchantment (nibbindati). It realizes that physical phenomena are just manifestations of the Citta/Soul-Spirit---they are worldly, they belong to the world (loka), and the citta must leave them behind and transcend them.

Through this root-level vision (yoniso), the practitioner violently extracts the citta from bhava (bhave cittaṁ udabbahiṁ), ensuring that they never again enter a womb and then to lie down with a broken head again, exiting the universe entirely into the Nibbāna-dhātu.

#### 2.20. Sirima

**Pare ca naṁ pasaṁsanti, attā ce asamāhito;\
Moghaṁ pare pasaṁsanti, attā hi asamāhito.**

**Pare ca naṁ garahanti, attā ce susamāhito;\
Moghaṁ pare garahanti, attā hi susamāhito.**

And others praise him (*pare ca naṁ pasaṁsanti*), if his *attā* (soul-spirit) is not firmly established in *Samadhi* (*attā ce asamāhito*); In vain do others praise him (*moghaṁ pare pasaṁsanti*), for his *attā* (soul-spirit) is not firmly established in *Samadhi* (*attā hi asamāhito*).

And others criticize him (*pare ca naṁ garahanti*), if his *attā* (soul-spirit) is excellently and firmly established in *Samadhi* (*attā ce susamāhito*); In vain do others criticize him (*moghaṁ pare garahanti*), for his *attā* (soul-spirit) is excellently and firmly established in *Samadhi* (*attā hi susamāhito*).

**Commentary**: *attā ce susamāhito: attā* (soul-spirit) is excellently and firmly established in *Samadhi*

**Attā** does not mean an abstract, external personality, a worldly social ego, or mere psychological self-image. Rather, it denotes the internal, essential spiritual core of the individual---the inner soul-spirit---that must be brought into absolute, unshakeable Concentration-*Samādhi* to achieve liberation from worldly bondage.

Across both early Pāli texts and ancient Jain Śramaṇa scriptures, this exact phrasing and understanding are repeatedly validated. When *attā* is paired with *susamāhito* (or its Prakrit and Sanskrit equivalents), it signifies that the inner soul-spirit of the practitioner has been made completely firm, collected, and immutable against all physical and worldly perturbations (*loka*).

***Dhammapada* Verse 379 (Attanā Codayattānam)**

**Attanā codayattānaṃ, paṭimāsetam attanā;**

**So attagutto satimā, sukhaṃ bhikkhu vihāhisi.**

With the soul-spirit (*attanā*), motivate your soul-spirit (*codayattānaṃ \[codayā + attānaṃ\]*); examine, and investigate your soul-spirit (*paṭimāsetam, often read as paṭimāseta attanā or paṭimāse attānaṃ\]*) by the soul-spirit (*attanā*). Thus (*so*), O bhikkhu, you will dwell (*vihāhisi*) with the soul-spirit guarded (*attagutto*), mindful (*satimā*), happily (*sukhaṃ*).

***Dhammapada* Verse 362:**

**hatthasaṃyato pādasaṃyato, vācāsaṃyato saṃyatuttamo;**

**ajjhattarato samāhito, eko santusito tamāhu bhikkhuṃ.**

Restrained in hands (*hatthasaṃyato*), restrained in feet (*pādasaṃyato*), restrained in speech (*vācāsaṃyato*), restrained supreme (*saṃyatuttamo*); taking delight in higher soul-spirit (*ajjh-atta-rato*), established in *samādhi* (*samāhito*), solitary (*eko*), contented (*santusito*)---him (*taṃ*) they call (*āhu*) a bhikkhu (*bhikkhuṃ*).

ajjhatta-rato: The prefix **ajjh-** is the Pāḷi/Sanskrit form of *adhi-*, carrying the spatial and dimensional sense of **\"over,\" \"above,\" \"superior to,\" \"pervading,\"** or **\"interior/inward.\"** When combined with **atta** (soul-spirit), it forms **ajjhatta**, which translates literally to:

- **The higher soul-spirit** (reflecting the dimensional aspect of *adhi* --- transcendent, above the mundane plane).

- **The inner soul-spirit** (reflecting the dimensional aspect of interiority --- deep within the layers of existence).

The soul-spirit is multi-dimensional. In ancient texts, *ajjhatta* does not merely mean \"mental\" in a secular, psychological sense which the mainstream academia lands; it refers to the profound, subtle layers of the true spiritual core that stand independent of the gross physical body and external sensory feeds. Thus, *ajjhattarato* means **taking delight in the higher, inner soul-spirit**.

**Ancient Jain Śramaṇa Texts**

In the early Jain Āgamas (composed in Ardhamāgadhī / Śaurasenī Prakrit), the exact equivalent of *attā* is **appā** (Sanskrit: **ātmā**). The Jain Śramaṇa tradition is even more explicit in defining *appā* as the essential soul-spirit (*jīva* / *dravyārthika ātmā*) that must be anchored in absolute steady concentration (*samādhi* / *jhāṇa*) to cut off karmic matter.

***Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (*Uttarajjhayaṇa*) 29.16 (Appā Samāhio) (Prakrit): Appā samāhio; appā gutto, sati-rakkhito; muni tarai oghaṃ duddaraṃ.**

\[When the\] soul-spirit (*appā*) is established in samādhi (*samāhio*); \[when the\] soul-spirit (*appā*) is guarded (*gutto*), protected by mindfulness (*sati-rakkhito*); the *muni* crosses over (*tarai*) the flood (*oghaṃ*), which is hard to cross (*duddaraṃ*).

This whole chapter 29 is detailing the direct causes of liberation (*mokṣa*):

- **Prakrit Matrix:** Through the practice of *samādhi* and mental control, the *appā* (soul-spirit) becomes completely motionless (*niścala*) and established in its own essential nature.

- **Structural Parallel:** The Jain text explicitly uses **appā samāhio** (Sanskrit: *ātmā samāhitaḥ*), which is the precise Prakrit counterpart to Pāli **attā susamāhito**.

- **Meaning:** It asserts that when the *appā* (soul-spirit) is firmly grounded in *samādhi*, karmic particles (*kamma-puggala*) can no longer attach to it. The external opinion of the world (*loka*) is entirely irrelevant; only the firm establishment of the *appā* in *Samādhi* halts the influx of rebirth-fuel.

**The Early Śramaṇa Core: The Soul-Spirit (*Appā*)**

In early Ardhamāgadhī Prakrit, **appā** (*ātman*) denotes the living, substantive soul-spirit (*jīva*) bound within karmic matter. The early Śramaṇa tradition places total emphasis on the direct governance and fortification of this soul-spirit. The text explicitly asserts that liberation is achieved not by denying the *appā*, but by securing and steadying it.

**Safeguard of Mindfulness and Samādhi**

The verse establishes two prerequisite states for the *appā*:

- **Sati-rakkhito**: Fortified and shielded against sensory intrusion by unceasing mindfulness (*sati*).

- **Samāhio**: Established in samādhi, anchoring the mind-will in unwavering, meditative absorption (*jhāna*).

**Crossing the Flood (*Ogha*) of Saṃsāra**

The \"flood\" (*oghaṃ*) represents the ocean of involuntary reincarnation in this world (*loka*). By holding the soul-spirit **appā** (*ātman*) established in samādhi, the *muni* disenchants these external structures, leaving them behind to cross the impassable torrent.

Similarly in the *Mahāparinibbāna Sutta*, Gautama Buddha also emphasize: **\"Atta-dīpā viharatha atta-saraṇā anaññasaraṇā\"** (\"Dwell with the soul-spirit as your island, with the soul-spirit as your refuge, with no external refuge\").

The World as the Submerged Sea (*Saṃsāra / Ogha*): a vast, raging ocean. It is swept by torrents of cause-and-effect, impermanence, and death. To sink into the world---seeking refuge in physical phenomena, sensory pleasures, or external forms---is to drown in *dukkha* and undergo endless death.

**The Soul-Spirit as the Only Dry Ground (*Attadīpa / Attasaraṇa*):** An island is defined by being elevated above the surrounding floodwaters. By turning inward and taking the soul-spirit as the island (*atta-dīpa*) and refuge (*atta-saraṇa*), the practitioner steps out of the surging currents. The soul-spirit, when established in samādhi (*appā samāhio* / *samāhito*), becomes an unassailable high ground that cannot be submerged by the worldly tides.

***Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (*Uttarajjhayaṇa*) 29.16: samāhi-sohīe ṇaṃ jīve, appāṇaṃ samāhaī; samāhiy-appā jīve, kamma-rajjaṃ vihuṇaī.**

By the purification of samādhi (*samāhi-sohīe*), indeed (*ṇaṃ*), the soul (*jīve*) establishes in samādhi (*samāhaī*) with the soul-spirit (*appāṇaṃ*); the soul (*jīve*) whose soul-spirit is established in samādhi (*samāhiy-appā*) shakes off and destroys (*vihuṇaī*) the karmic dust (*kamma-rajjaṃ*).

***Samayasāra* (Acharya Kundakunda) Verse 268:** *Appā jo saṃvara-sohido ya, samayehiṃ aṇekahiṃ taha ya; kamma-rajjaṃ vihuṇadī, taha jhāṇa-mayaṃ-aṇalaṃ paviṭṭho.*

The soul-spirit (*appā*) which (*jo*) is purified by restraint (*saṃvara-sohido*) indeed (*ya*), through many (*aṇekahiṃ*) spiritual teachings and practices (*samayehiṃ*) also (*taha ya*); shakes off and dispels (*vihuṇadī*) the karmic dust (*kamma-rajjaṃ*), having entered (*paviṭṭho*) the fire (*aṇalaṃ*) consisting of meditative absorption (*jhāṇa-mayaṃ*).

***Niyamasāra* (Acharya Kundakunda) Verse 165:** *jo saṃvudo ṇiccadā appā, samāhie ṭhio hodi; so ṇaṃ vaṁdhaï kammaṃ, joṇi-saṃsāra-sāgare.*

**That (*jo*) soul-spirit (*appā*) which (*jo*) is restrained and guarded (*saṃvudo*) always (*ṇiccadā*), and stands (*hodi ṭhio*) established in samādhi (*samāhie*); he / it (*so*) indeed (*ṇaṃ*) does not bind (*ṇa vaṁdhaï*) karma (*kammaṃ*), in the ocean of womb-rebirths and saṃsāra (*joṇi-saṃsāra-sāgare*).**

Now let's get back to *attā ce susamāhito:* **\"his attā (soul-spirit) is excellently firmly established in Samādhi\"**:

In the early Śramaṇa tradition, **the *attā* (the inner soul-spirit) is itself brought into absolute, unshakeable stability within *Samādhi***, thereby entirely severing the *citta* from any alignment with worldly identity, social roles, or external opinions (*pare*).

**The Total Rejection of Worldly Identity:** this involves a radical pivot away from worldly identity and toward the true *attā*---strikes at the very heart of why the verse immediately contrasts the *attā* with **pare** (the external crowd / worldly society): When the practitioner\'s inner spiritual core (*attā*) is *susamāhito*, they have stopped identifying with the physical body, social titles, or the fluctuating opinions of the crowd. Their entire awareness is directed inward, steeled against the matrix of decay and death (*dukkha*). By ensuring that the *attā* (soul-spirit) is *susamāhito* (excellently established in *Samādhi*):

a)  The internal enemies (**sapatte** like *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*) are starved of fuel.

b)  The accumulation of material substrate (**upadhi**) is halted.

c)  The cycle of wandering through womb-matrices (**yoni-saṁsṛti**) is severed.

d)  The current physical vehicle is secured as the **final body** (*antima-sarīra*).

Upon the dissolution of this final form, the purified consciousness exits the universe into the unconditioned and deathless **Nibbāna-dhātu**.

#### 2.21. Uttara (2nd)

**Khandhā mayā pariññātā, taṇhā me susamūhatā;\
Bhāvitā mama bojjhaṅgā, patto me āsavakkhayo.**

**Sohaṁ khandhe pariññāya, abbahitvāna jāliniṁ;\
Bhāvayitvāna bojjhaṅge, nibbāyissaṁ anāsavo.**

The aggregates (*khandhā*) have been fully comprehended (*pariññātā*) by me (*mayā*), craving (*taṇhā*) in me (*me*) has been completely eradicated from its root (*susamūhatā*); the factors of awakening (*bojjhaṅgā*) have been cultivated (*bhāvitā*) by me (*mama*), the destruction of defilements (*āsavakkhayo*) has been reached (*patto*) by me (*me*). Thus I (*sohaṁ \[so + ahaṁ\]*), having fully comprehended (*pariññāya*) the aggregates (*khandhe*), having pulled out and uprooted (*abbahitvāna*) the snaring net of craving (*jāliniṁ*); having cultivated (*bhāvayitvāna*) the factors of awakening (*bojjhaṅge*), will attain final unbinding (*nibbāyissaṁ*), free from defilements (*anāsavo*).

#### 2.22. Bhaddaji

**Panādo nāma so rājā, yassa yūpo suvaṇṇayo;\
Tiriyaṁ soḷasubbedho, ubbhamāhu sahassadhā.**

**Sahassakaṇḍo satageṇḍu, dhajālu haritāmayo;\
Anaccuṁ tattha gandhabbā, chasahassāni sattadhā.**

That king (*so rājā*) by name (*nāma*) \[was\] Panāda (*panādo*), whose (*yassa*) golden sacrificial pillar (*yūpo suvaṇṇayo*) \[had\] horizontally (*tiriyaṁ*) a measure of sixteen \[cubits/breadths\] (*soḷasubbedho*); upward (*ubbhaṁ*) they say (*āhu*) \[it measured\] a thousand \[cubits\] (*sahassadhā*). \[It had\] a thousand branches/knots (*sahassakaṇḍo*), a hundred rounded capitals/globes (*satageṇḍu*), \[decked with\] banners (*dhajālu*), adorned with gold (*haritāmayo*). There (*tattha*), celestial musician beings (*gandhabbā*)---six thousand (*chasahassāni*) in seven ways (*sattadhā*)---danced (*anaccuṁ*).

#### 2.23. Sobhita

**Satimā paññavā bhikkhu, āraddhabalavīriyo;\
Pañca kappasatānāhaṁ, ekarattiṁ anussariṁ.**

**Cattāro satipaṭṭhāne, satta aṭṭha ca bhāvayaṁ;\
Pañca kappasatānāhaṁ, ekarattiṁ anussarin.**

Possessing mindfulness (*satimā*), possessing wisdom (*paññavā*), a *bhikkhu* with aroused spiritual energy (*āraddhabalavīriyo*); for five hundred aeons (*pañca kappasatānāhaṁ* \[pañca kappasatā + ahaṁ\]), I recollected (*anussariṁ*) in a single night (*ekarattiṁ*). Cultivating (*bhāvayaṁ*) the four foundational mindfulness (*cattāro satipaṭṭhāne*), the seven \[factors of awakening\] and the eight \[fold path\] (*satta aṭṭha ca*); for five hundred aeons (*pañca kappasatānāhaṁ*), I recollected (*anussarin* \[anussariṁ\]) in a single night (*ekarattiṁ*).

**\**

**Commentary**: *cattāro satipaṭṭhāne* as four foundational mindfulness

*"cattāro satipaṭṭhāne"* was traditionally translated as \" four foundations of mindfulness.\" But Arahanism translates **satipaṭṭhāne** as **\"foundational mindfulness\", which** is more accurate than the standard mainstream academia's version, in many ways it captures the compound\'s actual morphological structure, but also has deep philosophical and spiritual meaning behind it. Details can be seen in my book "**The Complete Mindfulness Training Toward Liberation:** The Union of Satipaṭṭhāna (Foundational Mindfulness) and Anussati (Higher Mindfulness)."

**Etymological & Grammatical Breakdown of *Satipaṭṭhāna*: *Sati* + *Paṭṭhāna***

**1. *Sati* (sati): Root:** Derived from Vedic Sanskrit *smṛ* (to remember, bear in mind, keep present). **Meaning:** Mindfulness, active recollection, present-moment awareness, non-forgetfulness.

**2. *Paṭṭhāna:* Literal Meaning:** \"base,\" \"foundation,\" \"primary ground,\" or \"footing.\" Here, *paṭṭhāna* functions as a qualifying noun or locative base.

**Why \"Foundational Mindfulness\" Makes Sense Etymologically**

When analyzing the compound type (*samāsa*), translating it as **\"foundational mindfulness\"** shifts the structural emphasis in a grammatically sound way:

**Adjectival / Descriptive Compound (*Kammadhāraya*):** If viewed as a *kammadhāraya* compound, the second element (*paṭṭhāna* = foundation/base) acts as a modifier describing the quality or nature of the *sati* (mindfulness) itself. Thus, it is not merely general awareness, but **mindfulness that acts as the foundation**---i.e., **\"foundational mindfulness.\"**

**Functional Reality:** Traditional renderings (\"foundations of mindfulness\") treat *sati* as the object and *paṭṭhāna* as a container (the four domains: body, feelings, mind, phenomena). However, **\"foundational mindfulness\"** accurately reflects that *sati* itself is the foundational instrument to anchor the *citta* against the sensory ocean of *saṃsāra*. Thus translating *satipaṭṭhāna* as **foundational mindfulness** aligns precisely with the early Śramaṇa architecture. Mindfulness is not a passive observation of worldly phenomena; it is the active instrument built around the *citta* to execute restraint (*saṃvara*).

#### 2.24. Valliya (3rd)

**Yaṁ kiccaṁ daḷhavīriyena, yaṁ kiccaṁ boddhumicchatā;\
Karissaṁ nāvarajjhissaṁ, passa vīriyaṁ parakkamaṁ.**

**Tvañca me maggamakkhāhi, añjasaṁ amatogadhaṁ;\
Ahaṁ monena monissaṁ, gaṅgāsotova sāgaran.**

Whatever duty (*yaṁ kiccaṁ*) by one with strong energy (*daḷha-vīriyena*), whatever duty (*yaṁ kiccaṁ*) \[is to be done\] by one desiring to awaken (*boddhumicchatā*); I will perform it (*karissaṁ*), I will not fail (*nāvarajjhissaṁ* \[na + avarajjhissaṁ\]), behold (*passa*) \[my\] spiritual energy (*vīriyaṁ*) and heroic exertion (*parakkamaṁ*). And you (*tvañca* \[tvaṃ + ca\]) declare to me (*me maggamakkhāhi* \[maggaṁ + akkhāhi\]) the path (*maggaṁ*), direct and straight (*añjasaṁ*), plunging into the Deathless (*amatogadhaṁ*); I (*ahaṁ*) will practice *muni*-like silence and wisdom (*monena monissaṁ*), like the stream of the Ganges (*gaṅgāsotova* \[gaṅgāsoto + iva\]) \[flowing to\] the ocean (*sāgaraṃ*).

#### 2.25. Vītasoka

**Kese me olikhissanti, kappako upasaṅkami;\
Tato ādāsamādāya, sarīraṁ paccavekkhisaṁ.**

**Tuccho kāyo adissittha, andhakāro tamo byagā;\
Sabbe coḷā samucchinnā, natthi dāni punabbhavo.**

\"My hair is going to be shaved off (*kese me olikhissanti*),\" the barber approached (*kappako upasaṅkami*); then (*tato*), taking up a mirror (*ādāsamādāya* \[ādāsaṁ + ādāya\]), I looked upon and inspected (*paccavekkhisaṁ*) \[my\] body (*sarīraṁ*). The physical body (*kāyo*) appeared (*adissittha*) empty (*tuccho*), the dense darkness of delusion vanished (*andhakāro tamo byagā*); all inner knots of defilement were completely severed (*sabbe coḷā samucchinnā*), now (*dāni*) there is no more (*natthi*) worldly becoming again (*puna-bhavo*).

**Commentary**: Mechanism of Śramaṇa Liberation: the direct, unvarnished seeing of physical body for what it truly is acts as the immediate catalyst for total unbinding.

**1. The Catalyst: Direct Insight into the Empty Body (*Tuccho Kāyo*):** The entire process begins with a singular moment of unyielding seeing. When looking into the mirror, the physical body (*sarīraṁ / kāyo*) is recognized as *tuccho*---empty, hollow, worthless, and utterly subject to decay, *dukkha*. The physical body is merely a manifest of soul-spirit in this physical world. Recognizing its inherently hollow nature instantly shatters the delusion of bodily identity and attachment.

**2. The Shattering of Ignorance (*Andhakāro Tamo Byagā*):** The moment the mind sees the physical form as *tuccho*, the root cause of worldly bondage to the world---ignorance (*moha / tamas*)---is instantly pierced:

- **The Bondage:** The true tie to the world is not the physical flesh itself, but the *citta\'s* craving, longing, and attachment (*taṇhā*) directed toward the physical form.

- **The Severing:** Direct vision (*paccavekkhaṇa*) acts as light cutting through darkness (*andhakāro tamo byagā*). The illusion that sensory existence holds any permanent value or shelter collapses completely.

**3. Total Severing of Defilement Knots (*Sabbe Coḷā Samucchinnā*):** With the death of ignorance and bodily craving, all internal knots of defilement (*coḷā*) are completely severed (*samucchinnā*). By stopping the influx of craving through continuous sense-restraint (*saṃvara*) and Concentration (*Samadhi*), the practitioner accomplishes the complete cessation of craving and clinging, and the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. The soul-spirit (*attā*), having disenchanted all physical structures, stands fully liberated from worldly bondage.

**4. The Determination of Non-Becoming (*Natthi Dāni Punabbhavo*):** This wisdom leads directly to the absolute realization: **this will be the final physical body**. Because the fuel of bodily craving has been utterly consumed, there remains no driving force for future worldly manifestation (*bhava*). The *citta* disenchants the world (*loka*), leaves it behind, and realizes the permanent ending of involuntary reincarnation (*natthi dāni punabbhavo*).

#### 2.26. Puṇṇamāsa (2nd)

**Pañca nīvaraṇe hitvā, yogakkhemassa pattiyā;\
Dhammādāsaṁ gahetvāna, ñāṇadassanamattano.**

**Paccavekkhiṁ imaṁ kāyaṁ, sabbaṁ santarabāhiraṁ;\
Ajjhattañca bahiddhā ca, tuccho kāyo adissathā.**

Having abandoned (*hitvā*) the five hindrances (*pañca nīvaraṇe*), for the attainment (*pattiyā*) of security from yoke / saṃsāra (*yogakkhemassa*); taking up (*gahetvāna*) the mirror of Dhamma (*dhammādāsaṁ*), the knowledge and vision of the soul-spirit (*ñāṇa-dassanam-attano*). I inspected (*paccavekkhiṁ*) this body (*imaṁ kāyaṁ*), entirely inside and out (*sabbaṁ santarabāhiraṁ*); pertaining to the inner soul-spirit (*ajjh-atta*) and external physical form (*bahiddhā ca*), the body appeared empty (*tuccho kāyo adissathā*).

**Commentary**: When inspecting the body *sabbaṁ santarabāhiraṁ* (entirely inside and out), the practitioner examines it from the vantage point of the inner soul-spirit (*ajjh-atta*) looking outward at the physical form (*bahiddhā*).

Seen from the quiet stance of the soul-spirit established in samādhi, the physical body is revealed to be **empty** (*tuccho*)---a temporary physical construct, outwardly manifested (*bhava*) from the inner soul-spirit (*ajjh-atta*), it belongs to the world (*loka*). The *citta* disenchants the physical frame, lets it fall away, and remains anchored on its own unassailable island (*atta-dīpa*).

#### 2.27. Nandaka (1st)

**Yathāpi bhaddo ājañño, khalitvā patitiṭṭhati;\
Bhiyyo laddhāna saṁvegaṁ, adīno vahate dhuraṁ.**

**Evaṁ dassanasampannaṁ, sammāsambuddhasāvakaṁ;\
Ājānīyaṁ maṁ dhāretha, puttaṁ buddhassa orasan.**

Just as (*yathāpi*) a noble horse of fine pedigree (*bhaddo ājañño*), having tripped (*khalitvā*), stands firm again (*patitiṭṭhati*); gaining (*laddhāna*) even greater (*bhiyyo*) spiritual urgency (*saṁvegaṁ*), resolute (*adīno*), he bears (*vahate*) responsibility (*dhuraṁ*). Even so (*evaṁ*), a disciple of the Rightly and Fully Enlightened One (*sammā-sambuddha-sāvakaṁ*), endowed with direct vision of truth (*dassana-sampannaṁ*); consider (*dhāretha*) me (*maṁ*) as a fine pedigree noble practitioner (*ājānīyaṁ*), a breast/heart-born (true-born) child (*puttaṁ orasaṁ*) of the Buddha (*buddhassa*).

**Commentary**: *puttaṁ orasaṁ:* Born from the breast/heart, legitimate, true-born (orasika / aurasa, from uras \[breast/chest\]), a standard, formal idiom in ancient India---used across classical legal texts, epics, and religious literature. In ancient India, saying someone was \"heart-born\" (aurasa / orasa) was not a romantic poetic phrase; it was a formal designation of direct, legitimate lineage and true spiritual or biological inheritance.

**1. Philological Derivation: Root Noun:** *Uras* (Sanskrit/Pāḷi) literally means **chest, breast, or heart-region**. **Derivative Forms:**

- *Uras-ja* (*uras* + *ja* \[born\]) = \"born of the chest/breast.\"

- *Aurasa* (Sanskrit) / *Orasa* (Pāḷi) = \"legitimate,\" \"breast-born,\" or \"heart-born.\"

In ancient Indian physiology and poetic idiom, the chest/breast (*uras*) was viewed as the physical seat of deep emotion, lineage, and life force.

**2. Usage in Ancient Indian Legal & Social Contexts**

In ancient Indian law codes (*Dharmaśāstras*, such as the *Manusmṛti*), **aurasa** was the formal legal classification for a **legitimate, natural-born child** (born of one\'s own body/breast), as opposed to adopted children (*dattaka*) or children acquired through other means: **Manusmṛti 9.158--159:** Distinguishes the *aurasa* (the direct, legitimate son born from one\'s own chest/lineage) as the primary heir over eleven other legal categories of sons.

**3. Usage in the Śramaṇa and Buddhist Canon**

In the Pāḷi literature---such as the verse analyzed earlier (**puttaṁ buddhassa orasaṁ**)---the term is used as a technical epithet for an Arahant or true disciple:

- **Meaning:** \"A legitimate child of the Buddha, born from his breast/heart.\"

- **Context:** It signifies that the disciple was not merely a casual follower, but was directly generated through the spoken Dhamma arising from the Master\'s heart-breast.

Aṅguttara Nikāya (AN 7.70 / AN 10.60, also preserved in the Itivuttaka and Samyutta Nikāya): *Tumhe kho me, bhikkhave, puttā orasā mukhato jātā dhammajā dhammanimmitā dhammadāyādā, no āmisadāyādā. Tasmātiha, bhikkhave, dhammadāyādā me bhavatha, mā āmisadāyādā. Atthi me tumhesu anukampā: 'Kinti me sāvaka dhammadāyādā bhaveyyuṁ, no āmisadāyādā'ti.*

O bhikkhus (*bhikkhave*), you indeed (*tumhe kho*) are my (*me*) children born of my chest/heart (*puttā orasā*), born from my mouth (*mukhato jātā*), born of the Dhamma (*dhammajā*), created by the Dhamma (*dhammanimmitā*), heirs to the Dhamma (*dhammadāyādā*), not heirs to material things (*no āmisadāyādā*).

Therefore (*tasmātiha*), O bhikkhus (*bhikkhave*), be my heirs to the Dhamma (*dhammadāyādā me bhavatha*), not heirs to material things (*mā āmisadāyādā*). I have compassion for you (*atthi me tumhesu anukampā*): 'How can my disciples be heirs to the Dhamma (*kinti me sāvaka dhammadāyādā bhaveyyuṁ*), and not heirs to material things (*no āmisadāyādā*)?'

**Heart/Breast-Born on Father\'s Side vs. Womb-Born on Mother\'s Side**

**1. The Father\'s Side: *Aurasa* / *Orasa* (Heart/Breast-Born)**

- **Derivation:** *Uras* (chest, breast, heart) + suffix *-ja* / *-a*.

- **Perspective:** The father.

- **Mechanism:** In ancient Indian cultural and legal frameworks, the father\'s seed and vital force (*vīriya*) were held to originate in his heart/breast before being transmitted. Thus, a natural, legitimate child carrying the father\'s direct bloodline and lineage was termed **aurasa** (Pāḷi: *orasa*)---literally \"born from the father\'s chest/heart.\"

**2. The Mother\'s Side: *Gabbhaja* / *Garbhaja* (Womb-Born)**

- **Derivation:** *Gabbha* (Sanskrit: *garbha*, womb/embryo) + *ja* (born).

- **Perspective:** The mother.

- **Mechanism:** When describing the maternal origin or physical gestation, ancient texts use **gabbhaja** (\"womb-born\") or **kucchija** (*kuchi* = belly/womb).

**The Doctrinal Shift in the Śramaṇa Framework:** When the Pāḷi verses declare an Arahant to be **puttaṁ buddhassa orasaṁ** (\"the legitimate, heart-born child of the Buddha\"), it purposefully uses the paternal legal idiom:

- **Spiritual Lineage:** The Buddha is the spiritual father (*dhamma-rājā*).

- **Mechanism of Birth:** The disciple is not born from a physical womb (*gabbha*), but is born directly from the spoken Dhamma arising from the Buddha\'s heart/breast (*uras*).

Through hearing the Dhamma, cultivating *Samadhi*, and realizing that the empty body (*tuccho kāyo*) is merely a physical husk, manifested from the soul-spirit, belonging to this world (*loka*), the practitioner leaves behind physical womb-birth (*gabbhaseyya*) entirely, severs all ties to involuntary reincarnation (*punabbhava*), and attains complete liberation from worldly bondage.

**Arahanism on Patriarch**

Ancient **Āriyan** (Vedic and early Śramaṇa) society, lineage, property, and social identity were strictly established through the paternal line (*pitṛ-pakṣa*). The foundational framework rested entirely on male ancestry, male continuity, and patriarchal inheritance.

**1. Core Terminology of the Āriya Paternal Framework**

**A. Pitṛ-bhūmi / Pitru-bhūmi (Father\'s Land)**

- **Meaning:** Fatherland or ancestral homeland.

- **Context:** Land (*bhūmi*) and territory were owned and handed down through generations of male heads of the household (*gṛhapati* / *gahapati*). Claiming rights to a region meant asserting connection to the soil of one\'s fathers (*pitṛ*).

**B. Pitṛ-gotra & Pitṛ-vaṃsa (Father\'s Lineage & Clan)**

- **Meaning:** The paternal lineage (*vaṃsa*) and paternal clan (*gotra*).

- **Context:** A child's official status, social tier (*vaṇṇa*), and ritual rights were determined solely by the father's *gotra*. Upon marriage, a woman formally transitioned out of her father\'s *gotra* and was integrated into her husband\'s family structure.

**C. Pitṛ-dāya (Paternal Inheritance)**

- **Meaning:** Legacy, wealth, or transmission handed down from the father.

- **Context:** Ancestral wealth, sacred duties, and family property were passed from father to son (*putta*). This is why a direct, legitimate male heir was legally designated as **aurasa** (*orasa*)---born directly from the father\'s chest/heart.

**2. The Ancestral Duty (*Pitṛ-yajña*)**

In the traditional Āriya worldview, the continuation of the male line was tied to metaphysical duty:

- **Ancestral Rites:** Only a direct male descendant could offer funeral oblations and ancestral rites (*śrāddha* / *pinda*) to the departed fathers (*pitṛs*).

- **Continuity of the Line:** Failing to produce a male heir was viewed as breaking the chain of ancestral support, stranding the fathers in the ancestral realm (*pitṛ-loka*).

**3. The Śramaṇa Transmutation of the Paternal Model**

The early Śramaṇa traditions (such as early Jainism and early Pāḷi Canon) operated within this cultural matrix, but transformed the concept of \"father\'s lineage\" from a biological and worldly structure into a purely spiritual one:

**A. Renouncing the Physical Father\'s Lineage**

The practitioner (*bhikkhu* / *samaṇa*) leaves behind the biological household, relinquishing the physical father's land (*pitṛ-bhūmi*), wealth, and social clan (*gotra*).

**B. Entering the Lineage of the Āriyas (*Āriya-vaṃsa*) -- Change of Lineage (Gotrabhu)**

Upon attainment of spiritual fruits, the practitioner enters a new lineage: the **Āriya-vaṃsa** (the noble lineage of the Noble Ones).

**C. The Buddha as the Spiritual Father**

The Tathāgata takes on the role of the ultimate spiritual father (*dhamma-rājā*). The true disciple is no longer defined by physical womb-birth (*gabbhaja*), but is declared to be **putto buddhassa oraso**---a legitimate child born directly from the spiritual father's heart/breast through the realization of the Dhamma.

By cutting off attachment to physical lineage, ancestral lands, and worldly identity, the practitioner recognizes that all physical families and worldly constructs belong to this physical world (*loka*). The purified *citta* disenchants them, leaves behind the cycle of involuntary reincarnation (*punabbhava*), and realizes ultimate liberation from worldly bondage.

In the Arahanism cosmological order, the patriarchal structure represents the Brahma-lineage of the cosmic *manussa/mankind* system, directly contrasting with the broader, cosmic matriarchal architecture of the universe. Human race on earth as hybridized descendent of the *manussa*, can choose its own alignment, this is according to the law of free will.

#### 2.28. Bharata

**Ehi nandaka gacchāma, upajjhāyassa santikaṁ;\
Sīhanādaṁ nadissāma, buddhaseṭṭhassa sammukhā.**

**Yāya no anukampāya, amhe pabbājayī muni;\
So no attho anuppatto, sabbasaṁyojanakkhayo.**

Come (*ehi*), O Nandaka (*nandaka*), let us go (*gacchāma*) to the presence (*santikaṁ*) of our preceptor (*upajjhāyassa*); we shall roar (*nadissāma*) the lion's roar (*sīhanādaṁ*) right in the presence (*sammukhā*) of the Supreme Buddha (*buddhaseṭṭhassa*). Through whichever (*yāya*) compassion (*anukampāya*) for us (*no*) the *muni* led us into renunciation (*amhe pabbājayī*); that (*so*) goal (*attho*) of ours (*no*) has been fully attained (*anuppatto*): the complete destruction of all fetters (*sabbasaṁyojanakkhayo*).

#### 2.29. Bhāradvāja

**Nadanti evaṁ sappaññā, sīhāva girigabbhare;\
Vīrā vijitasaṅgāmā, jetvā māraṁ savāhiniṁ.**

**Satthā ca pariciṇṇo me, dhammo saṅgho ca pūjito;\
Ahañca vitto sumano, puttaṁ disvā anāsavan.**

Thus (*evaṁ*) roar (*nadanti*) those endowed with wisdom (*sappaññā*), like lions (*sīhāva \[sīhā + iva\]*) in a mountain cavern (*girigabbhare*); heroes (*vīrā*) who have conquered the battlefield (*vijitasaṅgāmā*), having vanquished (*jetvā*) Māra (*māraṁ*) together with his army (*savāhiniṁ*).
