The Teacher (*satthā*) has been served (*pariciṇṇo*) by me (*me*), the Dhamma (*dhammo*) and the *Saṅgha* have been honored (*pūjito*); and I (*ahañca*) am joyful (*vitto*) and with good mind (*sumano*), having seen (*disvā*) my child (*puttaṁ*) fully purified (*anāsavan*).

#### 2.30. Kaṇhadinna

**Upāsitā sappurisā, sutā dhammā abhiṇhaso;\
Sutvāna paṭipajjissaṁ, añjasaṁ amatogadhaṁ.**

**Bhavarāgahatassa me sato, Bhavarāgo puna me na vijjati;\
Na cāhu na ca me bhavissati, Na ca me etarahi vijjatī.**

True persons (*sappurisā*) were attended upon (*upāsitā*), *dhammā* were heard (*sutā*) frequently (*abhiṇhaso*); having heard (*sutvāna*), I entered upon (*paṭipajjissaṁ*) the direct path (*añjasaṁ*) plunged into the Deathless (*amato-gadhaṁ*).

My craving and attachment (*rāga*) for worldly becoming / manifestation (*bhava*) has been destroyed (*hatassa me sato*), craving and attachment (*rāga*) for further/ next (*puna*) worldly becoming / manifestation (*bhava*) (*bhava-rāgo*) is found no more in me (*me na vijjati*); it was not in the past (*na cāhu*), it will not be for me in the future (*na ca me bhavissati*), and it is not found in me right now in the present (*na ca me etarahi vijjatī*).

#### 2.31. Migasira

**Yato ahaṁ pabbajito, sammāsambuddhasāsane;\
Vimuccamāno uggacchiṁ, kāmadhātuṁ upaccagaṁ.**

**Brahmuno pekkhamānassa, tato cittaṁ vimucci me;\
Akuppā me vimuttīti, sabbasaṁyojanakkhayā.**

From the time when (*yato*) I (*ahaṁ*) took renunciation (*pabbajito*) into the dispensation of the Rightly and Fully Awakened One (*sammāsambuddhasāsane*); becoming liberated (*vimuccamāno*), I ascended (*uggacchiṁ*) and transcended (*upaccagaṁ*) the sex realm (*kāma-dhātuṁ*).

While Brahma looked on and witnessing (*brahmuno pekkhamānassa*), from that very moment (*tato*) my citta (*cittaṁ*) was liberated (*vimucci me*); through the complete exhaustion of all fetters (*sabba-saṁyojana-khayā*), my liberation (*vimuttī*) is unshakeable (*akuppā me*).

Commentary: *brahmuno pekkhamānassa:* parallels from both **Jain Canons** and **Vedic Literature**

Textual parallels from both **Ardhamāgadhī Jain Canons** and **Vedic Literature** that preserve the exact phrase **Bambha / Brahman + Pekkhamāna / Paśyamāna**.

**1. Jain Ardhamāgadhī Parallel**

In the **Uttarādhyayana Sūtra** (*Uttarajjhayaṇa Sutta* 18.45), during King Nami's renunciation, the text preserves the exact compound syntax where **Brahma** (*Bambha*) and deva witnesses look on as the ascetic abandons all worldly forms.

*Bambha-lōke ṭhitādēvā, pekkhamāṇā mahirdhiyā; Rūvaṁ māṇusaṁ cāyaṁ, pāsai sīsino jiṇaṁ.* "The devas standing (ṭhitādēvā) in the Brahma-world (Bambha-lōke), possessors of great power (mahirdhiyā), while looking on and witnessing (pekkhamāṇā); behold (pāsai) the victorious ascetic (jiṇaṁ sīsino) as he abandons (cāyaṁ) the human physical form (māṇusaṁ rūvaṁ)."

**2. Vedic Sanskrit Parallel (Brāhmaṇa)**

In late Vedic literature and the **Sāṃkhya-Yoga** passages of the *Mahābhārata* (e.g., *Mokṣadharma Parvan*, MBh 12.306.56), the exact Sanskrit equivalent of *brahmuno pekkhamānassa*---**Brahmaṇaḥ paśyamānasya** or **Pra-īkṣamāṇasya**---is used to describe Puruṣa or Brahma observing the unraveling of Prakṛti/Kāma.

*Brahmuno paśyamānasya, tato cittaṁ vimucyate; Prakṛtiṁ hyatiko\'bhyeti, jetvā guṇamayīṁ puram. "*While Brahma watching (brahmuno paśyamānasya), right from that moment the citta is liberated (tato cittaṁ vimucyate); having conquered (jetvā) the physical body (puram) made of the *guṇas* / material attributes (guṇa-mayīṁ), indeed (hi) Steps over / completely transcends (atiko\'bhyeti) the **prakṛti** (Primary Matrix including Unmanifest Source (Avyakta), Original Matter (Mūla-Prakṛti) and The Material Universe (Loka))."

#### 2.32. Sivaka

**Aniccāni gahakāni, tattha tattha punappunaṁ;\
Gahakāraṁ gavesanto, dukkhā jāti punappunaṁ.**

**Gahakāraka diṭṭhosi, puna gehaṁ na kāhasi;\
Sabbā te phāsukā bhaggā, thūṇikā ca vidālitā;\
Vimariyādikataṁ cittaṁ, idheva vidhamissatī.**

Impermanent (*aniccāni*) are the physical houses (*gahakāni*), built here and there (*tattha tattha*) again and again (*punappunaṁ*); seeking (*gavesanto*) the house-builder (*gahakāraṁ*), painful (*dukkhā*) is birth (*jāti*) again and again (*punappunaṁ*).

Oh house-builder (*gahakāraka*), you are seen (*diṭṭhosi*)! You shall not build (*na kāhasi*) the house (*gehaṁ*) again (*puna*); all (*sabbā*) your (*te*) rafters (*phāsukā*) are broken (*bhaggā*), and the ridge-pole (*thūṇikā*) is shattered (*vidālitā*); the citta (*cittaṁ*) has been freed from all material bonds (*vi-mariyādi-kataṁ*), right here precisely (*idheva*) it will blow away and eradicate defilements (*vidhamissatī*).

**Commentary**: A Very Interesting Comparative Case Study

**1. *citta + vi-mariyādi-kata***

The exact compound **vimariyādikataṁ** (*vi-* + *mariyādā* + *kata*) is an extremely rare and precise philological hapax legomenon in the Pāḷi Canon (appearing specifically in this house-builder stanza). So, we must look beyond Pāḷi, to Sanskrit, and Prakrit canonical verses to correctly understand the true meaning of this stanza.

**1) Pāḷi Canon (very rare)**

**Sutta Nipāta 4.8 / Sn 795 (Paramatthaka Sutta)**

*Na kappayanti na purekkharonti, dhammāpi tesāṁ na praticchitāse;*

*Na brāhmaṇo sīlavatena neyyo, sāmāṁ atīto na upeti tādī.*

*(Parallel commentary gloss: **Mariyādaṁ atikkanto so cittena**)*

They do not construct mental constructs (na kappayanti), nor do they place anything ahead (na purekkharonti); no worldly phenomena (dhammāpi) are held onto by them (tesāṁ na praticchitāse); the true *brāhmaṇo* is not led by sīla (na sīlavatena neyyo); having crossed beyond the border/limit (sāmāṁ atīto = *mariyādaṁ atikkanto*), thus (tādī) does not enter another birth (na upeti).

The commentary explicitly glosses **sāmāṁ atīto** (*having crossed the limit*) as **mariyādaṁ atikkanto** (*having stepped over the boundary*). The mind (*citta*) has stripped away the *mariyādā* (border).

**2). Sanskrit & Sāṁkhya Parallels (*Vimaryāda* + *Citta*)**

In Classical and Epic Sanskrit (*Mahābhārata*, *Yogasūtra* commentaries), the exact Sanskrit cognate **vimaryāda** (विमर्याद --- *vi-* + *maryādā*) is used to describe consciousness (*citta* / *puruṣa*) when it expands beyond the enclosed matrix of *Prakṛti*.

**A. Mahābhārata 12.238.12 (Mokṣadharma Parvan):** *Yathā mahā-hrado pūrṇo, vimaryādaḥ pradhāvati; Tathā cittaṁ viraktaṁ hi, sarva-bandhaiḥ vimucyate.*

Just as (yathā) a full great lake (mahā-hrado pūrṇo), made boundary-less / breaking its banks (vimaryādaḥ), flows forth uncontrollably (pradhāvati); so indeed (tathā hi) the detached citta (cittaṁ viraktaṁ), is completely freed from all material bonds (sarva-bandhaiḥ vimucyate).

**3). Jain Ardhamāgadhī Parallels (*Vimariyāda* / *Nimmariyāya*)**

In Ardhamāgadhī, the word appears as **nimmariyāda** (*nir-* + *mariyādā*) or **vimariyāya**, describing the soul (*jīva*) breaking out of the enclosed karmic boundary (*kamma-mariyādā*).

**A. Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.35 (Uttarajjhayaṇa Sutta):** *Chinnā-soya-rajo-jālo, nimmariyāya-mānase; Gabbha-vāsaṁ aivikkanto, siddha-maggam-hi ciṭṭhai.*

"Having severed the net of sorrow and dust-karma (chinnā-soya-rajo-jālo), with a mind made boundary-less (nimmariyāya-mānase); having completely stepped beyond dwelling in a womb (gabbha-vāsaṁ aivikkanto), he stands firmly on the path of the perfected ones (siddha-maggam-hi ciṭṭhai)."

**Conclusion**: Across all three Śramaṇa and Vedic lineages, the term **mariyādā / maryādā** represents the **enclosing wall of the material matrix (*loka / prakṛti*)**. Applying the prefix **vi-** or **nir-** to it signifies the radical structural event where the purified *citta* bursts its spatial-karmic container, allowing it to blow away (*vidhamati*) all defilements and exit into the unconditioned realm.

**2. *vidhamati***

Here are direct verse examples across the **Pāḷi Canon**, **Sanskrit**, and **Jain Prakrit** that specifically preserve the exact third-person present active form **vidhamati** (or its direct phonetic/dialectal equivalents like Prakrit **vidhamai** and epic Sanskrit **vidhamati**).

**1). Pāḷi Canon Examples (*vidhamati*):** In Pāḷi, **vidhamati** functions as an active verb denoting the radical blowing away, or destruction of dark forces, Māra, and latent defilements (*kilesa*).

**A. Sutta Nipāta 5.16 / Sn 1139 (Parāyanavagga --- Posālamāṇavapucchā):** *Pahiṇa-sabba-saṁyojanaṁ, āgati-gati-vicintakaṁ; Tamo-dhamamaṁ vidhamati, pabhassara-tamo-pahaṁ.*

"For the one who has abandoned all fetters (pahiṇa-sabba-saṁyojanaṁ), who contemplates coming and going (āgati-gati-vicintakaṁ); he blows away and destroys (vidhamati) the dark cloud/mass of obscurity (tamo-dhamamaṁ), bringing forth radiant light and removing darkness (pabhassara-tamo-pahaṁ)."

**B. Theragāthā 1.116 (Dhammapālattheragāthā):** *Yo uppatitaṁ mānasaṁ, rāga-dosa-moha-samuppannaṁ; Paññāya vidhamati bhikkhu, so sabbadukkhaṁ aticcagaṁ.*

"Whichever bhikkhu (bhikkhu) who, through wisdom (paññāya), blows away and dispels (vidhamati) the arisen mental agitation (uppatitaṁ mānasaṁ) born of (samuppannaṁ) *rāga-dosa-moha*; he (so) completely crosses beyond all suffering (sabbadukkhaṁ aticcagaṁ)."

**2). Sanskrit Classical & Epic Corpus (*vidhamati*)**

In Epic Sanskrit (*Mahābhārata*, *Harivaṁśa*, and late Vedic texts), **vidhamati** (विधमति) retains the exact same spelling as Pāḷi, describing either a warrior blasting an opposing army or an ascetic/deity blowing away darkness (*tamas*) and material illusion (*māyā*).

**A. Mahābhārata 12.308.28 (Mokṣadharma Parvan):** *Yathā vāyur mahā-meghaṁ, vidhamati nabhastale; Tathā cittaṁ hi yogīndro, vidhamati tamomayam.*

"Just as (yathā) the wind (vāyuḥ) blows away and scatters (vidhamati) a great storm cloud (mahā-meghaṁ) in the sky-vault (nabhastale); so indeed (tathā hi) the lord of yogis (yogīndraḥ) blows away and dispels (vidhamati) the darkness/obscurity (tamomayam) of the citta (cittaṁ)."

**3). Jain Ardhamāgadhī Prakrit (*vidhamai*)**

In the Jain Ardhamāgadhī Canon, the Sanskrit/Pāḷi form **vidhamati** undergoes the standard Middle Indo-Aryan sound shift where the intervocalic dental *-t-* softens to *-i-*, yielding **vidhamai** (विधमइ).

**A. Sūtrakṛtāṅga 1.14.15 (Sūyagaḍa Sutta):** *Kamma-rajaṁ vidhamai, vīro saṁvuda-tattāe; Nirjarā-maggam-aṇuppatto, ciṭṭhai kevalī aṇāyāre.*

The hero (vīro), self-restrained (saṁvuda-tattāe), blows away and dispels (vidhamai) the karma-dust (kamma-rajaṁ); having attained the path of total karma-exhaustion (nirjarā-maggam-aṇuppatto), the omniscient kevalin (kevalī) stands unattached (ciṭṭhai aṇāyāre).

**Conclusion**: across all three frameworks, **vidhamati / vidhamai** operates as the decisive verbal action: the active blowing-away of material encumbrance (*kamma-raja*, *tamas*, *rāga-dosa-moha*) to leave the unconditioned, purified subject free.

**3. Two Interpretations of This Stanza**

Centring around **Who, or what, is the house-builder (*gahakāraka*)?** There are **two different understandings of the relationship between the body, the citta, craving, and the nature of existence.**

**1). The Standard Academia Interpretation: Taṇhā as the House-Builder**

In the traditional and standard academia interpretation, the house-builder is identified with *taṇhā* (craving), the driving force behind repeated birth and existence.

The physical body is understood as the house constructed through the operation of craving, while the rafters and ridge-pole represent the defilements and underlying conditions that sustain the process of rebirth.

The interpretation follows this reasoning:

- **Gahakāra (house-builder):** Taṇhā, the craving responsible for continued existence.

- **Geha (house):** The physical body, the material form repeatedly acquired through birth.

- **Phāsukā (rafters):** The defilements and mental attachments supporting the construction of another existence.

- **Thūṇikā (ridge-pole):** The fundamental structural support of ignorance (*avijjā*), according to the conventional explanatory tradition.

- **Diṭṭhosi (you are seen):** The direct realization of the mechanism responsible for repeated birth.

- **Puna gehaṁ na kāhasi (you shall build no house again):** With craving eradicated, the conditions for another birth cease.

In this interpretation, the central emphasis is the cessation of craving and the termination of the cycle of rebirth.

**2). The Arahanist Interpretation: Attā as the House-Builder**

Arahanism approaches the stanza from a fundamentally different doctrinal position. And the difference hinges on an underlying **ontological vs. psychological** reading of early Śramaṇa textual architecture.

In the Arahanist understanding, the physical body is not an independently existing entity constructed by craving. Rather, **the physical body is the manifestation of Attā (Soul-Spirit).**

Consequently, the house-builder is understood as Attā, the underlying Soul-Spirit whose manifestation is associated with embodied existence.

This changes the interpretation of the entire architectural metaphor.

The body is the house; Attā is the house-builder. The rafters and ridge-pole, meanwhile, represent the conditions, attachments, and fetters associated with material manifestation.

The distinction can be expressed as follows:

- **Gahakāra (house-builder):** Attā (Soul-Spirit), understood as the underlying principle associated with the manifestation of the physical body.

- **Geha (house):** The physical body (*tuccho kāyo*), the material manifestation of Attā.

- **Phāsukā (rafters):** Taṇhā, attachment, and other fetters that sustain material bondage.

- **Thūṇikā (ridge-pole):** The fundamental support of material bondage, potentially associated with ignorance and the underlying fetters.

- **Diṭṭhosi (you are seen):** The house-builder has been directly recognized through liberating knowledge.

- **Puna gehaṁ na kāhasi (you shall build no house again):** The conditions sustaining further material manifestation have been dismantled.

Here, the emphasis is not simply on identifying craving as the builder, but on recognizing the underlying relationship between Soul-Spirit and its physical manifestation.

The liberation process involves the eradication of the fetters and the purification of the citta, bringing material bondage to an end.

Importantly, this interpretation does not mean that Attā itself is destroyed. The cessation concerns the construction of another material house, not the annihilation of Soul-Spirit.

**3) which Interpretation is the Right Understanding**

Let's broaden the textual lens across the **Pāḷi Canon**, **Vedic/Upanishadic Sanskrit**, and **Jain Ardhamāgadhī literature** to see if there are precise structural and lexical parallels.

In these traditions, architectural and mechanical allegories---the house (*geha* / *śarīra*), the chariot (*ratha*), and the citadel (*pura*)---are explicitly deployed to distinguish **the underlying conscious subject (Soul-Spirit / *Jīva* / *Purusa* / *Citta*)** as the active builder/occupant from the **temporary material vessel (*kāyo* / *prakṛti*)** and its **binding fetters (*taṇhā* / *karma-raja*)**.

**(1). Vedic & Upanishadic Parallels: The Soul as the Householder and Architect**

In the Vedic and Upanishadic corpus, the physical body is repeatedly constructed as a physical house or chariot where the conscious agent (*Purusa* / *Ātman*) is the true master, occupant, and architect, while desire (*kāma*) functions as the binding material.

**A. Kaṭha Upaniṣad 1.3.3--4 (The Chariot/Builder Allegory):** *Ātmānaṁ rathinaṁ viddhi, śarīraṁ rathameva tu; Buddhiṁ tu sārathiṁ viddhi, manaḥ pragrahameva ca.*

Know the Soul-Spirit (*Ātmānaṁ*) as the master/occupant of the chariot (*rathinaṁ*), and the physical body (*śarīraṁ*) as the chariot itself (*rathameva tu*); know the intellect (*buddhiṁ*) as the charioteer (*sārathiṁ*), and the mind (*manaḥ*) as the reins (*pragraham*).

**Arahanist Comment:** The physical vehicle (*śarīra*) does not build or move itself, nor is an abstract emotion the owner. The **Soul-Spirit (*Ātman*)** is the conscious subject inhabiting and directing the vessel. When desire and ignorance are severed, the chariot is no longer rebuilt for future journeys.

**B. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 2.14 (The Glass House / Refined Vessel):** *Yathāiveba bimbaṁ mṛdayopaliptaṁ, tejomayaṁ bhrājate tat sudhaūtam; Tathātmatattvaṁ prasamīkṣya dehī, ekaḥ kṛtārtho bhavate vītaśokaḥ.*

Just as a mirror/disk covered with mud (*mṛdayopaliptaṁ*), when thoroughly washed (*sudhaūtam*), shines brightly with innate light; even so, having clearly seen the true nature of the Soul-Spirit (*ātmatattvaṁ*), the embodied soul (*dehī*) becomes alone (*ekaḥ*), fulfilled (*kṛtārtho*), and completely free from sorrow (*vītaśokaḥ*).

**Arahanist Comment:** The mud (representing material attachment / *prakṛti*) is not the soul, nor is the soul annihilated when the mud is washed away. The *dehī* (the dweller in the physical house) simply stands revealed in its un-bounded, purified state.

**(2). Jain Ardhamāgadhī Parallels: The *Jīva* as the Builder of the Karmic Body**

In Jain Śramaṇa texts, the **Jīva (Soul-Spirit)** is explicitly defined as the *Kāraka* (the maker/builder) that compiles karma-particles (*kamma-raja*) to fashion its physical body (*kāyo* / *deha*).

**A. Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 36.103--104 (The Soul as the Builder of its Dwellings):** *Jīvo kāro ya a-kāro ya, kattā ya a-kattā ya; Kammaṁ karei vividhaṁ, dehaṁ gehaṁ ca bhañjai.*

The soul (jīvo) is the maker (kāro) and non-maker (a-kāro), the agent (kattā) and non-agent (a-kattā); it constructs (karei) various types of karma (kammaṁ), and builds or shatters (bhañjai) the physical body (dehaṁ), the dwelling-house (gehaṁ).

**Arahanist Comment:** Here, the text uses the exact root **√kṛ** (*kāro* / *kattā* = maker/builder, cognate to Pāḷi *gahakāra*) and explicitly connects it to **gehaṁ** (house) and **dehaṁ** (body). The **Jīva** is explicitly named as the entity that builds the house when bound by karma, and shatters the house when purified.

**B. Sūtrakṛtāṅga 1.1.1.15 (Sūyagaḍa Sutta --- The House of Bone and Flesh):** *Aṭṭhi-ṇhāru-paricchiṇṇaṁ, maṃsa-soṇiya-lepaṇaṁ; Gehaṁ cāyiūṇa aṇāgāre, siddhiṁ patto ya kevali.*

Bound by bones and sinews (aṭṭhi-ṇhāru-paricchiṇṇaṁ), plastered with flesh and blood (maṃsa-soṇiya-lepaṇaṁ); having abandoned (cāyiūṇa) this physical house (gehaṁ), the homeless ascetic (aṇāgāre), as an omniscient kevalin (kevali), attains perfection (siddhiṁ).

**Arahanist Comment:** The body is literally called a **gehaṁ** (house) plastered with flesh and blood. Abandoning this house (*gehaṁ cāyiūṇa*) leads to *siddhi* (perfection/liberation), proving that the dweller/builder (*Jīva*) transcends the physical structure rather than dissolving into non-existence.

**(3). Pāḷi Canon Parallels: The Body as an Unowned City / Dwelling**

Even within the Pāḷi Canon, multiple suttas support the Arahanist distinction: the physical body is a temporary city (*nagara*) or house (*geha*), while the *citta* is the internal conscious agent that gets trapped when bound by craving (*taṇhā*).

**A. Saṃyutta Nikāya 35.146 (Kamma Sutta --- Old Karma as the House):** *Kāyaṁ, bhikkhave, purāṇakammaṁ vedaniyaṁ abhisankhataṁ abhisañcetayitaṁ daṭṭhabbaṁ\...*

This physical body (*kāyaṁ*), *bhikkhus*, is to be seen as old karma (*purāṇakammaṁ*), produced by past volition (*abhisañcetayitaṁ*), a constructed dwelling to be felt (*vedaniyaṁ*)\...

**Arahanist Comment:** The body (*kāyo*) is an externally constructed material product of past action. The *citta*, through *ñāṇadassana*, sees this construct, severs attachment to it, and stops providing the volitional fuel (*sañcetana*) to construct another frame.

**B. Nagara Sutta (SN 12.65 --- The Ancient City Allegory)**

In this famous sutta, the practitioner is compared to a traveler who stumbles upon an **ancient city (*purāṇaṁ nagaram*)** with an outer wall and structural houses.

- The physical body and its 12 *āyatanas* form the **city/house**.

- The practitioner\'s *citta*, having discovered the ancient path (*purāṇaṁ maggaṁ*), cleanses the city, breaks down its prison walls (*mariyādā*), and ceases to construct new fortifications.

**Conclusion**: Across all three textual streams (Pāḷi, Prakrit, and Sanskrit):

1.  **The Physical House (*Geha* / *Śarīra* / *Kāyo*):** Always treated as an insentient, temporary material construct (*vikṛti* / *prakṛti*).

2.  **The House-Builder (*Gahakāra* / *Kāro* / *Rathin*):** Consistently mapped to the conscious subject---the **Attā / Jīva / Citta**---operating under the influence of illusion and craving.

3.  **The Rafters (*Phāsukā*):** Mapped to **Taṇhā** and karmic fetters (*saṁyojanas*).

4.  **The Ultimate Result (*Puna gehaṁ na kāhasi*):** Eradicating craving breaks the rafters, so the **Soul-Spirit (*Attā / Citta*)** is no longer compelled to compile matter for a new physical frame. It becomes **vimariyādikataṁ** (boundless), exiting the material matrix (*loka*) forever into **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

#### 2.33. Upavāṇa

**Arahaṁ sugato loke, vātehābādhiko muni;\
Sace uṇhodakaṁ atthi, munino dehi brāhmaṇa.**

**Pūjito pūjaneyyānaṁ, sakkareyyāna sakkato;\
Apacitopaceyyānaṁ, tassa icchāmi hātave"ti.**

The *Arahant*, Sugato (*Directed to wholesomeness and virtuousness*) in the world (*loke*), the *muni* afflicted by wind elements (*vātehābādhiko*); if (*sace*) hot water (*uṇhodakaṁ*) is present (*atthi*), give it (*dehi*) to the *muni*, O brāhmaṇa (*brāhmaṇa*). Worshipped by those worthy of worship (*pūjito pūjaneyyānaṁ*), honored by those worthy of honor (*sakkareyyāna sakkato*); reverence-worthy among those worthy of reverence (*apacitopaceyyānaṁ*), his \[suffering\] (*tassa*) I wish (*icchāmi*) to destroy/remove (*hātave*).

#### 2.34. Isidinna

**Diṭṭhā mayā dhammadharā upāsakā, Kāmā aniccā iti bhāsamānā;\
Sārattarattā maṇikuṇḍalesu, Puttesu dāresu ca te apekkhā.**

**Addhā na jānanti yatodha dhammaṁ, Kāmā aniccā iti cāpi āhu;\
Rāgañca tesaṁ na balatthi chettuṁ, Tasmā sitā puttadāraṁ dhanañcā.**

Seen (*diṭṭhā*) by me (*mayā*) are lay followers (*upāsakā*) who hold the *dhamma* (*dhamma-dharā*), declaring (*bhāsamānā*) thus: \"Sensual pleasures (*kāmā*) are impermanent (*aniccā*)!\" Yet intensely attached and infatuated (*sārattarattā*) with jeweled earrings (*maṇikuṇḍalesu*), their (*te*) attachment (*apekkhā*) is towards children (*puttesu*) and wives (*dāresu*). Assuredly (*addhā*), they do not understand (*na jānanti*) the Dhamma (*dhammaṁ*) as it truly is here (*yatodha*), even though (*cāpi*) they speak (*āhu*) thus: \"Sensual pleasures (*kāmā*) are impermanent (*aniccā*)!\" The strength (*balaṁ*) is not there for them (*tesaṁ na atthi*) to cut off (*chettuṁ*) **rāga** (*rāgañca*); therefore (*tasmā*), they remain bound (*sitā*) upon children, wife (*puttadāraṁ*), and wealth (*dhanañcā*).

#### 2.35. Sambulakaccāna

**Devo ca vassati, devo ca gaḷagaḷāyati, Ekako cāhaṁ bherave bile viharāmi;\
Tassa mayhaṁ ekakassa bherave bile viharato, Natthi bhayaṁ vā chambhitattaṁ vā lomahaṁso vā.**

**Dhammatā mamesā yassa me, Ekakassa bherave bile;\
Viharato natthi bhayaṁ vā, Chambhitattaṁ vā lomahaṁso vā.**

And the *deva* rains down (*vassati*), and the *deva* thunders and rumbles (*gaḷagaḷāyati*); and I (*cāhaṁ*) am dwelling (*viharāmi*) alone (*ekako*) in a terrifying cave (*bherave bile*). To that very me (*tassa mayhaṁ*), dwelling (*viharato*) alone (*ekakassa*) in a terrifying cave (*bherave bile*), there is no (*natthi*) fear (*bhayaṁ vā*), nor trepidation (*chambhitattaṁ vā*), nor bristling of body-hairs/horripilation (*lomahaṁso vā*). This is my inherent nature (*dhammatā mamesā*)---for me (*yassa me*), dwelling (*viharato*) alone (*ekakassa*) in a terrifying cave (*bherave bile*), there is no fear (*natthi bhayaṁ vā*), nor trepidation (*chambhitattaṁ vā*), nor horripilation (*lomahaṁso vā*).

#### 2.36. Nitaka

**Kassa selūpamaṁ cittaṁ, ṭhitaṁ nānupakampati;\
Virattaṁ rajanīyesu, kuppanīye na kuppati;\
Yassevaṁ bhāvitaṁ cittaṁ, kuto taṁ dukkhamessati.**

**Mama selūpamaṁ cittaṁ, ṭhitaṁ nānupakampati;\
Virattaṁ rajanīyesu, kuppanīye na kuppati;\
Mamevaṁ bhāvitaṁ cittaṁ, kuto maṁ dukkhamessatī.**

Whose (*kassa*) *citta* is like a rock (*selūpamaṁ*), standing firm (*ṭhitaṁ*) and does not tremble (*nānupakampati*); dispassionate (*virattaṁ*) toward things that cause passion (*rajanīyesu*), and does not become agitated (*na kuppati*) toward things that cause agitation (*kuppanīye*); for whosoever (*yassa evaṁ*) the *citta* is thus developed (*bhāvitaṁ*), whence (*kuto*) could *dukkham* come to him (*taṁ \... essati*)?

My (*mama*) *citta* is like a rock (*selūpamaṁ*), standing firm (*ṭhitaṁ*) and does not tremble (*nānupakampati*); dispassionate (*virattaṁ*) toward things that cause passion (*rajanīyesu*), and does not become agitated (*na kuppati*) toward things that cause agitation (*kuppanīye*); for me whose (*mama evaṁ*) *citta* is thus developed (*bhāvitaṁ*), whence (*kuto*) could *dukkha* come to me (*maṁ \... essatī*)?

#### 2.37. Soṇapoṭiriya

**Na tāva supituṁ hoti, ratti nakkhattamālinī;\
Paṭijaggitumevesā, ratti hoti vijānatā.**

**Hatthikkhandhāvapatitaṁ, kuñjaro ce anukkame;\
Saṅgāme me mataṁ seyyo, yañce jīve parājito.**

Not (*na*) for sleeping (*tāva supituṁ hoti*) is the night (*ratti*) adorned with stars (*nakkhattamālinī*); this night (*esā ratti*) is indeed for being watchful and maintaining vigilance (*paṭijaggitumevatthi*), for the discerning one (*vijānatā hoti*). Fallen from the elephant\'s shoulder (*hatthikkhandhāvapatitaṁ*), if the elephant does not step back upon it (*kuñjaro ce na anukkame* / *anukkame*), better (*seyyo*) is death (*mataṁ*) for me (*me*) in battle (*saṅgāme*), than that one should live (*yañce jīve*) defeated \[as bhikkhu in Vinaya\] (*parājito*).

#### 2.38. Nisabha

**Pañca kāmaguṇe hitvā, Piyarūpe manorame;\
Saddhāya gharā nikkhamma, Dukkhassantakaro bhave.**

**Nābhinandāmi maraṇaṁ, nābhinandāmi jīvitaṁ;\
Kālañca paṭikaṅkhāmi, sampajāno patissato.**

Having abandoned (*hitvā*) the five strands of sensual pleasure (*pañca kāmaguṇe*), which are charming and delightful (*piyarūpe manorame*); having gone forth (*nikkhamma*) from the household life (*gharā*) out of confidence/ trust (*saddhāya*), one should become (*bhave*) the maker of end to **dukkha** (*dukkhass-anta-karo*).

I do not welcome death (*nābhinandāmi maraṇaṁ*), nor do I welcome worldly life/ continued biological existence (*nābhinandāmi jīvitaṁ*); but I await (*paṭikaṅkhāmi*) the proper time (*kālañca*), fully aware (*sampajāno*) and mindful (*patissato*).

#### 2.39. Usabha (2nd)

**Ambapallavasaṅkāsaṁ, aṁse katvāna cīvaraṁ;\
Nisinno hatthigīvāyaṁ, gāmaṁ piṇḍāya pāvisiṁ.**

**Hatthikkhandhato oruyha, saṁvegaṁ alabhiṁ tadā;\
Sohaṁ ditto tadā santo, patto me āsavakkhayo.**

Having placed (*katvāna*) upon my shoulder (*aṁse*) the robe (*cīvaraṁ*) resembling a tender mango-sprout (*ambapallavasaṅkāsaṁ*); seated (*nisinno*) upon the neck of an elephant (*hatthigīvāyaṁ*), I entered (*pāvisiṁ*) the village (*gāmaṁ*) for alms-food (*piṇḍāya*).

Having descended (*oruyha*) from the elephant\'s shoulder (*hatthikkhandhato*), I experienced (*alabhiṁ*) spiritual shock / profound agitation over the material state (*saṁvegaṁ*) right then (*tadā*); that very I (*sohaṁ*), formerly arrogant (*ditto*), then became peaceful (*santo*), and the destruction of all impurities (*āsavakkhayo*) was attained (*patto*) by me (*me*).

**Commentary**: **saṁvega**

**Saṁvega** is indeed the primary operational catalyst for breaking the identification with the material matrix (*loka / prakṛti*). It is not a passive emotional sorrow, but a violent, purifying shock that forces the conscious subject (*attā / citta*) to wake up to its entrapment within a temporary physical vessel (*geha* / *tuccho kāyo*). **Prefix: *Saṁ-*:** Intensification, completeness, total, together, thoroughly.

***Vega /vij** /vijā:*

- **Pāṇinian Class:** *Tudādi-gaṇa* (*vijī bhayacalanayoḥ* --- \"to fear, to tremble, to shake, to recoil, to dart away quickly\").

- **Primary Meaning:** To tremble violently, to recoil in repulsion, to startle, to whip or impel forward.

- **Cognates:** Sanskrit *vega* (velocity, force, current, momentum, shockwave); Pāḷi *vigga* / *saṁvigga*; Proto-Indo-European *\*weig-* (to bend, yield, turn away; cf. English *shake*, *vanquish*, *wicked*).

Literally, **saṁvega** means **\"a powerful, violent shuddering,\" \"a radical recoiling from danger,\"** or **\"an intense inward velocity/impulse.\"** It describes the exact physiological and spiritual moment when the *citta* experiences an intense, shocking tremor upon recognizing the trap of material existence, causing it to **recoil sharply (*vij*)** away from *saṃsāra* and charge forward toward liberation.

**A. Pāḷi Canon Examples**

**1. Dhammapada 144 (The Thoroughbred Horse Metaphor):** *Assu yathā bhadro kasānitatto, ātāpino saṁvegino bhavātha; Saddhāya sīlena ca vīriyena ca, dhammānucārena samādhisampannā; Vijjācaraṇasampannā satimanto, jahissatha dukkhamidaṁ anantakaṁ.*

Like a noble fine-bred horse (assu bhadro) struck by the whip (kasānitatto), be ardent (ātāpino) and filled with spiritual urgency (saṁvegino bhavātha); by faith (*Saddhā*), *sīla*, spiritual energy (*vīriya*), and walking in accordance with the Dhamma, perfect in *samādhi* and clear vision, mindful, you will abandon this endless suffering (dukkhamidaṁ anantakaṁ).

**Structural Significance:** *Saṁvega* is directly compared to the sharp sting of a whip (*kasā*) that makes a noble horse leap into top speed (*vega*).

**2. Sutta Nipāta 3.2 / Sn 935 (Attadaṇḍa Sutta --- The Shuddering World):** *Saṁviggaṁ mumhi disvāna, taṁ me esaṁ suṇātha ve; Phandamānaṁ pajaṁ disvā, macche appodake yathā; Aññamaññehiabyāruddhe, disvā maṁ bhayamāvisi.*

Having seen the shuddering world (saṁviggaṁ disvāna), hear this from me: seeing people flopping about like fish in shallow water (macche appodake yathā), locked in conflict with one another, fear (bhayam) entered me.

**Structural Significance:** The Buddha describes his own initial awakening prior to renunciation as an intense experience of **saṁvigga**---seeing living beings like fish writhing/ struggling in shallow water invoked direct existential horror (*bhaya*), compelling his departure into the forest.

**B. Vedic & Upanishadic Examples:** In Vedic Sanskrit, the root **vij** appears with prefixes like **saṁ-** (*saṁvijate*) or **pra-** (*pravijate*) to describe the soul recoil from the elemental forces of nature or material bondage.

**1. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 6.6 (Transcendence Over the World-Tree):** *Sa vṛkṣakālākṛtibhiḥ paro\'nyo, yasmāt prapañcaḥ parivartate\'yam; Dharmāvahaṁ pāpanudaṁ bhageśaṁ, jñātvātmasthamamṛtaṁ viśvadhāma. (Parallel Epic passage MBh 12.239: **Saṁviggā bhītāḥ saṁsārāt\...**)*

He is beyond the tree of world-existence, beyond time and form, from whom this manifest universe evolves and turns; knowing Him who brings righteousness and dispels evil, who abides within one\'s own soul-spirit (ātmasthaṁ), one attains the immortal abode.

**Mahābhārata Commentary/Parallel (MBh 12.240.5):** *Saṁvignamanāḥ saṁsārāt, tyaktvā putradāraṁ dhanaṁ;*

*\"With a mind violently shaken by existential shock (saṁvigna-manāḥ) over the cycle of rebirth, having abandoned children, wife, and wealth\...\"*

**Structural Significance:** The Sanskrit **saṁvigna-manāḥ** (*saṁ-vij* + *manas*) is the exact morphological equivalent of Pāḷi *saṁviggamānasa*, marking the decisive moment a seeker flees material domesticity.

**C. Jain Ardhamāgadhī Examples:** In the Jain Prakrit Canon, the term appears as **saṁvega** (संवेग) or **saṁvigga-māṇasa**, standing as one of the fundamental prerequisites for soul-purification (*jīva-visuddhi*).

**1. Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.2 (Uttarajjhayaṇa Sutta --- The Fruits of Saṁvega):** *Saṁvegeṇaṁ bhaṁte! Jīvo kiṁ jaṇayai? Saṁvegeṇaṁ anuttaraṁ dhamma-saḍḍhaṁ jaṇayai; Anuttaraṁ dhamma-saḍḍhaṁ jaṇayitvā, ananturāyaṁ nirjarā-maggaṁ uvisampajjai.*

\"Venerable Sir, through **saṁvega** (*saṁvegeṇaṁ*), what does the soul (*jīvo*) produce?\" \"Through **saṁvega**, the soul produces unexcelled faith/conviction in the *dhamma* (*anuttaraṁ dhamma-saḍḍhaṁ jaṇayai*); having produced unexcelled faith in the *dhamma*, it enters without obstruction onto the path of total karma-shedding (*nirjarā-maggaṁ*).\"

**Structural Significance:** The Jain Canon explicitly sets **saṁvega** as the direct engine that produces *nirjarā* (the total burning off of karma-particles).

**2. Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.2.1 (Āyāraṅga Sutta --- The Shuddering Ascetic):** *Saṁvigge hiyaya-mohaṁ chittaṁ, dāūṇaṁ tavo-dhanaṁ; Gabbha-vāsaṁ aivikkanto, kamma-rajaṁ vidhamai. "*The one who is spiritually shaken (*saṁvigge*), having cut through delusion (*hiya-mohaṁ*) in the heart-mind *chitta*, relying on the primordial spiritual force of *tapas* (*tavo-dhanaṁ*); completely stepping beyond dwelling in a womb (*gabbha-vāsaṁ aivikkanto*), blows away and dispels (*vidhamai*) the karma-dust (*kamma-rajaṁ*).\"

**Structural Significance:** Connects **saṁvigga** directly to **vidhamai** (blowing away karma-dust) and stepping beyond womb-birth (*gabbha-vāsaṁ aivikkanto* / *jāti punappunaṁ*).

#### 2.40. Kappaṭakura

**Ayamiti kappaṭo kappaṭakuro, Acchāya atibharitāya;\
Amataghaṭikāyaṁ dhammakaṭamatto, Katapadaṁ jhānāni ocetuṁ.**

**Mā kho tvaṁ kappaṭa pacālesi, Mā tvaṁ upakaṇṇamhi tāḷessaṁ;\
Na hi tvaṁ kappaṭa mattamaññāsi, Saṅghamajjhamhi pacalāyamāno.**

Here is this fraud (*ayam-iti kappaṭo*), full of deceit and wicked cunning (*kappaṭakuro*), with a belly overfilled with food (*acchāya atibharitāya*); He is merely a wicker-basket of religious talk for the jar of the Deathless (*amataghaṭikāyaṁ dhammakaṭamatto*), having made a mockery (*katapadaṁ*) of diminishing the *jhānas* (*jhānāni ocetuṁ*).

Do not, O fraud (*mā kho tvaṁ kappaṭa*), shake in sluggishness (*pacālesi*), do not make me strike you on the ear (*mā tvaṁ upakaṇṇamhi tāḷessaṁ*); For you, O fraud (*na hi tvaṁ kappaṭa*), did not know your proper measure (*mattamaññāsi*), sleepy (*pacalāyamāno*) right in the middle of the monastic community (*saṅghamajjhamhi*).

**Commentary**: Rejection of Hypocrisy

This verse serves as a direct polemical reminder against spiritual pretense: wearing robes without internal mastery--- **a False Renunciant (*Kappaṭa*)**

This sharp, unsparing verse exposes the precise pathology of a false renunciant who has infiltrated the monastic community (**saṅghamajjhamhi**) not to uproot craving, but to exploit religion for bodily comfort.

**The Stuffed Belly (*Acchāya atibharitāya*):** Instead of living on minimal alms-food (*uñchāpattāgate ratā*) to starve the material substrate (**upadhi**), this fraudulent monk (*kappaṭa*) stuffs his stomach. Food inflation feeds *rāga* (sensual craving) and *moha* (delusion).

**The Wicker-Basket of Talk (*Dhammakaṭamatto*):** He treats the profound teachings of the *Tathāgata* and the jar of the Deathless (*amataghaṭikāyaṁ*) as mere vocal performance---a wicker basket used to carry superficial religious talk without embodying the internal architectural rigor of **Samādhi**.

**Dismantling the *Jhānas* (*Jhānāni ocetuṁ*):** Through laziness and indulgence, he cuts down or destroys his capacity for the deep meditative concentrations (*jhānas*), abandoning the structural gateway required to exit the physical universe.

**Physical Indiscipline and Public Reprimand:** The second stanza shifts from internal critique to immediate behavioral exposure:

**Sleepy in the *Saṅgha* (*Pacalāyamāno saṅghamajjhamhi*):** Instead of maintaining unremitting energy (**āraddhavīriyā**) and keeping the inner soul-spirit (*attā*) firmly established in *samādhi* (**susamāhito**), the fraud collapses into drowsiness right in front of the community.

**The Threat of the Warning (*Mā tvaṁ upakaṇṇamhi tāḷessaṁ*):** The senior master issues a stern, uncompromising warnings or verbal reprimands (such as threatening to strike near the ear, *upakaṇṇamhi tāḷessaṁ*). There is no sentimental coddling. Sluggishness, gluttony, and hypocrisy endanger the entire monastic discipline and block the path to the **antima-sarīra** (final body).

**The Hierarchy of Correction:** The authorized methods for handling a rule-breaking or drowsy renunciant (*kappaṭa*) involved formal reprimand (*codanā*), suspension from certain duties, or expulsion from the monastery if the individual proved incorrigible.

While the senior renunciant in the verse uses aggressive, unsparing rhetoric to expose the fraud\'s hypocrisy and sluggishness, physical violence was barred by monastic code. The uncompromising rigor was entirely mental and verbal: stripping away false pretense, demanding unremitting energy (*āraddhavīriyā*), and forcing the practitioner to anchor their inner soul-spirit (*attā*) in absolute Concentration (*Samādhi*) to escape the wheel of reincarnation.

#### 2.41. Kassapa the Prince

**Aho buddhā aho dhammā, aho no satthu sampadā;\
Yattha etādisaṁ dhammaṁ, sāvako sacchikāhiti.**

**Asaṅkheyyesu kappesu, sakkāyādhigatā ahū;\
Tesamayaṁ pacchimako, carimoyaṁ samussayo;\
Jātimaraṇasaṁsāro, natthi dāni punabbhavo.**

Oh, the Awakened Ones! (*aho buddhā*) Oh, the Dhamma! (*aho dhammā*) Oh, the magnificent perfection of our Teacher! (*aho no satthu sampadā*) In (*yattha*) such a profound Dhamma (*etādisaṁ dhammaṁ*), a true disciple (*sāvako*) will directly realize (*sacchikāhiti*).

Through incalculable eons (*asaṅkheyyesu kappesu*), taking on a true-body (*sa-kāya*) \[wrong view\] (*sa-kāya-adhigatā ahū*); Of those \[bodies\] (*tesam*), this is the final one (*ayaṁ pacchimako*); this is the last physical heap (*carimoyaṁ samussayo*); The cycle of birth, decay, and death is ended (*jātimaraṇasaṁsāro*); there is now no further worldly becoming (*natthi dāni punabbhavo*).

**Commentary**: **The Proclamation of the Final Body (*Antima-Sarīra*)**

This verse represents the victorious roar (*sīhanāda*) of an Arahant who has completely severed the wrong-view of the true-body (*sa-kāya*), will not treat the physical body as the human essence to cling-on.

**The Weight of the Past (*Asaṅkheyyesu kappesu*):** Across endless cosmic eons (*kappesu*), the *citta* was trapped by the wrong view to treat the physical body as something real and true (**sa-/sat**), repeatedly acquiring (**adhigatā**) a physical body (**kāya**). Each *kāya* was actually a temporary manifestation of the true human essence of **attā,** born of ignorance and craving. Now this ignorance and craving for another *kaya* is cut off by the Dhamma.

**The Absolute Terminal Point (*Pacchimako / Carimoyaṁ samussayo*):** The renunciant explicitly identifies the present physical vehicle not as something true, but only a **physical heap and now is the last one** (*carimoyaṁ samussayo*). It is the absolute last structural vessel (**pacchimako**) that will ever be generated.

#### 2.42. Dhammapāla

**Yo have daharo bhikkhu, yuñjati buddhasāsane;\
Jāgaro sa hi suttesu, amoghaṁ tassa jīvitaṁ.**

**Tasmā saddhañca sīlañca, pasādaṁ dhammadassanaṁ;\
Anuyuñjetha medhāvī, saraṁ buddhāna sāsanan"ti.**

Indeed, whichever young bhikkhu (*yo have daharo bhikkhu*) applies himself (*yuñjati*) in the dispensation of the Awakened One (*buddhasāsane*); He who is vigilant (*jāgaro sa hi*) even among those who sleep (*suttesu*), his life is not lived in vain (*amoghaṁ tassa jīvitaṁ*).

Therefore, faith (*tasmā saddhañca*), and *sīla*, clarity of mind (*pasādaṁ*), and the direct seeing of the Dhamma (*dhammadassanaṁ*); Let the wise man (*medhāvī*) continually apply himself to these (*anuyuñjetha*), remembering the dispensation of the Awakened Ones (*saraṁ buddhāna sāsanan*).

#### 2.43. Brahmāli

**Kassindriyāni samathaṅgatāni, Assā yathā sārathinā sudantā;\
Pahīnamānassa anāsavassa, Devāpi kassa pihayanti tādinoti.**

**Mayhindriyāni samathaṅgatāni, Assā yathā sārathinā sudantā;\
Pahīnamānassa anāsavassa, Devāpi mayhaṁ pihayanti tādino.**

Whose faculties (*kassa-indriyāni*) have attained total pacification (*samathaṅgatāni*), like horses (*assā*) thoroughly tamed (*sudantā*) by a charioteer (*sārathinā*)? Of him who has abandoned conceit (*pahīnamānassa*) and is free from the defilements (*anāsavassa*), even the *devas* envy (*pihayanti*) such a steadfast one (*tādino*).

My faculties (*mayha-indriyāni*) have attained total pacification (*samathaṅgatāni*), like horses (*assā*) thoroughly tamed (*sudantā*) by a charioteer (*sārathinā*); Of me who have abandoned conceit (*pahīnamānassa*) and am free from the defilements (*anāsavassa*), even the *devas* envy (*pihayanti*) such a steadfast one (*mayhaṁ tādino*).

#### 2.44. Mogharāja

**Chavipāpaka cittabhaddaka, Mogharāja satataṁ samāhito;\
Hemantikasītakālarattiyo, Bhikkhu tvaṁsi kathaṁ karissasi.**

**Sampannasassā magadhā, kevalā iti me sutaṁ;\
Palālacchannako seyyaṁ, yathaññe sukhajīvino.**

\[You whose\] skin is withered away (*chavipāpaka*) \[while your\] *citta* is noble and refined (*cittabhaddaka*), O Mogharāja, continually established in *samādhi* (*satataṁ samāhito*); During the freezing winter nights (*hemantikasītakālarattiyo*), O bhikkhu (*bhikkhu tvaṁsi*), how will you manage (*kathaṁ karissasi*)?

The region of Magadha is rich with abundant crops (*sampannasassā magadhā*), entire and whole, thus have I heard (*kevalā iti me sutaṁ*); You could lie down covered in straw (*palālacchannako seyyaṁ*), just like other happy soul-bearers (jīvino) (*yathaññe sukhajīvino*). \[**jīvino** = *\"of the soul-bearer\"* or nominative plural: *\"soul-bearers\"*), it denotes **the possessor of *jīva*:** the soul / living core / animating life-principle)\]

#### 2.45. Visākhapañcālaputta

**Na ukkhipe no ca parikkhipe pare, Na okkhipe pāragataṁ na eraye;\
Na cattavaṇṇaṁ parisāsu byāhare, Anuddhato sammitabhāṇi subbato.**

**Susukhumanipuṇatthadassinā, Matikusalena nivātavuttinā;\
Saṁsevitavuddhasīlinā, Nibbānaṁ na hi tena dullabhan"ti.**

One should not exalt oneself (*na ukkhipe*) nor demean others (*no ca parikkhipe pare*); one should not disparage (*na okkhipe*) nor provoke (*na eraye*) one who has crossed over to the other shore (*pāragataṁ*); One should not self-boasting (*na cattavaṇṇaṁ*) among the crowd (*parisāsu byāhare*); unagitated / free from mental restlessness (*anuddhato*), restrained in speech (*sammitabhāṇi*), well keeping vows (*subbato*).

By one who sees the exceedingly subtle and refined reality (*susukhuma-nipuṇa-attha-dassinā*), who is skilled in thinking/ analytical intellect (*mati-kusalena*), who is humble in demeanor (*nivātavuttinā*); And who associates closely with those of mature sīla (*saṁsevita-vuddha-sīlinā*)---by such Nibbāna (*nibbānaṁ*) is indeed not difficult to attain (*na hi tena dullabhaṁ*).

#### 2.46. Cūḷaka

**Nadanti morā susikhā supekhuṇā, Sunīlagīvā sumukhā sugajjino;\
Susaddalā cāpi mahāmahī ayaṁ, Subyāpitambu suvalāhakaṁ nabhaṁ.**

**Sukallarūpo sumanassa jhāyataṁ, Sunikkamo sādhu subuddhasāsane;\
Susukkasukkaṁ nipuṇaṁ sududdasaṁ, Phusāhi taṁ uttamamaccutaṁ padan.**

The crest-ornamented peacocks (*morā susikhā*), with beautiful feathers (*supekhuṇā*), deep blue necks (*sunīlagīvā*), lovely faces (*sumukhā*), and sweet cries (*sugajjino*), are calling out joyfully; And this great earth (*ayam mahāmahī*) is covered with fresh young grass (*susaddalā cāpi*), while the sky (*nabhaṁ*) is filled wide with well-watered, heavy rain-clouds (*subyāpitambu suvalāhakaṁ*).

With a healthy form (*sukallarūpo*), of a refined mind (*sumanassa*), meditating deeply (*jhāyataṁ*); Having made excellent renunciation (*sunikkamo sādhu*) in the dispensation of the Awakened One (*subuddhasāsane*); Touch that supreme, unshakeable, deathless (*phusāhi taṁ uttamamaccutaṁ*) footing/place \[*Nibbāna*\] (*pada*) ---which is exceedingly pure, subtle, fine, and difficult to see (*susukkasukkaṁ nipuṇaṁ sududdasaṁ*).

#### 2.47. Anūpama

**Nandamānāgataṁ cittaṁ, sūlamāropamānakaṁ;\
Tena teneva vajasi, yena sūlaṁ kaliṅgaraṁ.**

**Tāhaṁ cittakaliṁ brūmi, taṁ brūmi cittadubbhakaṁ;\
Satthā te dullabho laddho, mānatthe maṁ niyojayī.**

The *citta*, drawn toward delight (*nanda-mānāgataṁ*), is like one being hoisted onto a sharp execution stake (*sūlamāropamānakaṁ*); With that very \[*citta*\] you wander here and there (*tena teneva vajasi*), toward where the stake and the dry log lie (*yena sūlaṁ kaliṅgaraṁ*).

Therefore, I declare that *citta* a defeat (*tāhaṁ cittakaliṁ brūmi*), I declare that *citta* a treacherous betrayer (*taṁ brūmi cittadubbhakaṁ*); Though you have obtained the Teacher, who is rare to find (*satthā te dullabho laddho*), you yoke me \[into service\] for the sake of pride/vanity (*mānatthe maṁ niyojayī*).

**Commentary: An Internal Courtroom Scene**

The **attā** (the inner soul-spirit core, the true subject operating through the body) is directly confronting, exposing, and indicting the **citta** for treason.

**The Dynamics of the Accusation:**

1)  **The Attā as the Betrayed Sovereign:** The *attā* entered the dispensation of the rare Teacher (*satthā te dullabho laddho*) to achieve a permanent liberational exit from the *loka* into the *Nibbāna-dhātu*.

2)  **The Citta as the Hijacker:** Instead of serving that liberation, the *citta* acted as a rogue agent (*cittakali*, *cittadubbhakaṁ*). Driven by its addiction to sensory delight (*nandamāna*), it hijacked the physical vehicle and forced the *attā*---and the practitioner\'s entire existence (*maṁ*)---to labor for petty ego-gratification and social vanity (*mānatthe maṁ niyojayī*).

This internal courtroom scene highlights why the renunciant needs to tame the *citta.* It must be reined in through ***samādhi***, and starved of material fuel (**upadhi**) so that the *attā* can finally break free from the womb-matrix (*yoni*).

#### 2.48. Vajjita

**Saṁsaraṁ dīghamaddhānaṁ, gatīsu parivattisaṁ;\
Apassaṁ ariyasaccāni, andhabhūto puthujjano.**

**Tassa me appamattassa, saṁsārā vinaḷīkatā;\
Sabbā gatī samucchinnā, natthi dāni punabbhavo.**

For a long, extended stretch of time (*saṁsaraṁ dīghamaddhānaṁ*), I wandered and revolved (*parivattisaṁ*) through various destinations / realms of existence (*gatīsu*); Not seeing the noble truths (*apassaṁ ariyasaccāni*), being blind and shrouded in delusion (*andhabhūto puthujjano*).

\[But now\] for me, who has been spiritually zealous (*tassa me appamattassa*), the roots of *saṁsāra* have been destroyed (*saṁsārā vinaḷīkatā*); All realms of future wandering have been completely uprooted (*sabbā gatī samucchinnā*), and now there is no further worldly becoming / manifestation (*natthi dāni punabbhavo*).

#### 2.49. Sandhita

**Assatthe haritobhāse, saṁvirūḷhamhi pādape;\
Ekaṁ buddhagataṁ saññaṁ, alabhitthaṁ patissato.**

**Ekatiṁse ito kappe, yaṁ saññamalabhiṁ tadā;\
Tassā saññāya vāhasā, patto me āsavakkhayo.**

Beneath the *bodhi* tree \[the sacred fig tree\] (*assatthe pādape*), radiant green (*haritobhāse*), fully grown (*saṁvirūḷhamhi*); I attained (*alabhitthaṁ*) a single perception focused on the Buddha (*ekaṁ buddhagataṁ saññaṁ*), being mindful and recollected (*patissato*).

In the thirty-first eon from this present one (*ekatiṁse ito kappe*), at the time when I attained that perception (*yaṁ saññamalabhiṁ tadā*); By the power and momentum of that perception (*tassā saññāya vāhasā*), the destruction of all taints (*āsavakkhayo*) has been attained by me (*patto me*).

## PART II --- Therīgāthā: Verses of the Elder Bhikkhunis

Step into the living words of the Elder Bhikkhunis.

The **Therīgāthā**---the *Verses of the Elder Bhikkhunis*---preserves the voices of women who entered the Bhikkhuni Saṅgha of **Gautama the Supreme Arahant** (*Mahā Arahant*), undertook the holy life (*brahmacariya*), confronted the conditions of worldly existence, practiced Gautama's true Dhamma, and realized the final goal of liberation from the world. Together with the *Theragāthā*, it forms one of the most precious records of the early Arahanist Saṅgha: not merely a collection of Gautama's teachings about liberation, but the testimony of those who experienced the Path to Liberation themselves.

The *Therīgāthā* is traditionally organized into **sixteen chapters (nipātas)**, arranged primarily according to the number of verses attributed to the individual elders. Within these chapters are the voices of bhikkhunis whose lives could scarcely have been more varied. Some had known wealth and privilege; others poverty and hardship. Some had experienced marriage and motherhood; others had lived through bereavement, separation, aging, or profound personal loss. Some had entered the bhikkhuni life after lives of privilege, while others came seeking freedom after experiences of extraordinary difficulty.

Yet beneath these different circumstances runs a single direction:

**away from worldly bondage and toward liberation from the world.**

The power of the *Therīgāthā* lies partly in the immediacy of these voices. The bhikkhunis do not speak about Gautama's Dhamma as distant observers. They speak as women who had themselves encountered the forces that bind beings to worldly existence. Their verses frequently begin from intensely personal circumstances---grief, desire, attachment, family, the body, youth, beauty, aging, loss, fear, or the impermanence of everything once regarded as secure---and then turn those very experiences into occasions for seeing Gautama's Dhamma of liberation for renunciates.

This gives the *Therīgāthā* a distinctive character alongside the *Theragāthā*.

The Elder Bhikkhus frequently speak from the perspective of **renunciation and mastery**: leaving the household life, entering solitude, overcoming the mind\'s disturbances, conquering Māra, developing concentration, and completing the training. The Elder Bhikkhunis also speak of these things, but their verses often reveal with particular clarity **what had to be relinquished in order to become free from worldly bondages**.

For many of the bhikkhunis, the doorway into the Dhamma is strikingly personal.

A woman who has lost a child encounters the impermanence of life.\
A woman who once depended upon beauty confronts aging.\
A woman bound by sensual attachment discovers the cost of clinging.\
A woman who has experienced grief sees the unsatisfactory nature of worldly existence.\
A woman who has lived under the expectations of family and society discovers another possibility: the freedom of the holy life.

Their stories therefore bring the abstract language of Gautama's Dhamma of liberation into immediate human experience.

The *Therīgāthā* repeatedly demonstrates how **the very experiences that appear to define a person\'s worldly identity can become objects of insight**. Motherhood, marriage, beauty, youth, social status, suffering, grief, and the body are not transformed into permanent sources of meaning. Instead, they become occasions for seeing their worldly-conditioned, changing, and unreliable nature.

This is one of the most powerful features of these verses.

The bhikkhunis do not escape liberation by escaping their human stories. They pass through those stories with increasing clarity and learn to see beyond them.

Their verses can therefore be deeply intimate without being merely autobiographical. A personal story becomes a Dhamma teaching. A memory becomes an examination of worldly attachment. A loss becomes an insight into impermanence of the worldly. An encounter with desire becomes an opportunity for renunciation for sensual cravings. A reflection on the body becomes a means of seeing beyond identification with the body.

Again and again, the personal becomes universal.

This is also why the *Therīgāthā* should not be reduced to a collection of \"women\'s poetry\" in the modern literary sense. Its central concern is not the celebration of female identity. Its central concern is **freedom from bondage to the world**.

The women whose voices we hear here did not enter the bhikkhuni Saṅgha merely to acquire a new social identity. They entered the holy life to undertake the training that leads beyond the world. Their verses repeatedly return to the decisive movement from household life toward renunciation, from worldly attachment toward release from the worldly bondage, from confusion toward clarity, and from the worldly-conditioned toward the unconditioned-by-the-world.

In this respect, the *Therīgāthā* stands beside the *Theragāthā* as an equal testimony to the possibility of liberation from the world.

Yet the two collections should not be read as identical.

The *Theragāthā* often gives us the voice of the forest-dwelling elder, the renunciant celebrating solitude, discipline, meditation, and the conquest of the inner enemy. The *Therīgāthā* often allows us to hear more directly the voice of someone whose renunciation has emerged through the intimate circumstances of family, relationships, motherhood, beauty, aging, grief, and loss.

The difference is not a difference in the **goal**. It is a difference in the **human doorway through which the Path is described**. Both collections lead beyond the world. Both testify to the same possibility of liberation from the universe containing billions of worlds. Both speak of the destruction of sensual craving and the completion of the holy life. Both ultimately point toward the **Deathless Realm of Nibbāna**.

But the *Therīgāthā* makes especially visible something that can easily be overlooked in discussions of early Buddhism: the Path to liberation was not confined to one type of human experience. Gautama's Dhamma of Liberation was encountered within forests and villages, monasteries and households, solitude and family life, privilege and hardship, youth and old age, joy and devastating loss.

The Elder Bhikkhunis brought all of these experiences to the Path---and then learned to go beyond them.

Their verses therefore deserve to be read progressively: first as the words of individual women with particular histories; then as testimony to the transformation of those histories through Gautama's Dhamma; and finally, as declarations of a freedom that transcends the universe where each journey began. This volume follows that movement.

We will explore the historical setting of the early bhikkhuni Saṅgha, the distinctive literary character of the *Therīgāthā*, and Gautama's Dhamma for Liberation from the world embedded within its verses. We will encounter the women behind the verses, their struggles and turning points, their encounters with worldly attachment and loss, and the wisdom that transformed their lives.

But above all, we will listen to what they themselves say.

Their words remind us that liberation does not begin with having a perfect life. It begins when one sees clearly the nature of the life one has been living---and becomes willing to leave worldly-bondage behind.

The women whose voices survive in the *Therīgāthā* did exactly that. They left behind what had bound them. They entered the Path to Liberation from the world. They practiced. They saw. They were liberated from the world.

And now, across more than two millennia, their voices remain.

**These too are the voices of liberation.**

### 2. Dukanipāto --- Chapter of the Twos

#### 2.1. Abhirūpanandā

**Āturaṁ asuciṁ pūtiṁ, passa nande samussayaṁ;**

**Asubhāya cittaṁ bhāvehi,ekaggaṁ susamāhitaṁ.**

**Animittañca bhāvehi, mānānusayamujjaha;**

**Tato mānābhisamayā, upasantā carissasī.**

Look (*passa*) upon this mass piled up together / physical body (*samussayaṁ*), O Nandā---afflicted (*āturaṁ*), impure (*asuciṁ*), and foul-smelling (*pūtiṁ*); Develop (*bhāvehi*) the *citta* on the foul \[body\] (*asubhāya*), unifying it (*ekaggaṁ*) and firmly established in *Samādhi* (*susamāhitaṁ*).

Cultivate the signless (*animittañca bhāvehi*), and completely cast out the underlying tendency to conceit (*māna-anusayam-ujjaha*); Then, from that eradication of conceit (*tato māna-abhisamayā*), you will live (*carissasī*) in supreme peace (*upasantā*).

**Commentary: From Bodily Dukkha to the Deathless**

The physical body (*samussayaṁ*) is inherently bound to *dukkha*---a deteriorating, diseased vessel (*āturaṁ asuciṁ pūtiṁ*) that serves as the gross material manifestation of the *attā*, the inner soul-spirit.

Because the untamed *citta* (mind) takes this physical manifestation / sign as real and uses it to feed ego-conceit (*māna*), the path of liberation requires total structural reversal:

1)  **Cultivating the Signless (*Animitta-bhāvanā*):** This is the direct training and elevation of the inner spirit. By refusing to latch onto the physical sign or external sensory data, the practitioner withdraws attention from conditioned phenomena.

2)  **Unifying the Citta through Concentration (*Samādhi*):** The volatile, treacherous *citta*---which otherwise drags the soul-bearer toward sensory impalement---must be tamed, unified (*ekaggaṁ*), and made excellently concentrated (*susamāhitaṁ*).

3)  **Eradicating Conceit (*Māna*):** Through this deep stabilization, the root of ego and pride (*mānānusaya*) is completely torn out and cast away.

4)  **Attaining Supreme Peace (*Upasantā*):** When conceit and craving are starved of all fuel (*upadhi*), the soul-bearer reaches absolute stillness. This unshakeable, unconditioned state is toward the deathless---permanently terminating all future worldly becoming/ manifestation (*punabbhava*).

#### 2.2. Jentā

**Ye ime satta bojjhaṅgā, maggā nibbānapattiyā;**

**Bhāvitā te mayā sabbe, yathā buddhena desitā.**

**Diṭṭho hi me so bhagavā, antimoyaṁ samussayo;**

**Vikkhīṇo jātisaṁsāro, natthi dāni punabbhavo.**

These seven factors of awakening (*ye ime satta bojjhaṅgā*), which are the path for reaching Nibbāna (*maggā nibbānapattiyā*); All of them have been fully cultivated by me (*bhāvitā te mayā sabbe*), exactly as they were taught by the Buddha (*yathā buddhena desitā*).

Indeed, that Blessed One has been directly seen by me (*diṭṭho hi me so bhagavā*), and this present physical body is my final one (*antimoyaṁ samussayo*); The wandering through birth and death is thoroughly destroyed (*vikkhīṇo jātisaṁsāro*), and now there is no further worldly becoming/ manifestation (*natthi dāni punabbhavo*).

**Commentary**: **Seven Factors of Awakening** (*satta bojjhaṅgā*)

**Sati** (Mindfulness / Recollection): The foundational anchor. It maintains unbroken surveillance over physical and mental phenomena (*dhamma*), a crucial step to tame the *citta* through sense restraint, development of concentration and wisdom.

**Dhamma-vicaya** (Investigation of mental states *dhamma* / analytical discernment)*:* It actively dissects mental phenomena *dhamma* into first principles---distinguishing between wholesome mental structures and unwholesome inputs (*rāga*, *dosa*, *moha*), ensuring the practitioner executes **yoniso manasikāra**.

**Vīriya** (Spiritual Energy)*:* The driving force that powers the renunciant diagnostic. It sustains the internal drive (*appamāda*) required to starve the treacherous *citta* of sensory delight and push past mental sluggishness.

**Pīti** (Rapture / Intense Joy): The surge of wholesome elation that arises naturally when the mind breaks free from coarse sensory cravings. It elevates the consciousness core without tipping into sensual attachment.

**Passaddhi** (Tranquility / Absolute Calm)*:* The settling and cooling of both physical tension and mental agitation. It neutralizes friction, bringing a deep, steady equilibrium to the nervous system and the body.

**Samādhi** (Concentration / Unification of Mind)*:* It pulls the volatile, scattering *citta* into a single-pointed focus (*ekaggaṁ*), anchoring it firmly through the ascending *jhānas* and most importantly to bring forth the primordial spiritual force *tapas*.

**Upekkhā** (Transcendental Release)*:* The state of union with the inner soul-spirit (*ajjhātta*). Having cut away all mental taints and conceit (*māna*), the *citta* becomes tamed and purified, undisturbed by conditioned phenomena or worldly dualities.

#### 2.3. Sumaṇgala's Mother

**Sumuttikā sumuttikā, Sādhumuttikāmhi musalassa;**

**Ahiriko me chattakaṁ vāpi, Ukkhalikā me deḍḍubhaṁ vāti.**

**Rāgañca ahaṁ dosañca, Cicciṭi cicciṭīti vihanāmi;**

**Sā rukkhamūlamupagamma, Aho sukhanti sukhato jhāyāmī.**

Totally liberated, totally liberated am I (*sumuttikā sumuttikā*); well-liberated am I from the pestle (*sādhumuttikāmhi musalassa*); My shameless bald-cap roof-umbrella (*ahiriko me chattakaṁ vāpi*), and my boiling cooking-pot, like a water-snake (*ukkhalikā me deḍḍubhaṁ vāti*).

Worldly craving and attachment (*rāgaṁ*), and worldly survival instincts of fear, anger, hatred, violence or aversion (*dosañca*), I smash, shatter, and blast away (*vihanāmi*) with the sound of crackling and hissing: *cicciṭi cicciṭīti*! Approaching the foot of a tree (*sā rukkhamūlamupagamma*), saying \"Aha, bliss!\" (*aho sukhaṁ*), I meditate absorbed in bliss (*sukhato jhāyāmi*) \[burn away impurities in *jhāna*\].

#### 2.4 Aḍḍhakāsi

**Yāva kāsijanapado, suṅko me tatthako ahu;**

**Taṁ katvā negamo agghaṁ, aḍḍhenagghaṁ ṭhapesi maṁ.**

**Atha nibbindahaṁ rūpe, nibbindañca virajjahaṁ;**

**Mā puna jātisaṁsāraṁ, sandhāveyyaṁ punappunaṁ;**

**Tisso vijjā sacchikatā, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan.**

As far as the Kāsī country (*yāva kāsijanapado*), my valuation there was \[that of\] a costly royal tax/tribute (*suṅko me tatthako ahu*); Having reckoned that, the merchant guild (*taṁ katvā negamo*), fixed my market price (*agghaṁ*) at half the value of the city (*aḍḍhenagghaṁ ṭhapesi maṁ*).

Then, becoming **disenchanted** with form (*atha **nibbinda**haṁ rūpe*), and disenchanted, I grew utterly detached (***nibbinda**ñca virajjahaṁ*); May I never again run through the round of birth and death (*mā puna jātisaṁsāraṁ, sandhāveyyaṁ punappunaṁ*)! The Three Knowledges have been realized (*tisso vijjā sacchikatā*), the dispensation of the Awakened One has been fulfilled (*kataṁ buddhassa sāsanaṁ*).

#### 2.5. Cittā

**Kiñcāpi khomhi kisikā, gilānā bāḷhadubbalā;**

**Daṇḍamolubbha gacchāmi, pabbataṁ abhirūhiya.**

**Saṅghāṭiṁ nikkhipitvāna, pattakañca nikujjiya;**

**Sele khambhesim attānaṁ, tamokhandhaṁ padāliyā.**

Even though I am thin (*kiñcāpi khomhi kisikā*), sick (*gilānā*) and extremely feeble (*bāḷhadubbalā*); Leaning upon a walking stick (*daṇḍamolubbha*), I go (*gacchāmi*), ascending up the mountain (*pabbataṁ abhirūhiya*). Having cast down (*nikkhipitvāna*) my outer monastic robe *saṅghāṭi*, and having turned my alms-bowl upside down (*pattakañca nikujjiya*); Against the rock (*sele*) I propped up my body \[standing like an unbending pillar\] (*khambhesim*), my soul-spirit *attā* has shattered the mass of delusion (*tamokhandhaṁ padāliyā*).

#### 2.6. Mettikā

**Kiñcāpi khomhi dukkhitā, dubbalā gatayobbanā;**

**Daṇḍamolubbha gacchāmi, pabbataṁ abhirūhiya.**

**Nikkhipitvāna saṅghāṭiṁ, pattakañca nikujjiya;**

**Nisinnā camhi selamhi, atha cittaṁ vimucci me;**

**Tisso vijjā anuppattā,kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan.**

Even though I am afflicted with *dukkha* (*kiñcāpi khomhi dukkhitā*), feeble (*dubbalā*), my youth long gone (*gatayobbanā*); Leaning upon a walking stick (*daṇḍamolubbha*), I go (*gacchāmi*), ascending up the mountain (*pabbataṁ abhirūhiya*).

Having laid down (*nikkhipitvāna*) my outer monastic robe *saṅghāṭi*, and having turned my alms-bowl upside down (*pattakañca nikujjiya*); I sat down upon the mountain rock (*nisinnā camhi selamhi*), and thereupon my *citta* was fully liberated (*atha cittaṁ vimucci me*).

The Three Knowledges have been attained (*tisso vijjā anuppattā*), the dispensation of the Awakened One has been fulfilled (*kataṁ buddhassa sāsanaṁ*).

#### 2.7. Mittā

**Cātuddasiṁ pañcadasiṁ, yā ca pakkhassa aṭṭhamī;**

**Pāṭihāriyapakkhañca, aṭṭhaṅgasusamāgataṁ.**

**Uposathaṁ upāgacchiṁ, devakāyābhinandinī;**

**Sājja ekena bhattena, muṇḍā saṅghāṭipārutā;**

**Devakāyaṁ na patthehaṁ, vineyya hadaye daran.**

The fourteenth, the fifteenth \[day\], and also the eighth of the fortnight (*cātuddasiṁ pañcadasiṁ, yā ca pakkhassa aṭṭhamī*); And the miraculous fortnight-observance (*pāṭihāriyapakkhañca*), endowed with the eight precepts and well-assembled (*aṭṭhaṅgasusamāgataṁ*).

I approached the *Uposatha* observance (*uposathaṁ upāgacchiṁ*), delighting in the body of devas (*devakāyā-bhinandinī*); now, today, with a single meal (*sājja ekena bhattena*), shaven-headed, wrapped in *saṅghāṭi* (*muṇḍā saṅghāṭipārutā*); I do not desire deva-body (*devakāyaṁ na patthehaṁ*), having removed fear and anguish within my heart (*vineyya hadaye daran*).

#### 2.8. Abhayā

**Uddhaṁ pādatalā amma, adho ve kesamatthakā;**

**Paccavekkhassumaṁ kāyaṁ, asuciṁ pūtigandhikaṁ.**

**Evaṁ viharamānāya, sabbo rāgo samūhato;**

**Pariḷāho samucchinno, sītibhūtāmhi nibbutā.**

From the soles of the feet upward, mother (*uddhaṁ pādatalā amma*), and downward from the crown of the head (*adho ve kesamatthakā*); Examine this physical body (*paccavekkhassumaṁ kāyaṁ*), \[which is\] foul and stinking of putrid odor (*asuciṁ pūtigandhikaṁ*).

For one dwelling thus (*evaṁ viharamānāya*), all craving and attachment (*sabbo rāgo*) is uprooted (*samūhato*); Feverish burning (*pariḷāho*) is completely severed at the root (*samucchinno*), \[and\] I have become cool (*sītibhūtāmhi*), unbound (*nibbutā*).

**Commentary: From Body Disgust to Liberation**

The operational sequence of liberation begins with an unsparing anatomical autopsy of the physical vehicle (*samussayaṁ*). When the practitioner systematically inspects the form---recognizing it from the soles of the feet to the crown of the head as foul (*asuci*), putrid (*pūtigandhikaṁ*), and inherently bound to *dukkha*---a profound, diagnostic aversion toward the body takes hold.

This disgust is no mere emotional revulsion; it is the razor of analytical discernment (*paññā*) cutting through the illusion of physical form.

1.  **Disenchantment (*Nibbidā*):** Seeing the physical vehicle for what it is shatters the *citta*\'s blind infatuation. This total disillusionment spreads outward, inducing a complete disenchantment (*nibbidā*) toward all worldly manifestations (*loka*), conditioned phenomena, and sensory attachments.

2.  **The Fading of Worldly Passion (*Virāga*):** Starved of its material fuel, *rāga* (worldly craving and attachment) loses its grip. *Virāga* sets in as the internal fever (*pariḷāha*) cools, and the binding passions that chain the soul-spirit (*attā*) to worldly bondage fade away entirely.

3.  **Liberation from the worldly bondage (*Vimutti*):** With all taints (*āsava*) eradicated, conceit (*māna*) uprooted, and the volatile *citta* seized and unified through deep *Samādhi*, final liberation from worldly bondage (*vimutti*) is secured.

With the current physical vehicle as the **final body** (*antima-sarīra*), all future worldly becoming / manifestation (*punabbhava*) is permanently terminated.

#### 2.9. Abhaya's Mother

**Abhaye bhiduro kāyo, yattha satā puthujjanā;**

**Nikkhipissāmimaṁ dehaṁ, sampajānā satīmatī.**

**Bahūhi dukkhadhammehi, appamādaratāya me;**

**Taṇhakkhayo anuppatto, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan.**

This physical body is fragile (*bhiduro kāyo*), wherein ordinary worldlings abide (*yattha satā puthujjanā*); fearlessly (*abhaye*), I shall cast aside this physical frame (*nikkhipissāmimaṁ dehaṁ*), with mindfulness and clear comprehension (*sampajānā satīmatī*). Through manifold painful dhammas (*bahūhi dukkhadhammehi*), by my adherence to spiritual zealousness (***appamāda**-ratāya me*); The destruction of craving has been attained (*taṇhakkhayo anuppatto*), the dispensation of the Awakened One has been fulfilled (*kataṁ buddhassa sāsanaṁ*).

#### 2.10. Sāmātherīgāthā:Verses of the Elder Sāmā

**Catukkhattuṁ pañcakkhattuṁ, vihārā upanikkhamiṁ;**

**Aladdhā cetaso santiṁ, citte avasavattinī;**

**Tassā me aṭṭhamī ratti, yato taṇhā samūhatā.**

**Bahūhi dukkhadhammehi, appamādaratāya me;**

**Taṇhakkhayo anuppatto, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan.**

Four times, five times, from the monastery I went out (*catukkhattuṁ pañcakkhattuṁ, vihārā upanikkhamiṁ*); Not attaining peace of mind (*aladdhā cetaso santiṁ*), \[because\] the *citta* was not under my control (*citte avasavattinī*). \[Yet\] for me, this is the eighth night (*tassā me aṭṭhamī ratti*), since craving was completely uprooted (*yato taṇhā samūhatā*). Through manifold painful dhammas (*bahūhi dukkhadhammehi*), by my adherence to spiritual zealousness (***appamāda**-ratāya me*); The destruction of craving has been attained (*taṇhakkhayo anuppatto*), the dispensation of the Awakened One has been fulfilled (*kataṁ buddhassa sāsanaṁ*).

## About the Author

**Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera** is a Canadian Theravāda bhikkhu, meditation teacher, religious scholar, author, and founding Ācariya of **Arahanism---the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**. His work is dedicated to preserving, examining, and transmitting Gautama\'s original liberation teaching, with particular emphasis on restoring renunciation and Arahantship to the centre of religious life. Rooted in the Theravāda tradition, Arahanism upholds the Sutta and Vinaya foundations of the Pāḷi Canon while critically examining subsequent doctrinal developments and interpretations in light of what it understands as Gautama\'s original teaching.

Ordained as a bhikkhu in 2013 at Pa-Auk Forest Monastery and Meditation Center in Myanmar, Bhante Mudita undertook intensive training in *samatha--vipassanā* meditation, Pāḷi Canon study, and monastic discipline. He subsequently spent five years practising within the Thai forest tradition, deepening his training through solitude, simplicity, renunciation, and sustained contemplative practice. Returning to Canada in November 2024, he dedicated himself to the systematic teaching and development of Arahanism.

His scholarship encompasses early Buddhist literature, canonical studies, meditation, renunciant spirituality, and comparative religious research. Through translations, extensive commentaries, and doctrinal studies, he examines the teachings and spiritual experiences preserved in ancient religious texts. His particular interest in the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* arises from their importance as first-person testimonies of early Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs who undertook the renunciant life and pursued liberation. Rather than approaching these verses merely as historical or literary compositions, he seeks to understand their practical significance: how these elders trained, overcame worldly attachments, cultivated the mind, and described their progress towards liberation from worldly bondage.

His broader publications include studies of Gautama\'s Liberation Path, the *Dhammapada*, the *Suttanipāta*, meditation and Samādhi, the *Bhagavad-Gītā*, and early Christianity. Across these diverse subjects, his research is guided by an interest in renunciation, spiritual discipline, and the ultimate purpose of religious practice.

At the heart of his teaching is the progressive cultivation of **Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā**, leading towards **Nibbidā, Virāga, and Vimutti**. Through ethical discipline, restraint of the senses, concentration, and liberating wisdom, the practitioner works towards the complete eradication of *rāga* (worldly craving and attachment), *dosa* (worldly survival instincts of fear, anger, aversion, hatred and hostility), and *moha* (ignorance and delusion, not knowing the path to liberation from worldly bondage). Within Arahanism, this training culminates in the liberation of *citta* from worldly bondage and the realization of *Nibbāna-dhātu*, the Deathless realm.

As Founder and Spiritual Head of the **Arahanist Order of Canada**, Bhante Mudita seeks to establish a living Canadian Theravāda lineage dedicated to canonical study, monastic discipline, meditation, progressive renunciation, and the training of future generations of renunciants. His work also encompasses religious education, scholarly publication, and the open digital preservation of Arahanism literature through international research and archival platforms.

His life\'s work is dedicated to preserving and transmitting **the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**, and to encouraging sincere practitioners to study the testimonies of the ancient elders, undertake the discipline of renunciation, and pursue the ultimate goal of the path: liberation from worldly bondage and the realization of the Deathless.
